--- Page 3 ---
September 1, 1972

Today, Parvati and Gaumati, you both came to my birthday celebration. It was you who made it festive, but you did not know—right then, at that very moment, while there was singing and storytelling in this room, while I was laughing, I was, in truth, leaving this place. The current of time surged within my mind, it touched me, and I traveled—not toward the future, but into the past.

Now it is past midnight, perhaps two o’clock—I am standing on the veranda—from here, I cannot see the full sky, only half of the Saptarshi constellation gazes at me like an eternal question. Questions, questions, questions—why have these questions, after so many ages, come to me again tonight? Why did such an incident occur in my life, one that was never necessary? I see now, it has neither a beginning nor an end.

The stars in the sky are bright, witnesses to so many human agonies. My whole mind is drawn to that sky—I feel as if I am not here, and yet I am here! Can I go anywhere from here—this is my familiar world. In the bedroom, my husband sleeps peacefully. How utterly assured he is about me, though he does not know me fully, yet how deeply he loves me, how much he trusts me! I am his everything. His world revolves around me, and yet he is not my everything—surely, in some way, he knows this, but it brings him no resentment; nor do I feel any. My life is, in every way, full to the brim. Whatever I had to give to this household, I feel I have given; the glory of love, I feel I have known that too, its miraculous, upward offering mingled with reverence and worship for my Guru, has fulfilled me. Yet, since yesterday, why has the flavor of my life changed? What a terrible dissatisfaction, like the hem of a barren desert spreading over my lush, beautiful world! I know that beneath it, everything remains as it was. Still, in his subconscious, I am real—and upstairs, near my parents’ bed, my grandson sleeps; when he comes down tomorrow morning, his soft little hands will embrace me just as always—my world remains tender, vibrant, green. Yet why does molten lava flow over all this, why do I feel the heat of its breath upon my face? No, not lava—perhaps molten gold—it is not something to be cast away, for there is joy in it, it has its own worth. I know, this will not turn to ash, for “when all is ashes, what remains”—this is that remainder.

Yet for two days now, what pain, what terrible pain I have been feeling. What kind of pain? Like when, “gazing upon beauty, hearing sweet sounds,” the mind grows restless, and one remembers friendships from other births—“the heart grows eager, though the creature is happy”—is it that kind of pain? No, not even that, for this is not a story of another life—this is from just the other day, only forty-two years ago. I have crossed only forty-two years and returned—this is a long time for a human, but for eternity?

Time has no position—what is before it, what is behind, what is beside? Where is its beginning or end? Only for me, the endless, beginningless eternity is broken—only to reveal me, it is limited, but suddenly, the boundary of forty-two years has been lifted—I am immersed in eternity, with neither before nor after—I stand, fixed and unmoving, in 1972, and yet in 1930.

How did it happen, on what date did it happen?

On the morning of September 1, 1972. The day before, my childhood friend Gopal called me and said, “Amrita, do you remember your Euclid?”

“Yes, a little—why?”

--- Page 4 ---
"From their country, a gentleman has come, an acquaintance of yours. Euclid was your father's student, but he is no more, so this gentleman wishes to meet you instead."

A small spark of joy flashed through my body and mind for a moment.

Gopal was urging from the other end of the telephone—"Why are you silent? Shall I bring him over?"

"No—no, I'll go myself. Give me his address."

It had been raining since morning that day. Somehow, I managed to find a taxi and reached the destination. I kept wondering why I had come at all! What use is it to me to know the news of someone who doesn't even reply to my letters? Yet, curiosity refuses to let go. I thought, am I too curious? Is it so wrong to want to know about someone I once knew?

For the sake of honesty, I must admit, like any ordinary woman, I had dressed up a little, put on a nice saree. Yet, standing before the mirror, I felt a deep sadness—my face had grown so unattractive. Nothing withstands the tyranny of time—it shatters everything to dust—but is that all? Does time only make things old, does it not also make things new? My face may have grown old, but my mind? The mind that today seeks news of Mircea Euclid—that curious, eager mind is new, too, a creation of time.

Once, I wrote—

"The time that was behind  
The time that returns to face me—  
With an unfamiliar face, adorned in a star-studded robe—  
Who gave it ornaments, who bestowed its jewels,  
That fleeting springtime of transient wealth?  
In the untouched current of its formless passion,  
Who made flowers bloom?  
From the ocean of emptiness, in a moment, it gathers foam,  
The deathly illusion of a wave, in a timeless, broken hour."

When I wrote this, I did not know how the past comes to stand before us—how the old becomes new, or that to think the new is old is itself an illusion!

Sitting in the car, I laughed—how amusing, really, was it necessary for me to dress up? Why this regret over my appearance? After all, I was not going to meet Mircea Euclid, but rather an unknown man from his country.

The door was already open. The man was writing, tapping his pen on the table. His complexion was dusky, not the pale white of Europeans, his body compact, his face marked with intelligence. Hearing my presence, he stood up and said, "I am Sergei Sebastian." Then, extending his hand, he took my right hand and kissed its back—a custom in their country. This very familiar gesture felt like the echo of footsteps from a long-forgotten era.

"Are you Amrita?"

I knew that the foreigner who spoke, the one looking at me, was not seeing the Amrita of 1972. The wonder in his brief question would not be awakened by the Amrita of today. Now, my face is lined with wrinkles, my hair streaked with white, my body devoid of grace. He looked at me with a steady gaze, but his vision passed beyond me, seeing far away—the Amrita of 1930.

"Do you know me?"

"Everyone in our country knows you. You are the heroine of our fairy tales."

"Because of Mircea's book?"

"Yes, his book. He wanted to marry you, but your father refused, you both—"

--- Page 5 ---
“Hindu—he’s Christian.”
“Nonsense.”
“What nonsense?”
“Hindu-Christian, none of that matters. That’s just his arrogance.”

“Forty-two years have passed—now and then I’ve heard about that book, but I never asked anyone what it was—novel, poetry, or essay—tell me today, my friend, what’s in that book?” I ask the question, and I’m smiling. How easily I could ask now, why didn’t I all these years? She is just another Amrita. What does my relationship with the people from forty years ago matter now? Does the consequence of their actions touch me anymore? After twelve years, even a murderer is no longer punished. Why should I feel shame? Shame, because I am a moralist. I have thought deeply about right and wrong, about what should and should not be. I judge harshly. I do not indulge weakness. Even my friends do not tell me their stories of weakness. I sit on the high seat of honor, and I have never spared myself either. Whenever Mircea came to mind, I frowned at myself. Why did such an incident happen, it would have been better if it hadn’t—then shame, bitter shame, would engulf my consciousness, and I would exile his memory deep into my subconscious. But today, how easily I asked about that book. There is no hesitation in my mind.

Sergei said, “That book is an autobiographical novel—” He cannot speak English well, so he began to tell the story in broken, halting English.

“You know, through that book, people in our country were astonished to learn about India, to know Calcutta.” As I listen to his voice, familiar names come to mind, and my heart is struck, little by little. As if one by one, the shutters are being opened—the room inside is dark, but I know what’s there. I want to enter, but fear tightens my breath.

“Sergei, tell me honestly, am I in that book?”

He smiles gently, and then, with his continental accent, lingering on the ‘t’s, he says, “First she loved a tree.”

I am startled. Suddenly, a lamp of memory flares in my chest. Yes, yes, yes. “Tell me more, Sergei, is there anything in it that would shame me?”

Sergei lowers his head and says, “He wrote that at night you would come to his room. Of course, I don’t see anything shameful in that.”

I am stunned—“What a disaster! What an injustice! Believe me, Sergei, it’s not true, not at all true.”

He tries to reassure me, “That’s clear, that’s why he couldn’t describe you, he wrote you stood in the darkness like a statue—he had no other way, he was in such pain then.”

I feel helpless, as if I am drowning. I can prepare my mind to accept an unpleasant truth, but the blow of an unpleasant lie is unbearable.

Extinguishing his cigarette in the ashtray, this good-hearted foreigner says, “Forgive me, I told you everything, I had to speak the truth.”

“Tell me, Sergei, why did he write the book using my name?”

“He couldn’t escape the bond of your name, at that time he was in pain, great pain—you will be swept away in tears if you read the book.”

“And for that, he would give me such a false stain?”

“That was his imagination, at that moment it was his only way to escape his suffering. He still hasn’t forgotten you.” I feel lost, what can I say—“What a strange logic you have.

--- Page 6 ---
Sergei, seeking refuge in lies to escape pain! And if his love was truly so deep, then why did he abandon me and leave at just a stern word from my father? Does that ever happen? Have you ever heard of such a thing?”

“It doesn’t happen? Haven’t I heard? History is full of such examples. You were a sixteen-year-old girl then, he was a twenty-three-year-old youth—ah, your father ruined your life.”

A jolt of electricity shot through my body. What is this man saying! “Sergei, what do you know of my life! Who has the power to ruin my life! My life is rich. With children and grandchildren, I have an ideal family. I have received so much love, so much respect. Above all, my Guru—whom your friend envies so much—my mind, blessed by his extraordinary affection, has found a love so transcendental, so beyond words and thought. Do you think, in these fifty-eight years of my life, there is any place for that one twenty-three-year-old boy?”

I was speaking with great agitation, the veins in my head throbbing, and a fear rising in me—at my age, what if I have a stroke!

Sergei looked at me, his face troubled, wearing an awkward smile.

“No, no, not ruined—your life would have been different.”

“That you may say. Different, that’s all!”

“I still have your first book of poems. When he had to leave the country, I took his entire library, and your book was among them.”

“Tell me, what does it look like?”

“Bound in blue cloth, with a large golden flower design in the center?”

I laughed. “How extraordinary, that’s exactly right. That was so long ago! How did you know it was my book?”

“On the last page, running diagonally from one page to the next, is your handwriting. You wrote—Mircea Mircea Mircea—I have told my mother that you have only kissed me on my forehead.”

Sergei hadn’t finished his sentence—when a tingling shot up from beneath my feet. I was sitting on a low stool, my feet touching the floor—I felt as if my feet were no longer on the ground—this room had no boundaries. I was moving through emptiness, through the vast void—yet I knew I was looking at Sergei, he was smiling gently, and I was smiling too—but what a strange physical sensation—I was divided! I was here, yet not here. I could see myself on the balcony of our house in Bhabanipur—the floor of the balcony paved with black and white checkered stone, like a chessboard, and I was lying face down on the smooth stone floor. In my hand was that very book. There I was, there I was—I could see myself, and suddenly, with the sound of a waterfall in my chest, the tears of that day returned—how strange, yet I was still saying something to Sergei. He was answering clearly. My hand was on the armrest of the chair, but I could feel the smoothness of that stone floor—Khoka was standing before me. I could see his dirty fingernails, his toes—the edge of his soiled dhoti touching the ground. It was morning! Perhaps the morning of September 20th. On the 18th, Mircea had left. Khoka was saying to me—I could hear him clearly—“Write quickly, sister”—then, making a face, he whispered, “Spies are everywhere.” He said it as a joke, he was always one to make fun, to make people laugh.

Khoka was not really one of our family. But he was like a brother. They were very poor, my grandmother had raised his mother. Later, she arranged her marriage. That’s why we called Khoka’s mother Pishima. Pishima’s...

--- Page 7 ---
Eighteen children—so poverty never left them. Khoka and his sister Shanti are under our protection. Even though we are close with them, friends, playmates—still, they have no status, as is the fate of dependents; even if they receive kindness, they never receive respect. Even Mircea is not pleased with him. But that is for another reason—because Khoka makes me laugh. He tells the simplest things in such a way, with such expressions, that we are left breathless with laughter. Mircea cannot understand half of it, so he becomes serious. 

One day, I had hung a new curtain in the library room; decorating rooms with various curtains is a hobby of mine. Khoka entered the library, pushing aside the curtain with such a gesture as if he were moving a boulder, as if he could barely get in! The more I rolled with laughter at his antics, the heavier Mircea’s face became.

“Why was that fellow behaving like that?”

“He was joking. Seeing me change curtains every day, he says that when he has his own household, there will be so many curtains hanging at every door that as soon as you move one aside, there will be another, and another after that, so people will get exhausted just trying to enter a room—no one will be able to get inside at all. He was acting out what that would be like. Isn’t it funny? Why are you sulking?”

“Does it have some deeper meaning?”

“Look, what deeper meaning could there be? It’s just for fun.” This is Mircea’s nature—he looks for meaning in everything!

“You like him so much! Buffoon, joker, clown”—Ah, what irony! Now Mircea has to rely on that very Khoka for help. Now he is Mircea’s only friend. No one else will bring him news of me. And who will bring me news of him!

“Khoka, brother Khoka—”

“Ru, write quickly, brother, if uncle sees me here he’ll be furious. He might even throw me out of here!”

I am trying to write—but I can’t think of what to say—Mircea has asked for my book. Khoka told him I didn’t have a copy, so give him this one, so I am writing in the book itself. What should I write? My heart trembles with fear. What if Mircea tells the whole truth? He knows I don’t lie easily. And I know he doesn’t lie either. But I have lied, only to save him. Now I understand—not all lies are bad. How strange, how did such a thought come to me? That’s why Ma says, one wrongdoing calls another—lies chase after lies, and truth can never catch up! Shame on me, I have become so bad. What would my Guru say? I who have always lifted my face to the sunlight. I who thought I would remain pure and untainted. But no, it was not to be. 

I can see myself now, opening the back page of the book and writing—my hand is shaking. The letters are trembling. The lines are crooked. As if, from some darkness, my sixteen-year-old self is emerging. My hair is disheveled; I haven’t combed it in three days, and in these three days, except for Sharot, I have eaten nothing. I will not eat. I tell myself, I will never eat again, I will cut off my hair, tear my sari border—then Ma will learn her lesson. When Grandmother comes and asks, “What’s wrong with her?” then Ma will have to tell her. What will Grandmother say? I know, I know, I know—she will keep it in her heart: this girl has chosen her own husband, she can have no other. But she will not be able to say a word aloud. Because of Father. So many people fear one man. I wrote to Mircea, warned him. Not to admit to anything more. Will he understand? Who knows! I can’t write any more, I can’t even think of the English words.

Sergei said, “But Mircea has grown much older than you.”

I looked at him in surprise, my vision returning to my eyes—Sergei could not even understand,

--- Page 8 ---
I was not here just a moment ago. What was I seeing? Where is Khoka? He is now an old, withered man—living in Kalighat or somewhere, I haven’t kept track of him for years. Did he drift in here, floating on the stone veranda or a wandering carpet? How strange! How astonishing! The spell refuses to break. That song comes to mind: “With the light of my eyes I saw the world outside—today I will see within, when there is no light outside.” Is it possible to see without light, then? Are there other waves besides those of light? There must be, surely there are. I am beginning to believe in the supernatural, even though I am an atheist—for what happened just now, or in a single moment, or in something beyond time, was not memory, not recollection, but a real sensation. I was present on the morning of September 20th, 1930—the cold of the stone pressed beneath my hand, my eyes heavy with ceaseless weeping, I was hungry from three days of fasting, I hesitated a moment over the spelling of “forehead,” unsure whether it needed an “e.” I have no language to express this direct sensation of the past. The year 1972 had entered into 1930. Outwardly, I was speaking to Sergei as usual, but inside I was trembling. I shook like a small, helpless leaf in the face of a storm. I could not believe myself. I am here, but I might just as easily not be here at all. I feel as if it will happen again—as if I will slip back into the past. How is this possible? Though I understand that time has neither beginning nor end, its rise and fall belong to me alone, and with these five senses I know only a fragmented world—how can I dare to step beyond it? I am afraid to set foot in that unknown realm. Today’s experience was terrifying, painful, shattering. It has thrown all my beliefs and self-confidence into confusion. Who will save me from this peril? Like in 1930, I began to call out to my Guru—“Pratu, do not abandon me.” “O Lord, return—let the churning of all my joys and sorrows return to my heart.” I want nothing else, no one else; there is nothing in my life, there never was anything else. Across my entire past, present, and future, your songs have been a radiant festival—there is no lack in me, no poverty. Will some unknown stranger, with just a few words, shatter all of this after so many years? Will he dim the light of the pole star? Will I lose my way?

As Sergei came to see me off and kissed my fingertips, a sharp, piercing sensation shot up from beneath my feet. With great effort, I managed to compose myself. A whirl—1972; in 1930, this place was covered in jungle. I got into the car, gripping the door tightly, and said, “This is a taxi, not a Chevrolet.”

Our first car was a Chevrolet, tall with an open hood. By today’s standards, not beautiful, but to us then, it seemed so lovely. Whenever I got out of the car, Mircea would always offer his hand.

“Why, do you need help to get down such a little way?”

“It’s the custom in our country, women must be helped out of cars by hand. And to greet or bid farewell, it is customary to kiss the fingertips.”

“Custom?”

“Yes, custom. If you don’t, people will call you a barbarian. Don’t you have any such customs?”

“No. We touch the feet of elders in respect. There’s ‘namaskar’ for those of the same age, but hardly anyone does it. These manners started from the Tagore household. At Santiniketan, Rabindranath is teaching the students these ways. They greet everyone with folded hands. Students greet each other, too. People in the rest of the country laugh at this!”

“Does that one man do everything for you all?”

“Yes, yes, that one man fills our sky, puts words in our mouths—”

--- Page 9 ---
He gives, he gives us love in our hearts, because of him stars bloom across my sky at night,  
and in the morning, flowers blossom in the forest.”

Mircea stared at me in stunned amazement—“What is this? How can you speak like this about a person! I cannot understand you.”

“Does it matter?”

I have reached home. My daughter-in-law Lekha said to me, “Where did you suddenly go? Wasn’t Mr. ‘K’ supposed to come today? With someone for work?”

I am a little afraid of this girl. She is extremely intelligent and always stays close to me—who knows what she might understand! I have to be very careful around her... I listened to Mr. K’s statements with a smiling face. But none of his words reached my ears—my eyes filled with tears, my chest trembled. This won’t do. What is this, after so many years? I am smiling, but in a rather incongruous way. In the afternoon, my birthday celebration—they are expressing curiosity about it—but I have no curiosity, I don’t want to know. I cannot understand how the world has changed so much in such a short time! Why does it hurt so much..? My friends are going to celebrate my birthday, but I feel no pride in it—what use is their affection for me? Can they give me peace? I realize that Sergei’s arrival has cast a stone into the calm, currentless, still waters of my life, creating ripples that will not easily leave me; the whirlpool will not stop. The hope for peace is now far away.

I sit slumped in my chair with my eyes closed. What do I want? Nothing—work, social service, the progress of the nation—let all that go to hell, I want nothing—take me back to 1930, let me see him once more. Mircea, Mircea, Mircea!

Suddenly, I look up to see Lekha gazing at me intently—“Ma, are your eyes hurting again? Why are they filled with tears? Should I get your medicine?”

“Yes, bring it.”

The dangerous evening of my birthday has passed. I am pleased with my own achievement. I wore a new sari, adorned myself with a garland of flowers, recited poetry, listened to songs, but no one could guess how my insides were trembling the entire time! If I were to use a metaphor to describe it—if that trembling could be seen in my body, people would think I had Parkinson’s disease.

At two in the morning, I step outside—it is almost dawn. The stars have moved from one side of the sky to the other. In this house, one cannot go up to the roof, which is a real misfortune. I cannot see the sky properly. I have always loved to lie under the open sky. Mircea, too, loved to stroll on the roof. The first day he saw the roof, he was astonished.

“Do you know, in our country, we cannot go up to the roof?”

“How is that possible! Then how do you see the sun and the stars?”

“Astronomers see the sun and stars, ordinary people never think of such things.”

“In our country, people greet the sun first thing in the morning.”

“Do you?”

“My sun is within me as well as outside. I am always in reverence. There is no morning or evening for me.”

“What does that mean? Tell me, tell me, why are you laughing, you must tell me.”

“No, I won’t. You won’t understand.”

“You are insulting me—you think I won’t understand.”

“You must tell me who the inner sun is.”

“My beginning. He is the one who shows me this beautiful world.”

“Does he show only himself, or does he show something more?”

--- Page 10 ---
“I see everything in His light.”
“How?”
“For example, as I see you now.”
He was happy that day—“Will you read Whitman with me this evening?”
“Oh no, I can never understand such dry writing. I’d rather read Shelley—‘The Sensitive Plant’!”
I go and lie down. There is so much to do tomorrow—there’s a meeting in the afternoon. What is the use of remembering things from forty-two years ago! Where is that Mircea now, and where is that Amrita? Even if we met, perhaps we would not recognize each other at all!

Day after day passes by. I cannot keep myself anchored in my present. Again and again, sometimes by day, sometimes by night, I find myself returning to our house in Bhabanipur, in the year 1930.

I do not remember which month it was, the day Mircea Euclid first came to our house—that is, the day I first truly noticed him. My father was a learned man; just six years before, he had been teaching at a small-town college in East Bengal, and then he had come to Calcutta. In this short span, he had already reached the pinnacle of respect in Calcutta’s intellectual circles. Everyone knew him. He was a scholar, and a typical scholar at that. For this reason, many people were afraid of him; his scholarship had an aggressive edge. He could defeat anyone in debate in a short time and make them appear foolish, and he took great pleasure in this game. Yet, despite this, his power of attraction was remarkable. Even those he insulted could not stay away from him. His students were ready to make many sacrifices for him; he loved them too, but his love was not like that of ordinary people. There was no empathy for the other in it. His love was for himself—as he loved me, loved me deeply, but it was as much for himself as for me—look, see what a priceless jewel my daughter is, how beautiful she is, how wonderfully she writes poetry, how well she speaks English—she is my daughter, look, all of you! My father was absorbed in me. But I knew that if I moved even a little against his wishes, he would not hesitate to crush me. What would make me happy was irrelevant to him.

My mother was the complete opposite: my mother was supremely beautiful. In those days, her beauty was almost miraculous, heavenly. My father was very proud of it, but my mother herself was completely unaware—she never took care with her appearance, had no concern for her own comfort, desires, or dislikes. Her only purpose was to make my father happy. And my father kept her fully occupied in that task—especially when he was even slightly ill, he would create such a commotion that my mother lived in constant fear that something dreadful might happen to her husband. My mother loved to read Vaishnava literature, and she would often quote two lines:
“The desire to please oneself is called lust,
The desire to please Krishna is called love.”
It was not only her husband she wished to please. For everyone around her, my mother’s hands overflowed with the nectar of affection and compassion.

In those days, foreigners were always coming and going in our house. There were discussions on many subjects. Of all who visited, I remember most vividly the learned Stella Kramrisch and Professor Tucci; I think Farmico came as well. Later, Professor Tucci learned to speak good Bengali—he looked like a student, with unruly hair falling over his young face again and again. His wife had a rather square face, and wore a pearl necklace around her neck. Because of their frequent visits, the look of our house was gradually changing. A touch of European style was creeping over our Bengali ways. My grandmother had passed away about a year before, which made this transformation possible—otherwise, it would not have happened. I remember clearly, in 1924, the day a huge mahogany table was brought into the dining room, my grandmother stared at it for a long time, eyes wide open.

--- Page 11 ---
He looked at it, the golden prayer beads in his hand paused—“Why, should food be eaten on this? What’s the harm in hearing about it? After all, it’s just a bed!” But when he saw there was no way out, that his words would not prevail, he let out a long sigh and said, “There is no harm in large pieces of wood.” He would hardly ever go near that room. And the day he saw the forks and knives—I’ll never forget it—he made a face and said to Ma, “Why do you need so many tools just to eat rice?” His urge for retribution grew so intense that he would often say he hoped that “if someone’s tongue got pierced through with one of those forks, only then would they learn their lesson!” Thakur’s orthodoxies were so unyielding, so true for him, that he proved it even on his deathbed. He died after suffering from cholera for three days. For the first two days, Ma took care of him entirely, with Kaka helping her. Ma was pregnant at the time—the doctor grew very angry, and eventually began scolding Baba as well, until finally Ma had to step away—a nurse was brought in, a Christian nurse. She was instructed that if Thakuma asked about her caste, she should say “Brahmin.”

The patient, half-conscious, opened her eyes slightly and said, “Water”—the nurse came forward with water—“Here, drink this water, open your mouth”—the dying woman did not open her mouth, but opened her eyes, the voice at her ear sounded unfamiliar in her death-dimmed hearing, “Who are you, my child?”

“I am the nurse.”

“All right, let it be, I’ll drink water a little later.” The thirst of a cholera patient is relentless. She asked again—“Water”...

“Here, I’ve brought water, please drink”—the nurse held out the feeding cup—

“Call my daughter-in-law—”

“She has fallen ill, please take water from me.”

“Are you of my caste?”

“Brahmin.”

“Are you married or widowed?”

“Widow”—this time the dying woman looked closely, she knew everything—what her illness was, its course, her present state—nothing was unknown to her, for she was the daughter-in-law of a family of Ayurvedic physicians—she had spent many years with doctors. “Look, nurse, my stomach is bloated, when a cholera patient’s stomach swells, they don’t survive”—in the final moments, consciousness returned to the dying patient. That is the way of coma, sometimes the patient becomes alert. “O nurse, have you eaten? What did you eat with your rice?”

“Yes, yes, I just had rice with fish curry.”

Nothing more needed to be said, her suspicion was confirmed, “Look, nurse, you are a Brahmin widow, you have eaten rice with fish curry, at the time of my death, please do not give me water, my child, call my daughter-in-law.”

The daughter-in-law rushed in, “I have served her my whole life. And now, at this final moment, to cause her distress—let whatever happens to me, happen.”

After Thakuma passed away, the very appearance of this house changed, both inside and out. Vows, worship, Kalighat, priests, untouchability, leftovers—all these great problems that harassed Ma all day seemed to vanish in a single breath. We cleared away the accumulated debris of many years and turned our faces toward a new horizon.

I loved this change. As I moved from childhood to adolescence, and from adolescence to youth, I began to see more and more of the finest people of Bengal. How different their homes and customs were from our provincial ways! Back then, I would often hear the word “elite,” just as now I hear “bourgeois.” There is a kind of conflict in the word “bourgeois,” a hint of quarrel, but “elite” is not like that. I also used to hear another phrase, “cream of the...

--- Page 12 ---
Calcutta—now everything has blended together, like milk and cream. The most eminent figure in this high society was Rabindranath Tagore. From the time I was thirteen, my father would take me to him, so that I could show him my poems. I would hurriedly finish that task and then recite his poems to him, for I have loved reciting poetry since my childhood. Rabindranath enjoyed hearing his own poems recited by me. Sometimes, to encourage me, he would say, “You read my poems better than I do”—I knew he said this only to make me happy, yet my heart would brim over with joy. I would think, how much I receive from him, how sweet it is just to experience his presence here—even so, I have nothing to give him in return. Only this one thing I can offer. So, although I was always overwhelmed with shyness and embarrassment before such a towering genius—often unable even to raise my head and speak—once I began to recite poetry, my shyness, fear, and hesitation would melt away. I remember once reciting, one after another, ‘Sonar Tori,’ ‘Kautukamoyi,’ ‘Jiban Debata,’ ‘Hriday Jamuna’—he smiled gently and asked me, “Do you understand these poems?”

With great confidence, I nodded, “Yes”—and then, just as I had heard from my father, I began to rattle off the hidden meanings of ‘Sonar Tori,’ the philosophy of ‘Jiban Debata.’ He stopped me midway. Now I realize how strange such lofty philosophy must have sounded coming from my childish mouth. He said, “Enough, enough, just read. When the time comes, you will understand the meaning yourself. Even the bird’s song has meaning in the universal scheme, though the bird does not know it. That does no harm. In the same way, read for the joy of your heart; someone else’s interpretation will be of no use to you.”

At that time, a Russian scholar would often visit—I cannot recall his name, perhaps it was Bogdanov. The foreign scholars who came to Santiniketan would always stop by our house on their way in or out; I would join the philosophical discussions with confidence. The atmosphere of philosophical contemplation was, to someone as young as I was, nothing but a kind of mist—but I liked that mysterious nebula. Even as that nebula enveloped me, the sunlight would break through, keeping me warm and alert. On one side, the living truth of individual human feeling resonated in the pain of art; on the other, the riddle of endless, eternal questions played light and shadow over my eager, fresh, youthful mind. Our house was different from those of our relatives and friends—and to my peers, especially my schoolmates, I was incomprehensible! They thought I was absent-minded, because I would often drift into thought when I should have been paying attention.

In our time, boys and girls did not mix much, yet we were not secluded behind purdah. My mother, though she did not always appear before my father’s colleagues, would come out before his students—and I would appear before everyone. When we lived in the suburbs, I saw my mother sitting behind a screen, listening to the literary discussions in the drawing room, sending in snacks and sherbet from behind the veil. But after coming to Calcutta, that barrier was lifted. We went everywhere. I would recite poetry at literary gatherings where not a single other girl was present. Very few girls did such things in those days. Yet, even so, there was an invisible veil over our faces. We did not easily speak to anyone. If one of my father’s students, for example, approached us while talking to him, I would walk with my head bowed, not speaking to him. To tell the truth, we were unveiled, yet veiled. It was not that I did not wish to speak—I very much did, and I knew the other side felt the same. Then what held us back? No one forbade us. But we could not do it. In front of men outside our family, especially young men, we would turn to stone. So did they. As if we could not even see each other. How many ages of accumulated prohibitions—

--- Page 13 ---
Influence flows in our bloodstream; it is difficult to cross over. Yet, in today’s world, perhaps no one would even believe why such prohibitions existed, or what their underlying reasons were. We—at least I—did not know much about them. In these educated, upper-middle-class families, there was never any discussion about sexual matters. Through hints and suggestions, we understood that there was a hidden, covered world, but what went on there, we did not know. We were only allowed to read carefully selected novels. Certain famous books were strictly forbidden, among them ‘Krishnakant-er Will’, ‘Chokher Bali’, and ‘Charitraheen’. Permission was granted to read ‘Nouka Dubi’. I remember taking the cover off ‘Nouka Dubi’ and putting it on ‘Chokher Bali’ so I could read it. Of course, I could not understand why the book was so forbidden.

Although I never had the courage to speak much with my father’s Bengali students, I did not feel the same restriction when it came to foreign students. My father, too, would let us mix freely with Europeans! At that time, a Russian couple had come to Calcutta. They were performing all sorts of magic tricks at the Globe. The city was abuzz with talk of them. Father said, “Come, let’s go see this trickery”—At the Globe Theatre, a beautiful woman would come on stage, blindfolded with a black scarf and dressed in a flowing black gown. Her husband would descend into the audience. The lady would invite the audience to ask questions. If someone stood up to ask, her handsome young husband would take that person’s pulse, and then, from the stage, the lady would rattle off both the question and the answer. Sometimes the questions were meant to trick her, sometimes to discover some tragic secret. For example, someone stood up and immediately the blindfolded lady said, “You want to know how many matches are in the matchbox in your pocket?—Fifty-two!” Or, “You want to know if your wife is unfaithful to you? She is perfectly faithful; it is you who are unfaithful”—Hearing this, the whole room burst into laughter, and the gentleman was left embarrassed. Father said, “This girl is a real trickster; we must test her a bit.”

Nowadays, that is, towards the end of the nineteenth century, even among the educated upper classes, there is a tendency to believe in the supernatural and the occult; but as far as I remember, in our time, this was less common. Now, in every household, ash falls from pictures, strange objects appear—such stories are told even by learned scientists, but back then, it was not so. Educated people hesitated to believe in such bizarre, supernatural events; at least, whatever they thought in their hearts, they would not admit it aloud. My father was not entirely free from superstition either, but his public self would never admit it. So, to test them, the Russian couple was invited to our house. Father believed that there were accomplices among the audience. I asked, “Then why didn’t you ask anything yourself?” “My dear, that girl is such a trickster—who knows what she might say!”

At our house, several distinguished guests gathered for tea—professors, writers, and so on. Along with tea, this amusing game continued. The gentleman would take someone’s pulse, and the lady would reveal their thoughts. At last, my turn came. I thought I would try to trick her—how would she say something in Bengali? In my mind, I asked, “What is the name of my last poem?” The lady hesitated, stumbling a little, and then said, “Bhogpatra”—the pronunciation was distorted, but she had said it correctly. A young professor remarked, “Nothing but thought-reading.” Father said, “Well, does that explain everything, then?”

One day, we took this Russian couple to the Empire Theatre to see an Italian opera—Father, Mother, I, and the two of them. In those days, ears in this country were not accustomed to European music—it was generally dismissed as the howling of foxes and dogs. Now, with the transistor...

--- Page 14 ---
In the slums of Kalyan, jazz music plays on the porches, and even Beethoven can be heard. Back then, such a thing was unimaginable. For two or three generations, those who had received Western education—those who were called members of the Anglophile society—practiced European music in their homes; the girls would tinkle away on the piano. When guests or eligible bachelors arrived, they would play the piano in the drawing room to showcase their accomplishments, but it was all rather childish. So it’s clear that Italian opera did not particularly appeal to me. But I knew not to say so to anyone, because I had only just started coming to these places. There was a sense of pride in growing up. Besides, I wanted to prove myself knowledgeable, not ignorant. So, distractedly, I watched the opera as if I were taking medicine.

Suddenly, that Russian gentleman—though at that moment I’d rather call him ungentlemanly—extended his right arm and put it around my neck. I was startled. Although I was quite ignorant about such matters, we always behaved properly by instinct. I gave his hand a sharp shake and pushed it away, but like a spring-loaded doll, he brought it back again. I wondered what to do—this was not a place where I could make a scene. Leaning down, I quietly slipped off my shoe and gently placed it on his knee. Now it was his turn to be startled. I said very slowly, “I can take off your shoe.” This time, the spring worked in the other direction—he quickly withdrew his hand.

When I told this story at home, Mother became furious with Father for bringing such people so far into our home! But Father just laughed heartily. He knew the Western world well, so he wasn’t surprised at all. He said, “Amrita must learn to mix with all sorts of people in all sorts of situations; she won’t stay at home like you. If she tries, she could one day become a Sarojini Naidu.”

Anyway, even after this incident—which both Mother and I found terribly improper and unacceptable, and which left us both quite agitated—Father did not treat them badly. He even kept his promise to take them to see Rabindranath Tagore. I had already told him stories of their magic tricks. He, too, was eager to meet them. Though he didn’t believe in the supernatural, Rabindranath always thought it necessary to examine everything for himself. That was the scientific way. Why shouldn’t we investigate? So a day was set to take the Russian couple to him.

“Baba, I don’t want to go with them. That man is very unpleasant—”

“How can that be, dear? Everything is arranged. If you don’t go now, what will the poet think? Besides, in life you’ll meet all kinds of people in all kinds of circumstances. I know you’ll always stay on the right path with your own strength and intelligence. Just because there are bad people in the world, should you hide away in a hole?”

At noon, Father and I took the Russian couple to the poet. We seated them in the stone room on the second floor, and I went up to the third floor to serve him his meal. That stone room floats before my eyes—the low cots covered with Japanese mats, and the cushions too, wrapped in Japanese matting. I have seen many changes in that room since then—it was in that very room that he breathed his last. When I went upstairs, I found him already prepared, a slip of paper with a few questions written on it tucked into a book. He stood up, saying, “Come, let’s see the magician.” We descended the spiral staircase.

They were waiting. After a few words, the girl blindfolded herself, and her husband came to take her pulse. The girl couldn’t say anything, but it was clear she was trying. The poet didn’t want to embarrass them; he wanted to understand what was happening. He asked, “What would make it easier—would it help if I wrote something on a piece of paper?” The girl replied, “Perhaps.” He wrote something on a slip of paper and placed it face down. Still, nothing happened. Beads of sweat gathered on the girl’s forehead. She stood up…

--- Page 15 ---
He began to pace back and forth—then moved out to the veranda. “There is a wall before me, there is a wall before me—আমার সামনে একটা দেওয়াল, সামনে একটা দেওয়াল—” he kept repeating, running up and down the veranda, his voice growing louder—“I cannot see anything,” sweat beading on his forehead, his breath coming faster. Still repeating those words, he ran out of the room, down the stairs, and disappeared down the alley. His bewildered husband ran after him. I couldn’t help but imagine how the shopkeepers on either side of Chitpur Road must have enjoyed the sight of this English couple running through the street. I covered my mouth with my sari to stifle my laughter. Father said, “You stay here! I’ll see them off.” The poet made quite a few sarcastic remarks about the intelligence of our father-daughter duo, and added that one couldn’t expect much more from professors! Father, for his part, was also annoyed—his grievance being that they couldn’t answer the poet’s questions, but he could! Thus ended the tale of the Russian magician.

While all these events were unfolding, Mircea Euclid used to visit our house, but I hardly noticed him at the time. I don’t even recall ever having spoken to him. The day I do remember was one particular afternoon. Father was seated at his writing desk. On a chair opposite, facing him, sat Mircea. Father called me over—“This is my daughter Amrita, and this is my student Mircea Euclid”—he stood up. I glanced at him for a moment. He wore thick glasses, his hair was light, his jaw prominent, his face square. I always liked this habit of foreigners—they stand up when they see a woman. And our boys? They’d either lounge with one leg over the other, or act coy, or pretend not to notice a woman’s presence at all.

Father said, “Euclid is having a lot of trouble where he’s staying, so I’ve asked him to stay here. Arrange a room for him.”

For a moment, my heart sank. I said, “Father, why should there be an Englishman in our house?” Father, though a little irritated by my objection, replied carefully in Bengali, “He is not English, he’s from a small country in Europe! And even if he were English, is this what you’ve learned?”

Now I looked at Mircea Euclid. I had no experience with foreign names then. Otherwise, I might have realized from his name that it wasn’t Anglo-Saxon. Still, from his appearance, I could tell he wasn’t English. Though his skin was fair, his hair was black, not reddish—brushed back, rising from either side of his forehead. His cheekbones were high, almost like a hill person’s. He glanced at me quickly, then looked away.

Of course, my objection wasn’t really because he was English, but I thought it best to say so. At that time, as foreigners began to frequent our house and we mingled more with Calcutta’s ‘elite’, the décor of our home was gradually changing. I was at the forefront of this; Ma was completely incapable in such matters. Father and I would bring home new furniture from a shop called Egbert Andrews. I had to polish the brassware from Delhi and Kanpur, from door handles to latches. Keeping this house clean was quite a challenge for a half-Europeanized household like ours. There were always so many people at home, and on top of that, relatives from the village would arrive at any time—seeking treatment for illnesses, to bathe in the Ganges during an eclipse, or to offer puja at Kalighat, always with their entire entourage. My mother never turned anyone away. If someone came with an illness, Ma would nurse them like an expert. She herself never wished to go to Kalighat. Her father’s house was thoroughly Brahmo in outlook—but if an elderly relative from the village arrived, she would send them to Kalighat every day in our Chevrolet—fetching jars of Ganges water by car. Ma’s doors were always open to everyone. But the result of this was not always good for me. Because—

--- Page 16 ---
I have recently acquired a room of my own—though not entirely my own—for at night, Shanti and my eleven-year-old younger sister, Sabitri, whom we call ‘Sabi’, would sleep there with me. But the room was mine—I had decorated it beautifully, furnished it with low pieces of furniture. Even the bed’s legs had been cut to lower it. The furnishings were simple, of course. The floor was made of polished stone, patterned like a chessboard in black and white. I always kept the room fragrant with incense and flowers. Anyone who entered would say, “This isn’t a room, it’s a temple.” On the wall in the center hung a picture of Rabindranath Tagore, wearing a cap—that picture always struck me as extraordinary, for wherever you stood in the room, it seemed he was looking straight at you. There was no escaping his gaze.

Of course, whenever relatives arrived from the countryside with their bundles and bags, I would have to give up my room, as it was the only spare one in the house. They would leave handprints on the walls, wipe their hands on the curtains, leave water rings on the table, and after their ritual baths during eclipses, depart contented—leaving me to set everything right again. Mother was never bothered by any of this. She paid no attention to material things; for her, only people mattered. The idea of causing someone discomfort for the sake of a tidy room never even occurred to her.

But it mattered to me—I loved to sit in a neat, orderly room and read or write poetry. I also loved listening to stories of the freedom struggle from my uncle. The struggle was all around us, yet it never quite touched us. My uncle was my father’s cousin, and their family had some leanings in that direction. My uncle’s eldest brother was in jail, and I was very proud of that. I would tell my classmates stories about him. But there was none of that in our own home; Father paid no heed to such things. Looking back now, I realize that most highly educated, upper-class people of that time could not even imagine the possibility of independence. I often heard them say, “So, this is how they’ll drive out the English? As if that’s all it takes.” I never heard any actual plans about how the English would be driven out. Still, for us young people, these tales of daring acts created a kind of enchantment—the stories of attacks on British officials, the heat of those events seeped through the barriers of family restrictions and touched our hearts. We began one practice: a complete boycott of foreign goods.

That’s why it sounded so out of place coming from me—“Why is there going to be an Englishman in our house?” Though, truthfully, my real objection was the fear that I might have to give up my room again because of it. Father reassured me, “He can stay in the front room on the ground floor. If we put up a partition, people can sit in the front part, or your uncle can stay there, and Euclid can have the inner part.”

That evening, Father handed me the essay “Nationalism” and said, “Read this book. Is someone an enemy just because he’s English? The day will come when patriotism itself will be considered a crime.” That night, I turned the pages of the book for hours, but understood very little. Father always gave me books just beyond my reach. And I loved reading them, even without understanding; this world of light and shadow, of half-understood meanings, always enchanted me. As if I could almost grasp it, yet not quite—the elusive, mysterious veil over this world was the very inspiration for my poetry.

Within two or three days, all the arrangements were made. Even with the partition, the room was not too small: a little bed, a table, a chair, a large wicker sofa, a piano with its stool, and in the middle, a round table with a lamp standing beside it—wasn’t the décor pleasant? I even hung a curtain on the door facing the alley. This room was directly beneath my own.

In the mornings, at the breakfast table, Baba would discuss various topics with Mircea Euclid—what he would study...

--- Page 17 ---
He used to say so. One day Father said, "You two should read ‘Shakuntala’ with me. There’s no use learning Sanskrit from ‘Hitopadesha’. Read something enjoyable, you’ll like it." From the next day, we began studying together. Who knows what people of that era thought, seeing a sahib sitting on the floor with me, learning Sanskrit. I saw envy mixed with astonishment in the eyes of Father’s Bengali students; I saw suspicion and disapproval on the faces of the elderly women, and amusement in the eyes of my peers. But Father paid no heed. Both Mother and Father accepted his presence very naturally. Gradually, he was becoming one of the family.

When studying with Father, I would deliberately sit on the mat. Father would sit between us—on a sofa. I knew Mircea loved sitting on the mat; it was new to him, and he wanted to become one with us. He observed everything, examined it closely. He wanted to know all about us, and sought meaning in every little thing.

Mother would say, "Euclid is a very good boy, gentle, calm, polite. Why don’t you call me ‘Ma’, Mircea, why do you call me Mrs. Sen? Call me Ma."

After that, he would call her Ma. But he told me that in their country, no one calls such young women ‘Ma’, they get offended. My mother was only thirty-two or thirty-three then. But draped in a red-bordered sari, vermilion on her forehead, her feet adorned with red alta—our beautiful mother was the very image of motherhood; who would think of her age! I know, their country is so strange—if you call a woman ‘Ma’, she gets angry! So what need is there to count the years?

In the mornings, after everyone left the dining table, we would sit and chat. Then, standing by the library door, we would talk for another hour or two, and no one seemed to notice. Father’s library spanned three rooms, with seven or eight thousand books—there our conversations would flow. Father would go down the stairs, but say nothing; this happened so many times. Not even a word of reproach for wasting time chatting. If I had been talking with Milu or Gopal, I would surely have been scolded; but with Mircea Euclid, surely we were discussing philosophy, improving ourselves!

One day, as I was coming down from upstairs with a book in hand, Mircea stopped me in the middle of the corridor—"Did you write a philosophical poem yesterday?"

"Yes—Father was very excited about my poem yesterday. There’s a line in it—‘Moments will be lost in the womb of time.’ Father liked that line very much; within it lies a question about a profound truth—the question itself is the philosophy. I am fourteen now, that is, two years ago, I was sitting on the shores of the sea at Puri—at dusk, and suddenly it felt like morning—an odd sensation, which I wrote about in a poem—‘Take me, take me, carry me away, wherever this stream of dreams flows.’ What is that land like?—‘Where all ends, where there is no hope, no language, no traveler, no path, no sound! Where there is no birth, no death, no time remains, no night, no day, no morning!’ Father says my inquisitive thoughts about ‘Time’ are truly philosophical. ‘What is Time?’ I am very proud of Father’s enthusiasm! But when Father instructs me to write like this, I don’t like it. The natural flow of poetry is lost—like a bird that, about to spread its wings, folds them and falls at your feet. That’s why I am not happy with the poem ‘Bhogapatra’."

Mircea smiled faintly and said, "Will you write philosophical poems at such a young age!"

"I am not so young at all… Besides, I am a philosopher."

"You are a philosopher?"

"Of course. Whoever sees, or wants to see, is a philosopher! I see, I want to see."

"Alright then, come, recite your poem."

Na Hanyate-2

--- Page 18 ---
I entered his room. For the past few days, I have been going into his room—no one has told me that it is improper, yet still, my steps falter a little. What is this? I cannot quite understand this faint hesitation, this slight shyness. I am not at ease, yet I sit down on the large cane chair, trying my best to appear perfectly comfortable.

A table stands between us; on the other side, leaning back, he sits on his bed.

“Explain your philosophical poem to me.”

“No, no, let me recite a poem by Rabindranath—his new book has just come out, you know, and he dedicated it to me. Listen to that poem!”

“Really? He dedicated it to you?”

“Why are you so surprised? Why shouldn’t he?”

Inwardly, I am laughing. He believes me—believes me completely. I will trick him. If he finds out later from uncle, let him.

“Have you heard my words,
Have you drawn them to your heart,
I do not know your name, but to you I offer
The treasure of my meditation.”

I translated it for him, pausing here and there. What a strange thing a translation becomes—its essence so hard to grasp. He frowned, trying to understand the deeper meaning. I stood up.

He asked, “Why did he write, ‘I do not know your name’?” He realized I had deceived him.

“That’s the secret, isn’t it?”

Conversations like this bewilder Mircea, like Euclid puzzled by a riddle. There is already the barrier of language on both sides, and then the barrier of thought. He cannot quite grasp what we truly mean. In that darkness of not-knowing, he gropes about—his eyes sharp, his voice trembling. “The elusive shadow will not be caught... a bewitching illusion weaves itself into the soul”—is the enchantress descending even now? Who knows!

“The malati vine sways—beside the jackal tree—” To go from inside the house to outside, one must pass through a narrow alley in front of Mircea’s room. To the east of that alley is a courtyard. From the courtyard, a staircase leads up to the alley and also to the dining room. Directly above Mircea’s room is my bedroom. On the outside, to the south, there is a veranda below and another above. In the southeast corner of the lower veranda, not just one malati, but a thick vine climbs from bottom to top, always in bloom—clusters of white and red flowers. I swing that vine. If I stand sheltered by that vine in the southeast corner of the veranda, just across the alley from his room, I know that soon Mircea will come up—he has already seen me coming through the gap in the curtain. I am not entirely sure if I am waiting for him, because I do not want to know—but still, I am alert—why hasn’t he come? Did he not see me? What should I do then—should I hum a song here, or recite a poem? How wrong! How wrong! This is not how things should be—what is not right, I must never do. I will be a philosopher. A philosopher seeks truth, pursues honesty. He never plays hide and seek, does he? I hold on to the vine—inside, I am trembling softly, with hope and anxiety. At that moment, from the street, up the stairs, comes ‘M’-babu, in his khaddar panjabi, his slippers making an awful noise. ‘M’-babu is the younger brother-in-law of a close relative of mine. I know what is in his heart—still, I speak with him, for he is, in a way, family.

“There you are—namaskar.”

“Namaskar!”

“I’m going to England next month.”

“That’s good.”

“Who knows how long it will take to pass the bar exam.”

--- Page 19 ---
“If you study with focus, it shouldn’t take you very long.”
“How can I focus anymore?”
There it is! Now the flattery will begin. I can’t stand these simpering, sycophantic boys. There are quite a few like this. Meelu and I laugh at them a lot. But today I feel afraid. If Mircea comes out and sees him, he’ll frown. His face will darken. Who knows what he’ll think!

And just as I feared. Mircea pulled aside the curtain, stepped out, saw him, and immediately went back inside. Utter rudeness. What must Mr. M have thought! Such trouble with these boys, they’re all the same. I grew restless. “Why don’t you go upstairs? Ma is upstairs. Why stand here on the veranda and tire your legs for nothing?”

“Don’t your legs hurt? Or are you waiting for someone? Am I in the way?”

Mr. M went inside, wounded, pierced by words.

I feel like crying, truly crying. Why should I care what they think of me! What does it matter what they think! Don’t I have anything to think about? How long has it been since I last went to Santiniketan!

My mind won’t find peace unless I go there. On the stone plaque beneath the Chhatimtala at Santiniketan is written—
“He is the comfort of my soul, the joy of my mind, the peace of my spirit.” The great sage knew God, He was the comfort of his soul—perhaps so. But I do not know God. The other day, Baba was explaining the arguments of theologians—how intricately the mechanism of the ear is made, the hammer and anvil placed just so, could that have happened by chance? Someone must have made it—who is that? That is indirect proof of God’s existence. Maybe that’s how God can be proven, but can such a God be the comfort of one’s soul? Someone whose very sight soothes the eyes, whose presence sweetens the air—oh, what nonsense, why do funeral mantras come to mind—really, this is the mantra of love. That day we went to the big red house on Store Road, all of Calcutta’s ‘elite’ had gathered to hear Atulprasad Sen sing. Atulprasad was singing, playing the harmonium—“You are honey, you are honey, you are the fountain of sweetness, the ocean of sweetness, my dearest friend—” Surely he was singing of God. In Vaishnava literature, friend means God, Sri Krishna is God, Radha too is God. In the hall, everyone sat with eyes closed, some with tears streaming down their faces—why? Are they truly feeling God? Nonsense, I know what these ‘elites’ are like, each one a liar and selfish! As far as I know, liars have no connection with God. And Meelu? I know her limits too. God is nowhere near me, nor her. I closed my eyes—the song was indeed beautifully sung. The gentleman’s voice was full of feeling. The word ‘feeling’ is just right, it conveys the meaning perfectly—but I have no desire to look at his face—I too sit with my eyes closed—what a marvelous voice, truly “the melody flows on and on—” In the light of that melody, my inner self is radiant, I see another land, a low veranda roof with a blue pea vine climbing up one side, clusters of blue flowers, its English name is wisteria—and leaning over the veranda table, someone is writing, the morning light falling on his white, curly hair—he writes and hums a different song, but the two songs blend together in my mind—“Then in fire, in wind, in water, the stream of honey flows, singing sweet, sweet.”

Look at that, why had I forgotten this day? For me, there can be no source of such peace, such comfort of the soul, except for a human being—there cannot be, there should not be. My heart is trembling, it feels as if I am betraying my own truth! What is that truth? I do not know, I do not know.

Did you see what Mircea did! Rude, rude. Even though Mr. M passed right by his room, he didn’t come out—

--- Page 20 ---
He didn’t come—and I won’t go to his room either. Today I will keep this promise, I will keep it no matter what. Even if it means that all conversation between us ends forever, so be it. Absentmindedly, I plucked a large cluster of madhabilata flowers and started to go upstairs. As I passed by his room, I glanced through the gap in the curtain and saw him deeply absorbed in reading—he didn’t notice who passed by, or if anyone came or went! I parted the curtain, tossed the flowers inside, and moved on—behind me, I heard his voice calling, “Amrita! Amrita!”

“Ma... Ma... Ma...” The call seemed to float across a vast ocean. Not just an ocean, but from the farthest reaches of the universe. I looked ahead and saw my son standing there. My hand was on the telephone—the phone was ringing—“Ma, why aren’t you picking up the phone?” With great effort, I picked it up—my hand was numb, I was restless. How can one cross such distances in a single moment? I was not here, never was.

This is not memory. My body may have been slumped in this chair, but I was immersed in eternity, where there is neither before nor after. Where “there is no night, no day, nor even morning.” This separation of body and mind, this simultaneous presence in two times—what we are accustomed to calling two different times—surely wounds the body! That’s why I am exhausted. My son is thirty years old, a grown man, not a child. Who knows what he is thinking—but he is not like his father, he won’t ask anything. Quietly, he left the room. I watched his receding figure! Who left, where did they go, what comes next—I cannot understand. Lekha came and took the phone from my hand—someone was on the other end—she said, “Ma has gone to lie down,” and took their number. Now I understand everything—I should have spoken to that person, but perhaps it’s for the best. What would I have said, what if I made a mistake? Lekha said, “Ma, come, you should lie down. I’m telling you, you need to talk to someone.”

“Nothing’s wrong, Lekha, nothing at all.”

“Let me see, lifting the cover from her heart,
Does the mind still dwell there,
Within this tormented body,
At the feet of the assembly
Of the deathless, immortal soul,
Singing at every step the song of the impossible,
And so I still keep my ears open to hear it.”

Impossible, impossible. How life is filled with the possibility of the impossible—after so many years, can everything return so vividly—so much pain, so much bleeding...

“Ma, what are you murmuring with your eyes closed—are you chanting a mantra?”

“Poetry, poetry, after so long I feel like writing poetry—

That which weapons cannot pierce, nor fire consume,
I will seek it in the ocean of tears,
Stretching out my arms like a madwoman—
Once more, in the self-choice of the elusive soul,
Let us go far away, where Time stands waiting,
Breaking and shaking off all debris,
Carrying only the lamp of a free spirit,
To place a vermilion mark on the bride’s forehead...
Wherever we go, there waits, unmoving,
The bridal night, in the unwavering love of someone...

--- Page 21 ---
Only now am I beginning to understand why, for the past year, this urge to go somewhere—anywhere—has been growing so intense, so irresistible within me. I keep feeling, let me go somewhere, let me go somewhere, far away—very far away—and now it seems as if I could just step off this veranda and float away into the sky, to some unknown place—

The written words say, “Go to bed now.”
I reply, “Let’s go, let’s go, let’s go.”
I am laughing. The words are laughing too—“Come, go to bed now.”

It’s very late at night, and my husband is still awake. Sergei came in September, and now October has begun. After a whole month, he has noticed that I am not really here. He cannot bring himself to ask what has happened. That is not his way—through these long thirty-eight years of our marriage, there has never been any conflict between us. Surely, in some way, he knows that even though I am a devoted housewife in this household, there is some part of me that remains—something he does not know. But it does not matter to him, because he only knows how to give, not how to demand. I know I should not keep him in doubt. I ought to say something, but after all these years, what could I possibly say? How can I tell him about his book? 

Mircea, why did you write so many lies? Wasn’t the truth enough? Lies were written so the story would sell in the market. Nowadays, it seems, unless there is vulgarity, books are not valued—after all, it is from your country that this distortion of taste has been imported—ugly, flesh-bound love’s obscenity. Alas! In the end, you have dragged me into it. I can bear the burden of truth, but how can I bear the burden of lies? I have a future, I have a name, I have children—I am an Indian woman; to lose my good name is worse than death. My whole body burns with humiliation—I go and wash my face with water. 

It has been a month now, and I have not been able to sleep a single night. The fire of anger is burning. Many things are burning in that fire. For forty years, the honor, respect, and dignity I have carried are about to be destroyed—your book has gone through twenty editions, and before so many people, you have stripped me bare—do you call this love? I haven’t read the book, but from what I have heard, that is what it seems—now I must find a copy and read it. But who will read it to me? None of us knows that language. I am writhing in agony. I try to recall his face—if I cannot even remember his face, with whom shall I quarrel? I cannot remember his face clearly—it has been so many years—what I see is him sitting, leaning against the partition, his legs as white as egret feathers... 

I come and stand by the window. Nowadays, windows do not have bars, they have grills—you cannot stand holding onto them. When a restless mind wants to escape through the window, you can find strength by holding onto the bars. There is a painting by Nandalal, in the darkness of night, a shadowy figure—Sita imprisoned—holding onto the bars. I feel just like her—open these bars, let me go, let me go, let me cross the ocean of time, leave behind these forty years of household life, my name, my fame, my duties—let’s go, let’s go, let’s go—Mircea, I want to see you once.

I said to Father, “Before Rabindranath leaves for abroad, shouldn’t we go see him once?”
“Of course, Euclid wants to go too.” A letter was sent—as usual, the reply came the very next day:
“So be it. All of you, with your disciples, come.”

That time, we were staying at the big guest house. In the morning, we three would go to see him at Uttarayan: I, Mircea, and Father; Mircea and I set out a little earlier, strolling near the tennis court in the garden, Father was taking so long—perhaps he had met Kshitimohan Sen on the way—which was just as well, we did not mind. What a beautiful morning—after Calcutta, Santiniketan seemed to be embracing the two of us, its sky-filled lap cradling us gently.

--- Page 22 ---
My heart trembles—

Father came and said, "Let's go."  
I replied, "I'll come later, you all go ahead. I'll go meet my friends."  
I won't go with them. I have something to say alone. Of course, I'm not really going to meet any friends. I have no friends here—when I come for just two or three days, why would I go looking for friends? There is only one here who is worth a hundred! Only a hundred?

After meeting the poet, Father took Mircea to show him the library. He would see the collection of manuscripts. His thirst for knowledge is unquenchable. Father is very proud of this student. In Father's exhibition, both of us are objects of interest!

The poet was leaning back in an easy chair by the large window, gazing outside. I entered the room silently and placed a bunch of flowers at his feet. He reached out his hand—"Give them to me."

He looked at me with a sharp gaze! Smiling gently, a meaningful smile—"Well, what were you talking about with the Sahib? How much you were saying! A torrent of words. I told the Sahib, believe only half of what women say. The more they walk, the more they talk."

"How did you see us?"

"Why, did you not want me to see? Then you should have been a little more careful."

I noticed that the garden below was completely visible through the window!

"Ah, I’m getting angry now."

"Of course you are, what kind of anger is it?"

He took hold of the end of my sari and said, "What’s this, a blue garment?" Then, singing, "Pinhe charu neel bas, hridoye pranoy kusum rash"—my ears are ringing, my heart is pounding, the teasing feels delightful yet unbearable, my head droops forward, he tugs at my braid and lifts my face upward. My eyes are closed. I don’t have the courage to look into his eyes—tears are streaming from mine. Now he lets go of my braid—"Look at this! Why are you crying?"

Everything I had planned to say, I forgot. Nothing comes to mind, I rest my face on his knee. He placed his reassuring hand on my head and began to speak softly,

"Amrita, you are still young. Don’t stir your heart so much, it’s not healthy. Keep your mind as clear and still as a crow’s-eye lake, with depths far below, but there’s no need to make it restless and turbulent just yet—be calm. Let the various shadows from outside fall upon your mind, accept them simply. As I have written—‘Take the truth simply—within you is a sweetness, let its roots go deep, let flowers bloom above. Wait.’ Now sit up, let’s talk a little... How is your satinwood tree?"

"We don’t live in that house anymore."

In our previous house, there was a small satinwood tree. I had written a poem about that tree, and Rabindranath had liked it. Mircea was astonished when he heard the poem, he called it pantheism. Pantheism—I didn’t know what that was then. The line ‘the love story of a girl and a tree’ made him ponder, what could it mean! He couldn’t understand that it was not any ‘ism’, but simply poetry.

That day, when we returned to the guest house, I was severely scolded by Father for being late—though being late hadn’t caused any harm—we wouldn’t miss the train, nor was anyone in any danger. Yet suddenly he became furious. When Father is angry, he loses all sense. He scolded me so much that I was completely shaken, and Mircea watched my plight with helpless sympathy.

--- Page 23 ---
The beautiful melody of the morning was broken. When it comes to breaking the mood or disrupting the rhythm, no one could rival my father—if this were Indra’s court, he would have been exiled to the mortal world. But this isn’t that—this is his own household. Here, everyone must bow their heads and endure.

That day, after boarding the train, Father began incessantly criticizing Santiniketan, “This institution will not last, it cannot last. The day the Poet closes his eyes, it will cease to exist the very next day.” Mircea listened quietly, occasionally nodding in agreement. Father continued—now, leaving aside the institution, he turned to poetry, quoting that line from the poem ‘Prarthana’: “Deshe deshe, dishe dishe, karmadhara dhay, ajasra sahasrabidha charitarthatay”—according to him, the word ‘charitarthata’ lacks poetic fragrance. Its usage, he said, was not at all appropriate. Perhaps that’s true. But I did not wish to hear poetic criticism just then. I rested my head against the train window, turning my back to them, lost in a daydream. The train wheels rolled on with a rhythmic thud. The wheels spoke, they sang. A song was humming softly in my mind—someone from afar seems to have come close today... standing silently at the edge of darkness... Back then, Rabindranath’s songs were not so widely heard, how many people sang them? Whenever, by chance, I heard one of his songs, my heart would twist inside me—this particular song I had heard only last week, at Kalidas Babu’s place. Kalidas Babu sang very sweetly. I had gone to the home of Ramananda Chattopadhyay, who would tell stories of the old Tagore household—I often went there in the afternoons. That day he was not home, and after waiting until evening, Kalidas Babu escorted me back. As we crossed Ramesh Mitra Park, he was singing—“Buke dole tar birohabethar mala, gopon milon amrit gandha dhala”—the tune still lingers in my ears, and I try to hum it softly to myself. At the edge of darkness—the train wheels are speaking... standing silently. Father got up and went to the bathroom... Mircea came and stood behind me.

“Amrita—”

“Yes?”

“Look this way”—I turned around and looked at him—he gazed at me, steady and unblinking, his eyes hypnotic. I can still see him, just as he was then—leaning slightly toward me, holding onto the upper bunk’s rope, looking at me. After a moment, we both burst out laughing.

“What were you thinking about for so long?”

“I was trying to remember a song.”

Father emerged—“What are you two talking about?”

“Father, why don’t you translate that song for Euclid—‘Amar din phuralo byakul badal sanjhe’?”

“You do it, why shouldn’t you be able to—” Then, realizing this was beyond me, Father began, “Alright, ‘Buke dole tar birohabethar mala...gopon milon amrit gandha dhala’... In describing the monsoon, the mention of longing is inevitable—the rains torment the hearts of the separated, make them restless.” Father started talking about Meghaduta—“Here, it says, ‘the garland of the pain of separation,’ and there, ‘the secret scent of union’! Union and separation—these two opposing emotions are used to convey a sense of completeness.”

Father spoke at length about Rabindranath’s use of opposites in poetry.

But for the second time today, the rhythm was broken. Was all this talk really necessary? In that moment, when Mircea looked into my eyes, I understood how union and separation can exist together, and what that longing truly is. I could sense the fragrance of that invisible garland on his fair neck... ‘the secret scent of union...’

I can’t quite recall how long after Mircea began coming to our house that he first did something bold. At the dining table, Father sat at one end, Mother at the other, and Mircea and I sat facing each other in the middle. Slowly, he stretched out his leg and placed his foot on mine. I withdrew my foot,

--- Page 24 ---
I tried to convince myself it had happened by accident—even though my body and mind both jolted in surprise. But how strange, in that moment, looking at him, I suddenly thought—if he leaves, how will I survive?

After everyone had left the dining table, I asked, “Did your foot touch mine by accident? It didn’t seem so.”

“No, it wasn’t by accident.”

“What audacity! What courage! What if it had touched Father’s foot? What would have happened then?”

“Nothing would have happened. I would have immediately bowed to him.”

“How would you know?”

“Ha ha ha, how could I not tell the difference between your father’s foot and yours!”

Saying this, he moved his feet away, only to place them once again on top of mine.

An incident from forty-three years ago! How astonishing! How wondrous! I am sitting at that table—I can see him. He is wearing a panjabi, his chest exposed, his fair neck visible to me—his hands are on the table, but he doesn’t dare to hold mine. I am trying to move my feet away—I cannot, I cannot. Is this a sin? Is this a crime? What should I do now? Mother had said never to let a stranger man touch you, or your body will fall ill—and it’s true. My body is growing weak, but he will not move his foot—it remains firmly pressed on mine, even now, right here, in this very place—

A cool hand was placed on my forehead, and I awoke from a sleep of ages. Lekha was standing beside me. She said softly, “Mother, don’t you have anyone to whom you can tell all that is in your heart? A sister, a daughter, friends? You must tell someone what’s troubling you, or you won’t be able to bear it.”

Cries of the freedom struggle are everywhere, both violent and non-violent. There is constant unrest at Presidency College. One day, the students even demanded Principal Stapleton’s blood. Such incidents weren’t as common then as they are now. Although Father and Stapleton were fierce rivals, that day Father saved him. When a student was injured by police firing within the college grounds, an angry group of students surrounded the college. The police had left by then, but the students demanded that Stapleton’s blood be mixed with that of the injured boy. Father mediated, promising to escort Stapleton out safely if he apologized to the injured student. In those days, that was enough. Stapleton agreed, but as soon as he got into the car, he changed his tune and said he would apologize later. Father replied, “Very well, then I’ll get out now, and you can negotiate with the students yourself.” Stapleton refused to let Father go. He clung to Father’s coat, determined not to let him leave. Father, helpless, slipped off his coat and got out of the car. In the end, Stapleton was forced to apologize. Father recounted this comical episode of Stapleton hanging onto his coat at the dinner table that night, making us all laugh. Mircea decided then and there that he would go see the ‘revolution’ the next day.

Without warning, he left early the next morning. The whole day passed, and everyone grew anxious. Mother paced the house, worried that someone else’s son might have gotten into trouble. Just as Uncle was about to go inform the police, the culprit himself appeared—dusty, disheveled, exhausted, and utterly dejected. He had wandered wherever there was picketing, roamed the streets in search of danger, but nothing happened. Someone had thrown a clay pot of curd from an upstairs window, but it hadn’t hit him, only landed nearby. Nothing happened that he could show as a mark of adventure when he returned home.
