He feared that back in his country, everyone would ask, “When the revolution was happening in India, where were you hiding?” How would he convince his countrymen that he had truly been to India? This was his only worry.

24

--- Page 25 ---
Because he was the first person from his country to come to India. On top of that, his skin wasn’t getting tanned enough in the sun. So one morning he took a mat and went up to the roof, covered his face with a towel, and lay there until noon. By evening, his whole body was covered in blisters. We children laughed a lot; it wasn’t out of any special sympathy. Ma was very worried—this boy has started acting crazy. It’s hard to manage a wild European boy, especially when the language isn’t quite under control; Chandsi’s ointment arrived. Ma showed him how to use it; uncle, while applying it, began translating Ma’s scoldings into English.

Later, Baba explained to him, “Look, Mircea, everyone will know you’ve come to India just by listening to you, and you yourself will change from within. That’s what really matters. You can get a brown skin by lying in the sun anywhere—but the real transformation will come from Indian philosophy, from what you study here. That’s where the change will be.

And as for revolution? There’s no point running around to witness a revolution. Right now, revolution is happening everywhere in India. Is picketing and tear gas all there is to see? The very fact that you are staying in our home is a revolution—could you have stayed in my father’s house? Then my wife would have covered her face in front of you, and this Amrita, who goes to a boys’ college to recite poetry—could that ever have happened? Your utensils would have been kept separate, if you touched the rice it would have been thrown away—that was the way things were. Compared to that, even this house today is a revolution.”

There was going to be a grand literary conference at the Sahitya Parishad, where the Gokhale Memorial School now stands. For the first time, I would be reading an original prose essay—the topic was, “Where does beauty reside?” Is beauty outside? Or in the human mind? Why make such a complicated issue out of it? Beauty can never be an external thing; it is the human being who perceives beauty, that is, sees it with the eyes—in other words, in the mind, in a blue-shadowed illusion. I worked hard on this essay—whatever I wrote, Baba didn’t like it—but in the end, it came together. Now, a test lay ahead: Rabindranath was supposed to preside over the conference, but he wouldn’t be able to come, having fallen ill in Hyderabad. There would be much criticism of him at the venue, that is, at the literary conference. In this country, no one misses a chance to say a few words against Rabindranath. I was amazed to see how everyone was eager to speak with him, to visit him just once, and if they saw him in person, they would be overwhelmed, but behind his back, a thousand tongues wag in criticism. Baba’s feelings about him are much the same. He has read so much Rabindra poetry, discussed it endlessly, his attraction to Rabindranath is boundless, yet the criticism is constant, especially directed at me, as if I am responsible for all his “faults and failings.”

That’s exactly what happened at the conference. There were many unpleasant remarks—my heart grew heavy, even though that day I was highly praised—I had never before read a prose essay at such a large gathering. Of course, I had read poetry many times, even at the Senate. That day, I was the youngest writer; the eldest was Man Kumari Basu—Michael Madhusudan’s niece. She read her poem on the stage, barefoot, wearing the widow’s white sari. What a change between two eras! I thought, if Mircea could see this revolution, he would understand.

When I returned home, I found him sitting quietly. Here, he was alone, with no friends except us. So I said, let’s sit on the veranda and talk.

In the midst of our conversation, Mircea suddenly asked me, “Why are you so sad?”

I told him everything that had happened at the conference—how they had criticized him for not keeping his word. Now it was his turn to be melancholy. After a few words, he said, “How can a little girl like you love a seventy-year-old man so much?”

My ears rang with anger. “Why? Does a person become unworthy of love just because they are old?”

--- Page 26 ---
He was a little agitated, “Look Amrita—either you do not know yourself, or you are deceiving yourself, because you do not have the courage to face the truth, or you are knowingly telling lies.”

“Mircea, I have a terrible headache, please be quiet for a while.”

Father had said, “Ru, you should learn some French from Mircea. Without learning French, your accomplishment will not be complete.” Father was determined to complete my education. Anadi Dastidar would come to our house to teach me music, Ramen Chakraborty would come to teach painting, and a Goanese master would come to teach me violin! Managing so many lessons had become a burden for me, and I did not enjoy any of it. I had no perseverance—sitting by the window with a book of poetry in my hand, my mind would drift far away. Anyway, Mircea would learn Bengali from me, and I would learn French from him. We would sit down to study with the best of intentions, but the lessons would never progress. Why they didn’t, I do not know! We would sit in his room to study, a lamp burning between us, a standing lamp. So many days and nights passed like this—I would read poems from Balaka, and he loved to listen, but how could I possibly explain those poems in another language? There was one poem he managed to understand, and he liked it very much—

“You have given the bird a song, the bird sings its song.
It gives nothing more.
You have given me a voice, and I give even more in return—
I sing a song.”

Man is not satisfied with only what he has received, for he is a creator. The power to turn sorrow into joy is in his hands.

“You gave me sorrow, pressed to my burning forehead,
I washed it again and again with tears,
Turning it into joy, I return it to your hands—
On the night of union, at the end of the day.”

I would read these poems, yet I was not old enough to truly understand their meaning. But I could grasp that joy and sorrow blend together.

Some days we would study late into the night; nine, ten o’clock, and no one said anything—neither Father nor Mother. Still, I felt a certain shyness, a discomfort—what if someone thought something? I noticed a curiosity in the eyes of the servants, and in the eyes of my eleven-year-old younger sister, a suspicion and sharpness. Sweet Sabi, but she was always sad and complaining that no one loved her, that everyone loved Lidi! Now she could see that even Mircea Euclid was drifting in that direction, so Sabi declared that she too would study Bengali with him. Good. At least if she learned from him, she might actually gain some knowledge—whereas our studies were not progressing much at all. Still, I continued my Sanskrit lessons with Mastermoshai. Mircea was memorizing the dhaturupas diligently; he would soon surpass me. I did not have his perseverance.

Many people lived together in our house. None of them were close relatives. But we did not know that. To us, everyone was our own. Mircea could not quite understand this. In their country, there were only the words ‘cousin’ and ‘brother’. He could not grasp how someone with no blood relation could be as dear to us as a brother. For us, the world is indeed one family—vasudhaiva kutumbakam. That is why, even though he was a complete foreigner, everyone in the house had accepted him as their own.

Uncle was very affectionate towards me. When we sat for lessons with Mastermoshai, Uncle would come and start telling stories. He would tease Mastermoshai, Gourmohan Ghosh, a lot. One morning, Mastermoshai came with a sandalwood mark on his forehead. He had bathed in the Ganges and the mark made him look especially pure. Uncle suddenly knelt down before him with folded hands and said, “Mastermoshai, I am a great sinner, please bless me with a touch of your feet”—Mastermoshai, embarrassed, tried to withdraw his feet—“Ah, Shreesh Babu, what…

--- Page 27 ---
“It’s becoming too unruly.”

“Won’t Mastermoshai save this sinful soul?”—what a pitiful plea! And here we are, two students, laughing ourselves silly.

As I go upstairs, my second aunt glances at me with a frown. She arrived yesterday. Since then, I’ve noticed her suspicious gaze—so irritating. Aunt said, “Why were you giggling so much with that sahib boy?”

But I’m not one to be cowed. “Sahib boy? Doesn’t he have a name?”

“Ah, a name? You’re getting far too bold. Narenbabu is spoiling you completely.”

Let it be—I don’t want to stand here any longer. I don’t want to get into an ugly argument with her. Better to escape.

Past Mircea’s room, there’s a narrow corridor leading to the veranda where the ‘letter box’ is kept. The postman leaves our mail there—the box is locked, and I keep the key. Two or three times a day, I open it to collect the letters. Even though the post arrives at a set time, what’s the point of opening it at random? But I can’t help myself; several times a day, I feel compelled to check if any letters have come. Especially at noon, when the house is calm and half-drowsy—though not asleep, for no one naps in our house, everyone reads—I feel the urge to go in search of letters. I know why I go to check the mail; I’m not a fool. How long can I keep deceiving myself? Even though Mircea says I’m either a fool or a liar, I know I’m neither. All afternoon, I’ve been feeling restless—should I go see if a letter has come? Like in a Bankimchandra story, Sumati and Kumati are quarreling in my mind—Sumati says, ‘Never! You’re not going to check for letters, you want to see Mircea—why not admit the truth?’ And Kumati, that lying Kumati, says, ‘Nonsense, why? Someone might have sent a letter, after all.’

I get up, close the door, and bolt it. I sit on the bed—I must protect myself from my own self. I open up my Mahua notebook—

“Far away, on the temple shore, you have set out on your journey—
I am a tree, with my shade I touch you,
My roots kiss your path,
O pilgrim, some part of your devotion
Remains with me,
I will stay by your side as a witness to your journey—
In your worship, some part of me is lost
In the fragrance of flowers and incense.”

…These aren’t my words, they’re my mother’s. My mother has always cast her shade over my father’s life, absorbing his sorrows. She asks for nothing for herself, but with such love, such sacrifice, such endless service, she has surrounded him. Surely my mother’s gift to my father’s pilgrimage is immense. Her life is elevated, filled with selfless giving. But I do not want this. I do not desire such a life. The poem ‘Sabala’ speaks for me. I will conquer my own fate. Oh, what a boast—when I have no strength, no power, no wisdom, I am utterly defeated. Suddenly, I feel like crying—collapsed on the bed, I weep—‘Give me strength, give me power in my soul… to bear your work upon my head, to endure the heat of the world…’ What is this heat of the world? Is it this scorching flame that burns my body and mind? If so, let it burn, let the heat come—I want this warmth… ‘Touch my soul with the philosopher’s stone of fire…’

Suddenly, I don’t know when I’ve come downstairs. I see Mircea, lifting the curtain, standing by the door, “What—

--- Page 28 ---
“Did you get a letter?”
“No, it didn’t come. I feel so disappointed.”
“Whose letter are you waiting for?”
“Someone unknown.”
“Who is that?”
“I don’t know. It’s precisely because I don’t know them that the waiting is so sweet.”

I am talking about ‘Dakghar’—but he doesn’t understand, he’s frowning. What will happen! If we had to spend our whole lives together, half our words would remain unspoken, and we’d have to keep consulting the dictionary. Why did this thought come to me? How could I spend my whole life with him? He’ll leave in just a few days. I’ll be married off too. Who knows where, or to whom—I can’t think of a single person with whom I could imagine living my whole life.

“Won’t you come inside, Amrita? I brought Knut Hamsun’s ‘Hunger’ for you.”

For the first time, he gave me something. I took the book from his hand. He had written my name inside, and in French, a line: “Amitié—”

I’m standing. I don’t have the courage to sit. Why, I don’t know. He’s standing too—he won’t sit unless I do. I turn my back to him and stand by the piano, lifting its lid. Am I waiting for something? Is some impossible event about to happen? Do I want that? He’s standing very close to me. But he hasn’t touched me. He could put his hand on my back, but he hasn’t. There’s a sliver of sky between us, and we’re both standing—yet I feel him with every part of my being, I sense the touch of his presence in my mind. What is this! The sky isn’t empty, it’s filled with ether. I don’t know what ether is, but surely it’s with invisible hands that it has made this connection—around me, the air is fragrant with his breath—

“After the tired scent of Sayantan’s flowers in the wind,
In a bodiless embrace, every limb is filled.”

From upstairs, someone called, “Ru ru ru—”

“I’m coming….”

Mircea is twenty-three, and I am sixteen. But for our ages, we are both a little too philosophical—Baba calls me ‘Jyeshthatat’. But who is making me so? Baba himself. And he’s making Mircea the same way, always talking about Ashtanga Yoga.

And when it comes to Tantra, everyone in this house is a scholar. When Uncle starts a discussion about Tantra with Mircea, his face grave as he speaks of the mysteries of ‘He Vajra’, I get annoyed. I know that boys and girls our age talk in much lighter tones. I too once wanted to be a scholar. But now that desire is fading. Especially because Mircea’s taste for philosophy has grown so much. Whenever we meet, he tries to make me read world literature. Yesterday, he told me a strange, supernatural story all day, which I couldn’t make any sense of. A man is sentenced to death; the woman he loves lives far away. At the moment of execution, he remembers her. Then, ten months later, that woman has a son who looks exactly like the man. I couldn’t make head or tail of the story. I wish he would tell more ordinary stories, flatter me a little—what harm would that do? Like others do. But that’s not to be; instead, yesterday he made me quite angry, saying, “Do you think you’re very beautiful?”

“Why should I think so? Many others do.”

“Who, for instance?”

“Come walk with me on the street, you’ll see how people stare from both sides!”

--- Page 29 ---
“Oh, that’s always the case—in your country, you never see girls on the streets. Do you know what surprises people most when they come from Europe? They feel as if there are no women in this country at all.”

“Why wouldn’t there be women? They go about in horse-drawn carriages, in palkis—those who have cars go by car. Do you expect them to wander the streets on foot?”

“In other countries, they do.”

“Let them. Can’t you tell whether I’m dark or fair? Father, I, Sabi, Milu, Ma—are we all the same? Is Jharu like me too?”

“I can’t tell the difference between colors.”

“Fine, I’m leaving then.”

“Sit, sit, my dear, let me look at you properly, see if I can tell.”

“Ugh, you’ll need a microscope to see if I’m beautiful or not.”

“I don’t see your exterior, I want to see your soul.”

Now my crown chakra was burning, my eldest uncle—surely he would now launch into a debate about the individual soul and the supreme soul. Annoyed, I left. I was feeling terribly low. Standing before the mirror, I kept looking at myself, unable to decide—am I really beautiful? I was full of doubt—every flaw seemed magnified. Milu came and asked, “What are you staring at so much?”

“Listen, brother, there are so many kinds of attire in India—why do we always wear the same thing? Always that same old style of sari, or the Bengali way?”

“Truly, the Rajputani ghagra is beautiful, and the long Marathi sari isn’t bad either.”

For some time now, Milu and I had been absorbed in jewelry design. The jewelry usually made in our time was a crude imitation of foreign designs. There was no real beauty or artistry in them. In fact, from two or three generations before us, there had been a decline in artistic taste. In the Tagore household, there was a movement to revive it. In home décor and personal adornment, people were searching out old designs, bringing back the fashion of antique jewelry. This was called ‘Oriental’. Stella Kramrisch was an expert in this field—but Milu and I, ordinary women, would also take designs from old Rajput silver jewelry, from Orissan silver ornaments, and have jewelry made. We made a lot of jewelry. And what was the price of gold then? Eighteen rupees a tola! Then we decided—why not wear silver jewelry too? At that time, only women from lower classes wore silver ornaments. We started the trend, and soon it became popular. We even made shoes by attaching velvet to the anklets, though that ‘turtle-shaped’ thing wasn’t exactly beautiful, but people certainly stared at it. That day, we decided to dress like the women of Orissa. We wore our saris short, adorned ourselves with lots of jewelry, and I wore one of Ma’s partings. The parting had three strands—one over the center, and two chains running along each side, with a locket dangling over the forehead. When Ma wore that parting at her wedding, it had so enchanted Father that his fascination with it never faded. Ma didn’t wear it anymore, but if there was any excuse to put it on me, Father would be delighted. That day, I wore the parting, draped the Orissan sari short, and hung kadam flowers by my ears. We even smeared our faces with the pollen of kadam flowers. These days, we no longer use soap—we have renounced foreign products. We used to put Hazeline on our faces, and Johnson’s talcum powder. Now, we can’t even touch those. For now, we had kadam flowers, which was nice, but it wouldn’t last long.

“Ru, it’s good you’ve stopped using soap—your complexion has become much fairer from applying ground masoor dal.”

“Oh, stop your nonsense. Don’t you dare say anything about my complexion or looks. That’s a terrible mistake—”

--- Page 30 ---
I get the idea. That causes harm.”

“Is that so? All right, listen—if I don’t put on powder, at least a touch of alta on the lips wouldn’t have been bad.”

“Oh dear, I don’t have the courage, my friend, I just don’t.”

I’m parting my hair carefully, reciting Meghaduta—

“Meelu, if I had a lotus, my look would be complete.”

“Why, what would you do with a lotus?”

“Oh, not just any lotus—nilakamal, the blue lotus.”

Meelu doesn’t know Meghaduta. “Listen, I’ll tell you—

Haste nilakamalamalake balakundanuddham  
Nita lodhuprasava-rajasa pandutamanane shrih  
Churapashe nabakurubakam karne charu shirisha  
Simante cha tadupanajam yattra neepam vadhunam—”

“All right, what flower is kurubak?”

“I think it’s bakul.”

“Nonsense, how can that be? Bakul flowers are eaten fried—can you ever write poetry about that?”

“Why not? If you eat it fried, why can’t there be poetry about it?”

“Haven’t you read the debate between Sarat Chandra and Rabindranath? There can’t be poetry about things that are useful.”

“Is that so? But all flowers are useful in some way.”

“How so?”

“For bearing fruit, my friend—for bearing fruit. Just like you, who are like a flower...”

‘A’ enters the room—‘A’ is my mother’s cousin, but he’s about our age, so our relationship with him is like a brother. We love him very much—he has such a sweet nature, and he plays the flute beautifully. Twirling his flute, he enters the room and is stunned by our preparations.

“What’s going on, where are you off to?”

“Why, can’t we look beautiful at home?”

“Of course you can. Now tell me, where does beauty belong?”

He’s referring to my essay. Then he circles around, looking me over—“Ah, you’ve parted your hair.”

Mohit hare besh banade, sinthi lagade bhale—  
Urahi bilambita lol chikur mama bandhah champak male.

As he sings, ‘A’ tugs at my bun, “Let your hair down—” and all at once, my loose hair spills over my shoulders and chest, just like ‘lol chikur mama’. In that moment, the three of us look in the mirror—astonished, we gaze at ourselves—no doubt, my elder uncle has frightened me.

One event after another—how am I entering these memories again, how am I able to write this story—events from forty-three years ago. Could I have done this ten years ago? Fifteen? Twenty? Never—if I had, my whole life would have changed. All these years, I didn’t even know that a tragic novel was being written, slowly, in invisible ink, in unspoken language, in unheard rhythms, across my entire life. Forty-two years of life passed, and these scenes remained locked away. Completely sealed, like a gulmohar pressed and locked in a box. Sometimes, a jolt here or there made me realize something was stored inside, but nothing more. That day, suddenly, a word from Sergei’s mouth—

‘Mircea, Mircea, Mircea, I have told my mother that you have only kissed me on my...’

--- Page 31 ---
A frightened girl's lie became a golden key, unlocking the door to that inaccessible cave—Bhairava has emerged—I hear the footsteps of Mahakala—Nataraja is dancing. One foot in the past, one in the present. “Ta ta thai thai”—my diamonds and emeralds are scattering. Waves of laughter and tears are swelling—my breastbone is being crushed, this unbearable pain. Nataraja, stop your dance, fold your legs and stand still, become Keshava, the bearer of all sorrows, the one who holds the conch, discus, mace, and lotus, or come in the form of a dwarf, place your third foot upon my head, let me be trampled and crushed beneath your feet like a beast! Can you not become the compassionate-hearted Buddha, the remover of all impurity and suffering? Soothe my burning pain, wipe away my delusions—Keshava in the form of Buddha, grant me release, I do not wish to see him anymore. Let me wake from this dream, in my everyday, well-ordered household, in the arms of my husband, with my children in my lap, among my own people—I want to awaken there.

There is Mircea, bent over, making coffee on the gas ring, which sits on one side of the dining room. Standing in front of the kitchen door is Jhadu, our cook, with his very dark, round face, curly black hair on his chest—a little forest man. He will never wear a shirt—he cannot be made civilized. He is handing over the sugar. He only likes the coffee when foam forms on top, this is called Turkish coffee, he wants it every day after both meals. I can see him, pulling a handkerchief from his pocket to hold the saucepan handle, a cup in his other hand. Jhadu follows behind. On Mircea’s fair face, under the frame of his black glasses, beads of sweat have formed. That is the steam from the coffee. Mircea pulls a chair up to the dining table and sits down. And just then—

“Nataraja, mad with dance, seemed to keep time with both hands  
We did not see, but Mahakala entered there and then  
How could we see? We were each intent on the other  
Like moths drawn to the flame, trembling with desire.  
Upon the table in the Bhabanipur dining room  
The arranged tea was lifted away by the great, eternal, infinite  
Majesty of the everlasting.”

As we entered the library, our quarrel began again, ending on the upstairs verandah—
Mircea said, “What, you won’t talk?”
“No, if you behave like that, I won’t speak to you at all. Why should you even want to talk to me? I’m no better.”
“Did I call you bad?”
“Cruel, heartless, you don’t even know the basics of courtesy—if that’s not bad, then what is?”
“What did I do?”
“Why did you behave like that yesterday?”
“What did I do?”
“You and Milu, with your arms around each other, walked off together, and the moment I came near you, you moved away.”
“How strange! She is our relative, you are not.”

I remember my mother’s advice, from when I was quite small—about twelve years old, still wearing a frock. One of my mother’s relatives used to visit our house. He was much older than my mother. I don’t know his exact age—children can never tell how old grown-ups are. But he helped many people, everyone liked him. Whenever he came to our house, he would liven things up with much laughter and would sit me on his knee. I was just a little girl in a frock, no one thought anything of it—but he, one...

--- Page 32 ---
A strange thing would happen—suddenly, he would touch my newly blossomed chest. The first day, I did not understand. On the second day, I told Ma. Hearing this, Ma put her hand to her head, “Oh fate! Even after marriage, his nature hasn’t changed—such a scoundrel, I won’t let you face him again.” Ma never uses such obscene words; in our house, because of her, no one ever says anything vulgar. I’ve heard in other households words like ‘haramzada’, ‘shala’, etc., are used freely, but not in ours. Even if such words reach our ears, we find them unbearable. For the first time, I heard Ma use such a terrible word. She was so angry that she couldn’t find a more civilized curse. Then Ma told me, “Ru, if anyone ever does something like this again, come and tell me immediately. Never let any unrelated man touch you, nor should you touch them. The consequences can be very bad. Your health will suffer, even death could result.”

I didn’t quite believe her, “How could death happen?”

“I can’t explain everything to you now, but listen to what I say—girls must be very careful.”

It’s true that many people behave very strangely. As time goes by, I have seen lustful glances even in the eyes of very respectable men. It makes my skin crawl, I feel disgusted. Take Kamal Babu, for example—so educated, considered cultured, even elite, a true devotee of Rabindranath, knows many poems by heart—yet whenever he comes, he annoys me. The other day, I was in the sitting room, a cloth tied around my waist, dusting the shelves, when he arrived. Seeing me like that, he started reciting poetry—“The royal glory that your touch bestows, making it twice as magnificent, you sweep the dust in your own house with such beauty…” I always enjoy hearing poetry, so I too began to recite—when suddenly, the gentleman grabbed me from behind. It felt as if a snake was about to strike; somehow, I managed to break free and escape. When Ma heard, she said, “Just because someone wears a Punjabi and nagrai shoes doesn’t make him a gentleman.” Now I understand Ma’s words. I know there is an invisible, secret world behind all this, though I don’t know everything about it. Listening to Ma, I began to feel a deep sense of guilt. She was right. I shouldn’t have let ‘A’ put his arm around my neck, even if he was like an elder brother.

My mind was racing with many thoughts—when suddenly I saw Mircea looking at me intently—“Forgive me,” he said, and with both hands, he took my hands and kissed my palms.

I stood there silently, transfixed. He did not let go of my hands. My palms were crushed within his grasp, being ground down. He rested his face upon them—an eternity seemed to pass. Who knows how long it was; he would not let go, and I tried to free my hands, but could not—I tried to pray upward, tried to protect myself from myself, for now I wanted to run away, to escape his embrace. But he did not pull me any closer; there was still a great distance between us, and he knew I was resisting—“Let go, let go, Mircea, you’re crushing my hands, you’ve nearly killed me.”

Mircea lifted his face. I stared at him in amazement, not wanting to move away at all. My body was ringing with anklets—this could never be called falling ill. It was as if butterflies had spread their wings on either side of me—the sky had descended, the sun-drenched blue heavens seemed to have fallen to earth. The ‘blue firmament, bowed in a kiss,’ was resting motionless upon my hands.

That night was a wondrous night. All around, the interplay of moonlight and shadow had transformed that Kolkata street into Indrapuri. I stood on the veranda—one o’clock, two o’clock, the clock chimed—dong, dong—the moon rose from one side and traveled to the other—the constellation Orion moved across the sky—I stood alone on the veranda, my hair unbound, flying in the breeze, my shadow cast upon the wall beside me—I gazed at my own shadow like a nasisasa, my boat-like body-shadow wrapped in the enchantment of the full moon.

--- Page 33 ---
It astonishes me. What kind of mind dwells within that body? I am searching for it—  
Mircea wants to see my soul—Shankar has said that the world is false without the soul—everything is like that shadow. But even that shadow is not false—beside it, the madhabilata vine sways, that too is real, luminously real.

I feel so good, my body and mind are filled with a wondrous happiness—I had heard the phrase ‘floating in an ocean of bliss,’ but only now do I truly understand its meaning—my mind has become like the moonlight—like the Somapayi, I am drinking moonlight and floating in the Milky Way—there are star-lamps in my hands, a garland of stars around my neck, above me the red Brahma-heart burns, that is my bindi—star-anklets jingle on my feet—rumuku jhumuku rimi kim, those are not anklets, below, Mircea is playing the piano—surely he will play late into the night. He will not be able to sleep, just as I cannot, I know what he wants. The veil of that unknown realm of mystery is lifting—I can sense what lies beyond, my mother’s words in which I had unshakable faith are losing their hold—there is nothing sinful here, otherwise I would have recognized it, as I have so often recognized the poised serpent, but this is a garden of moonlight, this is a pond of lotuses, or as the poet wrote, a sea of beauty—is this the sea of beauty? Whenever I try to think of anything, poetry comes to mind, and when poetry comes, so does the poet. How long it has been since I saw him, yet I do not feel pain for that, is this wrong? Is this a betrayal of truth? Did I not once, at dawn, look towards the first sun and say—‘May all my love rush to you, Lord, to you’—I will let nothing and no one be greater than you—have I strayed from that truth? For some days this question has come and gone in my mind, I have not found an answer—today I have found it—today the answer has descended the steps of joy and stands before me. Can anyone stray from truth? Truth holds everyone within itself. All the strings of my mind are bound to its melody, it is He who plays this song—He has raised waves of music through my body and mind—it is through His song that my mind enters this wondrous realm of mystery—just as it is written—‘When I see the world through your song, then I know it, then I understand it’—He wrote of God, I think of man, I am just a little girl, man is enough for me—I have no need of God.

Shanti waits in the room, half-asleep, now she calls—“Ru, won’t you come to bed?”

“I’m coming, I’m coming.”

In my room, my eleven-year-old sister Sabi and Shanti sleep. Shanti is lying on the floor—I will sleep on the bed with Sabi. Some nights I too spread a mat on the floor and sleep there, I like it. Sabi is asleep. I sit beside her and adjust the blanket over her body. Moonlight falls on her beautiful face, framed by curly hair. How simple and innocent her light-washed face is! But is anyone ever truly simple? This morning, as I left the library, she was standing by the stairs, and in her bright eyes I saw a sharpness. What was that? The thought makes my skin prickle. An unknown, ominous fear stirs the ocean of my joy. I want to fold my hands and pray. But to whom shall I pray, I do not know. Human ears can hear only the waves of sound. They do not reach far. But is there any ear that is both far and near? Where everything reaches? Who are you that gave my guru the philosopher’s stone to awaken me? Who are you that have set this ocean of joy flowing? If there is such a one, then give—give me the formless jewel—do not send me back empty-handed. Sleep is coming over me—Mircea had made a small wound on my hand, I am stroking it and it is slowly growing, spreading over my chest—

--- Page 34 ---
I see myself lying wounded—he has gone away.

Father said, “If Mircea can make some time, then you and he can start cataloguing the books anew.” There are so many books in Father’s library. Several thousand—who knows how many—perhaps seven or eight thousand. There is a large square wooden box, divided into compartments, and cards have been prepared—we work together for four or five hours every day. Then, in the evening, we all go out for a drive together. Altogether, we spend seven or eight hours together each day. Yet, in the afternoons, when I go upstairs for a little while, I feel restless, longing to be near him. As if someone is binding me to him with an invisible rope. Can this bond ever be broken? If it is, I will not survive! But I have not breathed a word of this to anyone. Not even to Milu. She would not understand. She would think ill of me. But I know I am not bad—never!

I have not told Mircea either about the state of my heart; he is full of suspicion. It seems he has no faith in me at all.

Whenever we are together—whether talking about a story from a book or about poetry: “Have you read the book ‘Hunger’? I didn’t like it.” There was a particular reason I didn’t like it. That I cannot tell him. There is a scene described there, and my mixed curiosity and discomfort about it is the reason for my dislike. Besides, “Hunger”—a man suffering day after day without food—how can I understand such pain? I have never known the pangs of hunger. Not for a single day have I gone without food. Mother is always coaxing me to eat delicious things; even the hunger of the body is mentioned there—but I have never suffered from it. That world is foreign to me. I do not know the hunger of the mind either—for beauty, for taste, for color, for fragrance, arrangements are always made to fulfill them. I told him, “I cannot understand the book ‘Hunger.’”

He said, “Let me see?” As I handed him the book, he put his arm around my waist and drew me close, and perhaps, driven by the conventions of ages, with my left hand still holding him, I slapped his fair cheek with my right hand—oh, the shame still burns in me to think of it—a real slap, and quite hard. His cheek turned red. He stared at me in astonishment. Gripping my right wrist tightly, he said softly, “You hit me!”

“What could I do?”

“Do you know, in our country, if a girl does something like this, it is an impossible insult? It’s called jilting.”

“But this is not your country.”

“Fine, I’ll leave tomorrow, and never come back—”

My very soul shriveled in fear—what will happen if he really leaves?

“Forgive me, Mircea, forgive me. I didn’t do it on purpose.”

“Not on purpose?” he was surprised.

“I swear to you, I don’t know what happened. It was my hand that misbehaved, not me.”

It didn’t take long for things to return to normal. I was remorseful, very gentle. Look at the state of my mind again, I will say nothing. But he is cautious, very cautious.

Our quarrel was over, and after much thought, I realized that since he wants me so much, something must be done. My understanding of the relationship between man and woman is very vague, but through the mist, it is slowly becoming clearer. I am beginning to understand, not with my intellect, but with my feelings. When light falls on a flower and its petals open, the flower does not know from what unseen place the command to bear fruit has come. In the same way, love—bright and burning as sunlight—my...

--- Page 35 ---
Desire had entered my body and made me restless. I do not know what it is directing me towards! Indian women have inherited many deep-seated notions about physicality and chastity, and I am not free from them either. They do not love any man other than their husband—cannot love, cannot even touch. Any other man is a stranger, a forbidden man. Some women do act otherwise, but they are called wanton. I have heard these terms. For example, Binodini from ‘Chokher Bali’ is called a wanton woman. In the meantime, I have left ‘Charitraheen’ tucked inside the cover of ‘Noukadubi’. That wicked wife in ‘Charitraheen’ is quite similar to the man in the book ‘Hunger’—that young widowed wife says, “The thirst that makes a person scoop up and drink the black water of the gutter—I had that thirst.” What is this thirst? Surely it is not for water. Because, however poor they may be, there must have been water in their house. But these books are distasteful. A song hums in my mind—

“My eyes are thirsty,
Thirst spreads across my chest.”

No other writer can express the heart’s longing as beautifully as Rabindranath Tagore.

My heart trembles in the conflict between good and evil. Yet I have formed some notions myself, about which parts of the body, if touched, might be sinful. For example, when sitting at the dining table, if he repeatedly rests his foot on mine, that can never be a sin. How can touching feet be sinful? That is my irrefutable logic. Surely, touching hands is not sinful either, it cannot be, after all, we always shake hands. So one day, in the library, I told him, “Mircea, you can take my hand.” Saying this, I lifted my hand and offered it to him. He reached out both his hands and took mine. Suddenly I understood why hands are compared to lotus petals. Against the circle, the palm looks like a lotus—karakamal—how fair his hand looked against mine—I gazed with a trace of envy as he drew my hand into his with both eager hands—rested his head on my hand, his face on my shoulder. Again and again, I felt the touch of his lips on my hand, on my palm—gradually, my hand became detached from my body—or perhaps I became the hand itself. All my sensations gathered there—my focused being pulsed in that spot. I do not know how long, how long he kept my palm pressed to his chest, his body, his neck resting on my elbow, his face near my shoulder. He could not stay still. He kept moving. Gradually, my hand seemed no longer made of flesh and blood—the gross matter disappeared from it. It began to shimmer like lightning playing across the sky. The atoms and molecules inside him had become distinct—they spun, whirled like dancing planets and stars—planets, stars, moon, sun, all whirling in frantic haste—I kept my eyes closed—tears streamed from my eyes—“Mircea—what is this, what is this, what is this!”

My pillow was wet with tears—I was no longer in the Bhabanipur library—I was lying on my bed at our Ballygunge house, but strangely, my hand still felt like it was not my own. My body was numb—I tried to turn to my side, and saw my husband propped up on his elbow, looking at me—“Won’t you tell me what’s wrong?”

I have been married for thirty-eight years. I have managed my household with great skill, without any fault. I tried to reach out and hold my husband’s hand. Oh, shame, shame, why do these thoughts come after so many years, where is Mircea now! At that moment, a heartbroken sigh escaped me—it seemed to say—here I am! I have entered the breath of your life. Why have I forgotten for so long, why, why, why?

For some days now, a considerable commotion has started in our house. My grandmother has come here with a very close relative of ours, a girl named Aradhana. She is six years older than me—yet like a friend. Aradhana is extraordinarily beautiful. In their house...

--- Page 36 ---
There was a poor boy named Jatin who used to study with us. He was very good at his studies, handsome to look at, and obedient, so Aradhana’s father decided that she would marry Jatin. This marriage was settled from the time Aradhana was twelve years old. When she grew up, she would be married. They lived in the same house, saw each other often, but Aradhana could not stand the sight of Jatin. If Jatin entered the room, Aradhana would immediately get up and leave. If Jatin wrote her a letter, she would tear it up without even reading it. In those days, no one spoke to their parents about their own marriage. Still, again and again, she would cry and beg her mother, her father, her elder sisters, “I won’t marry him. I don’t like him at all.” Her parents and sisters were astonished. What kind of talk was this? Why wouldn’t she like such a golden boy? Too much pampering! Her sisters said, “He likes you so much, why don’t you? And who do you think you are, some princess, that we’ll turn down such a good match?”

So, when Aradhana was fourteen, she was married to Jatin, and they even had a son. Now Aradhana is twenty-three, her husband dotes on her, practically worships the ground she walks on. But Aradhana says her life is like a huge dose of quinine mixture—the bitterness just won’t leave her mouth. “Oh Ru, you don’t know how much I suffer living with that man.”

“But Jatin-mesho loves you so much!”

“Oh, enough of that nonsense.”

But one day, the bitterness of that quinine left her mouth—Aradhana tasted sweetness. A young professor, about her age, moved into the house next door. They got to know each other. Aradhana realized for whom she had been living all these years, enduring the taste of quinine in her mouth.

But that young professor—his name was Soumen—he too was born to an Indian mother, and like my own mother, his mother had taught him to know right from wrong, according to the ideals of this country passed down through the ages. Though those ideals were not meant for men; but Soumen was not just Indian, he was modern too, so in matters of principle, he made no distinction between men and women. Soumen’s love was even deeper than Aradhana’s; he proved it with his whole life. Yet, he told her it was better they did not see each other. He did not want Aradhana to fail in her duty to her husband. Hindus cannot divorce, so then? What would be the outcome? Therefore, he decided they would not meet anymore. But Aradhana did not have such a strong sense of propriety. She said, “Why shouldn’t we see each other? If Draupadi could have five husbands, why can’t I have two?” But that was just a joke. Soumen was tormented by his own mind—restless, unable to decide his duty. In the end, he wrote Aradhana a letter, urging her to be faithful to her husband, and shut himself away in the Christian penitentiary. He locked himself away. But Aradhana was determined to break her vow. One day, Aradhana said to me, “Ru, you write so well, won’t you write a letter for me, with lots of poetry and all?”

“How will you send it to the monastery?”

“I have my ways. You just write it, just as I say. Soumen is very learned, you know, so if there are any spelling mistakes, he’ll be upset. That’s why I’m asking you—otherwise, it’s not like I can’t write myself.”

I was quite proud. This was my first opportunity to write a love letter. Aradhana had brought out ‘Chayanika’ for the love letter; a huge problem, because it’s hard to tell which of Rabindranath’s poems are about love and which are about God. Still, ‘hidden love’ and ‘expressed love’—these two are quite clear. Aradhana found one—‘Why did you give love to my heart, if you would not give beauty, O Fate’—she insisted this line must be included. I said, “You’re so beautiful, this poem doesn’t suit you.” Aradhana...

--- Page 37 ---
Worried, he began to fidget, “But what will happen now, the poem was really good though.”

Anyway, in the end, a twenty-eight-page love letter was written and, unerringly, it found its mark. That arrow, sharp as a word, struck true, and the wounded Soumen left his self-imposed prison and appeared before us. Perhaps it wasn’t due to the merit of my letter. He himself had been waiting for an excuse. After that, one day, he fled to Agra with Aradhana and their son. What happened next is the cause of my current anxiety. There is a term for what Soumen did by taking Aradhana away, and unless I say it, one cannot grasp how terrible the act was: “taking the wife out of the house.” I hear every day that Soumen has committed this dreadful sin. As if Aradhana had no part in it. But I know it was Aradhana who freed Soumen from the prison of the monastery, and I helped her. Yet I truly did not know that such a great sin would result from this.

Jatin wrote that he has gone mad—so Aradhana’s father rushed to bring his daughter back, or rather, to snatch her away, and he succeeded. Arrangements are being made to suitably punish those two foolish, senseless children. My father will write to his university, and if he does, Soumen will lose his job. Amidst all these events, I am trembling with fear. If, by any chance, it comes out that I am the author of that historic love letter, what will happen then? I cannot even imagine what Ma would do. There is no measure for how much sorrow she would feel. She would weep and say, “Ah, you, my daughter, have done such a disgraceful thing? I am hanging my head in shame, is this what I taught you?” Ma might even jump from the second floor and end her life. And Baba! If Baba hears, the scolding I will get makes my hands and feet go cold just thinking about it: “You’ve grown up so spoiled! You’re a disaster at your studies. Have you even memorized the rules of grammar? Do you understand the principles of mathematics? And yet you’ve written a twenty-eight-page love letter?” He would shout so loudly that, just as the house in “Ha Ja Ba Ra La” collapsed at the sound of “Kingkartavyabimūḍha,” this Bakul Bagan house would similarly shatter to pieces. For two days now, I have been trembling with fear. My only hope is that Aradhana has enough pride not to reveal that someone else wrote her love letter.

These past few days, I haven’t even worked on the catalog. Who knows what Mircea is thinking, probably all sorts of nonsense. I can tell he doesn’t trust me at all. For instance, the other day I told him—I had sent my poem “Chhatim Tree” to “Kallol,” and the poet-babu there wrote me a very nice letter. He didn’t like that at all. Well, can’t one poet write a letter to another? I know why Mircea is so uncertain—I have never told him whether I love him, though he has said it many times. How can I say it? I don’t even know if this mixture of pain and joy I feel, this constant desire to be near him, is really love. I need to know if it’s true. Even my close friend Meelu—I haven’t told her. How could I? And what’s the point, she wouldn’t understand, she’s never loved anyone. Just to make me happy, she’d say something or other… There’s only one person I can tell, only him. Because he is my friend. Does age make a friend? Baba says, the definition of a friend is—atyag-sahano bandhu—the one whose loss or separation you cannot bear. I cannot bear separation from him. Is that it? That’s not entirely true. There have been so many days when I haven’t seen him, and yet I never felt like going to him. Why? Why? Why? Don’t I know? Because I never want to go anywhere, leaving Mircea behind.

Suddenly, I began to miss him terribly—I started to cry—“Companion of my eternal journey, my eternal beloved.” Meelu came into the room. “Ru, Euclid is asking if the cataloging will be done today?”

“Yes, of course it will.” I got ready to go downstairs. I fixed my hair, put on a bindi.

--- Page 38 ---
We no longer have our usual adornments. We cannot use perfume—after all, that is a foreign thing, so we use rosewater, kewra, and sometimes attar. Ma says I have no need for such things, I am a daughter of Padmini, my clothes are fragrant on their own. But then, why do I use fragrance? Do I not know? I am deceiving him. The poor thing thinks I am pushing him away, but that is not true. I want him close to me.

I am coming down the stairs, and my body is tingling—I cannot say for certain if this happened on that very day. Because I have no journal. I am writing of events from forty-two years ago, not from a diary, not even from memory. So I do not know if I am writing the events in the right order.

‘In order’—that is, as they seemed to me then, but now there is no before or after—now those days are all present at the same time, I cannot quite express it. Is it so hard to understand? In the vision bestowed by Sri Krishna, Arjuna saw the entire universe at once. I too am seeing that today. Parvati and Gautami, you must believe me—this is not memory, it is the present, I am entering 1930 again and again. 1930 touches me still. That is why I write—

Across the ocean of time  
The life that was lost  
If it returns once more  
As light in the sky of my mind  
With the moon and stars  
Seated together  
That sun-like form—  
Will reveal to me the cosmic vision—  
Then there is no other way  
With devotion, I seek grace...

That is why I have told you—Praseeda, praseeda, be gracious—my whole life—with all its mistakes, errors, sins and faults, I offer up to you, to humanity—I know no other god.

But unlike Arjuna, I will not ask this vision to be withdrawn. I want to see. I want to see again. I want to hold him with both hands... This miraculous longing has loosened the very core of my reason, I am praying, if truly there is someone, somewhere, who controls our fate, then carry me across these forty years. I cannot see the lines on his face, only the sharp gaze of his eyes—nothing else of his face is visible to me now—only, always, I see his fair throat and the small part of his chest peeking out from his shirt. I want to press my ear there—to listen, deep, very deep, to see if that strange music, at once separate and yet connected like a previous birth, still plays somewhere—the footsteps of first light, the first waves of love.

We are arranging books—writing names on cards—dropping the cards into boxes. We work in silence. I glance at him from the corner of my eye—his hand trembles slightly—who knows what is happening inside him. And then? What happened after that? I do not know which day. Where? In which room, I cannot see. I see a large window with bars—and in front of it, the two of us—I suddenly see myself in his embrace, his face descending upon mine—I try to pull myself away; I struggle, why do I struggle, who knows? Surely, only to be defeated. Not to preserve chastity, never that. I was defeated. Mircea’s face—

--- Page 39 ---
He placed his face upon mine. At the soft, sweet touch, my lips parted, and I felt the warmth of his lips inside my mouth. My whole body burst into song, yet tears streamed from my eyes—this is how I am—unable to tell which is joy and which is sorrow.

Where does he bring such happiness from? How does this happen? What I had heard, what Aradhana had told me—this is so much better than all of that. Of course, nothing can be understood by hearing alone. Nor by reading. Does everyone know this—I wonder—my mind is overflowing with questions, brimming to the edge—there is not the slightest sense of sin—why should I feel sinful? I have done nothing—I tried to resist. Let Mircea bear the sin, if sin there is. In his country, this is not considered a sin. From all the stories he has told me, I have understood as much. He lets go of me, and I fix my hair, straighten my disheveled sari. Inside, a tune hums—“There is nectar in my soul, do you want it?”

“Ru ru ru—”

“I’m coming, Ma—”

My body feels light, I can hardly walk, I am flying—my hair on either side like the wings of a startled bird—my heart’s peacock is dancing. I leap up the stairs—waves of joy ripple through the bamboo grove, trembling leaves quiver with the kiss of the southern breeze...

Suddenly, I see Shanti standing on the stairs, gazing at me intently. In her eyes, there is no jealousy, no, nor reproach—only a silent, searching question—the desire to know. But fear chills my hands and feet, the trembling leaves within me have stilled—the kiss of the breeze has vanished somewhere into the stale air, who knows where. But I need courage—fear brings disaster. So, with a calm face, I say, “What are you staring at, mouth agape?”

“Mouth agape? What am I staring at? Where have you been all this time?”

The thief’s mother always shouts the loudest. With a proud air, I reply, “Don’t you know? I’m cataloguing!”

Shanti keeps staring—now no longer questioning, but somewhat absent, almost wistful—are there tears in her eyes?

Shanti is five years older than me. I have just turned sixteen and will soon be seventeen; she, then, is twenty-one, about to be twenty-two. She looks just like her brother Khoka—broad face, same nose, not particularly beautiful—except for those two bright eyes beneath arched brows like bows. She talks a lot, always cheerful and laughing. They have always been poor. Very poor. Both her brother and she live with us. She knows little of books—where was the chance to learn? She was married off at nine to a nineteen-year-old boy from a village in East Bengal. The boy’s name was Romen. At nineteen, the boy soon turned twenty, and then twenty-one, the prime of his youth. But Shanti, in two years, was only eleven. She was still a tiny child. Uncle says Romen thought that upon waking, he would find his wife transformed into a full-sized woman—but when that did not happen, it was, of course, his wife’s fault. To punish her for this grave crime, Romen married a full-sized woman. Not only was this new wife older, she was beautiful too. So Shanti became a servant in her husband’s house. Her only duty was to serve those two. She had to get off her husband’s bed and sleep on the floor. The two of them would sleep under a mosquito net, and poor Shanti would sit and massage their feet. If she faltered, they would kick her. Shanti says, “I didn’t mind massaging feet so much—after all, it was my husband’s feet—but oh, the mosquito bites in the villages of Faridpur!” How long could this go on, how much could a person endure? At last, one day, Shanti swallowed poison, though she did not die. The neighbors informed Khoka, who brought her back. Shanti’s mother, who has borne eighteen children, wants to send her back to that wretched husband. She says, “Whatever happens, a woman’s home is her husband’s home.” My mother...

--- Page 40 ---
Hearing this, she quickly brought her here. Shanti’s mother says, “Sister-in-law, you’re not letting her go to her husband’s house—where will a young woman stay alone? Who will provide for her all her life?” 

Mother replied, “There’s no need to worry, she’ll stay with us and study nursing. I’ll make sure she stands on her own feet.”

When it comes to helping others in distress, my mother alone is an institution. There’s no count of how many people she has helped. Khoka too is staying in our house and learning typing, but Shanti and Khoka are not the same. Shanti works tirelessly from dawn to dusk to repay the debt of her food. Everyone orders her around, I do too. Who ever hesitates to say, “Shanti, bring me a glass of water”? But Khoka? He can laugh, but you can’t get any work done by him. Father can’t stand the sight of him. He avoids father as much as possible. If father could, he would throw him out of the house right now, but he can’t because of mother. Mother would never drive anyone away. And in this household, she is undoubtedly the mistress.

Poor Shanti has to live the life of a widow, even though her husband is alive. This pains mother deeply. But she’s not entirely a widow—she can wear sindoor, she can wear bordered saris, she can eat rice twice a day, even fish. But she’ll never marry again. A Hindu woman cannot remarry if her husband dies—let alone while he’s alive. But if mother could, she would marry her off. Mother says, “If there were any way, I would arrange Shanti’s marriage—she’s still a maiden—but there’s no way.” If she did anything illegal, Romen would come here and create a scene—just like the uproar when the demon Dhundhu was slain, he would make a commotion in the street. He’d gather people, go to the police station—he’s capable of all that, and he’s done it before over trivial matters.

But Shanti loves someone—he lives near her mother’s house—Shanti listens for the sound of his footsteps. But he never comes here. Mother would never tolerate that. He has a wife, he’s married. There’s no solution to this problem in Shanti’s life.

So I wonder, why did I see tears in Shanti’s eyes? She has suffered so much that her tears have dried up. A vague anxiety stirs in my chest. And just then, a song line hums in my mind—“My anxious heart, lest the circle’s thread break…” How did Rabindranath know so much? Surely he too experienced something like this—like Mircea, not like me, for I am a woman—or perhaps these are women’s matters, “in the blush of shame, her secret dreams awaken”—this is not about men, “shame,” “hesitation,” these are women’s things. So, some woman must have told him—who could she be? I must search through the books and see. No, I mustn’t think such things about elders.

I’m standing before the mirror, my anchal has slipped—my body longs to dance. I clap my hands and spin—

In the youthful lotus pond, union’s hundred petals,
In what restless flood, swaying, swaying, swaying—

I heard this song a few days ago at Presidency College, sung by Sushil De as he played the harmonium. How beautiful it was. I’ve thought so many times, if only I could hear it again from that gentleman, but how could that be? Could I tell father I wanted to hear that song from his student? But what if I had? Nothing would have happened. Father would have said, quite proudly, “Hey Sushil, my daughter wants to hear a song from you—come to our house sometime!” Anyway, I didn’t say anything. Besides, I didn’t really understand the song then. Now I do—every day, a song or a poem takes on new meaning in my mind or body. Does the body too understand the meaning of a song? Surely it does. This song finds its meaning in my body. That’s why I want to dance, swaying, swaying, swaying—I spin, spin, spin. Shanti enters the room and sits on the stool before the mirror.

--- Page 41 ---
Cell. The bright vermilion dot between her beautifully arched brows glows; I long to wear sindoor myself. Holding her face in my hands, I sang—

“In the midst of fragrant saffron, a single trembling drop of the eye—O Shanti, do you know what it is, the hundred-petaled lotus of union in the youthful lake?”

“Have you gone mad?”

“I want to learn to dance. At Shantiniketan, Rabindranath is teaching girls to dance.”

“Why don’t you go and learn?”

“As if it were that easy.” Would Baba ever let me? He would never allow me to go anywhere. I am imprisoned here—granted, imprisoned by affection, but no captivity is ever truly pleasant. Once, Rabindranath himself told me, “I will borrow you from your father for a few months. I am looking for someone for my play Malini. You could do it. Like Malini, you have a sky in your mind.”

“A sky in the mind”—such words I had never heard before. Until then, the sky was just the sky, but the moment I heard those words, it descended into my heart, its entire blue hue spreading within me. My soul filled with an indescribable joy. He is a magician of words; with words alone, he can gather clouds in the sky and bring down the Alakananda—he has no need for the Malhar raga. But did Baba let me go? He said, “Spend your life doing plays and theater, is that it? For a student, study is penance.”

“Look, Shanti, when I get married, when I am free, I will bring Sabita to my house and teach her to dance. No one will be able to stop me.”

I believed that marriage meant freedom for everyone. Of course, not if it was a marriage like Shanti’s. But who is truly free? Is my mother free? Not at all. What kind of marriage will I have? Mircea! Mircea! No, no, that will never happen. The circle’s thread will break. I clung to Shanti’s neck and began to cry. She was quite surprised, “Why are you crying? What will happen if you learn to dance? Or if you don’t?”

“It’s not for that. My heart feels so strange. It aches terribly.”

The large table lamp is burning, its shade like a big white bowl. On one side, I was leaning against the wicker sofa; on the other, Mircea sat on the bed, his legs stretched out. In that pose, his feet kept coming to my mind. I wanted to touch his feet—not with my feet, but with my hands. Oh, why should I want to touch his feet? He’s not that much older. Besides, he would be the one to touch my feet—if I sat with my face turned away like Radha, sulking, he would come to my feet, “Dehi padapallavamudaram”—that would be wonderful! Of course, I’m not really angry; it’s just that poem that makes me imagine such a scene. Reading the Gita Govinda, such images arise: Radha turns her face away, and Krishna bows at her feet. Krishna’s color is not dark, but fair. Our Brahmin priest sings—“What’s the use of sulking!” Now I am reading Jayadeva. I have received a book with a blue cover, with a picture drawn by Purna Chakravarti, and a Bengali translation. I don’t read the Bengali translation, of course—what need is there for such poetry? The Gita Govinda is understood as it is, though not every word. For example, “swargaralakhandaṇam”—what is ‘garal’? Where is poison in love?

To be honest, I don’t understand ‘Mahua’ either—Baba says there is a deep philosophical truth in the poem ‘Maya’ from Mahua, and he has explained it to me. But right now, I cannot focus on philosophical truths. I am understanding poetry in a different way now. Never before have I understood it so well. Every day I understand something new, but that poem from Mahua, “Let this moment be forever”—I cannot understand it. Why should this moment become forever, or why should I not have forever even in sorrow? Rather, I can understand, “We two shall not build toys of heaven on this earth”—I recited it to Mircea the other day. He was very...

--- Page 42 ---
I liked it.

I am looking at him—his face and eyes seem distracted, lost in thought—who knows what he is thinking about. If today he wants to read me Whitman, I won’t listen. That day he recited three poems, but they felt more like wooden sticks than poetry.

But today, Mircea isn’t thinking about literature. He doesn’t even want to read Bengali. He puts out his cigarette and pushes it into the ashtray, then looks at me sharply. After that, he takes off his glasses and wipes them—I can see his eyes without the glasses. When he takes them off, I get terribly anxious; his gaze changes—he seems like a different person. “Why can’t I bear it when you take off your glasses, Mircea?”

“It’s because I’m myopic.”

I worry so much about his eyes—what if he goes blind! He said, “Listen, I want to ask you something—will you marry me?”

I feel like laughing—so this is what they call a proposal—I’ve read about it in English storybooks. But it’s never done from so far away—one is supposed to kneel and hold hands. Nothing like that happened. Sitting seven feet away, he asks, “Will you marry me?” Oh dear!

The front door of this room opens right onto the main entrance of the house. People are always coming and going through here. There’s something hanging that’s supposed to be a curtain, but it’s so minimal, it might as well not be there—so, helpless!

On the piano, there’s a photo of his sister, a beautiful girl—I feel like befriending her. Mircea loves his sister the most. He said, “I’ve written to my mother and sister about you—they’ll be very happy. You won’t have any trouble at my home.”

Now my heart is trembling. There was a piece of paper and a pencil lying around, so I’m scribbling nonsense on it. What should I answer?

“Tell me, tell me, you don’t have any objection to marrying me, do you? Why aren’t you saying anything?”

“My answer doesn’t matter, Mircea, my father will never agree.”

He is utterly astonished! “They won’t agree? But they already have.”

“How do you know?”

“They love me so much, have accepted me so warmly—why else?”

“Well, so what? That doesn’t mean they’ll let me marry you.”

“Amrita, tell me about yourself, we’ll talk about them later—I want to know what’s stopping you.”

Look at that, he’s doubting me again—I’m thinking to myself, don’t doubt me, not at this moment—‘You are my life, you are my adornment, you are the boat to cross my ocean of existence’—but I can’t say it aloud, I can’t say anything, no! I keep my head down and keep scribbling nonsense on the paper—

“Listen, Amrita, I’m telling you, I won’t take you away from your loved ones, I’ll stay right here. I’ll take a lecturer’s job at the university for a salary of one hundred and fifty rupees—that’s all I need—it’ll be enough!”

“Mircea, my father will never agree, never.”

“Is it because I’m Christian? Because my skin is white?”

“I don’t know why, but surely it’s because you’re a foreigner.”

“Are you saying Professor cares about caste? For a philosopher, are there Hindus, Muslims, Buddhists, Christians?”

“There definitely are.” I’m writing on the paper—why did you come so close to me, why, why, why? Where did you come from, why did you come?

42

--- Page 43 ---
He says, “If that’s the case, then why did they let you be with me like this? We’re always together—”

“So what? Can’t people mix like siblings? If we couldn’t, that’s our own fault.”

“What!?” He’s astonished, wounded. As if he’s never heard such a thing in his life. I’m getting annoyed—does letting me catalogue books mean we’re engaged? What’s so wrong about letting me spend time with him? He keeps asking if I love someone else, and who I loved first.

“A tree, a tree, a satinwood tree.” What kind of answer is that! Though I’ve said it before, he still can’t grasp such strange poetic whimsy. He’s become very serious. The paper in my hand is now covered in scribbles. On the cover of *Mahua* was written “Shri Rabindranath Tagore”—we used to have a habit of imitating his handwriting. Everyone did it, and so did I. He stood up to look.

“Why are you writing his name here? Do you need his opinion, his permission, to decide if you’ll love me?”

“People all over the country imitate his handwriting. That doesn’t mean anything.”

“People all over the country imitate one man’s handwriting? Has the whole country gone mad?”

I get up, ready to leave. I want so much to go to him, to rest my head on his shoulder—if only I could, the sadness in his heart at this moment would vanish. But that’s not possible. I step onto the threshold and he says, “Amrita, listen to me, just one thing—forget Rabindranath.”

“How can you say that, Mircea? Can anyone forget the sun?”

“The sun! How can a person be the sun?”

“When you learn Bengali, you’ll understand whether a person can be the sun or not.”

In my heart I say, I will show you the sun—if I can, then we both will become worshippers of the sun together.

Now we’re both very busy with two things: uncle’s wedding, and the publication of my book. Uncle himself is eager to get married—he liked the girl at first sight, though Ma did not. Still, the wedding will happen there. From this time, Mircea has started regularly wearing dhuti and panjabi. He looks so handsome in dhuti and panjabi. Of course, I haven’t told him that. Why should I? He doesn’t understand when I say he’s handsome. That’s utter nonsense, of course, because I have plenty of proof in my hands.

For instance, just before Mircea arrived, my cousin Sita’s marriage was arranged with a zamindar’s son. The girl was shown from our house. The boy’s relatives came, examined her hair, checked her complexion, made her sing. Honestly, after hearing her song on the harmonium, they should have run away, but they didn’t—they liked her. They said, now that everything is settled, let the boy come and see the girl. The boy is modern, wants to talk to the girl. The girl’s mother is modern too. She said, let the boy come, let everyone meet and get acquainted. Let him see what a cultured family we are.

The boy came—his name is Mriganka. Very fair, with the plump, polished look befitting a zamindar’s son. Generally, you could call him handsome. Ma didn’t want me to appear before him—but the bride’s mother said, what’s the harm? The marriage is settled, if the bride’s sisters and cousins don’t come out, how will it be lively?

Ma couldn’t very well say, “My daughter is prettier than yours,” in case it caused trouble. So Mriganka came! Two days of feasting, going to the cinema, much merrymaking.

--- Page 44 ---
We did as we were told. I had always assumed that my sister would marry him, so I behaved with him as a proper sister-in-law would—laughing and making him laugh. Then Mriganka suddenly disappeared. After making inquiries at his hostel, we learned that he had gone back to his country. From there, his father informed us that Mriganka was going to England to study law, and now he absolutely refused to get married. There was much speculation as to what could have caused such a sudden change of heart. Anyway, my aunt said it was for the best that the marriage did not happen with such a restless-minded boy. And now I hear that a few days ago, from England, he has sent a proposal to my father—he wants to marry me. Both my parents are displeased. I told my sister at the time, “I will not let him take you away.” Such things are never good among relatives! Father said, “This is what happens when one is too forward.” And grandmother said, “One who comes to see one girl and looks at another with different eyes is of bad character. He can never be married to you.” That was the end of it!

But what if I told him this story today? Ha ha, what would happen then? It would be such fun. I could say to him, very seriously, “See, you cannot even tell whether I am beautiful or not.” Immediately, he would look at me with such longing—I have noticed lately that he tries to take back that statement, but I will not let him. Once said, it is final! Then I would say, “See, Mriganka could tell!” I know exactly what his reaction would be if he heard this story.

“Why, you never told me about this before?”
“Oh, what was there to tell?”
“What movie did you go to see?”
I would reply, very indifferently, “I don’t really remember.”

Ha ha ha, it’s so amusing. I love his jealousy so much. Is jealousy ever a good thing? It’s supposed to be terrible—‘Jealousy is black in color,’ or ‘Jealousy is a venomous serpent.’ Oh, but this is not that kind of jealousy. This is love, this is affection. The little bird of jealousy has awakened me from my sleep. That bird, flitting from branch to branch, from one day to the next, is calling out to me, “Friend, awake! Friend, awake! In the bower of my youth, the bird sings...”

But why do I never feel jealous? He tells me so many things—how, when he lived on Ripon Street, the girls there liked him so much—they were always after him, one even threw a pillow at him—so many stories, but they go in one ear and out the other. I don’t even feel like listening. Besides, how could I be jealous? He despises them, that’s why he ran away, so it’s not as if I’m being particularly noble! The way he talks about Anglo-Indians astonishes me. They all believe themselves to be English, our masters, and that Bengalis are a very lowly species—almost animals. Their hatred and anger towards Gandhi is intense. They themselves are extremely vulgar and uncivilized, yet they consider Bengalis to be uncivilized. It was truly difficult for Mircea to live among them. He was constantly nauseated—‘disgusted.’

How strange, we don’t even know this community. Where do they live, what do they do, what are their customs—we know nothing. We live in the same country, yet we know no one. They live among us, and we will always live together, yet we will never truly know each other. They have such ugly thoughts about us, and we? I never even noticed their existence. Who among us ever thought that they might be thinking something about us? If Mircea hadn’t told me, I would never have remembered that there is a community called Anglo-Indians. I see them only when traveling by train, or from a distance in the railway quarters. But I don’t dislike them—look at how neatly they hang curtains in their windows—Bengalis can never hang curtains so straight. They keep flowerpots in their windows, they garden—Bengalis cannot do that. But they hate us, truly hate us, even if Mircea hadn’t told me, I would know that they hate to travel with us on the train...

--- Page 45 ---
Not even in the same room. And don’t we despise them? Of course we do! Is the word “mixed race” a good one? They don’t know us, that’s why—if they did, they wouldn’t hate us. If I could mingle with those girls, they would never hate me. No one has ever hated me, not till today. That boy who sometimes comes to Mircea—if I befriend him, let’s see if he hates me. Oh—let him hate me, then! What if I say, “Mircea, introduce me to him, I want to test if he really hates me.” What would happen then? Ha ha ha, the birds would sing again—  
“In the grove of my youth, the bird sings.”

These days, Mircea wears dhuti and panjabi every afternoon—and I hear he will become Aryan, become Hindu. He has studied Hindu scriptures, so is it necessary to renounce his own faith? I don’t know why he wants to become Hindu; the main enthusiast in this matter is Kaka. Could it be that he thinks, if he becomes Hindu, there will be no more obstacles? Mistaken, mistaken. He doesn’t know our society at all. Here, nothing is governed by logic—doesn’t Baba know that among his students, Mircea is undoubtedly the best? But so what? He is of another race—are races the same? He is a foreigner, and then a Christian; even if he weren’t—even if he were a Hindu Bengali, still he would have to be of our caste. Even that wouldn’t be enough—he would have to be of our gotra. Alas, how many knots of “small and great prohibitions” there are, he does not know. He knows, he knows everything, he’s always hearing about it—his questions about our customs and traditions are endless, but he doesn’t know how much these are observed even in our own home. And Baba, who knows so much, whose boundless scholarship knows no end—even he does not know that neither gotra nor caste determines a person’s happiness or sorrow. And I? I do not believe in these things, I do not accept them; even if I do not marry Mircea, I will prove with my whole life that I do not accept them. No ritual or custom will ever be greater than a human being to me. Let alone caste, I do not accept anything of Hindu society. I don’t even accept the puja. What happened during the puja at our ancestral home?

Two years ago I went to the country, that is, to our ancestral village home in East Bengal. In that house, seventy people live together. They are all cousins—sons and daughters of my father’s brothers and uncles. Besides them, there are fifteen or twenty students who study at the tole in this Kabiraj house. Everyone’s food is cooked together. The head of the house is the eldest brother. He earns the most. The others are more or less maintained by his income. He is very powerful, and he has two wives. Since the first wife did not bear a son, he married again. For this act, Ma and Kaka are estranged from him, but there are many in the house who are not, or even if they are, they don’t have the courage to say so, because he is the provider.

During the puja, we went to visit for a few days—we live in Calcutta, my father is famous and wealthy, so we are treated with great affection. In such a prosperous, landed family in East Bengal, the arrangements for puja are grand—idols have been made for days in the Chandimandap—nadus are being prepared, khoi is being roasted, til is being ground. I had not seen much of village life, so I was very excited, and our cousins were proud and delighted to have a city-bred sister in a silk sari and velvet slippers among them. Though by the second day I had taken off the velvet slippers and joined their ranks. We became a large group—roaming about together. On both sides of the boat, there were bamboo groves, and in some places the long, drooping branches of great aswattha trees touched the water with their finger-like roots. Though it was my first time witnessing the puja at home and I knew nothing about it, I loved the preparations. The days seemed adorned with jewels. So many little details in the festival’s arrangements—  
“With the arrival of Anandamoyi, the land rejoices.”  
To tell the truth, in front of that village puja pavilion in East Bengal, I never saw a single beggar girl standing. Everyone was radiant with joy.

45

--- Page 46 ---
The first day was marred by an incident on the veranda of the tol, where many people were seated. As I went around offering my pranam to everyone, I saw a dignified, fair-skinned elderly gentleman. When I approached him to pay my respects, he almost jumped up and not only stopped me, but others also said, “You don’t need to pranam him, he’s a Muslim.” 

I was stunned—how could anyone be so rude? My uncle seemed apologetic for my supposed offense, saying, “She’s just a child, she doesn’t know any better.” What hurt me most was that even my father said nothing. Later, when I found him alone, I asked, “Wasn’t that terribly rude?” Father replied, “You did the right thing. You should show respect to your elders—what does caste matter?” 

“Then why did you stay silent?” I asked. 

“Your eldest uncle was there—when he speaks, how can I contradict him?” 

Now, as I think of Mircea, all these memories come flooding back. Even if Father understood what was right, he would not be able to act on it; these relatives of ours, with whom we have no real connection, who cannot even grasp half of what we feel, would become formidable obstacles. My second uncle would come all the way from the village—“Noren, don’t commit this great sin, don’t condemn fourteen generations to hell, don’t do it.” 

Nasu, who paints the backdrop behind the goddess’s idol, one day I saw him tying a medium-sized buffalo to a tree. 

“What’s this for?” I asked, though I already knew, painfully well, what was to come. Still, I asked, my heart twisting inside me. 

“On Ashtami, it will be sacrificed to the Mother, along with eight goats and this buffalo.” 

“Who will perform the sacrifice?” 

“I will. Who else can sever it in a single blow?” 

“Nasu, aren’t you ashamed to boast about killing a helpless creature in one stroke—how can this ever be called religion?” 

“Oh, what are you saying, Khuki? Shouldn’t there be sacrifice in the worship of the Mother?” 

Then all the brothers and sisters gathered around, and I began a passionate speech. Many of my peers sided with me. Together, we went to my second uncle. He was a simple, kind-hearted man, and even if there was no other reason, perhaps out of affection for me, he might agree. I liked him especially for one reason—when the students sat down to eat, he would supervise with a hookah in hand, making sure they got the best pieces of fish. They were other people’s sons, so they deserved special care—he feared the daughters-in-law might save the best for their own children. No other head of the household would do such a thing here. 

But my second uncle was not willing to discuss religious matters. He made it clear that he was not the head of the family. “Go to the eldest, the head of the house. Whatever he says will be done.” 

As I approached his room, I turned around to find my troop of siblings had vanished. Alone, I entered that formidable war camp. The all-powerful head of the family was a small, frail man. He sat hunched on the bed, hookah in hand. He was nearly sixty, and his young wife was nursing an infant nearby. I presented my case as clearly as I could. My request was that the buffalo and goats be released. If sacrifice was absolutely necessary, then, like the Vaishnavas, we could offer gourds or pumpkins instead. He kept puffing on his hookah. Finally, he lifted his face and said briefly, “We are Shaktas.”
