I could have argued a lot more. I used every reasoning I could, even brought up the debates of atheists with Brahmins. Those who had challenged the sacrificial Brahmins, saying, “If the animal goes to heaven when sacrificed, then why not bring your old father and sacrifice him? Isn’t that the easiest way to heaven?”

--- Page 47 ---
But the old man was adamant. He would not agree to anything. Later I heard he had said, “Noren’s daughter is a hair-splitter.”

Then I told my loyal younger brothers and cousins, “We will not take part in this Puja. For these four days—Saptami, Ashtami, Navami, Dashami—we will not stay at home.” In the mornings, we would take chiwda, puffed rice, naru, murki, bananas, and such, and go boating on the canals and lakes. In the evenings, we would return and dash straight to bed. We stopped speaking with the elders. It had become quite a picnic for us, and we didn’t feel bad at all. But at home, the aunts were heartbroken. The children didn’t stay home during the Puja, didn’t even see the festivities. In the end, at their request, we returned home to join the immersion ceremony.

I remember thinking to myself, if any real power had entered the idol, then when they sacrificed that poor buffalo, there should be some sign—a mark of prohibition. But nothing happened; the blood of the poor buffalo just flowed over the sacrificial block.

In this matter, Baba gave me freedom; he didn’t scold me. The fact that I could do something on my own, show strength of mind, pleased him. Perhaps this was my first step toward becoming a Sarojini Naidu.

But in Mircea’s case, would I get such support? No, never. And could I show such strength then? Not at all. Doing something for others is one thing—be it for a buffalo or a human—but to speak for myself, that I could not do. Shame, hesitation, guilt would choke my voice. There was nothing to be said about this. I am a prisoner, I am a prisoner, I am a prisoner. Who will give me strength?

In my mind I said—Don’t become a Hindu, see what good it has done me? Hinduism will not give you strength—it will only imprison you.

“O first tremor of love, in the garden of the mind,
The cuckoo calls again and again—”

What a strange song this is! Fear, yes, true fear! I am standing in his room—the song hums in my mind, I am restless in anticipation—he has come up very close behind me. One of his arms encircles my waist, the other hangs over my neck like an unfinished garland. His face is near my ear, above my cheek, he is whispering something in my ear, perhaps in his own language, I cannot understand the meaning.

“Mircea, I am afraid—I am very afraid.”

“Afraid of what, what are you afraid of?”

I am trembling—I know what is about to happen—just as it was in that book, Bengal Nights. He has seated me in a chair—my body is motionless—now it is afternoon, the whole house is sunk in laziness—I had come down to look for a letter in the mailbox. I am thinking in my mind that I should get up and leave, but his arm is around my neck, I have no strength to move his hand away—and now he is resting his face on my bare chest. My body is limp, I have no power to do anything, my breath is filled with the scent of his hair. He is saying something half-formed, perhaps “gods, gods”—I am saying, “I am sinning, Mircea, this is a sin.” Just a few moments, a sound, someone opens the door, he lets go of me and moves away. I sit up straight—fix my disheveled sari, not even three or four minutes have passed, but in that time the world seems to have changed for me.

That such a thing could happen in my life—I could not even imagine. I am saying with my lips that it is a sin, but I feel no sense of sin—no remorse at all—he lifts my hair away from my ear—

“I am telling you, Amrita, there is no sin—this is love, why should love be a sin?
Who has given us love? God—love itself is God.”

--- Page 48 ---
The storm has descended onto the courtyard, entering the kitchen. She hasn’t seen anything; at least now we are a hand’s breadth apart. I remain impassive and call out to her, “Jharu, make some tea—” Well, this act of mine, this false behavior, is it wrong? ‘If punishment must be borne, still do not utter a lie—’ But this is false conduct, isn’t it? Yet, what’s the use of telling Mircea this? It’s just as well that he doesn’t consider it a sin. If he did, if he thought it sinful and went away, what good would that do me? Then I would never have him again. I want him.

I look at him—his lips are pressed tight, his hand trembles slightly. He seems restless, impatient. Has he lost his patience because I spoke of sin? Is he angry with me? I feel an urge to go to him, to wrap my arms around his neck. But that’s impossible, isn’t it? Jharu has already come in. My other mind frowns now, saying, no, no, don’t stay here another moment—run, run. Leave him right now. I get up—I don’t even look at him again. With unsteady steps, I quickly go upstairs.

That night, I cannot sleep. For a long time, the touch of him lingers on my skin. It’s difficult to remain well, to keep such an experience suppressed. I am not used to hiding anything. Is that why I feel so restless inside? Or is there some other expectation? What could I possibly expect? My own eyes turn inward, gazing at myself. They look, irritated and inquisitive, toward some unknown cave’s darkness.

Tonight, I lie on a mat on the floor—rolling on the cold stone, I wonder—could I write this experience as a poem? Oh, what would happen then?

There’s someone I fear more than my parents—Sajanikanta Das—I have never seen him. But his ‘Shonibarer Chithi’! Oh, dear! Just because I read poems at the boys’ college, he’s written all sorts of things, hinting at me as the ‘bird of the bakul grove’—if he ever gets a hint of this incident, he’ll have me dealt with. Surely, many people have written about such things—can it be that only my mind is so filled with wonder? Then what need do I have to write? There’s a poem in ‘Kari o Komol’—it’s not a good one, I never read it, I keep it hidden. Why did the great poet ever write such a poem? I like to think about those two or three minutes—yet I am also afraid. I am becoming enchanted, I don’t know—the source of this feeling, is it in my body or my mind? Another poem by my beloved poet comes to mind: “words are but loads of chain in my flight of fire. I pant, I sink, I expire!” I keep thinking, ‘flight of fire, flight of fire’—the phrase whirls in my head—I am sinking, sinking, my mat has turned into a bed of feathers.

Returning from shopping for my uncle’s wedding, I am at his door; seeing me, he takes the things from my hands. My burden is eased. These are customs from their country. This custom is good. And look at uncle—he just loads everything onto my shoulders and scurries up the stairs. And Khoka? Even if I were carrying bricks, he wouldn’t come forward to help. When women enter the room, he stands up, won’t sit until they do, holds up the curtain and stands waiting—these are European manners—the women in our house like these very much—even the aunt who once scolded me for laughing with the ‘Sahib boy’—she told mother the other day, “Mejdi, this Sahib boy is really very good. How nicely he ate, finishing every grain of rice. Who would say he’s a foreigner! He’s just like a boy from our own home.” I feel like laughing—because he laughed with me, he was a ‘boy’, but because he ate with his hand, he became ‘one of us’.

My mother says, “What a boy! So good at his studies, and so well-mannered. When he calls me ‘Ma’, it sounds so sweet!”

48

--- Page 49 ---
Shanti said, “And when he wears dhuti and panjabi, he looks like Gauranga.”

For uncle’s wedding, he brought many beautiful things from his country for aunt—various toys, a dressing table set with poker work on wood and embroidery on georgette, handkerchiefs, so many things—how lovely they all are. My heart felt quite heavy; they are getting married, of course he will give her gifts. But he could have given me something too. Not even a handkerchief. He never gives me anything. No, that’s not true—he does give me books. He gave me a two-volume biography of Goethe. The day after the earthquake. The only evidence of Mircea’s presence here—of his time and existence—these two books, a tiny marker in this infinite ocean of time, are still with me, even now in 1972.

The night of the earthquake was truly beautiful. Deep in the night, the earth shook, and we all came downstairs. For a while we stood outside on the street, then sat on the stairs in the inner courtyard. Mircea made coffee. What an astonishingly beautiful, star-filled night that was. I have never seen him at such an hour, neither before nor since. That night lingered in my mind for a long time, like a faint fragrance. Night has its own form—and surely it gives a special vision to human eyes as well. The happiness I felt seeing him so late at night, he made it last by giving me that book the next day, writing in it—not I, but he wrote—as a token of friendship after the earthquake of 28th July 1930. He said that in his country, it is customary to give a friend a gift after an earthquake. Mircea told me many things about Goethe’s life—he even read to me from the book, about the poet who asked for light—“More light, more light.” I understood that he was not asking for literal light, not the sunlight his dying eyes longed for. It was a metaphor, as in the light of knowledge, the light of intellect.

Our own poet has written—“Where is the light, where is the light, kindle it in the fire of separation.” But what is the use of lighting a lamp in the fire of separation? Does light not burn in union? In all poetry, in the poetry of every land, only the glory of separation is sung! Why? If Mircea leaves, the pain I will feel—that is separation. How terrible. When he is downstairs and I am upstairs—even then I feel the pain. And if he leaves completely? And then to light a lamp for that? I cannot even imagine that deep darkness. It was from such pain that the Yaksha in Meghaduta, extinguishing the light of separation, dreamed of the heaven of union. Heaven? That is heaven, where tears do not fall except from joy, where there is no separation except for lovers’ quarrels, where there is no age without youth. Thinking of Meghaduta, my heart lightens—I climb the stairs, reciting—

ānandokhaṃ nayana-salilaṃ yatra nānyai-nimittaiḥ
nānyastāpaḥ kusuma-sharajādiṣṭa saṃyoga-sādhyāt
nāpyanyasmāt praṇaya-kalahād viprayoga-upapattiḥ
vitteśānāṃ na ca khalu bayo yauvanād anyadanti!

Father came out of the sitting room—“You’re reciting Meghaduta? Pronounce it properly with me—say yatra nānyai-nimittaiḥ—‘ya’ ‘ya’, not ‘ja’, why the tongue of a shudra?”

“How did you hear me?”

“Is it possible that a single drop of Sanskrit pronunciation falls within my hearing and I do not notice?”

There’s quite a problem with Sabi these days. She won’t leave us alone for even a minute. For some days now, she’s been saying she wants to teach Mircea Bengali. Mircea also says that’s good, that way he’ll learn Bengali. Because as much as I’m teaching him Bengali, he’s teaching me French. The chance that we’ll both become proficient in these two languages is slim. The biggest problem is that Mircea thinks Sabi is a completely innocent little girl, who understands nothing. In front of her, we can indulge in a little romantic conversation,

--- Page 50 ---
He can hold my hand—things like that. But that’s a mistake. I am about to turn seventeen, and she has just turned twelve. A twelve-year-old girl is not so little. Besides, she has already entered adolescence. But she still speaks with such childishness—when she was just six years old, one of our Brahmo relatives got divorced. That unprecedented event caused a stir throughout Bengal; whenever women gathered, there was no other topic—whether leaving one’s husband was good or bad, that debate. My mother and aunts would discuss these things—if someone told her to leave, she would refuse, saying, “Don’t say it, say it in front of me, I’m just a child, I won’t understand anything!” At that time, she truly didn’t understand, but now she does, I’m sure she does. She is very curious. Curiosity is good, it leads to questions, and without questions, how will people learn?

Besides, I notice something new in her. She feels pain that father makes such a fuss over me, that Rabindranath Tagore loves me so much, or that I love him and get to visit him, that such a great person is so close to me, but she cannot reach that place. This pain is sharp and intense, but it’s not her fault. If someone sees another being so favored right before their eyes, it can hurt. She doesn’t yet understand that her time will come, that she too will have her turn—she’s not old enough to grasp that. She pouts, cries, “Everyone loves Didi, no one loves me.” Everyone laughs at this sweet complaint of a little girl, but her sorrow does not go away.

When I went to Delhi, she once said to Ramananda Chattopadhyay, “If Didi isn’t here, you never come to this house.” Now she’s clinging to Mircea—she senses that I have a special bond with Mircea, a place where she is not included—I feel she has understood a great deal, and she fears that this man too will love Didi. I cannot discuss this with anyone. With whom would I need to? My special fear is that Mircea doesn’t understand any of this! Who knows what she might reveal in front of him. One day Sabi even said, “What are you two saying with your eyes?” When I told Mircea, he found literary delight in it—he laughed a lot—“Saying things with our eyes, a good expression. Let us try it again.” The more I observe Sabi, the more my heart trembles with an unknown anxiety—will she shatter this heaven of happiness, even if unintentionally, from the heat building up inside her? No, she’s just a child, what can she do? But every day I am plagued by this worry. I know Shanti knows everything! Khoka knows. They approve. They will never say anything. Perhaps even Kakima knows, but she won’t say anything. My only fear is that little girl. Sometimes it makes me laugh! She is nothing. But fear will always remain—‘Fear is ever awake. Close to the pillow of love, in the bosom of union’s joy, in the heartbeats of happiness, the fierce god of sorrow trembles every moment.’

That poem is terrifying—it makes my skin crawl.

Every day we go out for drives in the Chevrolet—sometimes along Jessore Road, sometimes along Barrackpore Road, sometimes by the canal on Tollygunge Circular Road. In the car are father, mother, myself, Mircea, Sabi, and the two younger brothers. Mircea sits in front with the boys. It is only during these outings that we get to see a bit of greenery. I long to wander alone with him among the trees, in the moonlight or in the darkness under the stars—so many times I have read about such things in books—will we ever have that, how could it ever happen?

Prafulla Ghosh was a professor of English, he taught Shakespeare well. Sometimes father would invite him over, and whenever he came, he would recite Shakespeare. Once, he was invited for dinner and to read Shakespeare in the evening. We sat waiting for him—he didn’t come, didn’t come—when we were growing impatient, sleepy, and annoyed, finally he arrived. Dinner was served. The whole...

--- Page 51 ---
He finished eating—then, as he washed his face, he said, “See, today I’ve acted just like a proper professor—I completely forgot about the invitation—only as I was washing up after the meal did I remember—‘Oh, today’s the invitation!’ It’s just like that story of the sister-in-law and the younger brother’s wife—the sister-in-law and the wife went to the river to wash dishes—the sister-in-law was taken away by a crocodile—the wife came home and forgot all about it, didn’t tell anyone. After eating, when she went back to the ghat, she remembered, and called out to Shandi, saying, ‘A thought came to me as I was scrubbing, they took elder sister-in-law away as she was dancing’—” The gentleman, with his imposing figure, recited the rhyme with great gusto—“It happened just like that with me.” 

That day he had told us the story of Merchant of Venice, the scene where Jessica and Lorenzo are walking in Portia’s garden—Lorenzo says—‘the moon shines bright in such a night as this, sweet wind did gently kiss the trees.’ The room was full of people, we were listening to Shakespeare, Mircea and I were looking at each other; even in a room full of people, one can feel utterly alone. But even better than that is to truly be alone. I would turn those lines over and over in my mind—Would we ever be able to walk alone together? Perhaps on some moonlit night or in the glow of fireflies at dusk, we might walk a little way off, away from everyone else, but no, Sabi would never let us go in such a night as this. Even while sitting side by side, there is longing. And sometimes, even from afar, there is union. Sometimes, just seeing him sitting in the seat in front of me would fill my heart with joy. The line of his neck visible above the collar of his panjabi—looking at it would send a shiver through my body—I would think, I need nothing more—let him stay far away, just seeing him is enough for me. Some days, I would pray silently that he wouldn’t get off the car before me. Then, as I got down, I could just brush his neck with my hand. But does that ever happen? He always gets down first, then helps everyone else out, holding their hands—that’s their custom. But I never told him any of this—I never told him how much I loved him. Because even now, I’m not sure what this is, whether it is really love. And besides, if I told him, he would be sure of it, his jealousy would disappear. So, deliberately, I would do things to make him jealous. Like that day, when writer ‘A’ Babu was sitting in his room, I started chatting with him—threw in a few English words, but mostly spoke in Bengali—why, aren’t we Bengalis? Just because he’s there, do we always have to speak English? I didn’t even glance at Mircea that day. His face was clouded, like ‘the first day of Asharh’—overcast with monsoon clouds. When he’s jealous, he becomes a little cruel—that’s what I like best. What is this feeling in my heart? Keketee! I’ll have to look it up in the dictionary.

For some days now, his possessiveness has grown—he has assumed that I am his property. He believes that Ma, Baba, and everyone in the house knows this and approves. So what else is left? It’s all settled. I had beriberi, so my feet are swollen—I massage them with herbal oil, but that doesn’t mean I can never walk barefoot; I like the feel of the cold stone floor under my feet—but he won’t allow it—‘Wear your slippers.’ Eating chilies has nothing to do with beriberi—beriberi comes from mustard oil—he won’t allow that either—‘Wear your slippers.’ Eating chilies has nothing to do with beriberi—beriberi comes from mustard oil—I don’t understand any of it—every day he says once, don’t eat chilies. 

That day, after lunch, Kakima, Mircea, and I were sitting at the dining table, chatting. He was angry with me because I had eaten chilies, hadn’t listened to him. He said to Kakima, “Kakima, you’re newly married, your sweetness is needed, you shouldn’t eat chilies—let all the fiery girls do as they please.”

I said, “Those boys who are too sweet, whom everyone calls good boys, actually have no manliness at all.”

“What do you mean? You mean I can’t eat chilies because they’re too hot? I have no courage?”

Saying this, he pulled the plate of raw chilies and lemons towards him and, one after another, began to chew the chilies.

--- Page 52 ---
He began eating with only his mouth. What a disaster. A complete bandit!

“Throw it away! Throw it away!” I said, getting up to leave, but he stretched his legs under the table and trapped my feet—such strength he had! I couldn’t struggle too much, or Kakima would realize I was captive.

He had already eaten two or three chilies in succession—his lips were swollen, his chin flushed red. Red patches here and there on his fair face—yet he remained unflinching. I reached out, trying to snatch the plate away. Kakima was expressionless, rolling with laughter! She didn’t understand a thing, didn’t see that a man was committing suicide right in front of her. Sabi understood, of course—her mind and mine were cast from the same mold:

“Ma, Ma, look, Didi is fighting with Euclid-da!” Ma rushed in from the kitchen—he immediately let go of my feet and stood up. Ma was stunned, astonished. That quiet boy did such a thing?

“What is this, Euclid? Why are you chewing raw chilies?”

Kakima said, “Because he didn’t listen to Ru!”

“Didn’t listen to Ru! What’s that supposed to mean! What’s that supposed to mean!”

For the first time, I saw a shadow of suspicion in Ma’s eyes. She offered him a spoonful of butter, handing him the bowl and saying, “Eat some butter.” Mircea took the bowl, lowered his head, and went off to his room!

Now Ma was truly angry—“Everyone’s done eating, so why are you all still sitting here chatting? Don’t any of you have anything to do?”

I was terribly frightened, but immediately, as if nothing had happened, I said, “No, Ma, I was just saying it takes courage to eat chilies. That’s why he was showing off his bravery, right, Kakima? What a fool! Who eats chilies just like that!”

My voice was calm—as if nothing at all had happened. Ma believed me, the cloud of suspicion drifted away, because Ma wanted it to. She never wanted to harbor doubts about those she loved. She didn’t have the courage to face unpleasant truths. Not only then—she never did. When someone else was taking her husband away from her, Ma kept thinking it was nothing—ten years that game had been unfolding. Ma hadn’t really noticed. When her ruin was complete, only then did she suddenly wake up. Did she think that because I believed her, I had to be deceived? Is it wrong to trust? That sixteen-year-old girl deceived Ma that day, too. Out of fear, my heart kept pounding for a long time. I can be quite cunning, can’t I! Am I becoming such a liar because of Mircea? Not at all, not at all—didn’t Dushyanta say—

“Strinam asikshita patutvam—”

Ma was going upstairs—the staircase was visible from the dining table—I thought, if Ma still has even a shred of suspicion, I must dispel it. I’ll go up and lie beside her, wrapping my arms around her neck.

“Kakima, I’m going up to sleep with Ma, will you check on Mircea for a bit—see what’s happened to him?”

Kakima winked and said, “I couldn’t care less.”

Lying next to Ma, the things I thought—she couldn’t hear them. No matter how close two people are, no matter how much love there is, how easily one can deceive the other. Because if you don’t say it aloud, no one can know another’s heart. If they could? Then love, respect, trust—all those precious things would be torn to shreds. Why did Mircea suddenly do such a strange thing? I couldn’t figure it out at all—was it just because I didn’t listen to him?

--- Page 53 ---
Why did it hurt him so much? Was it to prove something, to show bravery, or was there another reason? Was it for revenge? Whatever the reason, I could never have done such a thing. Can one deliberately inflict pain on oneself? Some people can. Like my grandmother—she could restrain her thirst even in the face of death; she could fast for two or three days. Could I do that? But she always had a reason—some vow, some ritual observance. She never did it without purpose! Perhaps he too had a reason; perhaps he was testing how much he could endure for my sake. Witnessing his capacity for self-torment, I felt very small. He is much stronger than I am. I will never behave that way with him again. This time, I will tell him that I want him too. Not in the way he says it, but sitting very close to him, placing my hand on his white, seagull-like feet—not at his feet, but what harm is there in touching his feet? After all, he is older than I am. In age, in knowledge, in strength of mind—he surpasses me in every way. I felt defeated by him—yet I felt no pain in that. I am happy. By showing his strength in self-denial, he has become so much greater—dehi padapallavamudaram!

Father’s blood pressure is very high; there is a commotion about it because blood has accumulated in his eyes—a serious illness. But even if it weren’t serious, any illness of Father’s feels grave to us. Not just in our family, but in every household—the master of the house, the man, is worth fifteen annas, while everyone else together is worth just one anna. That is, everything depends on his comfort, his wishes, his whims; the happiness or desires of others have no value. This attitude is especially pronounced in our home, because here he is not just the master—he is the presiding deity. So, when he is ill, it means everyone in the house is ill; no one else has any other concern—least of all Mother. Mother can stay awake night after night with a smile on her face—this unique service Father accepts as his due. This is the mindset of all household masters. For the wife, the value of this service lies in the joy of giving, in satisfaction, perhaps even in virtue; from the husband, no gratitude is required. No one even thinks of it. Even when there is no illness, the best food in the house is for the master. When he sleeps, the entire house must be silent, but when others sleep, he may shout if he wishes. No one considers this unjust.

I have seen household masters who, when traveling by train, would take first class, sending their aged mothers and children in third class. Even very good men did this; no one criticized them or felt any remorse. The master of the house—the one whose earnings support everyone else—has the unchallenged right to trample on everyone’s happiness, sorrows, wishes, and run the household as he pleases. Perhaps this helps maintain discipline in a large family, but it also gives the master the chance to become utterly selfish and arrogant. He comes to believe that he alone is a person in the house. Yet he is not a god; he is, like the most destitute, insignificant relative in the house, a fallible, joy-and-sorrow-stricken ordinary human being. Just as a king is the arbiter of a nation’s fate, so is the master of the house. And if the master possesses many special qualities, then there is nothing more to say. Father’s qualities are perhaps immeasurable—especially his learning. There is no subject about which he does not know something—his insatiable curiosity is ever reaching in many directions. He never needed help to understand the most difficult Sanskrit philosophical texts. His memory is sharp. Some even believed he was a jatismara, one who remembers past lives. Bijoykrishna Goswami also believed this—when Father was seven years old, he asked him if he remembered how, in their previous birth, they had performed austerities together on the bank of a river. Father, of course, did not remember. Then Bijoykrishna said, “You fell from your path last time, and you will again this time—you will have to return.” Father was not afraid of this; if he must return to this delightful world, why should he be dismayed?

Page 53

--- Page 54 ---
His abilities were astonishing as well. He had read every single book in his library of seven or eight thousand volumes. Moreover, his personality was extraordinary, with a magnetic force that drew people in. Even the greatest scholars could not match him; through his relentless questioning, he would prove that they lacked true understanding.

Not only in this country, but abroad as well, many respected and loved my father. He knew how to command service. I had heard stories about his professor, McTaggart—a man of immense scholarship, who would actually serve his foreign, enthusiastic student—he would even leave his own chair and pipe for him at home! My father would recount this story with considerable pride. There was a sweetness in the relationship between students and professors in those days, and that sweetness was present in their bond. My father loved Professor McTaggart as much as was possible for him. The philosopher-scholar McTaggart had one weakness: he drank heavily. My father would often tell a story about him. After dining at someone’s house, McTaggart would have to cross a field to reach the road—an immense field with nothing in it, except for a single tree. The professor would walk straight up to that tree and then, unable to figure out how to get past it, he would turn back and return home. Then he would set out again, only to find himself once more before that same insurmountable tree. After repeating this many times, the exhausted professor finally sat down under the tree—“I am lost in a dense forest.”

That story was always delightful. Yet, I was always troubled by a question: how could someone my father revered so much, such a venerable scholar, be a drunkard? Wasn’t drinking bad, even sinful? We had never learned to think that a person could possess both virtues and flaws and still be worthy of respect. Overwhelmed by society’s many reproaches, we did not know how to see human weaknesses and faults in their proper perspective. Just as we could never imagine that my father might have any flaws. He was entirely faultless, godlike—that was the idea my mother had instilled in us. She did not understand how dangerous this could be. Not just my mother, this was the custom of the time. They believed that any attempt to judge parents or elders was harmful. “Father is heaven; father is religion; father is the highest penance”—even in those days, the influence of this mantra was waning in some households, but in ours, because of my father’s extraordinary talent, it remained unshaken. Still, at that time, a faint spirit of criticism was beginning to stir in me, and though I was ashamed of it, I could not completely suppress it. The first time I dared to answer my father back was during his illness—

Since my grandmother’s time, there had been a woman living in our house. She was a little older than my father—we knew her as a relative. In truth, she had come to work as a maid, but she had become like one of the family. All of us children loved her dearly. She suffered terribly from asthma and eczema. I noticed that my father never bothered to call a doctor or bring her medicine—my mother did what she could, but after all, the money was my father’s. One day, I saw her crying out in pain—I went straight to my father and said, “There is always medicine and doctors for you, but why not for Champa-pishi?”

My father was stunned—“There are doctors for me, but not for Champa?!” His astonished reply made it seem as if he could not believe his own ears, as if he and that servant woman were suddenly equals in my eyes!

I quietly walked away. I felt ashamed of myself. How could I have said such a thing? But I knew that, little by little, rebellion was building up inside me. We had no power to go against my father’s wishes, not even a little. No one in this house could do anything against his will. So—what value did my wishes have? Nothing, nothing at all.

54

--- Page 55 ---
Just as a king, if he wishes, can have someone trampled under an elephant’s feet or beheaded, he cannot kill with his own hands, but he holds complete dominion over minds—over hearts. If he were to find out about us, he would do exactly what Akbar did— not to the body, but to the mind, the mind, the mind. He would roll a stone over this Anarkali of love. No one would be able to stop him! And Rabindranath? If he were my father, would he have done the same? The one who wrote, “In the sky of the heart, the dolonchampa of love blooms”—could he ever crush that champa bud? Never. Should I tell him? What would be the use? He has no claim over me. He is not mine. No one is! No one at all! How strange.

One day he asked me, “Have you seen the sculptures of Konarak?”
“I’ve never been to Konarak. Why?”
“I used to think people couldn’t look like that.”
I thought to myself, he could go to Konarak because it’s a ruined temple—“The greedy for virtue do not crowd your empty courtyard, O you, long, worn temple of gods”—but they would not let him enter the temple of Puri or Bhubaneswar. He is a foreigner, a mlecha. They didn’t even let Rabindranath enter the temple at Puri. What misfortune for this country. That’s why he wrote such a poem, which sounds like a curse.

I am standing on the lower veranda—leaning against the railing, my back against the wall, behind me the clusters of madhabilata flowers are swaying, sometimes brushing my head, my cheek. He is standing a little distance in front of me, looking at me, and suddenly, softly, he says, “You look like a sculpture on the temple wall.”

For the first time, he said something about my appearance that sounded like praise. But is it really praise? Who knows if looking like a sculpture is good or bad. I want to hear many sweet, tender descriptions of beauty, but he never says the things others do. Perhaps in his country, they don’t know how to describe beauty as we do—

“Her nose like the til flower, her speech like nectar,
Her forehead like the full moon,
Her thighs like the trunk of an elephant, her waist like the middle of a drum,
Her brows arched like Kama’s bow,
Her hair dark as a raincloud.”

But no, just—like a sculpture! Oh, it’s nothing at all.

For some days now, my book has been getting printed, a book of poems. Father, with much hope, has named it ‘Bhasita’. Before the book went to press, Milu copied most of it. Milu’s ideas about spelling are quite novel. “What’s this? Why did you put an ‘ee’ sign on the ‘da’ in ‘jodi’?”
“So what? It looks good next to the river, doesn’t it?”

There’s no question of carefulness. My spelling isn’t flawless either. When faced with a particularly tricky word, I become hesitant. Ramananda Chattopadhyay, the famous editor, used to say that except for Rabindranath Tagore, he had never seen any poet whose spelling was completely perfect.

Learning to proofread has made me feel quite grown up. As I gravely correct proofs, I feel rather elevated. But I don’t look up spellings in the dictionary. I check them in ‘Chayanika’, ‘Balaka’, or ‘Mahua’—that is, I find out in which poem, on which page, a word appears; this is easy and delightful for me. If, at that time, I had been asked to make a concordance of Rabindranath’s poems, I think I could have done it.

Father has decided that on my birthday, ‘Bhasita’ will be unveiled, and at that celebration he will invite all the poets, writers, and artists of Bengal, and make it a grand occasion.

--- Page 56 ---
It was as if, just as in England when a girl comes of age she is introduced at court, in the same way, a new poet was to be introduced to the literary society. This whole arrangement was being made around me; both my father and mother were absorbed with their eldest daughter, and as a result, the others were left somewhat in the shadows. Of course, my two brothers were still young, but what about Sabi? She was not so little. She was feeling neglected and was pouting. Her health had also suffered, both in body and in mind. This malaise of the mind grew even worse on the day we left her behind and went to see Uday Shankar’s dance performance.

Some time ago, Anna Pavlova had come to Calcutta. My mother and father had gone to see her dance. The ‘elite’ of Calcutta had been abuzz with talk of Pavlova’s performance! It was the first time in her life that my mother had seen such a dance. I had not been taken along, as I was not yet old enough. I had heard my mother discuss it with others. She was by no means an expert on the subject. But her sense of art and beauty was much keener than my father’s. Thus, Pavlova’s “Dance of the Dying Swan” had left her deeply moved. The enchantment lingered in her mind for quite some time. Yet, despite this, she was not without her reservations. Pavlova, after all, had not worn a full-length gown while performing the swan dance. To my mother’s eyes, it was as if she wore almost nothing at all. Her thighs were bare, and her skirt swirled, leaving much of her lower body uncovered. According to my mother, even if this could be accepted for the sake of art, it was certainly harmful for society. As we were leaving the New Empire, she saw her nephew in the distance, who quickly slipped away upon seeing her. Her nephew was not so young—he was studying for his M.A.—but seeing him there made my mother very anxious. Debu too had seen the performance, and he was no less worried that his aunt had caught him at such a forbidden place.

In any case, in 1930, Uday Shankar came to Calcutta to perform at the New Empire. A disciple of Pavlova, he was to present Indian dance. The people of this country were not yet accustomed to watching dance performances. Occasionally, a few individuals from certain circles might have seen a ballet or two. Native dances could only be seen in temples. We had seen the devadasis dance at the temples in Puri and Bhubaneswar. Those dances were unrefined. As for the baijis, who knew where they performed? We, as members of respectable society, had no idea. Our impression was that dissolute zamindars brought them from some mysterious world to dance at their garden houses. No cultured person was eager to watch such dances. Then there were the tribal dances—the Santals, Mundas, and Gonds danced at various festivals. I had seen Santal girls with hibiscus flowers in their hair dancing. Among folk dances, Manipuri, Garba, and others were performed during the festivals of their respective regions. In the larger, educated, middle-class Hindu society, I had never heard of any kind of dance, except for the Bratachari. I had also heard that the thandis danced in the bridal chamber to tease the groom. The idea of a gentleman’s son or daughter dancing on stage was utterly unthinkable.

In 1926 or 1928, Rabindranath had gone to Shillong. There, after seeing Manipuri folk dance, he was the first to introduce the dance of boys and girls from respectable families onto the stage before an audience. Spectators bought tickets to watch these dances. The thought of a Bengali girl performing before the eyes of a thousand people kept many awake at night. It was a kind of rebellion—perhaps even more difficult than a political revolution. That is why Rabindranath had to proceed with great caution. The first play in which this dance was incorporated was “Natir Puja”—the poem “Shrimati” was adapted into this play. “Natir Puja”—that is, the dance as worship by the dancer—was used as a subtle means to redeem the dancer’s honor. The song with which the dance was performed—“Amay kshama he kshama”—the dancer, in the name of Shrimati, addresses Lord Buddha: “Your adoration now dwells in my gestures and my music,” or “All my consciousness, all my pain, has become a kind of worship…” Seeing this image of worship, the critics were somewhat stunned.

I did not see “Natir Puja.” I was not old enough to watch dance performances then. For the same reason—

--- Page 57 ---
Uday Shankar’s dance—Sabita wasn’t allowed to see it either. She was upset because Didi went, and she too was yearning to grow up, entering her own youth.

Father called from the college to say there wasn’t a single seat left, only a box was available. The box was much more expensive, so Mircea would contribute as well, and the four of us would go. That day, Ma wore an Egyptian shawl adorned with sequins on a silver plate—she looked like a queen. Mircea wore a dhoti and panjabi. The entire erudite society of Calcutta was present that evening. Among this intellectual society, a large group belonging to the Brahmo Samaj suffered from a certain arrogance. Not all, of course, but many. They never considered us quite civilized enough—how many generations of ours have mingled with Calcutta’s high society, after all? Besides, we are Hindus. Even if they know us well, they often fail to recognize us. That day, several distinguished elites from this group not only shed their usual air of disdain, but came in groups to stand below our box, calling out to us and chatting animatedly. Father’s bearing was no less dignified—an exquisitely beautiful wife, a sufficiently pretty (whatever Mircea might say) poet-daughter, a foreign student, and the box itself—what a spectacle it all made! The pride of the Brahmos and Brahmikas was utterly shattered.

Uday Shankar performed a strange dance that night—I remember how his shadow seemed to transport me to another world—his quivering arms like waves of water—his entire body fluid! Though Simkie, as Parvati, was enchanting, Uday Shankar seemed to have descended from the heavens—I had never seen such rhythmic beauty in a human body, and I doubt anyone else present had either. Mircea was like Sudas Mali—speechless, lost; if he had had a lotus in his hand, he would have placed it at Uday Shankar’s feet that very moment. All night he played the piano, unable to sleep—and didn’t let me sleep either, as my room was right above his. Since then, I have seen many dances in many countries at many times, but never again have I felt what I felt that night. Both our hearts were open—so we experienced the creative agony with our whole beings. For several days, we had nothing more to say. Mircea kept repeating, “This is India! This is India!”

If someone were to ask me how long he stayed at our house—how many days, months, or years—I could not say. In my sixty years of life, perhaps I have truly lived only six or seven years; the rest are mere repetition. If the days with Mircea add up to a year, then it is not just three hundred and sixty-five days—there is no instrument by which I can measure them. They do not revolve with the earth; they are fixed, like the sun. I did not enter those days in a single moment, but I do now—whenever I wish, I enter that time, that joy, that pain. Whom do I find there? Is it only him and me? No, everyone is there—Shanti is there, Khoka is there, even Gopal, who looks at him with envy because he loves me. They are all present. There are those library books, even the cataloguing box. That era, with all its beauty, is blended into the consciousness of my being, indestructible.

My mother has always loved reading Vaishnava literature, and she is quite learned in it. She even learned Oriya to read Vaishnava texts. When uncle sat for his M.A. exams, Ma read all the Bengali M.A. books with him. Charyapada, Mangal Kavya—these are at her command. But most of all, she discusses Chandidas and Vidyapati. One day I asked her, “What does this line mean—‘Janam abadhi ham rup neharinu, nayana tirpit bhel, lakh lakh jug hiye hiya rakhinu, tabu hiya jurana gel’—what is the meaning of this poem? It can’t really be lakhs and lakhs of ages, because no one lives that long. So isn’t this an exaggeration? Isn’t exaggeration a fault in poetry? Then why do you praise it so much?”

57

--- Page 58 ---
Mother was smiling—“No, this is not an exaggeration—”
“Not an exaggeration!”
“How can I explain it to you—whatever refuses to end, the joy or sorrow that never exhausts itself—whatever cannot be measured by time, this is its description.” I could not quite understand. I thought about it for a long time—could not recall exactly where in Rabindranath’s songs or poems this idea appears. ‘Lakh lakh jug’—that is, eternity. Is a happiness that is never satisfied good or bad, who knows? Thinking about this eternal dissatisfaction, tears began to fall from my eyes—

Sabi’s illness has worsened. Kabiraj Kaka is treating her. She says all sorts of strange things. Among these, she muttered something while looking at a picture of Rabindranath—then she sings devotional songs. And she will not let Mircea leave her side for a moment. He must sit by her bedside. He must hold her hand. To cool her head, oil is applied to her scalp, parting her hair down the middle—the oil too must be applied by Mircea. It is actually a good thing. Because of this, Mircea often stays upstairs in my room, for Sabi’s sickbed is in that room. Many people come and go in the room, and Mircea sits by Sabi. If I were the one who was ill, it would not have been possible. But since Sabi is a child, it is easier. Yet, my heart is filled to the brim—he has become so much one of us, like a member of the family—this brings me a strange happiness. I remember one day, Kabiraj Kaka was standing by the door, explaining the details of the medicine to Mother—Mircea was listening to Sabi’s words—I was standing quite a distance away, near my bed. Suddenly, he glanced at me and smiled a little—and at that very moment, at that exact moment, a strange wave of sensation swept through my whole body. My spine tingled, and I sat down on the bed. Since I am the ‘eldest aunt’, it is my habit to analyze everything. This strange feeling filled me with questions—what is this? How does it even happen? It is a matter of the body, entirely of the body, there is no doubt—there is nothing of the soul in this—yet the body itself has not even been touched. Can such a thing happen just from a glance? Whom could I ask! It would not be right to ask him, it would only make things worse, besides, how would he know? He is not a doctor. Only doctors or kabirajs know about the body, but of course, one cannot ask them. Suppose I were to ask Kabiraj Kaka, “Kaka, why do I sometimes feel this way when I see Mircea”—what would happen then? Ha ha ha—then I would be laid down on another bed and have Madhumanarayan oil applied to my head. Or else, straight to the lunatic asylum in Berhampore.

We often sit on the roof or the veranda and read or recite little plays. We read the meanings, I read, the others listen. Little plays like ‘Gandhir Abedan’, ‘Karnakunti-Samvad’ or ‘Biday-Abhishap’. I like ‘Biday-Abhishap’ the most. I don’t know why. In these gatherings are Milu, Gopal, Khoka’s Kakima. Shanti is there too, though she doesn’t understand much of it. Mircea doesn’t understand either. Still, sometimes he is there. Everyone likes sitting on the roof, it is the only bit of nature we get. As we read, darkness falls, stars begin to appear one by one in the sky, then slowly move from one side to the other. The air turns cool. The jasmine bush in the neighbor’s yard gives off its fragrance. Even after it gets dark, I don’t need to stop, for I know it all by heart. One day Gopal said to me, “You keep reading ‘Biday-Abhishap’ over and over—what if it actually comes true for you?”

“What do you mean?” I frowned.

“Figure it out yourself.”

“You’ve become very insolent—saying whatever you please to me.”

“If you get angry, I can’t help it. I was just warning you, Ru.”

58

--- Page 59 ---
I have heard this comparison hinted at by a few relatives. It made me angry, but not afraid. Why should I be afraid? That is poetry—this is life. They belong to two different worlds. Mircea would never act like Kacha. And why didn’t Kacha take Devyani to heaven? He certainly could have, but then there would have been no story.

One day Mircea said to me, “I am curious to know what the lives of newly married women are like in your country.”

“I don’t understand—”

“For example, your uncle and his wife—I don’t see any excitement between them.”

“Why should they display their excitement to you?”

“In their country, it is seen—and besides, I keep looking at your aunt’s face.”

“What!” I was quite angry. “Why do you need to look at my aunt’s face? That’s very improper.”

“No, no, that’s not what I meant—they’re newly married, but I don’t see any marks on her face.”

I was astonished—getting married isn’t like having chickenpox, why would there be marks on her face?

He was laughing. Laughing with his lips pressed together—

“In our country, there are. Do you want to see what kind of marks?”

“Yes—”

He reached out with both hands and held me. I felt a warm pressure on my face—sharp and sweet—a moment later, he let me go. “Look in the mirror—”

I got up and looked at my face in his mirror hanging on the wall, and was startled.

Right in the middle of my lower lip was a small, round, dark spot—clear and bold. I stared at it—my eyes wide with fear—“What will happen, Mircea, what will happen?”

He sat there, unperturbed, flipping through the pages of a book.

“Mother will find out. Won’t she? Tell me, tell me, Mircea.”

“It’s quite possible!”

“Oh God, then what should I tell her? Teach me!”

“How would I know what you’ll tell your mother?”

“Why did you do this, why did you do this?”

Helpless, I was on the verge of tears. He, still leafing through his book, said without looking at me, “If you cry, I’ll make another mark.”

I was climbing the stairs, my hands and feet trembling. Fear, terrible fear. Perhaps even the fear of death is not like this. I don’t know what the fear of death feels like, but if, at that moment, God had appeared and asked, “Do you want to die right now, or go upstairs and face your mother?” I would have chosen the first a thousand times over. But that did not happen. I don’t know where God was; He did not hear the frightened girl’s call. Soon enough, I had to face my mother. She was startled to see me. Then, fixing her gaze on me, she asked, “Ru, how did you get that ugly mark on your lip?”

“What mark?” My voice was utterly calm and nonchalant.

“Look in the mirror—”

“Oh, I see, I must have bumped into the door.”

“Which door?”

59

--- Page 60 ---
"Which door! Oh, who knows... maybe... maybe—the library door."
"Maybe the library door! If you were hurt that much, you wouldn’t even know where you bumped into. Ru, tell me the truth.”
"No, no, it didn’t really hurt. Oh, I remember now, I bit down on it myself."
"That’s what I thought. A bump on the door never does that. Go put some cream on it and cover it up."
My simple, good-hearted, trusting mother breathed a sigh of relief, reassured by my answer.

My birthday was approaching. The house was abuzz with preparations for the celebration. The festival would be held in the manner of ancient India, following the old customs of hospitality. All the literary figures would come. The elderly Jaldhar Sen would release white doves—the doves would fly, the captive life set free. The arrangements for the celebration were underway.

Meanwhile, Sabi’s illness was getting worse. She spoke incoherently—though perhaps, to her restless mind, those words were not incoherent at all, but who would try to understand her? The elders did as they pleased, never considering that they should pay attention to the feelings of their children. They believed that things would turn out as they ought to, that everything would resolve itself quietly. Despite Sabi being so unwell, the preparations for the festival continued in full swing. She was certainly being neglected, which made her restless, but how could the poor girl express her impatience or protest? The root germ of this illness was a small, sharp, poisonous suspicion: “Everyone loves Didi, but no one loves me.”

Even among the relatives, there was a faint murmur that Narenbabu was making too much of his daughter. It would go to her head. My friends, too, were anxious that I seemed to be drifting away from them. Even Mircea, who had assumed that my parents were eager to marry me to him and would tell him so at the right time, suddenly said to me one day, “Even if I don’t marry you, I want to see you three times. Once after you become a mother, once when you are very old, and once at your deathbed.” I was quite annoyed to hear this. I knew it was poetic talk—I too often spoke in such poetic terms, words that had no real meaning. But what did it mean, really? Just as the Buddha witnessed old age and death, he too wanted to witness them in me. Not a bad joke.

On my birthday, all the renowned writers of Bengal came. Only Rabindranath did not come. I don’t remember why—perhaps he was not in town. But it’s not as if he had never visited our house. He had come several times, even to this very house, just a few days before, one afternoon, without any prior notice. I was sitting on the steps of the courtyard, reciting poetry aloud—unfortunately, not a poem by Rabindranath, but perhaps from Achintya Sengupta’s collection ‘Amabashya’ which had just been published. Just then, someone pushed open the half-door on the alley side and said, “Rabibabu has come.” At that time, everyone called him Rabibabu, though I have not referred to him by that name in this book—I didn’t understand what the man meant at first. The event was so unexpected. Then, gathering myself, I opened the door and saw him standing in the middle of the alley, in front of Mircea’s room—‘Niranjan Anand Murti’, tall and straight, black ribbons hanging from his spectacles—those glasses were called ‘pince-nez’, with no arms over the ears. His gathered dhoti touched the ground. It was as if a god had suddenly descended from heaven into the midst of our home. Seeing my state, he laughed and said, “Should I have sent word ahead like a bandit?” I thought, thank goodness we were all at home. Climbing the steps, he said, “I had gone to Ramananda Babu’s house, he said you lived nearby, so I thought I’d come see you. His man showed me the way.”

Ramananda Babu lived nearby, on Townsend Road. Every morning, he would go out for a walk, cane in hand, and slowly make his way to our house. At our breakfast table—

--- Page 61 ---
For a while, his presence became an everyday occurrence. Of course, he did not drink tea—he would have a glass of milk. As far as I remember, it was during such a time that he was greatly agitated over a particular incident. A famous scholar had included his student’s research—essentially, the student’s own writing—within his own book. The entire country was in an uproar over this. Ramananda Babu had taken the side of the weaker party, believing that the poor student had no one to speak for him—he would discuss this matter with my father.

That afternoon, at Ramananda Babu’s house on Townsend Road, the poet had come with the manuscript of *Shesher Kobita*. Not only were all of Rabindranath’s works always published in *Prabasi* magazine, but that magazine also supported him in every way. Ramananda Chattopadhyay seemed to spread his vast wings to protect him, just as a mother bird shields her fledgling. The metaphor may sound strange—how could Ramananda protect Rabindranath, was he greater than the poet? Not so. But he did possess the power to protect—he held in his hands India’s finest magazine, a publication respected not only for literature but also for fearless journalism, honesty, and much more, with the widest circulation. Nowadays, there are many magazines with a far greater circulation, but none would be mentioned with such reverence. In those days, the other two major magazines, *Basumati* and *Bharatbarsha*, as far as I recall, never published a single piece by Rabindranath. Their editorial temperament clashed with the spirit of Rabindra’s poetry! There was no shortage of magazines opposed to Rabindranath—perhaps they were even the majority. Ramananda fought tirelessly against the voices of criticism, the envy and jealousy that pervaded every level of society. With sharp, skillful arguments and brilliant, varied references, he supported all of Rabindranath’s work and launched barbed, reasoned satire at the opposition. If anyone asks whether this was necessary, I would say it was. Without Ramananda’s support, Rabindranath’s gentle heart might have been torn apart by the harsh winds of hostility.

I felt a special spiritual connection with Ramananda. He, too, loved the poet as I did. Therefore, surely, Ramananda had the right, at times, to feel a little jealous. So, when the magazine *Bichitra*, adorned in modern style and enriched with two major works by Rabindranath—*Rituranga* and *Tin Purush*—began to be published, it was no surprise that he felt a little hurt. That is why, on that day, Rabindranath himself brought the manuscript of *Shesher Kobita* to appease the editor of *Prabasi*. As a result, I was the one who benefited—what could be more delightful than such an unexpected appearance?

At my birthday celebration, the editor of *Bichitra*, Upendranath Gangopadhyay, also came. He was a frequent visitor, a man of sweet speech. Like Ramananda Babu, Upendra Gangopadhyay would also slip a girl’s writing into his pocket. In those days, the literary world was not so crowded. If the editor liked a piece, it would be published, and that preference depended on the quality of the writing, not on the writer’s pedigree.

If I were to write about everyone who attended the celebration that day, it would become a Mahabharata. The revered Kamini Roy was then a poet of national fame—and for another reason, we girls felt a special sympathy for her. We had heard she loved Jagadish Chandra Bose, and he loved her in return. Their marriage had been arranged, but it never happened. I heard that her book of poems, *Alochhaya*, was born of that separation. And the lifelong bachelor P. C. Ray remained a bachelor, having loved this very Kamini Roy. The beautiful Kamini Roy was worthy of the respect and affection of great men.

That day at our house, a young woman poet suddenly remarked to Kamini Roy that she had become outdated, that her writing was no longer acceptable in “these times.” Kamini Roy, in her heart...

--- Page 62 ---
She, radiant among the radiant, was not at all perturbed by this insult—she remained silent, her gentle face calm.

My mother was deeply hurt by such arrogance. She said, “Kamini Roy has become old, as if this one will never become old herself. And does something lose its value just because it becomes old? As Rabibabu says, ‘Just as a young man with a new moustache keeps looking at himself in the mirror, so do these people constantly twirl their invisible moustaches!’ I have never liked this idea of becoming ‘old’... What becomes old? The person? Or their thoughts? The part of the self that is immortal—that is where literature is born. So, what was once good—if it was truly good—does it become worthless on another day? And who is the judge? The judge, too, does not sit with some immutable standard in hand. That was the first time I saw Sarat Chandra—the first and last, for I never saw him again. Though by then I had eagerly devoured almost all his works. ‘Bipradash’ turned me away from him. ‘Bipradash’ was being serialized in Bichitra. Even at that young age, I wondered—why is the author of ‘Pallisamaj’ writing ‘Bipradash’? Bipradash, who upholds Hindu superstitions about food and touch, and the author seems to support him. Even though Bipradash speaks much like the Bipradas of ‘Jogajog’, his words are meaningless. Even if it is akin to Gora, the author of Gora, no matter how much he makes Gora speak in favor of orthodoxy, does not himself support it... By making Gora an Englishman, he uprooted all Hindu orthodoxy and bigotry. But Sarat Chandra’s Bipradash—by refusing food from certain hands, or by saying ‘it will not do if touched by so-and-so’—what truth is he attaining? Yet I did not have the courage to ask him this.

Poet Priyambada Devi had given me all her little volumes of poetry. I saw her from very close. Such affectionate, sweet-natured, learned women are rare today. Perhaps few know that there was a proposal for her to marry Rabindranath—after Rabindranath lost his wife. Maharshi Deb also wished it. Rabindranath did not. I have heard that the famous Japanese artist Okakura loved her and even painted her portrait. I heard these stories only in hints—sweet, tender tales of happiness—none of them wrote their own life stories, so we never learned much. Some people’s lives are like lamps that cast light on the paths of others. They teach us to recognize the world.

When I think of Priyambada Devi, many images of women come to mind at once—all of them erudite and multi-talented, each with her own distinct qualities—Sarala Devi, Hiranmoyee Devi, Priyambada Devi, Indira Devi—all their names are noteworthy. We were accustomed to seeing them together at gatherings—each draped in a Brahmika sari, a Japanese fan in hand—icons of elegance. I have heard Sarala Devi was a woman of stern temperament. But the affection I received from her makes me reluctant to believe it. In truth, I liked everyone connected to the Tagore family so much that my opinions about them are inevitably biased. I did not see Anurupa Devi from up close; she was not of this circle.

Kantichandra Ghosh had a moustache, but he did not twirl it. Though he was then particularly famous for his translation of Omar Khayyam’s Rubaiyat—he recited beautifully—“Oh! take the cash and let the credit go—nor heed the rumbling of the distant drum”—those words felt so true then—I could see no shadow of future disaster—both my hands were full of cash—filled with tangible wealth, the coins jingling merrily.

A comparison comes to my mind now: those who are considered ‘intellectuals’ today seem far more arrogant. Even the young boys—one writes for such-and-such a paper, another has won such-and-such a prize, and that’s enough for us, even at this age, to feel afraid to raise our heads before them! Not that there are no exceptions, but they are just that—exceptions. Nowadays, I truly suffer from an inferiority complex.

--- Page 63 ---
Because I have grown old. Yet that day, among these renowned literary figures—who surpassed me in knowledge, virtue, and age—I did not sense the slightest hint of condescension in their behavior. No one showed any scorn, even though my father was making such a fuss over this little girl. They were all pleased, content, and satisfied. They reached out to me as if inviting a fellow traveler into their carriage.

On the 1st of September, 1930, I suddenly became a contemporary of Kamini Roy, Priyambada Devi, Kantichandra Ghosh, Jaladhar Sen, and others.

We had already been quietly enchanted by Uday Shankar, and now Father brought him home as well. Wishing to speak with him alone for a moment, I served him tea separately on the upstairs veranda. The house was teeming with people. I tried to make conversation, but Mircea, managing the edge of his garment, appeared there too—he was even more devoted than I was, so let him speak if he wished. Did I ever forbid him? If he wanted, he could have taken Uday Shankar to his own room downstairs to talk privately; I had no objection. But instead, he wandered around once and then left, looking dejected.

To tell the truth, seeing Uday Shankar up close left me a little disappointed. For conversation was not his medium of art; the man who, on stage, had seemed at times a mountain peak, at times the crest of a wave, suddenly became just a man!

Earlier, we all used to go out together for long drives, but after Father’s eye illness, he could no longer join us. So it was just us—I, Sabi, my two younger brothers, Shanti, and Mircea—who would go for outings to the lake. At that time, the Dhakuria Lake had only just been dug, or was still being dug. The landscaping of the big lake was finished, and work was ongoing on the smaller lake across from it. There weren’t so many crowds there then. I doubt if Kolkata had even a quarter of its present population; perhaps only two or three cars would pass down Southern Avenue in an hour.

Whenever we went out, Mircea would always wear a dhoti, panjabi, and sandals—he seemed like one of the family. The younger members of the household more or less knew about us—no one said anything. In fact, Shanti would often give us opportunities by taking the children away on walks.

During the birthday festivities, Mircea had withdrawn a little, but soon we were as close as before. Gradually, it became so that we could not bear to be apart from each other, not even for a moment. Yet we had to be. Thus, sorrow and joy mingled constantly, leaving my heart restless. Mircea said he would go home for a few days, but what difference would that make? I was not yet of an age to worry about the future. I knew, more or less, that we would not marry. Yet I also knew I could not bear to be apart from him. Still, it never occurred to me to reconcile these two truths, nor did I try.

One day, a sudden storm broke and rain poured down. I came downstairs at noon, thinking I would stand in the courtyard and get drenched. Mircea called out, “Amrita, come here.”

We stood by the door at the front of his room; he was leaning against the threshold, and he reached out and pulled me close.

“Why do you pull your hand away from mine, Amrita?”

“I’m afraid.”

“I’m afraid.”

“Afraid, or jealous?”

“Jealous? Of whom?”

“Do you envy yourself, Amrita? Do you envy your own body? Do you think I love your body more than I love you? No, Amrita, it’s not that. I am searching for you, for your soul. You are within your body, but I seek that part of you that cannot be seen or touched. Beyond the body, it is your true being that I find when I touch you.”

--- Page 64 ---
He is looking straight into my eyes.

“I don’t understand, Mircea, I just don’t understand.”

The story of 1930 comes to an end. My formidable philosopher father and experienced mother had no inkling of the love play between these two young people. They had no suspicion, none at all; they learned of it from an eleven-year-old girl.

Sabi’s illness is unpredictable, fluctuating with her moods. When she wants attention, her rambling increases. That day, all of us—my brothers and sisters—were out by the lake, playing and running around. The two of us sat together by a bush on the north side of the new lake. Evening had fallen. There were hardly any people around. I can’t recall if Shanti was with us that day. We sat close together, wrapped in the silent darkness of the sky, our twin shadows stretched long on the still, wave-less water by the light of the lamps behind us. All around was quiet, and my mind was at peace. I was immersed in a strange tranquility. But he was restless, impatient, losing control. Suddenly, he touched my thigh—

“No, no, Mircea, no!”

“Why not? Won’t you be mine? Don’t you care for me?”

“No, Mircea, it can’t be. They will never agree.”

“What do you mean? They’ve already given you to me.”

Poor Mircea, he just couldn’t understand us. No matter how much he heard about our customs, religion, rituals, and ways, he couldn’t grasp them. He couldn’t understand me, either.

“Let go, Mircea, I’m afraid.”

“I’ll never let you go. Never, not in this life.”

Just then, suddenly, there was a commotion—someone called out. We rushed over to find Sabi rolling on the ground, shrieking nonsense. Mircea picked her up and laid her down on a bench by the big lake. I stroked her head and body, trying to calm her. Slowly, the moon was rising, its reflection falling on the lake’s water. Sabi was tossing and turning, “Euclid-da, sit by me. Give me a little affection.” Mircea came closer, “What hurts, what’s wrong?” He kissed her lightly on the forehead. Sabi said, “Now hug Didi, do it, do it,” becoming insistent. “Ah, Sabi, be quiet, what nonsense is this?” The more I tried to quiet her, the more stubborn she became, and Mircea was not about to let this opportunity slip by. “Alright, alright, I’m doing it,” he said, and embraced me a bit too eagerly. But at that very moment, Sabi started screaming again, “What did you do to Didi, what did you do?” No matter how much I tried to explain—nothing happened, see, Mircea is right here beside you—she wouldn’t stop. I kept talking to her, trying to calm her down, lifting her up—and inside my chest, a trembling had begun. The poison of fear spread through my whole body, overpowering the nectar of love, as if I had been bitten by a venomous snake. We got into the car. Mircea seemed unconcerned, unaware of what the future held. He couldn’t even imagine what was waiting for us.

We reached home. Sabi was quiet in the car. I thought perhaps she had forgotten—she was ill, after all. Mircea got out and went to his room downstairs... I went upstairs.

From here and there, I could hear my parents’ voices, and my heart trembled within me. Later, when I thought back to this time, I realized that fear was inextricably woven with the joy of that day, never leaving me for a moment. What a strange, intimidating society we lived in.

It wasn’t even an hour before Ma came into my room. “Come to the roof.” Her face was grave, her voice trembling. We went up to the roof, under a cold, star-filled night—I wasn’t as afraid now. I could manage.

--- Page 65 ---
I might be able to talk to Ma.

“What have I just heard from Sabitri, Ru? I can hardly believe it.”

There’s a large devotee’s cushion on the roof, Ma is sitting on it—her arms wrapped around her knees. Her hair is loose, falling over her shoulders, moonlight bathing her face. She looks like a goddess. And how old is Ma, really? Only sixteen years older than me. We are slowly becoming more like friends.

Ma said, “Tell me everything.”

I lay face down on the bed, resting my head on her feet—“Ma, Ma, Ma…”

“Tell me, Ru, tell me—do you want to marry him?”

Even at crucial moments, lighthearted thoughts often came to me—I was thinking, now I’ll have to say with my own mouth that I want to marry him, how embarrassing! It would be just like something from my childhood. Since I was little, I’d always heard how shameful it is to talk about one’s own marriage, that the bride can’t say a word, just hangs her head in embarrassment. I used to wonder, if just talking about marriage is so mortifying, how do people actually get married—why don’t they just die of shame? Then once, I spent a whole day with Thakuma at a wedding— the bride was so lively—she was seating everyone herself, serving them, opening the almirah to show her jewelry, not a trace of shyness. I asked Thakuma, “Thakuma, isn’t the bride feeling shy?” Thakuma was annoyed, “Shameless, brazen girl—” And today? If Thakuma were here today?

Ma said, “Tell me, Ru, tell me truly if you want this. If you do, then you will certainly marry him. I will never let my daughter be unfaithful, not even in her heart.” With my head still on her feet, I said, “Yes, Ma, I want it, I do. I can’t live without him.”

“Is that so?” Ma sighed deeply, “All right, then so be it.” I lay in her lap for a long time, under the sky, the cool touch of night wrapped around me like Ma’s affection. My mind was calm, at peace. What profound, silent tranquility all around. All my long-held fears washed away in a single moment. I should have told Ma long ago. I had completely misunderstood her. Not once did she say that loving him was a sin. And even if Sabitri told her everything, she didn’t say a word. After a long while, Ma said, “Go to your room now, Ru. Don’t come downstairs tonight. I’ll bring your dinner to your room.”

That night, I didn’t see him again. He didn’t play the piano that day, either. When he played the piano downstairs, I would feel, in my heart and body—in every layer of my consciousness—the deep joy of union. Some days, I would think—even if I don’t see him, just the news of his existence carried on the music is enough. But that night, I kept thinking—I’ve told him so many things about my fears, but today I couldn’t say, “No more fear, no more fear.” Tomorrow morning, perhaps Ma will tell him first. With a bindi on my forehead, I lay awake deep into the night, daydreaming about the festivities of our wedding night. I see him standing on a stool adorned with alpana—wearing a silk dhoti and shawl—his blue form anointed with sandalwood, like Banamali in yellow robes, though as beautiful as blue might be, I think white would suit him better. Will he have to wear the sacred thread? No, how could that be? And during the auspicious first glance? When the shawl is lifted over our heads, Srimati Malabika—Maladevi, who will die of jealousy, or perhaps is already dying, for she has already curled her lip at me once and said, “How is the Kacha and Devyani drama going?” She said, “This half-ripe girl has already had her first glance.” And Ranu, my school friend Ranu, she’ll look at me with wide eyes and say, “So you actually went through with a love marriage, you’re so brave!”

Everyone’s words are coming to mind—what will Milu say, what will Didima say, Aradhana? I feel like everyone will be happy for me. No one will blame me. And through it all, below, his sleeping—

Na Hanyate-5  
65

--- Page 66 ---
His face floats before my eyes—poor thing, he still knows nothing, his wish will be fulfilled. He wants me so much, yet we can never be together for more than four or five minutes at a time—now? “In you I will dwell, through long days, long nights, long months of the year.” But can I live away from my parents? Why should I even have to? He said he would stay right here. Wonderful! But whatever he says, surely I’ll have to go to his country too—won’t I see his mother? His sister? The thought of going to his country reminds me of Prinsep Ghat—once, when Rabindranath was leaving for England, we went to see him off, to say farewell, “see off” as they called it. I remember that day, what a gigantic ship! This time, the fear is gone, but shame—intense shame—my ears are ringing. I’ll have to tell him, but who will say it? I will. When? When he comes to Calcutta. He’s been abroad for almost a year now.

The night grows deeper, in the dim half-light I can see the stationary ship, swaying as it moves through the narrow waterway between the two shores—this is the Suez Canal—on the other side, the blue waters of the Mediterranean, as the ship sails on, it turns into a peacock’s plume.

The next day, seeing Ma’s face, my heart trembled. Her eyes were swollen, her voice grave. Had Ma not slept all night?

“Ru, don’t go downstairs today, stay in your room, don’t talk to anyone—not Shanti, not Chutki, no one. I’ll come in a little while.”

I stood there, stunned—what had happened now?

My hands and feet tingled, I couldn’t stand any longer, so I lay back down on my bed from the night before. After a while, Ma entered the room with a glass of milk.

“Drink this. I have something to talk to you about.”

I don’t like milk, not at all. But now, I accepted it without protest. Ma sat beside me on the bed and said in a very stern voice, “Your father has asked me to find out exactly how far things have gone between you two.”

I stayed silent. I thought—neither Sabi nor Shanti would ever make up lies! Even if Shanti could spin a story, Sabi never would. We siblings don’t lie. Ma always said, “Even if you must suffer punishment, never a lie.” We hadn’t gone anywhere far, we were right there. Ma spoke again, “Answer me, tell me, how far have you two gone?” I didn’t quite understand her question—I said, “Why don’t you ask them? We never went anywhere far from the lake.” Ma was reassured. She was pleased with my ignorance.

We sat in silence for a long time. Downstairs, the children’s chatter could be heard. In the waking house, the usual daily life had begun, but it seemed to have nothing to do with us—at this moment, Ma and I had drifted far away to another world, another time. After a deep sigh, Ma said, “Ru, tell me the truth, what has Mircea done to you?” I buried my face in the pillow. I will not answer these questions, not for anything, otherwise I’ll have to lie. How can I tell the truth? Then it will all be his fault. But is it only his fault? I am no less to blame, not in the least. He has no mother here, no father, no one—he is in a stranger’s house. Here, my father is everything to him, and if even he turns against him, where will he go?

Ma said, “Ru, get up, lift your face, look at me.” Her voice was hard, devoid of affection. I lifted my face but couldn’t look at her—how could I lie, looking into her eyes? My mouth was dry, my throat parched, I couldn’t speak. Why must one stoop so low to gain something so beautiful?

“Why aren’t you speaking, Ru? Why won’t you look at me? Why is your face so—

--- Page 67 ---
Why has it gotten so late? Where is my spirited, truth-speaking daughter who never compromises with falsehood? Why is she in such a state today? Shame! I had to witness your head bowed under the weight of guilt!” Ma’s voice was choked with emotion. I knew what Ma was saying was true, but no matter how unjust it was, I would still lie. I would never put him in danger.

“Tell me, Ru, did he kiss you?”

“Yes—”

“Where?”

Now I remembered, I had to give a safe answer—

“On the forehead—”

“Only that?”

“Yes—”

“You two didn’t do any kind of gandharva marriage, did you?”

“What is that?”

“Don’t you know what a gandharva marriage is? Exchanging garlands, exchanging rings, or anything like that?”

“No, Ma, we never even thought of such things.” Later, I realized Ma was thinking that if something like that had happened, it would be easier for her to help us.

“Look, Ru, I told you I would try, but I couldn’t. Your father simply wouldn’t agree.”

I was trembling—Ma placed her hand on me—“Calm down.”

“Why, Ma? Why?”

“Your father said we know nothing about them—we don’t know their family, what his father is like, what his grandfather is like, who knows? He might even have some terrible disease.”

I was astonished. What a strange thing to say—“Ma, he’s been here with us for almost a year, and nothing like that has ever happened. Why would he have a disease?”

“Not that kind of disease, there’s a lot you don’t need to know. You don’t know what the French are like, they’re utterly uncivilized.”

“But he’s not French—”

“It’s all the same—their culture is French culture.”

“Why would French culture be bad? All of Europe imitates the French.”

“And has that made them any better?”

“Europe hasn’t progressed?”

“That’s not what I mean—you don’t know what their lives are like. When you’re older and read Maupassant’s stories, you’ll understand. There’s no fidelity between husband and wife—one is always deceiving the other—marrying one and running off with another, in such a wretched society you could never survive.”

“I’ve read Maupassant’s ‘The Necklace’—there’s nothing bad in it.”

“Oh no, there are so many stories—and the things your father said, they make my blood run cold with fear. This can never end well, Ru.”

“Ma, Ma, oh Ma.”

“What can I do, Ru—he said if I insist on this, he’ll die. Do you want to kill him? Don’t you love him at all? Is that boy all you care about now?”

I was surprised at myself—even the mention of Father’s illness brought me no anxiety—rather, I felt angry—he always used the threat of illness to bring Ma around to his way. And can’t anyone else have blood pressure? Why don’t I have blood pressure? Oh God, right now—

--- Page 68 ---
Blood pressure might rise. Ma says, “Ru, if you make a scene about this, he could have a stroke. Is that what you want? Compose yourself—can one ever get everything one desires?”

I don’t know how the hours and minutes passed. At noon, Ma came in, “Ru, get up. Mircea is leaving. He said he wants to see you once before he goes.”

“I can’t get up. It feels as if all the bones in my body have been crushed—how can I stand?”

“Ru, get up, get up. He’s standing outside in the sun—you stand on the upstairs veranda. Your father has agreed, he’ll just see you once and then leave.”

In this year, 1972, when I stepped back into 1930, it was as if I was reliving that eighteenth of September all over again! Once more, my bones felt crushed, my chest twisted with pain—how strange, I never realized that for forty-three years, a part of my being has remained fixed in 1930—“Ajaḥ nityaḥ śāśvato’yaṃ purāṇo na hanyate hanyamāne śarīre”—the body is gone now, but that self remains, still there, immortal.
