I stood near the gap in the middle of the veranda, he stood below—a faint shadow from the madhabilata vine fell across his face—he lifted his pain-stricken face towards me—it seemed as if someone was piercing him with a burning rod. To this day, I have never seen such agony on anyone’s face.

He raised his hand in farewell—“Goodbye—”

“No, no, Mircea, no”—and in that very moment, I realized, I never told him. So many times I had thought, this time I’ll say it, but I never did. And now, I never will.

After that, nothing is clear in my memory—I think Ma laid me down. Later I heard I had collapsed. Not surprising. My bones had turned to jelly. When I regained consciousness, I found myself lying on the narrow veranda next to my room, the one that overlooked the little alley in front of Mircea’s room—Ma was sprinkling water on my head, and tears were streaming from her eyes. She was murmuring, “Durga, Srihari, Durga, Srihari—what do I do, what do I do now?”

Mircea was gone—where, who knows? Who would tell me? I saw no one—Ma wouldn’t let me out, wanting to keep my grief-stricken form hidden from everyone—people would laugh. I ate nothing but fruit, eating was impossible. Ma, who could have been a small-time doctor herself, didn’t force me. She said—grief, sorrow, anger, whatever the state, it all becomes poison in the body. Then, just some liquid is enough. Sometimes I wanted to call Sabi and ask if he had said anything before leaving. Then I thought, no, let it be, he’s just a child, what happened in front of him was already too much, it shouldn’t have happened. Besides, what would I say to him?

Later I heard he had said—“What have you done, Sabi, what have you done?” Poor Sabi had cried and said, “I didn’t understand, Euclid-da, I didn’t understand. You would suffer so much, didi would suffer so much.”

A few days later—I don’t know—a certain evening comes to mind—outside, the light was fading, inside the room was dark—a blue lamp in one corner, covered—most of the doors closed. I wasn’t imprisoned, just kept out of sight, hidden from the world. Suddenly, someone slipped in through the half-open door—I remember her words, but I can’t see her face. The border of her sari, her feet, slowly approached me—who was she? Kakima, or Shanti, or Champa Pishi, or someone else? She came and stood by my bed—“Listen, Ru, don’t try to run away. You should know, you’re still a minor—if you run, you’ll be brought back anyway—of course, nothing more will happen to you, but he will be sent to jail. Not a gentleman’s jail, but a thief’s—”

--- Page 69 ---
I have to stay with the doctor. They’ll make him break stones in half-pants.”

“Why are you all threatening me?”—I bury my face in the pillow, how helpless I am, how helpless—“Am I going anywhere? I’m just lying here alone in this room—what’s the need for all these words, this torment?”

“We’re saying it for your own good.”

A couple of days have passed. My greatest pain is that I couldn’t say anything to him. There was so much to say, and now, in this life, I’ll never be able to say it. If only I could have spoken to him alone for even an hour before he left. All the while he was here, I gave him nothing but pain, nothing else, nothing else—

Now, early in the morning, the door opens a little, Khoka enters the room, and immediately starts his usual clowning—“Ah, lying here and crying, shame, shame, where have you come from, merciless stranger...” He’s reciting lines from Debjani’s farewell curse—“Get up, Mircea has asked for a book of yours, he doesn’t have it.”

“Do you know where he’s gone?”

“Of course I know, to that Ripon Street.”

“To that Anglo-Indian house?” Oh, what a fate! He absolutely despises them, he doesn’t like staying there—the girls there aren’t good at all—

“Where else could he go? Driven out in a day, where could the poor foreigner go in this land?”

“Oh Khoka bhai—oh Khoka—what do I do now, brother...?”

“Shh, shh, shh,” he puts his finger to his lips, spins around dramatically—“Spies everywhere—I’m escaping—quick, get the book, I’ll wait on the veranda.”

I took out the book and went to the veranda—just then I thought I should warn him—if Father asks, he mustn’t admit anything—he doesn’t even know he’s done anything wrong, so he might blurt it out—I opened the back of the book to write—I was trembling, the lines went crooked, I made a lie eternal with the price of love, and I didn’t even realize it—Mircea Mircea Mircea I have told my mother that you have kissed me only on my forehead.

“Khoka bhai, I’ll give you a letter too. Take it to him, bring back a reply.”

“Hurry up—if uncle finds out, he’ll send me away this very moment.”

I took a small piece of paper and sat down to write. What should I write? There was so much to say, but nothing comes to mind. Let it be. There are just two things I must say, with all my strength. With absolute faith in my heart, this truth I will keep, I will keep... I wrote, I shall never forget you, “I shall never forget you... never forget you... never forget you, never forget you,” and I wrote, I shall wait for you—“I shall wait for you, wait for you.” Line after line, I filled the page with the same words and gave it to Khoka—

“Khoka, give him this letter, then whatever happens, happens.”

My hair has become tangled in these few days. I haven’t let Ma touch it. I’m always snapping at her, and she endures it all. After giving him the letter, I returned to my room and lay down again. Letting my long hair hang down, covering my eyes with my arm, I began to vow—“I won’t forget, I won’t forget, I won’t forget.” Father has no hold over my mind—so that vow, on silent feet, began to descend, unnoticed by me—stepping carefully, down, far down into the depths of my mind, where the outside world cannot reach, where neither day nor night arrives, where there is no morning or evening, where the sun does not rise, the moon and stars do not shine—there it descended and fell asleep in a deep, yogic slumber—who knew then that forty-two years later, that sleep would be broken?

69

--- Page 70 ---
A couple of days passed, and there was still no sign of Khoka. At last, one day I caught hold of him.  
“What happened? Did you give her my book and letter?”  
“Yes!”  
“And then?”  
“What do you mean, ‘and then’?”  
“I mean, did she say anything?”  
“No.”  
“No! Khoka, please, go once more today and tell her I want a reply!”  
“She’s not there. No one can find her. They say at her house that she isn’t there.”  
“Oh, my! Why didn’t you tell me all this time?”  
“What would you have done if I had? If she’s jumped into the river, is there anything you could do?”  
“Oh Khoka, please, do something, I beg you, bring me some news of her—”  
“All right, all right,” Khoka slipped away—

Morning comes, night falls, the wheel turns with its inexorable rhythm. That revolving cycle within us exhausts our joys and sorrows, our grief and happiness, transforming one feeling into another. Ma says, the burning pain of grief lasts only three days—at first it scorches like fire, then slowly it subsides. Even a mother forgets the loss of her child—a widow rises again. Every day there is loss, and every day there is compensation. Everyone knows this, whether they’ve heard it or read it in books—but that kind of knowing is not true knowledge. This suffering is teaching me how to truly know reality. I had thought I would cut off my hair—but I couldn’t—and now I no longer even wish to. My other mind says, what’s the point of cutting my hair, it will only look ugly. This, too, is the will to live. I know that.

Ma sits beside me, telling me stories—how uncle has started behaving so badly, how they’re going to move to another house. The daughter-in-law is no good. She goes to her father’s house and complains about her in-laws, about my father and mother. Even about me! I’m not really listening—my face is buried in the pillow. What’s the use of hearing all this? Let them do as they please. This household has become utterly alien to me.

Ma gently untangles the knots in my hair with her fingers, then braids it. Softly she says, “Ruu, even suffering has its value—all the wise and learned have said so. Call upon God, He will set your heart right. He will bring you peace. It’s only through sorrow that people truly surrender to Him, it doesn’t happen otherwise—‘He who falls pierced by the arrow, he alone clings to your feet.’”

Ma turns off the light and leaves the room—the lines of the song keep circling in my mind, but in my dazed, spellbound state, their meaning escapes me. “Whose wounds are hidden in comfort, whose disgrace is fragrant,” “whose disgrace is fragrant”—have I become disgraced? Surely. Baidyanathbabu from next door has said, “These are the deeds of big houses! Keeping some servant boy in the house, all sorts of goings-on.” Father is furious, we’ll leave this house. There’s no need to stay in this uncivilized neighborhood any longer. Everyone is gossiping—my dazed mind is tangled in a strange trance—“whose disgrace is fragrant”… “cling to your feet”… “grant me the touch of your lotus feet”… “grant me the honey of your lotus face”… cling to your feet—cling to your feet—the lines of the song have become the bow in Pinaki’s hand, striking straight into my head with a ringing clang, clang, cling to your feet… I’m tossing and turning on the bed, and suddenly I fall off.

That day, when I regained consciousness, I saw everyone in the room, even Baba. This was the first time I saw my father face-to-face since Mircea left. Baba was saying to Ma, “With the milk, add a little…

--- Page 71 ---
“Give him some brandy to drink.”
“Call Shyamadas the physician tomorrow—”
Uncle uttered a few harsh words and left the room. For the first time, I saw him being insolent in front of Father. I felt angry at my uncle. How dare he speak to Father like that—was it not Father who had made him what he is? I sat there, speechless—two lamps glowed in the room, yet the people seemed like shadowy figures in the darkness—even Father. Father’s shadow had moved to stand near my bookshelf. He was searching through the books. The first one he pulled out was a Japanese fairy tale book, bound in bright blue cloth, with a strange golden animal embossed on the cover. Slowly, Father opened the pages and tore out the dedication page. That book—he had given it to me. Then he did the same with the book ‘Hunger’. One by one, he took out the books and tore out the dedication pages. When he took out Goethe’s biography, he couldn’t find the inscription—it had been on the first page, stuck to the cover like a lining. That little bit of handwriting, that single mark, is all I have left of him for my whole life—nothing else, not even a photograph.

Father slowly tore the papers into tiny pieces and threw them out the window. In another house, perhaps the books themselves would have been destroyed. But that could never happen in this house—even though Chinggis Khan may live here, he cannot destroy books—he can burn people, but not books. Books are his gods.

Father’s friend was going to Madhupur with his family for the Puja holidays—we too needed a change of air—Father said that a new place would be good for both body and mind.

But my mind was only getting worse. Above all other reasons, one thought stabbed at my heart like the edge of a sharp knife, leaving it raw and aching—if only I could ask him once—why don’t you answer my letters? But how could I? Who would take me to Ripon Street? It’s not that I don’t know the streets of Calcutta, but going out alone is impossible and dangerous. I have never walked alone on the streets of Calcutta. I could send the driver, but I don’t like that man—his eyes are like those of a beast, always watching us. He frightens me.

Khoka never comes anymore.

The day we were to leave for Madhupur, he came. He would go to the station with us, help load the luggage.

I found a moment alone with him and asked, “Khoka, why don’t you come anymore? At least I could talk to you about him.”

Khoka was silent. “Why don’t you come, tell me?”

“I can’t bear to see you suffer so much, Ru. There’s a limit to what a person can endure.”

“But if I could talk to you, my pain would lessen. What is he doing now?”

“He’s not here anymore, he’s gone to the Himalayas.”

“The Himalayas! You mean Darjeeling?”

“No, no, the Himalayas—not Darjeeling, Rishikesh—he’s become a sannyasi.”

“He’s become a sannyasi? What does that mean? What is it to become a sannyasi? Is that why he isn’t replying to my letters?”

On the train, I wrapped myself in my shawl and lay down. I couldn’t make head or tail of this strange news. Instead of writing to me, or seeking advice, he had gone off to the Himalayas. I wept. Both Father and Mother knew I was crying. They spoke softly to each other, fed and put the younger siblings to bed—though, of course, Mother did all this alone—Father is like a child, I doubt he’s ever even poured himself a glass of water in his life. Crying and crying, my tears slowly dried up—my pain dulled—a miraculous

--- Page 72 ---
There is a sense of contentment that is lulling my mind to sleep—suddenly, in that drowsy world, I heard Baba telling Ma, “Close the windows on her side, in case she jumps out.” In those days, the windows had no railings. I thought to myself, I would never do such a thing—life is beautiful, even sorrow is beautiful—this life will burn bright like a flame—“Set it alight with the fire of separation”—now I understand the meaning of that song.

The house in Madhupur is lovely, open nature all around, my body feels soothed here. Perhaps he, too, is feeling better in the Himalayas. Nature serves us silently—like a mother. And Ma is with me. He has no one, he is utterly alone. One day, sitting by the pond’s ghat, Ma quietly said to me, “Steady your mind, dear—you will never see him again.”

“Why, Ma, why?”

“Your cruel father made him swear a terrible oath that he would never have any relationship with you.”

I had studied in a missionary school—I’d heard of the vows taken by nuns, so I laughed—“Why would he keep such a promise? Is he a nun?”

But the thought that I would never see him again kept circling inside me, coiling into a sigh—a sigh called longing.

While we were in Madhupur, there was much talk about the Chittagong Armoury Raid; perhaps it happened around that time. Besides, the family whose house we were staying in, and we ourselves, were closely acquainted with the place and the people involved in that incident. The battle at Pahartali happened in a place we knew—the boys were our age. Their reckless courage made us proud. When Baba started saying that such madness was meaningless, we argued fiercely. Who knows how much closer this would bring us to independence, but at least they moved forward, wasn’t it something to have done such a great deed? I thought, if only I could be part of something so grand, my sorrow would surely vanish. But I had no way to find my own path. They wanted to free the country, but who would free the people? Who would free me, my mother? Sitting by the pond, I would think, if I ever got the chance, I would fight against all the narrow rules of this society. I may be young, but it is my mother’s bondage that pains me most. She has no rights over her own daughter. I know how much it hurt her. Ma honors this love, but she has no power.

When I think of the year 1930, I can mark it only because I know it now; otherwise, my feelings have no dates. Days, years, months—all are meaningless, just as there are no directions in the sky, and we understand direction only through the limited earth, so too the feelings that merge into eternity have no dates, no mornings or evenings. With their vastness, they transcend the fleeting present; then, what is distant is also near. Movement and stillness become the same—“That moves, that moves not, that is far, that is near.”

So I cannot say exactly when it was, before or after we went to Madhupur—for sometimes, when turning the pages of a book, the event I expected to see on the right, I find on the left. Such things happen.

I am lying in my room. Ma sits beside me, telling stories, and the subject is Baba’s illness. She wants to make my mind sympathetic to him by describing how serious his illness is. My respect for Baba has not diminished at all, nor my love. Yet, Ma is so anxious about Baba’s illness, but when Ma is ill, neither Baba nor Ma herself pays any heed—how strange is that? So I...

--- Page 73 ---
I do not agree with Ma. Sensing my unwillingness, Ma feels helpless, just then I hear the sound of Baba’s footsteps; he comes and stands by the door. Baba’s eyes are very large, aquiline, his complexion a bright copper, which turns even redder when he is angry. Now Baba’s face is red!

“Will you just lie there crying like this? Won’t you take your exams? Even Chutki is taking hers. Incredible! What is there to mourn so much?” Baba is scolding me loudly, almost shouting.

Ma quickly gets up. She moves Baba away from the door and leads him toward the closed door! But that door isn’t fully shut, there’s a little gap—I can hear everything. Because I am straining to listen.

Ma says, “Can everything be forced? Even cruelty has its limits.”

“But that doesn’t mean she’ll ruin her life before our eyes like this. Won’t she take her exams?”

“If the exams were so important, then why did you take her out of school? She could have taken them this year.”

“Oh please! Does anyone study in school? If she’d gone to school, would she have read so much literature? She knows all of Rabindranath’s poetry by heart. Even M.A. students haven’t read so much—could she have written so much poetry? Those teachers just give homework, homework! With red ink they write ‘good’ and ‘bad’—all nonsense!”

“Well then, she has studied. She can take the exams later.”

“No, no, that won’t do, she must take the exams—what, my daughter won’t pass? What a disaster, nothing will come of this, I was preparing her so carefully. Everything is ruined, she won’t write poetry anymore, won’t study, all my hopes and dreams are shattered, then just go ahead and arrange her marriage,” Baba cried out in despair.

Ma comforts him as if he were a small child, “Give me a little time, dear. It will take a little time. I’ll make everything just as it was for you.”

“You’re not handling this properly. You’re turning her mind away! Tell your daughter, they are Europeans, they are hunters.”

“That I simply cannot do. Poor boy, he’s a stranger, wandering like a monk in the wilderness. Even if I can do nothing for him, I cannot slander him.”

“What harm will it do if you tell her that? He won’t even know. Am I asking you to treat him badly? To win a war, you must use your wits, sentiment alone won’t do—Nothing is wrong in love and war—”

“That’s not my view. What is wrong is wrong, my conscience will not be overridden by cleverness.”

Ma is arguing fiercely with Baba. She always does. Ma has her own opinions but cannot enforce them. She is defeated by her own affection.

I have heard Ma and Baba’s argument, but strangely, my tormented heart has accepted Baba’s words. ‘Hunting’—that’s what it is! Otherwise, why hasn’t he replied to my letter? Why didn’t he tell me what I should do? He is a man. Older than me. If he doesn’t say, who will show me the way? That means he doesn’t want me anymore. The game is over. Hunting!

One day, Baba was teaching us Shakuntala, we were sitting on mats on the floor—Baba in the middle. I can see him. Whoever hasn’t heard Baba recite Sanskrit cannot know its extraordinary beauty. The Sanskrit language, with all its sweetness and grandeur, lifts the meaning of poetry beyond mere words, resonating through the chambers of the mind! Baba says, “Na khalu na khalu bāṇaḥ sannipātaḥ ayam asina mṛḍuni mṛga-śarīre tulā-rāśā-vibhāgniḥ—”

“The skilled city-dweller has come to the forest to hunt. The forest deer are simple, helpless, gentle—

--- Page 74 ---
That is, gentle—Dushyanta stands on his chariot, bow drawn, about to release his arrow. The forest dwellers have pleaded with him not to commit such cruelty—they raise both hands and say, “Don’t kill—don’t kill, don’t set fire to the heap of cotton with your arrow!” Whose arrow is this? Madan’s! And who is this deer? Shakuntala, soft as a blossom, enchanting even when bound, adorned with flowers, innocent, simple—she knows nothing of the manners and customs of city folk, Mother. She is the deer. Dushyanta will soon pierce her with his arrow. He will set fire to the heap of flowers. Here, a foreshadowing is given, it is suggestive, a hint of what is to come.

I am thinking, thinking, thinking—Father was right, it is a “hunt.” But I am certainly not so naïve. I have gained much experience. I will surely cast off this body.

The next day I told Ma, “Ma, tell Father I will take the exam. There are still three months left. Bring me the syllabus, there’s plenty of time.”

“There’s plenty, of course. If you wish, how long will it take you?”

Ma was very pleased—she was flattering me, “Your father says—with such hope I named you, she will drink the nectar of knowledge, she will be a Brahmavadini. Fulfill your father’s hope, my child. Don’t ruin your life for a trivial reason.”

The first day I sat down to study with Father, the experience was not only painful, but nowadays, it would be considered unbelievable. Father told me, “Write down a few examples of ‘abhootapurva chiri’.” I simply could not concentrate. Father paced around and then sat down. I sat there with an empty notebook, staring blankly—what they call the burning of the Brahmarandhra had happened to Father, he could not bring even such a small girl under control.

“What’s wrong? Have you swallowed everything? Why aren’t you writing? Write, write.” My hand became even more rigid, nothing came to mind. Suddenly, Father slapped me hard across the cheek. The mark of his five fingers was imprinted, but strangely, I did not feel any pain. Ma rushed in, “What’s the matter? How could you strike such a grown girl?”

Father threw down the book and got up, “Intolerable, intolerable, this stubbornness of hers, she simply refuses to study.”

Ma remained silent. That day, my resentment was not towards Father, but towards Ma. Because Father did what he thought was right. Ma, though she knew what was right, could not do it. Ma bows her head before force—she has had to pay the price for this mistake. Stepping outside—meaning, leaving the sitting room where I had been studying—I saw Shanti standing by the stairs. She looked at me with tearful eyes—“Look, Ru, I will not stay in this house any longer.”

I thought, but I have to stay, because he never even looked for me. Where else would I go?

After going to Madhupur, no one’s health improved. Bathing in the pond water, everyone contracted some illness or other. My little brother, only four years old, suffered a terrible typhoid. Sabir fell ill with the maladies of adolescence, and at that time, he suffered a mental blow because of me. And then I had already worn Ma out. Ma’s suffering reached its peak—amidst all this, Father said he needed every kind of test, so he would stay in a hospital cabin. A cabin was taken at Medical College—a huge room, how clean the hospitals were in those days, stone floors, gleaming beds, wonderful furniture. All sorts of tests were being done on Father, but he had no physical pain, piles of books were coming in—he was reading—every evening, countless devotees and disciples gathered—conversations, stories, discussions. The food from the hospital and from home was plentiful, enough to feed guests. Relatives were allowed to stay in the cabin, but Ma could not come, so I stayed there all day. Ma, one...

--- Page 75 ---
Struggling to care for a typhoid patient—back then, typhoid was a truly terrifying disease. There was no one to help Mother except Shanti; as for me, I spent all day tending to Father. 

One day, at the hospital, Father quietly asked me, “Ru, won’t you write poetry anymore?”  
I remained silent. In truth, I was still writing poetry, but what I wanted to write was so vivid, so stark, that there was no veil left to hide behind. How could I show such writing to anyone? It was as if everyone would see right into my heart. I had almost written a whole book—I had heard that the task of literature is to make the personal impersonal. What is mine becomes everyone’s, but I could not manage that—my words remained stubbornly, painfully my own. I still remember a poem from that time, where I asked Shakespeare, “Where were you then, Shakespeare, when I stood on the veranda? Not a window, a veranda—does it make a difference? That house was just a modest two-story rental, not the colonnade of a wealthy home, and it was on the ground floor, above the street, not in a garden. There was no wall, but there was a madhabilata vine—so what if there was no garden? The street ran alongside, and in Calcutta, no flowers bloomed on that drab road, but does that mean the moment was insignificant? O great poet! I had hoped that at the moment of his departure, you would give him—yourself, standing behind me, haloed—like the aureole behind an idol—I thought you would breathe life again into my wounded love! Can a golden statue make Juliet immortal? I have made him immortal, so you will become my devotee—praise me, great poet—come into my home—”

A few years after those poems, I performed my fire-ritual, but now I know that even if I had shown them to Father then, he would not have been angry. If what is written is good, then it has become divine—he would not have concerned himself with who wrote it, or why. He objected to my falling in love, but not to my writing love poems! He would have said, “It is good to have many experiences. All this is ignorance, maya; only through the world of ignorance can one reach the world of knowledge—‘avidyā mṛtyuṃ tīrtvā, vidyayā’mṛtam aśnute’—” Father was always eager to drink the nectar of knowledge—he was not so concerned for those who dwell in the world of ignorance, tossed by joy and sorrow. “Above all, man is truth”—that was not the motto of his life.

I would go to the hospital to tend to the patient, and I did so. All day, I would read to Father, and in the afternoons, I would prepare food on the stove for the guests who would come. Outwardly, I appeared normal, but inside, nothing was right—whenever I thought of wandering the forests as an ascetic, my heart would break and I would be overcome with tears—burning with remorse. I thought, if a snake were to bite him, then I would be his murderer. How could he survive in the jungle—he was, after all, a sahib. They are used to such comfort. Father would always say to me, “Ru, change the tablecloth every other day so there are no stains from the curry—do you know how clean they are in Europe?” When I remembered such things, I had to keep my outward composure, and so my insides bled. I was ashamed to show any weakness in front of Father. My pride would not allow it. So I tried very hard to remain cheerful, yet one day, in the hospital, I fainted. I cannot recall the exact reason—perhaps there was none. The doctor said I was on the verge of a nervous breakdown. Mother was beside herself with worry. Father said, “A little strength of mind and you’ll be fine—what else can you do! She’s doing this on purpose—to get the better of me.” If the doctor had been anyone else, Father would have cross-examined him on medical matters, exposing his ignorance and making him bow his head in shame. But the doctor was none other than the eminent physician Nilratan Sircar, who could diagnose typhoid or pneumonia by scent alone—when he entered a room, even the fear of death would retreat, and in our home—

75

--- Page 76 ---
That ever-benevolent doctor cured me. Within seven or eight months, I recovered. I sat for my exams, and the results were good. When the illness was gone, I felt a deep sorrow, as if my main weapon in the battle had become blunt—what would I fight with now?

Speaking of that exam, one thing comes to mind especially today, when I see even parents searching for question papers for their children, unhesitant in cheating and forgery. Since it wasn’t decided in advance that I would take the exam, Baba had set the Sanskrit question paper himself, but when I did sit for the exam, he resigned from his post as examiner. He had prepared the question paper, he had taught me as well, but I couldn’t understand a thing about what was in the questions... After the exam, Baba laughed heartily—“So, you couldn’t understand anything beforehand, could you?” Today, this might sound like an impossible, far-fetched tale. In those days, parents wanted their children to truly learn, now they only want degrees. That is indeed a change in the country!

Nine or ten months passed, or maybe a year, and in that time, I heard nothing of him. Khoka didn’t come either. Even when I went to his bookshop, I couldn’t catch him. I had a little sister now. My uncles had left, after a big quarrel with Baba—not face to face, they didn’t have that courage, but they scolded Ma, and Ma, taking her husband’s side, argued back. I was angry with my uncle too, I quarreled as well. Kakima had gone back to her father’s house, beyond our reach. I never understood the real reason for Kaka’s anger, but his leaving like that made me very sad; I loved him so much, I vowed never to see his face again.

Ma said, the household had begun to break apart. The home she had arranged with relatives, dependents, guests, which had sparkled for the past year with stories, laughter, poetry, and joy—Ma was the queen of it all, and now, one by one, it was as if each lamp was being extinguished.

One day, I was standing by the stairs when Baba came out of the sitting room—“Mircea came yesterday.” My heart began to pound—oh God, what am I about to hear? “He’s grown a beard, a full beard. He’s become an ascetic, I almost didn’t recognize him. Ha ha ha.” Without answering, without turning to face Baba, I started down the stairs. Baba called out, “Ru, Ru, he’s going back to his country—put his curios that are lying around into the car.” I wondered, why did he grow a beard? Is it out of grief? I couldn’t do anything for him. I didn’t even cut my hair. Selfish me, so selfish.

When Mircea had once gone to Darjeeling, he brought back some beautiful, large Tibetan curios, which were displayed on the stair shelves—I used to see those tokens. Today, with my own hands, I put them in the car. Well, that didn’t make me sad. Never, not even as a child, was I an idolater. That is, no object has ever been a symbol of emotion for me. I don’t even have a single photograph of him, and that doesn’t make me sad. What use is a photograph? A photograph won’t bring back the person!

When Baba spoke of his bearded face, my heart felt crushed inside—I wondered, why is Baba laughing? Does that mean I should see the whole matter as a childish joke? Is it so easy to wander the mountains just because one is sad? There was sadness in his heart, and now physical suffering too. But he is skilled at this—no one can match him in inflicting pain on himself—though there’s no use in self-torment—perhaps it’s foolish, yet people honor such acts. There is nothing more terrible than sati, yet when Grandma spoke of an ancestor who had been a sati, her face would light up. The austere, fasting widowhood is not a thing to be mocked. As I loaded the curios into the car, I wondered if Baba could do anything for anyone...

--- Page 77 ---
To inflict pain on oneself, to deprive oneself a little? Impossible. Why did he mock—he jests at scars who never felt a wound! Suddenly, the wisdom of Shakespeare opened its eyes within me.

None of us had recovered our health properly—yet again we would go out. We would go to Kashi. I could not see myself, but I believed I was behaving quite normally, and no one could sense my inner turmoil. Inner turmoil! Again, what is that! Love and all that nonsense. But Ma found no peace—her unrest stemmed mainly from within herself! Ma was wondering how she would arrange my marriage. Would I ever be able to love another man? And even if I could, would it be right? Love is not some object to be stolen from one and given to another; I suppose I did not fully understand that then. Perhaps people knew it, but society did not.

One day, Ma asked me, “Ru, did you ever recite any marriage vows or perform any ceremony?”

“Ma, you’ve asked me this before, and I’ve told you—we never even thought of such things.”

“Then why did you recite that vow the day you fell ill in the hospital?”

“What vow? ‘Mamabrate te hridaya dadhaan’?”

“No, no, the English vow.”

“English vow! Have I ever seen their marriage that I would know such a vow? Why are you falsely accusing me?”

“It’s not an accusation, Ru. If you did say it, tell me!”

“What is the vow?”

“I didn’t quite understand how it could be a vow—it didn’t sound like one, but your father said it was a marriage vow. Since he said so—”

“What is it?”

—“in sickness and in health.....”

“What kind of vow is that—I’ve never heard it before.” Ma let out a long sigh.

Much later, I remembered that I had seen that vow written in a film—back then, films were silent. Its meaning: in happiness and sorrow, in sickness and in health, we shall not forsake each other—till death do us part—perhaps I had unknowingly uttered it. Consciously, I had never thought of that vow. So the matter was not important at all. But Ma had sought refuge in that idea—my suffering, Mircea’s suffering, all that could be overlooked, but if a vow had been recited, that would be insurmountable—people are nothing, the ritual is everything. Such was our country. Perhaps it still is.

But to think that Ma truly valued rituals so much, or was so steeped in orthodoxy, would be unfair to her—it wasn’t that. In this case, she was simply searching for a weapon to fight with Baba, nothing more.

When I was seven and Sabitri was two, Thakuma, Ma, the two of us sisters, and Champa-pishi spent two years in Puri, while Baba was in England. One day, we were standing in front of the sanctum of the Jagannath temple—it was a holy day—crowds everywhere—people pushing and shoving all around. Suddenly, a middle-aged widow and a boy of about fifteen or sixteen approached us. Coming straight to us, she placed a sweet in Sabitri’s hand and a garland of flowers around my neck, saying, “Jagannath is witness, I give you this garland in marriage.” Then, she and her son quickly disappeared into the crowd.

Thakuma cried out, “Who are you, who are you—” and then went searching through the crowd. Ma calmly pulled the garland from my neck and walked us home. Thakuma returned a little later, distraught—“Bouma, what a disaster—”

--- Page 78 ---
“It’s done, Jagannath is our witness!”

“Be quiet. What does it matter what some madman has said? Jagannath does not listen to anyone’s delirium.”

“Oh, daughter-in-law,” Grandmother is striking her forehead, “today is that particular tithi—”

“You must be absolutely quiet. Not another word from you. If you say one more thing, I’ll leave for Calcutta tomorrow itself.” Later, Mother told me that perhaps they had made a vow to Jagannath to garland a virgin girl on this particular tithi. What a strange country this is! Its peculiar customs can turn even the wisest people into fools.

There are many examples of how truthful and free from superstition Mother was at heart. One incident will make this clear. Even today, few women in this country could do what she did.

It was 1924 or 1925. I was ten years old then, and we were living in a large house in Kalighat. On either side of the house were two rooms for the gatekeepers. In one room lived our formidable gatekeeper, Surajpal. One day I saw him, looking dejected, taking his belongings out and moving them into the other room where two servants stayed.

“Gatekeeper, why are you vacating this room?”

“Majhi’s orders.”

“Why? For whom?”

“For the Maharani.” After much questioning, I learned that this ‘Maharani’ was actually Panchi. Panchi was our contract maid who washed the dishes. A few days before, I had seen Mother scolding her harshly, and Panchi was clutching Mother’s feet, weeping. Now the gatekeeper had to vacate his room for Panchi—I thought perhaps she had managed this by crying and pleading. I was always a fighter against injustice, so I was angry and astonished—the gatekeeper refused to reveal the mystery. So I went to my all-knowing grandmother, who was also the one to whom I brought all complaints—she believed that I was the only one in the house who truly loved her.

“Grandmother, why are you crying?”

“I won’t stay in this house anymore, child—this house has become impure—”

“What happened, Grandmother, what happened?” I threw my arms around her neck. “If you leave, I’ll leave too.”

“That Panchi—she works in seven houses, who knows what sin she’s brought with her—she’s a sinner, and if you live in the same house as her, fourteen generations will go to hell. But your mother, she’s melted with pity—she’ll let Panchi stay in the gatekeeper’s room, she’ll look after her. And your father, he’s become his wife’s sheep. Whatever she says, he does—Mother is nobody anymore!”

The image of Father as a sheep suddenly made me laugh... But I was still desperate to know why Panchi was a sinner, what terrible sin she had committed, so I pestered Grandmother relentlessly. She was afraid of Mother and didn’t want to say—but how could she resist me? And she herself was eager to speak. “She’s going to have a child—having a child as a widow is a great sin, even stepping in her shadow is a sin.” I went to Mother and said, “Mother, why is Panchi going to have a child? Having a child as a widow is a great sin—why are you letting her stay in the house?”

Mother looked at me with wide eyes. “Who told you all this? Grandmother, surely!” After a moment’s silence, Mother said, “Ru, having a child is not in human hands, is it? It’s God’s doing. Can a human create another human? A widow shouldn’t have a child, but sometimes even God makes mistakes, and then people suffer greatly—when people suffer, when there’s danger, we must help them. That is never wrong.”

With relatives, children, and servants, a large group of us set off for Kashi. From there, Mother, Father, I, and my newborn baby sister were to travel to Dehradun, Agra, and then to Rishikesh and Laxmanjhula. The thought of going to Rishikesh filled my heart with joy. It felt as if...

--- Page 79 ---
If I go there, I’ll see him—yet I know he has gone back to his country. Then why does it feel as if he’s here now? This is one of those thoughts that play hide and seek with us, beyond the reach of reason.

I remember nothing of our journey except for Haridwar and Rishikesh. From Haridwar, we arranged for a car and a guide, and set out towards Swargadwar. For the first time, I found refuge in the Himalayas—a peace as certain as the shelter of the gods. The sea breeze has its effect on the mind, but not like the mountains. All around, tall, straight trees—I do not know their names. I had never seen a river flow with such force—never before had I felt such a blissful touch in the air. The cool breeze is soothing every burning wound inside my chest; it feels as if the fire will be extinguished! Mircea had done well to come here too. The air is not just cold—there is something more in it. What is it? What do I know of the majesty of the king of mountains, the Himalaya? I had never imagined before that nature could bring peace to the human mind. Shakuntala lay on lotus leaves—I used to think that even in poetry, there must be some plausibility—if someone made a bed for me out of lotus flowers, I would not find peace.

We were traveling through a plain. On both sides, fields of hemp, taller than the roof of the car—I never recognized the place again. Suddenly, the car broke down. Mother and I were flustered, trying to manage the crying child—just then, the guide suddenly said, “There are shers in this hemp forest.” I was completely ignorant of Hindi—I thought, “Ser” means weight, but what kind of thing is a “sher” that lives here? Father was startled—“What a disaster! There are tigers here! Then why did you bring us here, you wretch!” The more father got angry, the more the guide calmly replied, “Don’t worry, even tigers fear for their lives—if we honk the horn, they’ll run away.”

I thought to myself—look, there are so many of us, yet we are afraid—how would it feel to wander here alone? It’s no laughing matter. When we reached Swargadwar, we crossed to the other side by boat—this was the first time I saw a mountain river; later, I would spend my life on its banks. Flowing down the slope, the river was wild, its clear waters swirling, striking against the rocks, sometimes rising up in shattered spray. Cool drops of water touched my face and eyes. Father scooped up a handful and drank. We did too—what an exquisite taste! I had never drunk such sweet, life-refreshing water before, it was like Alakananda! We had reached heaven—crossed the gates of paradise;—I offered water to my lips and praised the river—Sur-nara-nistarinī, pāpatāpa-nivāriṇī, patitapāvanī, sāgaragāminī Gange—Father was delighted by my spontaneous recitation—“Say, say the whole thing—” We had reached the other side. We walked along a mountain path—mother and I took turns carrying my little sister. There were small cave-like hollows along the mountainside—the guide said that ascetics meditated on the other side. In each cave sat a solitary figure—in various postures—mostly in padmasana. Some were ash-smeared, matted-haired; some had shaven heads—a few monks with water pots slung from their hands, some with tridents, wandering here and there. Their food was supplied free of cost from the Kalikambaliwala’s dharmashala. Pious, wealthy people had made wonderful arrangements; those who wandered came and ate, and those who sat in meditation had food brought to them.

As we walked on, the guide pointed to a cave and said—a sahib stayed here for some days, but he has gone now. The cave was empty; father stopped. He let out a long sigh, “That’s it, surely—this is what is called karma. Came to study, but instead wandered in the forests and returned to his country.” Father continued to scold as he walked, but I could sense the state of his mind. He loved him. As much as he was capable of loving, he did. All this happened because of me. But who started this game first? I defend myself. Yet I cannot recall where the beginning of this desire was—do I even know where it ends? I know, do I? The rocky, shadowed path beneath the tall trees—we walked in a line—at the next cave...

--- Page 80 ---
A sadhu is sitting, his chin resting on a small stick. His eyes are large, gaze fixed far away. From his face, it is clear that he is different from all the other sadhus I have seen so far. The guide said, he was once a Bengali judge—he heard the call of God and left everything to come here two years ago. He goes nowhere. He eats only once every two days. He does not utter a single word. No one has ever seen him sleep; he just sits like this. Whenever I hear about Krishna devotion, I feel a deep reverence—though I myself am incapable of such devotion. In my mind, I wonder if the sahib who was in the neighboring cave was Mircea; I must find out from this man. After all, in this forest of Swargadwar, there aren’t crowds of sahibs roaming about. I stumbled in front of the judge-sadhu’s cave. My foot struck something. I said to Baba, “You two go ahead if you want, I’ll wait here, I’ve hurt my foot.”

As Baba and Ma went ahead, I approached the judge-sadhu and said, “You do not speak, but you are a sadhu—removing the world’s suffering is your task, isn’t it? Will you tell me something about the sahib who was in that cave? Was his name Mircea?”

There was no change in the sadhu’s face. In his unwavering, distant gaze, there was no sign of emotion. I began to cry—“Please tell me, please, I need to know so much.” Another visitor had come and stood nearby—he was astonished by the scene. Suddenly, in the midst of this forest, the sight of a beautiful young woman weeping before a matted-haired cave-dweller had deeply moved him—he had not yet attained the perfect detachment required to be utterly indifferent to human suffering. He said, “Don’t cry, don’t cry—no matter how much you weep, he will not speak. What do you want to know, Ma?”

“The sahib who was in that cave—what was his name?”
“I don’t know his name. Who asks the names of sadhus and ascetics?”
“Did you see him?”
“Yes.”
“What did he look like?”
“Like a sahib.”
“What do you mean, like a sahib?”
“I mean, fair-skinned.”
“How tall was he? Did he wear glasses?”
He showed the height with his hand. He could not say whether he wore glasses. I returned and entered that cave. I looked all around to see if there was a name written anywhere, if anything had been left behind in writing. I searched thoroughly. My heart was pounding, as if seeing his name on the wall would mean seeing him. I searched like a madwoman, knowing Ma and Baba would arrive any moment. There was nothing written anywhere. Only a few mathematical sums in Bengali script. Once again, my reason and unreason began to battle. Unreason said, this is just to torment me—couldn’t he have written his name here? Baba wouldn’t have scolded him for that—what harm would it have done? My reason said—how could he have known you would come here? Why are you so agitated over this? Why are you so angry? But I could not control the ocean of my wounded pride. Its waves were surging—what strength, what spiritual discipline do I have to fight against it? That there is no name written here was done deliberately, to sever all ties with me—nothing else. In the distance, I could hear Baba talking to the guide; I came out and sat beside the judge-sadhu. It’s just as well that this man does not speak, otherwise he would have told Baba.

We crossed the path and walked toward the ghat. The boat was crowded. One has to sit holding onto a rope. Not long ago, a boat had struck a rock and capsized, killing thirty people—on this river, it is said...

--- Page 81 ---
Once you enter, there is no escape. The current is so strong that, crashing against the rocks with tremendous force, a human body would be shattered to pieces in an instant. I am sitting at the edge of the boat, stretching out my right hand to touch the water. And I feel that now is the time to mete out the proper punishment for leaving without writing my name. If I were to fall in here, I would be finished in a moment. Since I cannot practice austerities, let me at least experience one final, ultimate physical pain—perhaps then I will find true peace. But how would that be a fitting punishment? He wouldn’t even know—wouldn’t know at all! But the letter Father would write—when that gentleman on Number One Melody Street reads it, he’ll faint on the spot. Father would write, “I trusted you, gave you shelter, gave you food, gave you education. In return, you have given me death. My affection for you was the very breach through which you entered like Saturn and destroyed my household.” And both Mother and Father would receive their due lesson. The thrill of revenge is swirling in my head.

Suddenly Ma said, “Ruu, sit back a little—why are you leaning so far out?” I imagine Milu; she would beat her head and weep. I remember Didima, Shanti, Khoka—everyone comes to mind. Thakuma used to say that at the moment of death, everyone’s face comes before your eyes—surely that’s what’s happening to me, I’m remembering everyone. Everyone? Suddenly, not a person, but the picture hanging on the wall of my room in Bhabanipur—the one with the velvet cap—comes to mind. No matter where you go, the gaze in that picture follows you. I see that picture now. Those piercing eyes are looking straight at me. They seem to say, “Shame on you! Is this all you are? Defeated so easily? Then my song is not for you.” It was as if, from the enchanted darkness, I lifted my face and saw the morning light.

Father reached out and pulled me back. “Why are you sitting so close to the edge?” The very father I was about to punish by jumping in—I clung to his neck and wept, resting my head on his chest. Ma grew very anxious and began to say, “Why is she crying again?” Father said, “Whenever she sees beautiful places, beautiful sights, tears always come to her eyes.” Father was lightening the moment—he couldn’t bear it anymore, nor could they—this burden of sorrow was too much.

We returned to Haridwar. I sat on the veranda of the bungalow—listening to the sound of the river—  
the rippling river, the sacred, life-giving Ganges of this land, washing over me. With my eyes closed, I thought—at last, I understand the meaning of that song:

If ever this heart should stray from the path,  
One glance at that face, and shame will bring it back.

## Na Hanyate // Part Two

In our youth—that is, in the time I am writing about, and up until just before Rabindranath’s death—everyone, that is, all Bengalis, called him ‘Robibabu’, young and old alike. Abroad, they called him Tagore, Tagoreh, or Tegor. Nowadays, I see even Bengalis add a ‘y’ sound and say ‘Tyagore’—a more jarring pronunciation could hardly exist. ‘Robibabu’ was not quite a fitting salutation either, but that was the custom. Some called him Robi Thakur; ‘Rabindranath’ became common much later. Ramananda Babu used to call him ‘Kobi’—the Poet—and I learned that from him. I never called him Gurudev in my life. When he was called ‘Gurudev’, it was not as a spiritual master. His students at his school called him ‘Guru’ in the way one calls a teacher ‘Guru’. He was not a religious teacher, but a teacher nonetheless. In that sense, everyone in this country could call him Guru—but he considered his identity as a poet to be truer. In 1930, he was almost entirely abroad. Towards the end, he went to Russia. The Russian scholar Bogdanov once visited our house; I think he made some unfavorable remark to Father about the poet’s trip to Russia, and Father, too, after being invited from Russia...

--- Page 82 ---
He had tried, but did not receive official permission. There was a story he often told—when he was in England, a high-ranking Russian official would invite him to dinners. Father had understood that the man had some purpose. Finally, one day, the Russian official requested that Father connect him with Indian revolutionaries—because they wished to help India in her struggle for independence. In response, Father told him that he would indeed make the introductions, if the Russian could tell him: after we drive out the English with your help, with whose help shall we drive you out? Needless to say, the Russian official could not give a satisfactory answer. Father would tell this story with great pride, for he had no involvement in any kind of political activity. The pure pursuit of knowledge was the sole purpose of his life.

Rabindranath had returned to the country almost eight or nine years ago. I had seen him there once or twice, but never had the chance to speak with him. Whenever I went, I went with Father, or someone else was present. When the dance festival 'Nabin' was staged in Kolkata, I visited Jorasanko twice. Still, nothing was said. After returning from Rishikesh, I wrote him a letter. I cannot at all recall what I wrote in that letter. But I had given a hint of my mental state. I did not dare to write openly, and I was also ashamed. I did not dare because, if Father wished to see the reply, he would find out—

Here, let me say a little about Father’s relationship with the Poet. Father had read Rabindranath’s poetry thoroughly, and was adept at interpreting it. His attraction to the Poet was deep and intense, yet the two of them were completely opposite in character. For this reason, Father also had a fair amount of criticism for the Poet—and, on the other hand, the Poet was not particularly favorable toward Father either. In such a situation, my predicament is easy to imagine. Father knew I could reach the Poet easily. The Poet was kindly disposed toward me. So whenever I wrote a letter, Father wanted to insert his own message into it; my letter would cease to be my own. This was another pain for me. I had suffered this since childhood. Father would dictate, I would write the letter, then Father would correct its spelling, refine its language, insert his own ideas—only then would the letter be sent, and when a reply came, Father would critique it—how could I endure such captivity? The Poet once laughed and said to me, “When you write to me, if you write simply what comes to your heart instead of philosophy, it would be better.” I know that even if there are a few spelling mistakes, he would not mind. The freedom that his other admirers have, I do not. I cannot even go to him if I wish.

This time, without showing anyone, I wrote him a letter. In due course—that is, two days after he received the letter—I received the following reply, which I quote here:

Dear One,

The pain expressed in your letter has caused me great distress. If there is a discord between life and the world, it is not easy to bear; slowly tuning it into harmony is not possible at your age and with your experience. I do not know what advice to give you. Remembering my own youth, when days were made thorny by intense sorrow, I could find no way to pass through them. I thought that this hardship would never end. But the true culmination of life is not only through memory; day by day, carrying pain into awareness, transforming the harsh into the gentle, the bitter into sweetness—this is fulfillment. I do not believe that this will not happen for you—for you have imagination, and that is the power of creation. You will not be able to surrender yourself passively to circumstance—you will create by making yourself more complete. I know that for people in our country, it is not easy for generous spirits to develop freely—their field of expansion outward is blocked; even the pursuit of entering the inner world is not favored for them.

82

--- Page 83 ---
Rare! Still, do not lose hope—keep faith in yourself, withdraw from everything around you, and in that deep solitude, quiet yourself where your greatness surpasses even your destiny. If I had the power to console your troubled heart, I would have tried. But apart from silently wishing you well, there is nothing more I can do. If any petty matter from the outside world has hurt you, do not feel ashamed to admit defeat to it.

Yours—14th Shravan, 1338

Exactly one year before receiving this letter, that is, towards the end of Bhadra in 1337, Mircea left. According to Hindu custom, not even animals are driven away in the month of Bhadra—let alone people—but we, being a modern family, do not believe in such superstitions.

I read the letter many times. I liked it, yet my heart was steeped in melancholy; the state of my mind is far beyond what this letter can reach. Back in Calcutta, I am carrying on as usual. Father believes there is no longer any scar on my mind, but now he is very watchful. The house feels empty now. Shanti and Khoka have left, so have uncle and aunt, and Mircea’s room stands desolate. A skilled nurse has come for my little sister, so I no longer have much to do. That day, I was sitting with the letter in my hand when Ma entered the room. I handed her the letter. She read it and returned it to me:

We, mother and daughter, sat together—the darkness was deepening. I said, “Ma, I want to go to Santiniketan once. Will you send me?”

Ma replied, “I was just thinking of telling you to go for a few days.” My mother could speak so beautifully. I have seen many scholars, even quite a few PhDs, but rarely have I seen anyone who could understand and speak with such heart as my mother:

That evening, in the gathering darkness, Ma pulled me onto her lap and began to speak, “Ru, you have received something wondrous in your life. How many are so fortunate? How many get to see that heavenly form of love—free from worldly ties, selfless, untouched by earthly needs, not bound by the relationships of this world, by reverence, devotion, and love? How many does it happen to? It’s as if you have been prepared for this even before you became aware—like a flower that spends the night awake, not knowing it waits for the light. Every day, through his poetry, his songs, his thoughts, your mind has turned upwards. This is the fruit of a kind of penance—do not shatter yourself and waste this rare beauty. Go to him, and without any hesitation or reservation, tell him everything. I can see that you held back in your letter. That is not right. When you have such a place where you can surrender yourself completely, where you can confess your joys and sorrows, sins and virtues, and be purified—do so, my child. He will show you the way.”

“Ma, I want to go alone.”

“Of course!”

But I could not keep my word to Ma; I do not even remember on which date, with whom, I reached Santiniketan, whether it was noon or afternoon.

Whenever I go to him, I always have to cross a difficult path. From the very corner of Bentinck Street, my heart starts pounding. It beats faster as I reach the bend of Dwarkanath Tagore Lane. Until I reach him and begin to speak, I am truly stricken—just as one feels when climbing a mountain. That day, the road to Santiniketan felt even more arduous—it seemed as if all the oxygen had been sucked from the air.

When I entered his room, he was sitting in an easy chair, pictures scattered all around—it was the era of painting. He was not surprised to see me suddenly; I am such that I come without notice.

--- Page 84 ---
Suddenly, as I arrived, he said, “None of the daughters-in-law are here, so now Ganapati is my only hope for hospitality.”

I sat down at his feet.

“Will you read those ghostly writings?”—meaning the planchette writings. But my mind was not on ghosts at that moment—I was overwhelmed by the torments of the present.

“I’ve come to tell you something.”

“Yes, I received both your letters. What happened, Amrita?”

I sat there silently—my head bowed—I clung to the arm of his easy chair. How could I say it, what could I say? And what would be the point of saying it? I absolutely did not want any misunderstanding between him and Father; if that happened, my ruin would be complete.

He lifted my hand, trying to help me speak. “Tell me, Amrita, what happened?”

“There was that boy at our house, wasn’t there…”

“What boy?”

“You saw him.”

“Who is he? Where did I see him?”

“We came here, together—”

“You came here? When? Are you talking about your uncle?”

“No, Baba’s student—”

“Which student? From the Rabindra Society?”

“No. I’m telling you… Remember, we were walking near the tennis court, don’t you recall?”

“Oh yes, that sahib? What happened then?”

Instead of answering, I asked, “Why did you leave for so long?”

He smiled a little; he could answer such questions so well. With just a few words, he could pour forth an ocean of nectar. He could nurture the sweetness in every relationship, without disturbing it in the least, keeping everything in its proper place, taking only what could be received, and paying its full worth. For he knew the wondrous art of maintaining a ruthless distance while reaching the deepest chambers of the heart—‘Let my mind remain distant—let me touch only with the melody of my flute, his soul and mind.’ This is an art that harms no one—no one is destroyed, only the lamp of life burns brighter.

“Why did I leave? To show pictures… to show pictures… No one in this country knows how to look at pictures—well, you didn’t forbid me, otherwise I wouldn’t have gone.” He gently shook my head. I knew it was a jest, but a sweet one.

“Tell me, what happened to that sahib, where is he?”

“He’s gone!”…

I couldn’t say anything more. I got up from beside him and, lying face down near the western door, began to cry.

“Come here, Amrita, come here, let me hear it all!”

But I had no strength left to get up or to speak. I had collapsed, and to stop crying, cross those three or four yards, and go back to him again? Impossible. I wasn’t listening to Ma. To say all those things would break my heart. How could I say it? Where would I begin? Whose fault was it? His, or mine? I realized I was being disobedient—if it were possible, I would have flown back to Ma through that door right then, I could not pass this test. Suddenly I realized he had gotten up from his chair. He sat down beside me on a stool—“Amrita, get up, calm yourself—”

--- Page 85 ---
I got up. He helped me sit up against the pillows—  
“Is it difficult for you to tell me?”  
“Yes.”  
“Then you don’t have to say anything. There’s no need. Be calm, be calm.”  
I tried to compose myself.  
He spoke again, “You don’t have to say anything more, Amrita. Just tell me—can I do something for you? Do you need any help?”  
Usually, when he spoke to someone, he didn’t look directly at them. Most of the time, his gaze would be fixed on the ground or into the distance. He would address humanity, not the person in front of him—their problem was the problem of mankind. I understood this only later; at the time, I didn’t. Back then, I would feel hurt. Now I realize that people came to him with their love and joy, their pain and longing, their hearts full of questions. It was not just one individual. Now I understand that without this seeming harshness, his sensitive mind could never have crossed the vast, difficult ocean of work, could never have reached the summit of knowledge; it would have been wounded and battered by every blow.  
He never told anyone, “Don’t go today, stay a couple more days.” Of course, I’m not speaking of the time when he was ill and weakened—then, I have seen tears fall from his eyes for us—  
But that day, in that rare moment, he spoke directly to me—“Tell me, Amrita, tell me, look at me”—he looked into my eyes and said, “Whatever you ask, I will do.”  
...“Whatever you ask, I will do?” The words seemed to dance—‘the resonance that stirs your seven seas and ten horizons.’ At that moment, I cannot describe exactly what I felt—but I found the description later, when I went to see Chandalika. Chandalini says—‘my well has become a boundless sea’—how can a well become a boundless sea in a single utterance? Do the hundreds of spectators watching know that? Do they know it is true? Like ‘a handful of water,’ a single phrase can bring infinity into the depths of life.  
He repeated the words again, “Tell me, tell me—what do you want? Whatever you ask, I will do.”  
My body and soul were suffused with peace, I smelled the fragrance of sandalwood—what more could I want? I said, “Let me stay with you.”  
“What’s so difficult about that? Enroll here. In the early morning, when I sit on the veranda, you will come with gardenia flowers and sit with me. In the evening, we’ll read poetry together. But you mustn’t disturb me when I’m writing.”  
My heart was filled with tranquility, I thought—this is a description of heaven, it will never happen in my life, Father will never allow me to enroll here.  
Later that day, he said to me, “I can’t let you go today—think it over carefully tonight, tell me tomorrow if there’s anything I can do. These things can’t be decided in two minutes—if you’re in a hurry to leave, let it go—Nepal Babu can take you tomorrow.”  
That night, lying alone in the downstairs room, I couldn’t sleep. Everything was silent. As long as the servants were awake, there were faint sounds, the voices of Shaktipada or Ganapati, but gradually everything fell quiet. I kept thinking—what should I say to him? Will he be able to do anything? Why not? He could ask Mr. Eguz—“Please take this girl to Europe, that will be a great help to me.” Surely Mr. Eguz would do it. Whether he is a saint or a monk, a human being—

--- Page 86 ---
They understand sorrow. But can they truly understand it? Without including Baba? If Ramananda Babu and he, the two of them, called Baba and said, “Look, Naren Babu, what you have done is not right—” then what? Then Baba would be furious. He would say, “Look, Ravi Babu, when you married off your daughter, did you consult me? You write very good poetry, that’s true, but my household is my own.” Then? How would that feel? Would he be humiliated because of me? Besides, where would I even go? To whom? It has been a year—he hasn’t sent a single word—since he walked out of this house, there’s been no trace of him. He could have written a letter to Khoka, couldn’t he? But he didn’t. So then? Should I go begging? All those Sanskrit words come to mind—swairini, upayachika! My heart fills with shame! Enough. You, the European hunter of illusions, I will pull out your poisoned arrow. I will forget you, forget you, forget you. Tomorrow morning I will tell him there is no need to do anything for me. My mind is at peace. The poet has taught me that when the mind is troubled, I should say—“Anandam paramanandam param sukham parama tripti”—I kept repeating it, and the fragrance of sandalwood once again filled that dark night. The raw wound in my chest that hasn’t dried even a little in this past year, now received a balm of sandalwood. Through the open window, I can see the sky outside—I am thinking, how easily I have let go. Once, in my subconscious, I vowed never to forget—but today, with conscious, firm resolve, I have decided I will forget, I will forget for sure. The bright star is watching—it has eyes, it is smiling, smiling, casting its compassionate gaze upon this restless mind.

We left the Bhabanipur house in 1931 and moved to another home. From this time, cracks began to appear in my mother’s beautifully arranged household. All the old people had left—now a new person had come, who was gradually able to loosen all the bonds of affection and love in this family. That girl’s name was Rama. She was not beautiful, her face was round, her eyes bulging, her nose slightly curved and small compared to her face. She was very short. But her voice was sweet. She spoke softly, gently, and politely. Her calm demeanor was unlike our own boisterous, impetuous ways. I loved her very much, her conversation was pleasant, she was learned. Learned, yes—in the sense that she had scored well in her exams. But she never read poetry. She had no artistic taste. She couldn’t write a single line or speak beautifully, but she had patience—she could persist with one subject, what is called perseverance—she proved it, starting in 1931, it took her ten years to complete her doctorate. I could never have done that. I have no patience. We drew her close as one of our own. Just as Ma always took care of us, she took care of her too. She helped Baba a lot. She was always with him, even when he went to work. This eagerness did not bother me. Only, gradually, Ma’s place in the household was shifting, and that pained me. Baba was always scolding Ma. Her faults seemed only to increase. Once, Ma was the all-powerful mistress of the house, but as the days passed, it seemed she was receding, little by little, every day. Ma became anxious, excessively flattering. That made me feel ashamed.

If there is even one person in this world who knows, then I know that love does not require any familial relationship. But I also know that love is like light—it brightens everyone! It is not a substance to be taken from one and given to another. I also know that when you love, everyone connected to that person becomes dear—even the servant in their house feels like your own. What do I see in this case? Only an attempt to increase household rights and to humiliate Ma, and what is even more unbearable to me is the resort to lies—one should simply accept the truth. No matter how painful, I can face the truth, but I cannot close my eyes like Ma. So each day has become unbearable for me. On the other hand, upon us too—

86

--- Page 87 ---
Father’s attachment is fading. The reason he refused to let me marry abroad, I’ve heard, was because he couldn’t bear to live without me. But now, even his interest in my studies has waned. His attachment to me, too. I have enrolled in college. No one from outside can sense the different current flowing within our household. Mother covers everything up. But she has lost her sense of security. She feels I must be married off quickly. I agree. I have no wish to remain in this household—not even a little—I want to escape from here. I can feel myself burning inside with anger and sorrow, forced to compromise with falsehood. I must pretend not to know, not to understand what I know all too well, as if it isn’t so—this is how I must go on.

Year after year passes, and every day something humiliating or unpleasant happens to Mother. Especially now, outsiders have begun to criticize openly. Even the newspapers write about it! Mother must listen to and endure criticism of Father. I can see how much self-interest taints Rama’s love. Can anyone insult and humiliate the one they claim to love and respect? Can they bring disorder and unrest into his household? In our country, slander is an ‘institution’—this institution’s business has grown greatly—no matter how much Mother tries to conceal things, introducing me as “This is also my daughter,” people’s tongues refuse to be restrained. However, this story is not Mother’s biography, so there’s no need to elaborate further. I have only understood this much—love itself has two forms that live side by side, pressed close together. One can lift a person up, where petty self-interest disappears, where everything is filled with light, where even the smallest things become extraordinary, the beloved becomes dearer—where even the unlovable becomes loved, and no one needs to be cast away. The other, though it comes under the same name, pulls tight like a noose, binds with chains, demands, “Come, withdraw the provident fund money, make the insurance in my name. It would be good if I got the house. Sign over the copyright of your books to me, why not your name and fame as well? At least let me take your name.”

It is as if people are walking a tightrope like circus animals—if you know how to walk, you’ll reach the other side safely; if not, you’ll fall flat on your face.

Mother has no sleep, day or night, nor do I. How can I leave this house, yet how can I leave Mother behind? Father scolds me a lot these days, perhaps because he sees silent reproach in my eyes. When I was studying for my B.A., I went to the zoo with friends—nothing scandalous—yet Father scolded me so much I was left bewildered. And then, I can’t forget that day when Jatin Das’s funeral procession passed along Russa Road. I stood on the balcony with a few girls, watching, full of excitement and fervor. Subhas Bose was leading the procession. So many people walked barefoot. Strange chants pierced the air, the victorious cries reached our ears like mantras, overwhelming us, pulling our hearts to join the procession below. Placards bore many slogans—among them, I saw those lines—“On the path of dawn, whose voice do I hear? Fear not, oh fear not, he who gives his life completely, his sacrifice never perishes.” The poem runs in my blood, it stirs me restlessly, and from the balcony I recite the next lines, which were not written there—“O Rudra, tell me how I may sing your song, Lord, let me beat my heart’s damaru to the rhythm of the dance of death—let me fill my basket with bitter sorrow and offer it as your garland—” As I recite, my mind becomes entranced—as we gaze at that flower-covered bier, we are hypnotized. “Chutku, come, let’s join the procession too.”

“Let’s go.” Then, after a moment’s thought, “But your father will scold you.”

--- Page 88 ---
"Scold me if you want. Let’s go.”

We plunged into that sea of people. I had never seen such a crowd before. I had pulled the ropes at Jagannath’s Rath Yatra, but this was even more. Suddenly, a white horse carrying a white-skinned sergeant galloped into the throng. In that brutal assault, the crowd was trampled and crushed. We, too, were somewhat injured. When we returned home, our clothes were torn and tattered, covered in dust and dirt.

I had thought of lying about it—but I didn’t. My mind was strung so high at that moment that I felt I would dishonor a noble death if I lied. So I told the truth. That day, Father scolded me so much that my head felt battered and bruised inside.

How could I ever be free? This became my constant worry. Mother had never been free either; she couldn’t even give someone twenty rupees as she wished—unless she did it in secret. Yet, back then, she still had considerable influence over Father; she could get many things done by persuasion. Now, she had no influence left. Father scolded her constantly over every little thing. Sometimes I wondered: those who love their own freedom, by what logic do they keep others in bondage? Of course, this lack of logic is everywhere—in the family, in the nation! “Rule Britannia, rule the waves—Britons will never be slaves,” and yet it is those very Britons who have enslaved us, not just us but so many other nations too, and they are proud of it—“The sun never sets on the British Empire.” People rarely apply logic.

Mother was frantic in her search for a suitable groom. She had even started a proper notebook, listing the names, qualities, and addresses of eligible bachelors and their guardians. A good match—meaning a man with a lucrative government job, property, and learning—was always rare. Even more so then. Whenever such a candidate appeared, all the mothers of unmarried daughters would cast their nets. At such times, Mother would start her own branch of the much-maligned “matchmaking” business. She lived in constant fear. After all, many relatives already knew about Mircea—and now Father had started this campaign. Otherwise, I wasn’t lacking in looks, talent, or learning, so why so much anxiety?

From this time, I began to think that the greatest injustice in this country was caste discrimination, and that I must do something about it. But how? What could I do? Who would listen to me—except myself? So one day I said to Father, “You’re going to marry me off anyway—why not to someone from another caste? That would be wonderful.”

“Another caste?” Father was astonished. “Why, what fault has our caste committed?”

“Then the marriage would happen, and it would also serve the country.”

Father’s astonishment only increased. “What! The marriage happens and the country is served too! I won’t have anything to do with these reformist marriages.”

A suitor came—a doctor, a well-paid government employee, with, I suppose, plenty of property. But the real problem was that his complexion was very dark. We, too, are not free from color prejudice. Mother, deeply distressed, said, “He’s so dark, what am I to do?”

I found it quite amusing. I thought of saying, “But you don’t like fair ones either.” Better not to bring up such things. The doctor’s elderly father was unwilling to let me go, but the doctor himself didn’t like me. He had his own ideas. He was concerned about the advancement of the Bengali race. I can’t blame him; just as I worry about the spiritual progress of Bengalis by eradicating caste, he was striving for physical improvement. He himself was about five feet three or four inches tall, so he was looking for someone at least five feet eight. I was only five feet two or three. He believed that if the son was short, he needed a tall wife. Otherwise, if two short people married, what would become of the future of the Bengali race? Since he held a high position, he measured and measured every unmarried girl in his community’s homes.

--- Page 89 ---
He is going around, and finally, at one house, he sees that the bride’s grandmother is very tall. Thin and tall, her body like a stick of wood. But she is not lacking in wit. Seeing her, the doctor liked her—“You are very tall.”

She twirled her thin neck and replied, “Even so, there’s no chance you and I will get married, dear.”

I heard that the groom never married after that; he never found a suitable bride. The groom’s elderly father was very fond of me. From his deathbed, he wrote to my father, “My foolish son did not bring such a precious girl into my home, but I bless her—may she marry a son a hundred times better than mine.” For that affectionate old man, I offer my respects here.

At last, a groom was found. I was saved. Mother decided it was best to complete the arrangements before the news reached the office at Bhagirath. So, within exactly five days, the wedding was arranged. The groom was a learned man. Father said, “He earned his doctorate in five years, that’s no small feat!” Mother said, “Your students keep dragging it out, how many years will they take?”

Father became quite angry, “Science and arts are not the same! In science, it’s finished quickly!”

My father invited the groom. But he did not come. Later, I heard the reason he didn’t come was that he had heard the bride was beautiful. If he came to see and fell in love—which was very likely—and if the girl, upon seeing him, turned away—which was even more likely—then what? That would be a mess! Better not to try to see each other at all. In his life, this was probably the first and last time he resorted to such cunning for himself.

The wedding preparations moved forward. No relatives would be able to come. Why should they, with such short notice? Mother herself was not well. Her illness had begun from her sadness—like a gastric ulcer. Mother was restless; after all, her daughter was not from a village in East Bengal, and after so much education, poetry writing, and fame, now to be married off without even a meeting, like a rural wedding! Mother was uneasy. At last, one day she said, “Let me go and bring him here. You’ll see him once, won’t you? How can it be done without seeing?”

“Never—not necessary.”

“Not necessary? Don’t you want to see him?”

“Mother, aren’t you ashamed to say such a thing to me? Why should I see him? Suppose I see him and say, I don’t like him, I want to marry someone else, he’s from another caste, but I like him, then will you listen to me? You’ll just start arguing, won’t you?”

“Why wouldn’t you like him? A man’s looks aren’t everything. How many ugly men are there?”

“Mother, don’t talk nonsense. You’re all the same—you don’t have the courage to accept your own truth. And you, you are the most guilty. You keep your eyes shut.”

Mother started crying—“The more I do for you all, the more you all scold me.”

It’s true. I really do torment my mother. I get angry at every little thing. Sabi went to that house. She came back and hugged me, crying—“Oh sister, don’t get married here, don’t, I don’t like it at all.” I laughed a lot. In my heart, I thought, I like Mother, but you drive her away. I said nothing aloud; surely she has forgotten all those words. She’s a child. It’s better to forget. Why did such an unpleasant thing have to happen in my life? You can’t erase life with a rubber. The picture that has once been drawn cannot be wiped away. In literature, you can. I see the poet crossing out lines, page after page…

--- Page 90 ---
The writing is getting ruined—but in life, what has been written cannot be erased with a rubber.  
If it could, I would, with all my strength at this very moment, rub out the year 1930.  
Let that year fly away from the circle of my life like a dry leaf, just as my love for Mircea has gone. I no longer remember his face, and even if I do, it brings neither joy nor sorrow.  
It has been four years now—how much longer will I remember?
