From the day of the blessing ceremony, I have started to cry. I am crying incessantly, unable to restrain myself at all.  
I do not even know why I am crying. If someone had said, “Well then, you don’t have to get married”—would I have agreed? Never.  
With such difficulty, I have finally found the door to leave this house. I will be able to be independent. If nothing else, at least I will be able to go to Santiniketan whenever I wish.  
Yet still, I keep crying—why, who knows? People think I am a devoted daughter, crying because I have to leave my parents.  
But it is just the opposite. Nothing feels good to me.

On the morning of the wedding day, the shehnai is playing. The Dadhi-mangal ritual is over.  
One of my Brahmo uncles sang very well. He had a beautiful inner life. I respected him.  
He sang a few songs. I told him, “Uncle, tonight you must sing me a song before you leave.”

The wedding took place.

At this time, the poet was in Ceylon. Father had sent a telegram asking for his blessings, and the blessings came by telegram.  
From the very moment of the wedding, I realized my life had become disordered—would I be able to gather it together and reach my destination in an orderly way?  
That night, in the bridal chamber, the kauri game was being played. I saw my uncle passing by the door.  
Leaving the game behind, I got up—I needed to hear a song much more than play kauri.  
Uncle could not believe that I would get up and come to him. He was surprised and delighted.  
Uncle sat on the veranda and sang—

“Who is our own, who is the other,  
Where is outside, where is home—  
O helmsman—looking at your face,  
With joy in my heart, I will bear all burdens—  
Our journey has now begun,  
O helmsman, I bow to you—  
Go, my child, go to your home—  
May your journey through life be smooth—”

Wishing my husband well and blessing me, my parents left, closing the door of the bridal chamber behind them.  
My husband is thirty-four years old, fourteen years older than I am.  
We are looking at each other. He is not good-looking, there are other flaws too, but I have realized he is a truly good man.  
I am not afraid, nor am I annoyed. I am wearing a green striped cotton sari, sandalwood paste on my forehead, but no vermilion in my parting—because the kush-bhika ritual has not been done.  
Without kush-bhika, the marriage is not complete.  
According to the usual Hindu custom, in such a situation, the bride and groom do not stay in the same room; at least a few children are posted as guards.  
But we do not believe in such superstitions—we are modern, after all.

I am thinking, I do not know him at all, I am seeing this gentleman for the first time—how can I sleep in the same bed with him!  
I am not afraid, just uncomfortable.  
I said, “I will sleep on the carpet on the floor.”  
He replied, “That won’t do, then I will sleep there.”  
How could that be? After all, this is our house, and he is the guest.  
After quite a bit of debate, I said, “Then I’ll just settle here.”  
A little disobedience on the very first day is nothing.  
I could not find anything to say. He never drinks, and he did not that day either.  
I did not notice that.  
Wrapping a shawl around myself, I turned my back to him and said, “I want to get up at five in the morning, Uncle will come then—if you wake up before me, will you wake me up?”

We turned off the light and lay down, and then something happened.  
The Mircea whom, for the past four—

--- Page 91 ---
I had not seen him for years, and then I saw him. Crossing a continent, crossing the Indian Ocean, he suddenly entered through this closed door. When he used to stay at our house, except on the day of the earthquake, I had never seen him so late at night, nor had I ever sat with him on the same bed. And now? Now he has come and sat on my bridal bed. I was astonished—

“Mircea, I had forgotten about you all this time—”
“Is that so?”
“Yes, but since this morning I’ve been thinking.”
“What are you thinking?”
“I’m wondering why you aren’t coming. You did all that penance in the Himalayas, so I was thinking—maybe it’s possible you’ve gained some power, you’ll come and convince Baba, but you never came.”
“But here I am—”
“Now it’s much too late—”
“Too late for what? What is before or after, what is late or soon?” Saying this, he rested his face on my heart—right there, the veins and arteries, under his kiss, were crushed and bruised, and blood began to drip, drop by drop, running down the bed, flowing off the veranda, and falling onto Pratapaditya Road below, its stream searching for the Ganga—O purifier Ganga, take my blood-stream to the ocean—let all the blood leave my body—new blood will come, a new life will begin, I will awaken in the morning to a new life.

When I woke from sleep, I saw it was my husband who had woken me—he said,
“You said you wanted to get up at five—it’s five o’clock now—” I looked at him in surprise, and it seemed to me—this man was Binid. I asked, “Did you not sleep all night?”
He smiled gently, “No, you asked me to wake you at five, so I stayed awake, lest I fall asleep.”
This is my husband. After that, for a long time, we have walked together, and he has always been this vigilant towards me.

In the morning, Baba gave me a letter. This letter was written to me by my husband, but out of excessive politeness, instead of sending it directly to me, he wrote my name on an inner envelope and sent it inside another envelope addressed to my father. The letter was written in English. Without translating, I will quote the English as it is. The letter arrived two days ago, but I received it today. The writer’s intention was, since we had not met, to give some sense of his personality through the letter. The letter is as follows—

Mademoiselle,
Understanding that you are going to choose a partner in life I beg to offer myself as a candidate for the vacancy. As regards my qualifications I am neither married nor am I a widower; I am in fact the genuine article—a bachelor what is more I am a real ripe bachelor being one of long standing.
I should in fairness refer also to my disqualifications. I frankly confess that I am quite new to the job and I cannot boast of any previous experience in this line—never having had occasion before to enter into such partnership with anyone. This my want of experience is likely, I am afraid, to be regarded as a handicap and disqualification. May I point out, however, that though want of experience is likely, I am afraid, to be regarded as handicap and a disqualification in other avenues of life, this particular line is the only one where it is desirable in every way. A more likely handicap is the fact

91

--- Page 92 ---
After reading the letter, I was astonished—it was like a burst of sunlight in the midst of a storm.  
Is there anything like laughter? Laughing, I handed the letter to my mother, “Have you all read this letter?”  
“Yes, your father read a little…”  
“Why didn’t you give it to me yesterday?”  
“We thought, you know, who knows what someone might have written—”  
“What do you mean, who knows? The name is written right at the bottom—”  
I thought, if I had received the letter yesterday, I would have had something to say, at least I could have said—“You really know how to make people laugh!” But that didn’t happen. What do these people do!

From this house, I will go to my in-laws’ for the first time, and then I will leave for some remote corner of the Himalayas, to a lonely place whose name no one has ever heard. There’s a huge map hanging on the wall; I’ve searched for the name of that forsaken land, but it’s nowhere to be found. When I left home, Father cried like a child. I, too, was weeping without end. All my grievances against Father have vanished; I realize now that I misunderstood him, whatever he did, he did for my own good. My heart aches for Roma too; she worked so hard for my wedding.

Three or four days after my wedding, the poet returned from Ceylon. We were supposed to go see him, but I refused to go with Father. I gathered my courage and said, “You all go ahead, I’ll go alone later.”

Father said very gravely, “Very well, I’ll go in the morning with my son-in-law, you can go at noon.”

I thought I would go alone by car at noon, but Roma came along with me. I don’t know why she came; I didn’t even feel like talking to her. Inside, I was trembling with a strange emotion, restless and distraught for reasons I couldn’t understand. I hurried up the spiral staircase, my Benarasi sari trailing behind me, and Roma kept picking up the end for me—jewelry on my hands, neck, and ears, vermilion in my hair, anklets on my feet, and my bridal look was complete. He was waiting for me—as soon as he saw me, he stretched out both arms—“Come, Amrita—”

I fell into his lap and began to cry. I heard him say, “Roma, go sit in the next room for a while, I want to speak with her alone.”

Roma left. He gave me a moment, then said, “Sit up.” I sat up. He began to speak—“I don’t like to give advice. I don’t want to burden a wounded heart with the heavy stones of big words. Still, today I must say a few things to you.” After a moment’s silence—“This morning your father brought the boy to see me, what more could I say to him? Only scholars can be so cruel. But just because someone has been cruel to you, does that mean you must also be cruel to yourself? That is of no use.”

--- Page 93 ---
Not words. No one else is in our hands anymore, Amrita; we are only in our own hands now. I do not know what kind of man you have married, whether your circumstances are favorable to you or not, but whatever the situation, whatever fate may bring, you will rise above it. I hope that you will bring happiness to everyone around you, and above all, that you yourself will be happy. If you are not happy, Amrita, if you break down, if you are defeated by adversity, then I will consider it my own defeat. But I know that will not happen. You will surely succeed. If you can build a nest, a home, where everyone in your joyous household is happy, then I promise you, I will come to your house.”

With deep affection, he stroked my head. I bowed down and rested my head on his feet—kissing the soft, smooth soles, I said, “So it shall be, so it shall be, so it shall be.”

When I returned home, Ruma secretly told everything to Father. Who knows what she said? How could she have known unless she eavesdropped? And even if she did, there was nothing said that was blameworthy, nor did I make any complaints! And if tears are complaints, then nothing can be done. That day, Father scolded me so harshly—I had never been scolded so terribly before. He simply would not stop, would not stop at all. Our times were strange; elders believed they could stop tears with stern words, make us laugh with scolding, even show love through reprimand. Anyway, that day I thought, now that I am leaving, I will never return—but that was just wounded pride speaking. From my lonely mountain retreat, I came every two months, spending beyond my means, just to see Father.

People today would not believe how far a scolding could go in those days. For quite some time, Mother would inquire how things were between my husband and me; when she realized nothing much was progressing, she would get very angry.

But there was no cause for worry; affection grew between us.

When I arrived at that lonely mountain home, it was the rainy season—crossing deep, primeval forests, I finally reached the house, and it seemed as if that garden-surrounded home smiled at me. The tranquil, solitary woodland—the distant blue horizon marked by a white line of snow. The house was beautifully arranged, the servants refined. I did not have to come to a bachelor’s untidy dwelling. Though we were better off than them, to tell the truth, I had never lived in such comfort before. But our relatives began to criticize. They said the marriage was not proper, and so on and so forth—especially those my age, who began to melt in sympathy for my supposed misfortune—“So this is how it ends!” I used to get very angry. I had a lifelong quarrel with Gopal over this. One friend, covering her mouth, said, “I hear a virtuous wife, when she hears criticism of her husband, becomes fiercely angry—” Another said, “If you like him, what can we say? But I am amazed at your choice—”

When I argued with them, it was not for show or to save face; I was truly astonished by the men in this house. They did not assert authority; everything was left to the women—the women here were completely independent. In other homes, I saw the best things set aside for the master of the house, but here that would never happen—the masters would raise a commotion if it did. If the wife was cooking, the husband might take a fan and cool her! Truly, I had never seen so many dutiful, generous, and honest people in one house before.

I am not trying to paint a full picture of my married life here. This is not my autobiography—this is only a part of my life, where the protagonist is Mircea. But to complete this story, I must say something about my husband, for without him, I am not myself. For thirty-eight years, we built a life together without flaw, one could say, never—

--- Page 94 ---
There was never any conflict between us. From making every piece of furniture to raising our children, we have always acted together, in agreement, and these tasks have, by and large, been accomplished successfully. Though our children may not be prodigiously talented, as human beings they have managed to belong to the finest class of people. The emptiness with which I began my life has never played its hollow flute within my household. My greatest desire before marriage was independence, and that too I have received in full measure. The moment I set foot in my new home, I realized my shackles had fallen away; I could do whatever I felt was right. Freedom does not mean license, and I have never used my boundless liberty for anything unworthy of me.

A few days after our wedding, I felt I should tell my husband about Mircea. One day, I began, “There was a foreign student in our house. He wanted to marry me, but Father refused, so he went off to the Himalayas!” In a single sentence, I summed up the story. My husband replied, “Oh, is that so?”

And that was the end of it. He had no curiosity. The fewer words spoken, the better; if ever compelled to say a few more, it would feel as if the sky had fallen on his head. If he could end a conversation with a brief, humorous remark, he would be relieved. So, Mircea went to the Himalayas, and with that, my story was over. What more was there? So, nothing more was said.

Much later, I resolved once again, preparing myself—today, I must tell him. Not saying it felt unjust. “Tell me, on our wedding night, when I wrapped myself in a sheet and lay down, what did you feel?” Silence, impenetrable silence.

“Tell me—did you feel bad or not? You must tell me.”
The silence was unbreakable. But I was determined—I would speak today.
“Surely you must have felt something; my behavior was not exactly normal…” After many questions, the answer finally came: “Well, suddenly having to spend the night in the same room with a complete stranger—anyone might feel uncomfortable. It could feel bad.”

“You didn’t feel uncomfortable or bad?”
“Not at all.”
“You felt good?”
“Very much so. I was amazed when I saw you.”
“Then did it never occur to you why I was feeling bad?”
After being subjected to this endless barrage of questions, he finally said, “I remembered my friend Bhupesh—it wasn’t like that with us!”

The story is this: Bhupesh’s marriage was arranged in the usual way, and he had never seen the girl before. On their wedding night, the girl said to Bhupesh, “Look, I am not your wife, and I cannot be. I have already accepted someone else as my husband, and he loves me too, but my father would not agree under any circumstances. He drove him away. Now what am I to do? You shouldn’t have married me either.”

Bhupesh was stunned. The girl’s words were true, logical. Bhupesh took down the boy’s name and address, and then, dressed as a groom, he left the bridal chamber, left the wedding house, and set out into the night in search of the fugitive lover. Their ‘kushandika’ had not yet taken place—only the ‘samprasad’ had been performed, meaning the essential part of the wedding was not yet done. That girl was not Bhupesh’s lawful wife; they had not performed the religious rites together, had not taken the seven steps. Only the girl’s father, along with other elders, had formally handed over the adorned bride to Bhupesh—“Bhubhyam aham sampradade”—the property had merely changed hands! Now this girl was Bhupesh’s property. Taking her address, Bhupesh arrived at the young man’s house: “What kind of man are you, sitting here mourning? Don’t you have any responsibilities?”

--- Page 95 ---
The boy brought Bhupesh back to the wedding house. He had made up his mind about his duty. Waking the girl’s father from sleep, he said, “You have committed a grave injustice. Nevertheless, you have already given your daughter into my hands, so you have no more rights over her—now I am giving her to the man of her choice. They will be married, you have no right to object.”

“And then?”

“Then they got married.”

I thought to myself, goodness, this girl has such strength of mind. “Is there more after that?”

“There is! That boy fell in love with the girl that very night, he seemed to change—became restless—he would visit their house from time to time.” Then one day he died. There was a fire burning in the stove in his room, he fell from the bed and caught fire. Poor thing, he burned to death. Some say he committed suicide.

What a terrible thing, in the outcome of thwarted love there is no beauty—Satyam Shivam Sundaram—there is neither Shiva nor beauty—if beauty is lost from love, what remains? In my mind’s eye I saw a burned man—his mother’s flesh raw and festering, his hair singed, his face blistered—he is groaning! How terrifying! If loving me were to bring someone to such an end, I too could not go on living. I began to feel afraid, if his friend is like this, perhaps he is too. There is no point in saying anything more, I cannot cause him even the slightest pain. If Mircea were to come right now and say, “Come,” could I go? Never, not anymore. I cannot pluck happiness from any heaven by causing pain to this gentle man’s heart. I am old enough now, I know happiness does not reside in any external circumstance or object—only if there is a full arrangement for it within, can it be awakened by outward touch. If the heart is scorched by the fire of conscience, if it is licked by the tongues of slander and harsh words from base people, will I find happiness just by embracing Mircea? The body cannot give happiness, happiness comes from the mind, and my mind cannot be happy by causing pain to another, this much I know.

One cannot be happy by hurting another, one cannot be happy by depriving another—these are simple truths, I have heard them again and again. I did not discover them, everyone knows. Do they? Then why is Roma at this very moment burning my mother in a living fire? Let it be, let everyone do as they wish. I will never hurt this gentle, uncomplaining man. Is this only duty? Never, I love him, I love him deeply. I realized it that day when, around eight at night, the bearer said, “Sahib may lose his way returning from where he has gone. If Sahib had gone by horse, he would not have lost his way, the horse would have brought him home; but…” The rain was falling incessantly—a pitch-dark night, not a sign of life anywhere—they were saying that bears come out on that road. I became so restless that if they had not gone, I would have put on my mackintosh, pulled on my gumboots, taken a lantern and gone out myself—whether there were tigers or bears, is this not love? Then? What else? Is there still something left? Yes, yes, yes—sitting amidst this well-arranged household, sheltered by my husband’s loving care, why does my heart still feel so empty, who can say? “I return empty-handed, O Lord—on every path…”

Our married life has moved along a smooth road, whatever relatives may say, whatever friends may hint, we are quite well. The thing in which my husband and I have found the most harmony is that he too has no prejudices. He is not bound by any ritual, he does not believe in caste, he understands social systems through reason. I was astonished when I learned that he did not eat meat, and on the first day he arrived in the hills, at an Englishman’s house, there was a meal arranged, but the memsahib was terribly stingy. She invited guests and served only a single piece of fried fish, saying,

--- Page 96 ---
One must eat at least one piece of fish. That day, he hadn’t kept any fried fish; instead, there was beefsteak.

“Why did you eat it?”  
“There was nothing else. They would have been unprepared. If one must eat meat, what difference does it make whether it’s goat or cow? In fact, cow is better—by killing one animal, you get a lot of meat. Elephant would be even better.”

An irrefutable logic. In those days, for a Hindu child from a middle-class home, this was a difficult thing to do. But it wasn’t done as if it were some great feat—he was no revolutionary, nor was he, like me, intent on breaking caste barriers. He simply and naturally accepted the truth.

Later, when Rabindranath got to know him well, he said,  
“In my poem ‘Galpaswapner Bhalomanush’ I have left a trace of him—”

Moniram is truly wise,  
He does not let the world’s blows trouble him.  
He prefers to show himself less,  
As if to avoid any confrontation—  
If he has any merit, he does not flaunt it,  
He keeps it covered with a lid,  
Pushing himself into a corner,  
That is where he finds peace in his heart.  
Wherever he is called forward,  
He stays away, does not join the gathering,  
He never says, ‘Give me more, or much more,’  
He does not push to take advantage.  
If he sees there’s a scramble for food,  
He says, ‘Ah, my stomach is full, truly.’  
If there’s no salt in the curry, he’ll eat it still,  
You won’t be able to tell by his face.  
If he hears people talking nonsense,  
He says, ‘Ah, they’re just boys, let them be.’  
If Panchu didn’t take the book,  
He says, ‘Don’t scold him for that!’  
If a friend cheats him, he’ll bear it—  
He’ll say, ‘It was a mistake in calculation, perhaps fate.’  
If someone doesn’t repay a loan,  
He’ll say, ‘He’s not in a hurry, that’s all.’  
No matter how many blows he receives,  
He’ll say, ‘Perhaps the fault was mine!’  

The philosophy of this poem is the very teaching of the Gita—dukkheshu anudvignamanah, sukheshu vigatasprihah—unmoved in happiness and sorrow, unattached, and without ego. All these qualities my husband possesses in full measure—not through effort or discipline, but as his natural, ordained virtues. I, on the other hand, try to cultivate them and fail utterly. I am anxious, impatient, a wellspring of countless faults—just as water is the source of fish, so am I the source of faults, they wriggle within me ceaselessly. Perhaps that is why my heart is almost always in turmoil—like that evening, when I sat thinking, or rather, listening to myself, counting how many words I had spoken all day. I counted—no more than seven or eight. They were like these: Why are you half an hour late today? Is there tennis in the afternoon? The day after tomorrow—

--- Page 97 ---
Will the sisters-in-law come to eat? Can Shamsher, the bearer, manage alone? And what will we talk about? We are people from two different worlds—what he has read, I have not—and that’s all right. I could have studied chemistry if he had taught me; I wouldn’t have objected. I can find joy in any field of knowledge, but he does not listen to such things. Speaking at length is not his way. And what I am studying, he has not; except for textbooks, he has never read a line of poetry in any language. But could I not recite some to him? Is that possible? Poetry cannot be recited to one who does not understand it. Though if I did, he would listen with great patience, never behaving like the gentleman I once heard about from a relative. My poetry-loving, obedient cousin, after her marriage, carefully prepared to recite a poem from ‘Mahua’ to her husband. The husband showed great enthusiasm; before she began, he reached out—“Let me see,” he said, taking the book in hand, glanced at it, and returned it. The girl was surprised—“What did you see?” “I checked how long the poem was.” My husband would never do such a thing. If I were to recite to him the entire collected works in the thickest edition, he would listen with utmost patience and say, “It’s very good!”

Some days—no, almost every day—solitude presses down on me. I do not nap in the afternoons, so what should I read? There are no books here. The few that are here, I have read countless times. How can I buy so many books? Who is sending me books? Still, whenever I go to Kolkata, I manage to gather a few. My husband does not like to talk about this at all, yet even he finds this solitude oppressive. Once he wrote to me, “For fifteen days, I have seen no educated person except in the mirror.”

Evenings feel even more desolate. My husband and I sit together on the veranda—I try to say a few words; but in what language should I speak? Our very languages are different. So, after a while, we both fall silent. In this land devoid of people, sounds seem only to deepen the solitude—a night bird calls from the dark forest, a bat swoops down with a sudden flutter. The cicadas keep up their relentless chirping—ji ji ji—and the nearby stream never stops, its murmur going on and on—these sounds are not companions to humans—they only keep saying, you are alone, you are alone—I realize, every day I realize, my world has vanished! I no longer enjoy writing—what should I write? My writing has lost its way in this deserted world. Many believe that natural beauty inspires writers—that sitting in a flower garden and writing poetry enhances the quality of the poem. I hear that in Santiniketan, guides now point out, “Under that tree the poet wrote ‘Raktakarabi’, beside that grove he wrote ‘Mahua’, and so on—there is no end to such foolishness. In the lonely corners of Santiniketan, the poet called the world to him, for the sake of writing.

The writer’s inspiration springs from contact with people, from being churned daily in the clash of happiness and sorrow, in the struggle between good and bad. A life of undisturbed calm, a forest empty of people, may be necessary for monks and ascetics, but not for me. This nature does not give me life; rather, it takes it away. People need people. At least, I do. Would it have been better if, instead of Mircea, there had been a flowering tree? Ha! Ha! Ha! I remember a saying—“Who was your first love, tell me, tell me—” “A tree, a tree”—let it hang from the tree now!

Of my twenty-two years in the forest, only three are memorable—those three years were when we truly lived. The rest of the days were mere repetition. When that most beautiful chapter of my life was about to begin, as far as I remember, it was 1938 and I had come to Kolkata for a short time. Father said to me, “Euclid has dedicated a book to you, and in the dedication, he has asked your forgiveness.” I was completely unprepared to hear this—my heart began to pound, it would not be restrained—I stood petrified, saying nothing. A few minutes later, he added, “He was imprisoned for writing pornography.”

97

--- Page 98 ---
I had no desire to ask any questions. Anything that, if written, would land one in jail, could hardly be considered true literature. Without giving any reply or asking any questions, I simply walked away. When I looked up that word in the dictionary, I was astonished. What a disgusting thing! The very book that was dedicated to me—could it be like that? I could not understand any of it: why was it necessary to dedicate the book to me, or to ask for forgiveness? I felt no curiosity about the book. Instead, my heart shrank with revulsion, I felt unclean, and the thought that I had once been so close to such a person, that I had been so eager for him, made me shudder. I felt so wretched that I forgot about that book altogether—in other words, I refused to even think of it. From then on, I sealed the entrance to that radiant cave of memory with a stone... To say "I sealed it" would be wrong—it simply happened.

The days drift by, each evening following the same routine as all the rest—some days I think, the way my marriage happened so suddenly, it was an injustice to both of us. Whenever this thought comes to me, I feel ashamed—I ask myself, is this not a kind of disloyalty to my husband? But no, despite his greatness, there is no doubt that we are people of entirely different natures. I do not know whether this difference troubles my husband, for he never expresses any emotion; if he did, perhaps the question of difference would not arise at all. I know how much he loves me, not just I, but everyone who knows him knows it—if I stay in Calcutta for fifteen days, he falls ill with fever. I too cannot stay away long; my heart aches, I worry for him. And yet—yet the emptiness of those days in the hills presses down on me. It is something I cannot speak of. I have such a need to speak. Sometimes I ask myself, was there any other way than to think of this difference between us? If, instead of marrying him so suddenly within four days, I had married that doctor striving for the progress of the Bengali people, what would have happened? Would we have been more alike? That would have been disastrous—a man who does not know people, who does not look at faces, only measures the height of bodies! So the match is never quite right. There is no use regretting it, and yet—yet it will not let me go! If someone were to ask me, what have you not received—your husband’s love, respect in your in-laws’ house, freedom? Which of these have you not had? I would have to admit there is nothing to complain of. If I say, nothing else, but last month’s poem by Rabindranath that appeared in *Prabasi*—I could not read or discuss it with anyone in a whole month—would anyone grieve for me? Of course not. People would laugh. But what many people do not need, I do. There are some people whose offerings are never filled by any material thing. Whose desires never cease. Who wander this world searching for some subtle, indefinable thing that has no tangible value.

Who made our minds like this? Who is responsible for the differences that arise between so many people? Rabindranath Tagore, who else? Those of us who see the world through his songs, and those who do not—their worlds have become separate, there is no doubt. The rhythms of these two worlds do not match.

Though I live in the hills, the current of my life is not like a mountain river—swift and tumultuous, breaking against stones—it has become the still water of a pond. There is no connection with the outside world; I have set my little boat of household afloat and sit here—alone, deeply alone. Sometimes I wonder, the promise he made—that he would come—will he keep it?

I love my household dearly. I love to decorate my home—I could never quite manage to set my mother’s house in order—there was always some quarrel about it—someone wiped their hands on the curtains, scratched the wall, scattered papers from upstairs down to the courtyard. I cleaned the sofa covers, and then some devoted scholar would come and sit on my father’s stool—

--- Page 99 ---
Squatting down, they would leave footprints, and perhaps as soon as tomorrow, some renowned person from Europe might visit. I would exhaust myself working over these things. Here, I have complete freedom. My house is as sparkling as a piece of jewelry. Once a week, the wooden floors are polished with fragrant wax in every room. They shine so much that you can see your reflection in them. The glass window panes are wiped gleaming clean with methylated spirit. Not just the vases and curios, even the door hinges and handles are polished to a shine with Brasso. I paint the flower pots on the veranda. In my kitchen, the utensils are arranged neatly, spotless. Inside every cupboard, on every shelf, everything is in its proper place. The trees and plants in my twenty-bigha garden are tended with meticulous care. The cutlery on the dining table gleams, and the napkins are folded differently each day—flowers are carved into the butter in the butter dish. At lunch, we eat from stone plates; at night, we use the dinner set.

At dawn, the bearer comes and knocks on the door—his head wrapped in a turban, wearing a spotless white chapkan, bringing the tea things on a tray—bed tea. In the morning, coffee is already brewing in the percolator, its aroma filling the entire house—the gardener brings heaps of flowers, and sometimes I myself go out with scissors alongside him, showing him which flowers to cut. These days, I see a lot of Japanese flower arrangements. I don’t like them much; they seem artificial, like a toy. And the women here don’t know how to arrange flowers at all. One glance at the bouquets sold in Bowbazar tells you that—tightly bound with thread and pressed with deodar leaves. How long can a flower survive such torment? Otherwise, they just stick two or four flowers into a vase at random. Here, I have seen an English lady arrange flowers beautifully. Sometimes, a whole branch with flowers is placed in a corner of the room. The vessels for holding flowers should also be such that the leaves and petals can spread freely. I learn by watching, as I saw in the Tagore household. There, even the furniture bore indigenous designs. Here, we get wood of many colors, so we make furniture with various native patterns; our curtains and cushions are not made of English chintz—rather, we weave whole bolts of cloth from the end pieces of the dhotis worn by Oriya priests from the villages of Orissa. At that time, nothing with indigenous designs was easily available in the market. Samples of folk art could be found with villagers from various provinces. When I asked a weaver to weave two bolts of cloth with that same end piece—he was stunned. He simply refused. “What harm will it do you? You’ll get paid!” “I’ve never done such a thing, Ma.” That’s the trouble with people here—they won’t do what they’ve never done before—whether they’re weavers or scholars. Small, immovable islands of habit sit everywhere, filling the mind.

Our house bears the stamp of our personality; people can tell it is ours. I am learning household management from English women too—not from the “mems,” but from one of the four English families here, the only woman who truly runs her household. The others? There’s no point talking about them. Tennis, bridge, drinking, ballroom dancing, and occasional flirtation—that’s all they know. The men are ignorant and hopeless drunkards. Perhaps, hidden somewhere within them, there is a trace of humanity, but I have neither the eyes nor the heart to see it. My mind has become too sensitive—it shrinks in their company. I know this is a flaw.

Of course, this flaw appears differently to them—they are English, we are insignificant natives; we should be grateful that they deign to mix with us, invite us to their parties, come to our house—but no, I object to everything. Will playing cards ruin your character? No, playing cards doesn’t ruin character, but it does waste time. Besides, I don’t find anyone to my liking, there is no exchange of ideas with anyone. On the level where I need companionship, there is a boundless Sahara, and if I mingle too much with them, my personality will change—this thought occurred to me even at that young age. For, seeing their exuberance, I am tempted; it’s not as if it doesn’t attract me at all. But this temptation must be restrained. I know all

--- Page 100 ---
An immense emptiness surrounds me from all sides, so if I do not hold my own reins with a firm grip, I too could become lost. I know my husband is such a person that no one can harm him, but I could be harmed, for there are many weaknesses within me. That is why I have stationed a kind of police within myself, so that not even the slightest slip occurs—like that song of Radha’s, “They keep watch over me, lest I lose my way, they keep watch!”

A conflict has arisen over alcohol—but I will not yield in this matter—for, as I said, “Sangsargaja doshagunāh bhavanti”—one acquires the virtues and vices of those with whom one keeps company. If I allow it, the very melody upon which I have strung the thread of my life will descend.

Nowadays, people might call this narrow-mindedness; even then, they did. But to tell the truth, fighting against drinking was quite exhausting for us—had I not insisted so strongly, my husband, even if he did not drink himself, might have arranged it for them—in fact, he did, meaning he was hesitant. I did not allow that to happen. We do not touch foreign things. Not a single foreign item was bought for my wedding, even the pen was a Guptas’ fountain pen—“And now I must serve them foreign liquor?”

“They’ll think us stingy if we don’t.”

“Why, shall we serve them sandesh?”

“They won’t eat sandesh in the evening”—he laughs, “Do as you wish, this is your household, you will give what you wish, or not.”

My will and opinion were firm, but that day I did not know that I was entering into a small battle, for I had made the Englishmen my adversaries. I did not know that, in time, they would take their revenge. I do not know what the situation is in this age, but then I saw that those who drank were very angry with those who did not—for they felt it was somehow a slight against their character. This attitude is not unnatural. Whatever one does, one cannot give up the desire for others’ approval.

I told the bearer, “Take all the bottles and pour them into the stream.” He stared at me, eyes wide—the sparkling bottles of foreign liquor to be thrown into the waters of the stream! No one could satisfy the Gurkhas’ men and women’s thirst for this substance. The next morning, when the coolie women came to cut grass, they found the bottles—some had not broken, some had sunk into the earth. In those days, the Gurkhas were so honest that even things thrown away would not be taken without permission. You could leave your house open for months, and even if the door was ajar, no one would take your watch. My sorrow is that it took me so long to learn to see these people for who they were. My gaze was turned inward. Like the opposite of that song, “I have opened my eyes to the outside, but not to my own heart, I did not wish to.” In this case, it was the outside that was to be seen. The bearer said, “You have thrown them away, will they take them?” They took them. Then, as bottle after bottle of that pure Scotch whisky, undiluted, entered their stomachs, the result was not hard to guess—they had to be carried to the hospital on stretchers—and my deed became the talk of the English community. In today’s world, this attitude may seem incomprehensible, when substances far more intoxicating than liquor have been imported and are celebrated in cultured society.

At their dinner parties, planters from distant hills would come—I would see, as the night deepened, glass after glass of liquor would make them ecstatic—someone who could not sing would suddenly burst into song in a hoarse voice, someone would start swaying, someone would fall upon the shoulder of any woman sitting nearby, or someone would suddenly sit at my feet and, in a tearful voice, say, “I shall be your dear”—making a pun on the word “dear,” because I had kept a pet deer. I could never understand how people could willingly lose their senses. Watching these grown men, sitting in well-appointed rooms, stuffing themselves with delicacies, deliberately making themselves mad—

--- Page 101 ---
My state of mind in the palace of Dushyanta, seeing the happy people there, was like that of Shakuntala as described by Sharadvat: “Just as a bathed person feels about the oily, the pure about the impure, the wakeful about the sleeping, the free about the bound”—that is how Sharadvat felt!

In the deep of night, my husband and I would return home through the lonely forest. Ahead of us, a bearer walked with a lantern and a stick. Other than the sound of our footsteps, there was no other noise—the silence of the forest, broken only by the soft echo of our steps bouncing off the mountains on either side, seemed to make the silence itself palpable. Above, through the gaps in the treetops, moonlight filtered down, painting the path with a play of light and shadow. I knew exactly what lay at each turn—where the fireflies ruled, where the scent of flowers mingled with the aroma of raw rice.

There were so many snakes here that we had lost all fear of them. They would flee at the sight or sound of humans. Even cobras would slip away, but pythons did not—how could they, when it would take them a year just to move? Of course, unless you fell upon a python’s neck, there was nothing to fear—it would not reach out to grab anyone. Yet, a man died just from seeing a python—he glimpsed the coiled serpent under a tree, and as soon as it raised its head and looked at him, the man, terrified by its gaze, began to run, of course in the opposite direction; otherwise, he would have fallen under its hypnotic spell. The python does not reach out with its hands, but it does with its eyes, drawing you in with its gaze. The man ran uphill for two miles, collapsed near our house, and died—he died of fright alone.

On the paths we usually took, there were no wild animals; they too feared for their lives. But on the forest trails, we often met people who walked eighteen miles from the city to come to the market. Each day, everyone survived by some trick—one day, a man threw down his basket full of food, and as it tumbled and rolled down the hill, the bear left the man and chased after the moving object. The bread-seller escaped. We called him the bread-seller, but he brought not just bread, but all the necessities from eighteen miles away—fish, meat, medicine, vegetables—nothing was available here. If you forgot one thing, you had to wait three days.

The antics of leopards, bears, and pythons here seemed like stories from children’s books. How different it was from the life I had come from! Yet, slowly, I was blending in with them. The primal nature, the primitive man, was gradually entering my soul—very slowly. For we did not know how to enjoy ourselves as the sahibs did. On holidays, they would sling guns over their shoulders, fishing rods in hand, and load their porters with food and drink, heading out for the hunt—there would be drinking, hunting, and flirtations with someone else’s wife or a guest’s daughter. They lacked nothing—even in the jungle, they were at ease.

We, who were scholarly people, could we do such things? We did not enjoy killing innocent deer. The cries or the piteous gaze of a wounded deer would move us to tears. Still, gradually, we began to learn from them. There was much to learn from them. Whatever the situation, they could adapt. Much like the Marwaris—they too could adjust to adverse conditions.

On the slopes of the Himalayas, there are tiny villages, twenty-five or thirty miles away, reachable only on foot or horseback—yet even there, Marwaris set up shops, selling rice and lentils. Because of them, we could get rice, lentils, oil, and spices here.

Except for drinking and deer hunting, I too tried to partake in the joys of forest life. On every holiday, I would gather as many people as I could and set out for a picnic in the woods. We would travel far along forest paths, either to a more secluded waterfall or to the bank of a mountain river. The trees in this forest stood perfectly straight, yearning for light, stretching their long necks over the hills, as if trying to touch the sky. The earth beneath those trees was shaded, filled with the damp scent of moss—under the horses’ hooves, wildflowers and ferns...

--- Page 102 ---
I become distracted, holding the reins absentmindedly. As we walk through the forest, my mind suddenly grows wistful—I recall the story of that exiled king, ‘Alone, traversing the dense, mysterious forest—’ Where am I going, and with whom? These are all shadowy figures—was this the life I had wished for? Oh God, even insects find companions, yet you did not grant me even one? My melancholy lifts when, emerging from the forest, I suddenly catch sight of the mountain river—its waters flowing over beds of stone, gurgling and singing. Sometimes, where the current is blocked by rocks, it forms little pools, and there we leap in to bathe. There is a kind of fragrant leaf called ‘titapapati’—if you crush it on a rock and throw it into the water, the fish become intoxicated after drinking that water—then it is easy to catch them. We build a stove of stones, light a fire with twigs, and fry the fish. Large rocks are scattered throughout the river. The water swirls around them, foaming white. Sitting atop those stones, I feel like reading my poem ‘Sagarika’—‘You sat on the stony shore—’

“Macdonald, do you know there’s an island called Java?”
“Of course I do, they grow tea there—”
“Oh, so that’s what you know. Did you know, long ago, Indian civilization reached there? It went to Siam as well, did you know that?”
“Never heard of such a thing”—Was there even such a thing as Indian civilization before the English arrived?

Enough! What’s the point of talking to them? My husband rarely wishes to speak, and he certainly avoids arguments, but sometimes, out of necessity, he is compelled to say a word or two.

Macdonald says, “You people are educated, you’re very intelligent, so why aren’t you Christians? How will you be saved?”
He replied, “We don’t want to join the minority—”
“What do you mean—?”
“Do you know the population of the world?”
“No, how much?”
“Two thousand million—out of that, how many are Christian, do you know?”
“I don’t know. How many?”
“Perhaps six hundred million. So tell me, isn’t it better to stay with the majority?”

Gradually, a few people start to come, two or four at a time. I keep calling relatives—whenever someone has a holiday, they come. In this remote place, if anyone comes, they must visit our house at my request. Many come for work as well. Guests are happy when they come to my home. Even though the market is eighteen miles away, I never let there be any shortage of food. Preparing delicious meals for guests in my well-decorated house gives me satisfaction. Their beds are covered in Chinese silk, I scent the pillows with lavender—I try to ensure that anyone who has stayed in my home never forgets it. When they wake, they find tea in a dainty pot by the bedside—polished shoes by the door, freshly ironed clothes in the bath. For the first two or three days, everyone is delighted, “How happily you live! It’s just like a first-class hotel.” But within a week, everyone begins to feel the weariness of solitude—then it’s always, “Let’s escape, let’s escape.”

Once, Sir B. L. Mitra came for work—seeing me, the elderly gentleman was utterly astonished—“You live here! Why, this is like Sita in Ashokavana.” But the comparison isn’t quite right—no one has imprisoned me here. There is no Ravana here.

My energy for household work never seems to run out. From the English, I learned how to raise poultry—at that time, a special method of breeding foreign chickens had just begun. I learned to keep bees. Reading about the lives of bees, I memorized their entire life cycle. There are so many strange insects, they—

--- Page 103 ---
They may not be of much use, yet their lives are precious too—each with a strange beauty, their ways so varied. The parts of the mountain thick with deep jungle are always damp and soggy, for sunlight never reaches there. Moss grows on the branches of trees—long strands of moss, as if the trees have let down their tangled hair. Here, some trees even glow with a phosphorescent light. My father once told me to look for the legendary 'divyaushadhi' mentioned in the ancient texts, to see if it truly exists. I have seen this luminous herb. There are also firefly-like insects, some with thirty lights on their backs and sides—ten in each row—an incredible creature. And the butterflies? Large and small, painted in a myriad of colors—some are moths, with vast, terrifying, empty eyes on their gray bodies.

In the flower garden, butterflies flutter, and with them, just as colorful, another little one darts and chases after them, calling out, “Pajjati, Pajjati”—that is my daughter, who came into our home in 1936. She seems to give form to that song—“How joyfully you spread your wings, little firefly, oh firefly—”

Here, my greatest task is to step into the fields and do real farming. For six or seven years, I have cultivated potatoes in Samanog. My harvest was twenty times the seed I planted, and I took pride in this accomplishment. Some may laugh at my so-called decline, but incredible as it may sound, I say that any poet can surely find joy in growing potatoes. A tiny seed is buried in the soil—who knows how it grows? We heap earth at its base, we water it, but when harvest time comes and we clear away the soil, we find that little shriveled seed has given life to plump, juicy potatoes clustered around it—how wondrous it is! Bit by bit, the earth is brushed aside, and one by one, the potatoes tumble out. I spend whole afternoons standing with a basket, gathering potatoes with the gardener. The basket piles high with potatoes, and my heart fills with a strange delight—“I marvel at your wonders”—I feel as if I have painted, like Van Gogh, the “Potato Eaters.” Where is there no wonder, where is there no joy! See that insect, looking exactly like a dahlia leaf, blending into the foliage—until it moves, you would never know it is a creature, not a leaf. At the slightest disturbance, it becomes as still as a leaf, trying to protect itself—isn’t that a marvel? It is hard to believe that an insect’s body can so perfectly mimic a leaf. When the insect dies, it dries up and curls just like a leaf—nature’s mimicry. Once, Jyotiprakash Sarkar brought such an insect in a paper box to show at Bose Institute. Even after it died, it looked like nothing but a dry leaf. Only when ants gathered on it did one realize it was a living creature.

My days pass in this land, where there is so much to see—a riot of color, fragrance, and form, and my eyes are slowly learning to see them all. Yet, my old “but”—my “tabu”—has never left me. Sometimes, in the middle of the night, I rise from my husband’s bed and sit on the veranda, thinking of the people I once knew. Surely, they are content with their lives—why am I not? I have everything, and yet why does my sense of self not fade? It feels as if what I came to do remains undone, what I came to say remains unsaid, what I longed for remains unfulfilled—this vague yearning, this perpetual dissatisfaction has no name, for perhaps it is not a very natural thing—it is as if I create sorrow in the midst of happiness, and yet, that is how I am. I once read a poem by Mayakovsky that seemed to capture my state of mind in these times:

“Listen now!—If there are stars shining in the sky, then surely there is someone who wants to see them, someone who says, let them burn, someone who asks, what is that tiny dot, is it a gem? Struck by the dust storm at noon, kissing the outstretched hand of God, he says, ‘I cannot live without stars.’”

103

--- Page 104 ---
In the cold wind of a dark, lonely night, I come and stand on the veranda—hoping to see a single star. “Among the sky full of stars, where is my star?”

If anyone thinks that the story with which I began this writing is woven with the memories of that sorrow, they would be mistaken—utterly mistaken. This sense of emptiness within me is not for any one person, not just once—this emptiness, I know its meaning—I was searching not for someone else, but for myself. It is the pain of that part of my being which could never find expression. It twisted inside my chest. Another part of me was cheerfully engaged in the world. When evening fell, the servant would close the windows and doors, and light a fire in the fireplace with thick logs—I would sit by the fire, knitting wool, while my husband turned the pages of a book, both of us silent. At those times, I did not think of anything subtle. My heart never longed for anyone. I worried about my husband’s career, the injustice of his superiors, my daughter’s lack of appetite—sometimes I would think about the design of a new piece of furniture.

As I set out to write about my life, I wonder—there is no real narrative in this life—how could there be? Narratives are born from the contact and conflict between people, or from the many events of an active life—what stories could nature offer here, even if the events are grave, they do not make up the story of a life, just as a forest fire burns every year. Many have read the word ‘forest fire’ in poetry, but how many have seen one? In summer, the deep forest’s leaves dry up and become fuel, then suddenly, from the friction of a dry branch, a spark flares up, or if some forest dweller carelessly drops a burning ember, the dry leaves catch fire, and slowly, gradually, the fire spreads, until the whole mountain blazes and can be seen from afar. From tree to tree, from mountain to mountain, the greedy god of fire leaps—there is no escape for the animals, their cries can be heard. Once a forest fire starts, it cannot be put out. The only way is to cut a circle of trees so the fire cannot spread. In this dangerous work, my husband too must go forward, for the advice of a wise man is needed. Here, I often see how far human folly can reach. The earth is being dug up to widen the road, two men are cutting and cutting—above them is a huge rock, and as they dig away the earth below, the rock is loosened and then that massive boulder rolls down upon them, burying them alive. My husband rushed to the scene. I heard that the two men were completely covered, only one hand of one of them was left sticking out. He saw it with his own eyes, and even hearing about it, I could not forget that dead hand for a long time.

I am speaking of a primeval forest—primeval, meaning the first trees that ever grew here are still standing—trees from the age when coal was formed—free-fern, fern trees, but as tall as great date palms! Forests where no human has ever entered, now to be newly cultivated. Riding on elephant-back up those steep hills, we would choose a suitable plot for ourselves, and there the tree-felling would begin, with no machinery, only giant forest trees being cut down with axes. Accidents happen often—the endurance of the Gurkhas is unparalleled—if a village doctor saws through bone without anesthesia, they can remain silent. From the refined environment of Calcutta, I had come here to spend the best years of my life.

In this wilderness, there is no telephone, not even a telegraph office—there is only a village doctor in a ramshackle hut—the telegraph office is seven or eight miles away, down at the foot of the hills. To come to this forsaken land, one must climb six miles of steep mountain by a very narrow path. The car is almost upright, and at some sharp bends, only two or three inches of land remain between it and the precipice.

But I am brave, the courage of inexperience. I am calling the poet to this inaccessible place, even though he has only recently recovered from a terrible bout of erysipelas—upon receiving my letter, his...

--- Page 105 ---
Bona knows that I have kept my promise—now it is his turn to keep his. Receiving a letter from Ma, my excitement grew even more. Ma writes, “We went to see Chandalika. After the dance ended, when we went to pay our respects to the Poet, he said—‘Amrita can no longer see any of this—where have you sent her, Sudha?’” Ma also wrote, “I feel that when he sees us, his heart aches for you.”

Receiving this letter, I wrote to him again, asking him to come. I usually do not write to him often, because I know he cannot help but reply, and amidst all his work, among a thousand gatherings with a thousand people, why should I add to his burdens? But now I write, I write such letters that he feels I am utterly awash in a tide of joy.

From the moment his visit is decided until he actually arrives, the two or three months in between are like waves swelling in that tide. Among the few Bengali families scattered on these hills and the nearby ones, everyone is astonished. Now, many relatives wish to come and stay at my house, but I do not want a crowd at this time.

We are redecorating the house once again—polishing, scrubbing, tending to the flower beds, mowing the lawn, painting the pots, changing the curtains—I have arranged everything—‘I have prepared the golden temple with a lotus seat’—my solitude’s languor has vanished somewhere, the waterfall that used to torment me with its incessant splashing has changed its tune, now it murmurs gently, and the cicadas that used to drill into my head day and night with their shrill, relentless chorus have suddenly become a melodious symphony! The wind that used to sigh through the silver fir leaves, bringing the forest’s long breath, now plays like a golden harp—‘Come, come into my heart, O beloved of my soul.’

From 1938 to 1940, these three years are like light-years—or years of darkness—in my life. I am not writing here about that time—only marking the flow of my inner life. I have understood the relativity of time in many ways during these three years—when he comes, or leaves and comes again, there is no break in this time—it is a seamless period, or, in the midst of my lifelong yearning, an immortal cup held steady, giving my insignificant life immense value.

He enjoys this domesticity. I rise in the last hours of night to prepare—when he sits facing east at dawn for an hour and a half, I sit by his chair—thus my day begins, and ends when I tuck him into bed, cover him with a sheet, and fix the mosquito net. His presence in the house is like an unending royal sacrifice—though this place is so secluded, suddenly at ten or eleven in the morning, a carload of people will arrive, having found their way here—there is no way to send word in advance, for there is no telephone here. Yet I am not defeated by these sudden invasions. I arrange their lunch well enough. Solitude has fled from this hill and village—there is always the music of celebration in the house, not only in my heart but in everyone’s. Even my husband speaks more than before, his witty remarks have increased in number. The air all around seems lighter with happiness—we are floating, a little above the earth. I do all his work with my own hands, from copying his writing to dusting the table, making the bed, polishing and cleaning the silver plates—everything. The servants at their house are astonished by this, for they have never seen such a thing. The room where he writes is next to his bath, and then his bedroom. One day, I was washing his clothes in the bathroom—the garden door was open, and as he was passing by on the garden path, O-Babu suddenly saw me and hurried over—“What is this? Why are you washing clothes? What are all these people here for?” I felt terribly embarrassed, trying to finish the work quickly and quietly.

“Leave it, leave it—what is this? Where have all the others gone?”

--- Page 106 ---
"Please leave, O-Babu, I do this every day, I will do it today as well."
"You wash clothes every day?"
"Yes, does that harm you in any way?"
"If he finds out, he will be very angry."
"Why would he find out?"
"Because I will tell him"—saying this, O-Babu suddenly opened the door towards the study.

Then, looking at the poet, he said, "Look, Amrita Devi is sitting and washing your clothes, not listening to me—"
I was completely unprepared. My ears were ringing, and I stood there like a culprit, leaving the clothes aside. He was leaning back in his chair, reading a book. Lowering the book from his face, he looked at the scene and smiled gently—"You stop, O—you don’t understand any of this! Close the door. You carry on with your work, Amrita. I was just wondering how my clothes have become so white these days."

In truth, the matter was surely the opposite; I could never have washed better than they did, but this was a reward.

The poet had come to this remote village four times in three years—the fifth time, he fell ill in Kalimpong on the way and could not come. The fullness of these three years washed away all my losses and sorrows—within these three years, that self of mine was formed which learned to see itself beyond the boundaries of time.

I have felt the ineffable—"Crossing death, it will go with me, only in joy, only in melody, only in feeling." At that time, I could not truly grasp the greatness of this gift. How easy it sounds to come to this difficult place, crossing that dangerous path! Even among my closest relatives, those much younger than him would not have done it. But the ever-youthful poet paid no heed to external obstacles. And what delighted me even more was what O-Babu often reminded me—that there was one place where he had not gone twice before.

In this lonely forest, with the world poet, the world itself arrived; I found companionship with humanity. I heard stories of distant lands. Bergson, whose philosophy I had read, whom I never imagined as a living person—I heard about him. Bernard Shaw, Romain Rolland, Bertrand Russell—the names on the spines of books began to speak in many ways. I saw visions of unknown worlds. Most of all, I heard about Russia. He had endless interest in the new experiments happening there. I saw the large-format Moscow News for the first time—he had me translate three essays, which were published in various journals. I was becoming fulfilled in every way—he made me human—he said, "Read the history of mankind." For six years, I read the Mahabharata, two years the Rigveda, and five years "Historians' History of the World"—all at his instruction!

He liked our simple household, complete in every way—he called my husband a perfect gentleman. The beautiful nature outside and the companionship of people together made for a sense of completeness. Even my husband now spoke much more than before. One day, I asked him—"Don’t you feel jealous that I am always so absorbed in him? Why are you laughing? Tell me, tell me."

"Why should I be jealous? You are who you are, why would I want to change you?"

"Still, you should feel jealous."

"Jealous of whom? If you were Mirabai, would I be jealous?"

One day, the poet was sitting in the study, on the chair beside the writing table—I had just bathed and came to stand behind him—the sunlight was dazzling all around that day—a pond for fish shimmered in the distance—

--- Page 107 ---
I arranged them along the edge, and tiger lilies have bloomed—he is looking at the orderly rows of long, colorful lines and says, “How beautifully you’ve arranged these flowers—who is this foreign lady?”

“That’s just wild lily. There are no flowers now—the real profusion comes in winter.”

“You know, Amrita, you two have made a beautiful home—I always knew you could do it. When it was first decided that I’d come here, everyone said, ‘Who knows what kind of arrangements there will be, you’ll have a hard time.’ But I thought, she knows what I need, surely there won’t be any trouble, otherwise why would she invite me? Your home is lovely, flowing along like that stream, singing as it goes—the thing I like most, you know, is that you two never quarrel. Look at ‘P’ and ‘R’—they can quarrel endlessly, never want to stop, never want to stop. In the end, ‘P’ falls silent, but ‘R’ never stops, keeps coming back like a snake.”

“Don’t you ever say anything?”

“I say so much, but it makes no difference! Creating a beautiful home is also a work of creation, a kind of poetry. You two are happy with this child, like a flower—that makes me very happy, Amrita, that’s why I keep coming back—”

Standing behind the chair, my body and mind are motionless—I must speak the truth, I must say it now—will I build my whole life on a lie?

“It’s not as you say, I’m deceiving you.”

“What are you saying?” He reached back, pulled me forward—“What is it, daughter? Whom are you deceiving?”

“Everyone. I haven’t done anything, it’s all on the surface. Half of it is fake—there’s no companion for my lonely nights.” Now I am ready, completely ready—if he reproaches me, perhaps it will be good.

“Why are you torturing yourself needlessly? I’ve told you before and I’ll say it again—what people can give each other is limited by country, time, circumstance—you can’t give or receive everything, so what’s the point of lamenting over what you didn’t get? Isn’t what you have enough? Pointing to the row of flowers, he said—‘This, what you have done, for this I am grateful, madam, grateful to you—’”

Despite these words, I knew how much of this deep solitude belonged to him—he understood. That’s why, every time he left, he had to come down with us. The thought of us sitting in an empty house while they all left together—his compassionate heart could never bear it.

A few days before leaving, he would start saying, “It would have been better not to come at all—just causing you trouble—I won’t come again!”

Once, before he left, we were packing, I was putting books into the suitcase when suddenly I felt terribly sad. He was leaning back in his chair, reading a book. I went and sat at his feet, laid my head on his lap, and began to cry. He was holding the book in his right hand; he shifted it to his left and placed his right hand on my head, saying, “Don’t be afraid, don’t be afraid—”

Just then, Su-babu entered the room—seeing me crying like that, he was taken aback, and I could tell the poet was embarrassed too—his fingers, which had been moving through my hair, stopped. He didn’t like to show emotion openly. It was only a moment, then he said very naturally, “Su, what are you doing here as a witness? Go call Milu instead”—Milu came and grabbed a fistful of my hair, pulling me up, “What madness is this? If you keep bothering him like this, he’ll never come again”—the poet said, “You’ve got it all wrong, Ram, did you think I called you to save me from Amrita? I called you to save me from Su!”

That evening, he told me something—a precious gem of a thought, which I have kept in my heart ever since. Evening had fallen, the tall trees were soaking up the darkness, turning black and blacker—

--- Page 108 ---
A tall figure stood like a specter. The next day, they would leave—I sat silently at his feet. Not everyone had returned yet, so the silence around us was even deeper. Suddenly, the poet said, “Let me tell you something, Amrita. If it brings you any comfort—though I have received much in life, the offering of reverence, devotion, and love that you have given me, I have rarely found such a thing. Before parting, I too needed this—otherwise, I would not have come back again and again.”

He knew that my energy for work was trapped in the caverns of a mountain—that was my greatest loss. In any case, at that time in our country, the scope for women’s work was narrow—and in a place like this, it was almost nonexistent—he too was searching for a path for my work.

From the writing room, one could see a mountain path, along which groups of laborers passed with their packhorses. One day, gazing in that direction, he asked, “How many hill people live here?”

“Perhaps four or five thousand.”

“Then why are you alone? Are they not people too? Invite them into your home, go to them—people have so many problems, they need friends, why don’t you become their friend?”

That is why, on his birthday, we organized a festival with the hill people—for the first time, perhaps not only in the history of this district, but in any government official’s house, laborers were invited as guests. Just as in the tea gardens, there was a rule here too: no laborer could enter an officer’s house wearing shoes. If a laborer was riding a horse and met an officer, he had to dismount immediately. Once, because one did not dismount, Richards had beaten him with a stick. In this society, I found a new task, a signpost to a new path. Gradually, the doors of our home opened toward them.

During the famine of 1942, I saw the true face of the English—their opposition to us was always there, but now, by conspiracy, they incited the Gurkhas against the few Bengali families here, and against all the Bengalis in the district. I realized then how skilled the English were at this. They had already, by inciting Hindu-Muslim riots, dug deep pits of division and wounded the very soul of this vast country; now their new reason for anger was that there was a Bengali ministry—Indianization was underway, and as Bengalis surpassed them in learning, they were rising above the English, gaining high positions. So, once again, they sharpened the same old weapon. They incited the laborers, saying, “There was war before too, but did rice ever cost so much then? Now that Bengalis rule, look at this plight. And after we—the English—leave, what will become of you Gurkhas? No one will get jobs, the Bengalis will flood the hills.” So, beat the Bengalis—after all, they are the ones driving out the English, so finish them off now.

Yet we sensed none of this! We were simple, good-natured scholars, knowing nothing of politics.

At dawn, my husband would leave for meetings—to give explanations for why rice was unavailable. When they brought him back at noon, his whole body was bathed in blood—yet he was smiling—“There’s nothing to be afraid of—it’s nothing serious—”

That was when I saw clearly how the conspiracy was carried out in collusion with the police—otherwise, such widespread riots could never happen. It was rumored that all the Bengalis would be burned alive that night; with two children, I waited for it, yet the sahib S.P. came, spoke only with the sahibs, and left. He would not stay—his wife, it seemed, was waiting for him at dinner. She could not eat unless her husband was by her side. It was then I understood: those who riot, who kill with weapons in hand—

--- Page 109 ---
It can hardly be called their fault—because they feel something must be done, and for that, they themselves are facing danger. On the other hand, those who are scheming, without taking any responsibility themselves, are misleading others and hoping to reap the benefits. Ever since then, I have always seen that those who are adept at stirring up cruelty in people are the very ones who make grand speeches and come to meetings all dressed up... In truth, this is not the monopoly of the English. Whoever finds an advantage will do the same.

After the riots, when he recovered, I began to think of the poet’s words—‘Look at humanity.’ I thought, such a great calamity has occurred, and none but ourselves are to blame; if so much discontent had accumulated right beside us and we failed to notice, then the blindness is ours alone. I then looked at them closely, and only then did I realize that there was a way out of the loneliness that had tormented me for so long—I simply had not recognized that path!

I became withdrawn, sitting with my books—how could I fill this emptiness?

There, except for the year of famine, the hill people’s homes never lacked food, but people have needs beyond food, and it was for those needs that I began to make arrangements—the doors of our home began to open wider. My next fourteen years became filled with laborers and workers at our festivals and ceremonies.

One of the higher-ups said to my husband, “Have you noticed that your wife is mixing a lot with the laborers?”

“Yes.”

“If this continues, how will discipline be maintained?”

“I used to notice a lack of discipline before—now I do not.”

“Will you forbid your wife?”

“No.”

“Then shall I speak to her?”

“No, no, that might break the discipline.”

I saw that even among completely ignorant and uneducated people, there are those who can subtly understand, whose minds yearn for depth, whose voices carry songs, whose hearts hold poetry. When I saw the sparks of fire glowing beneath the ashes, my heart filled with joy. An artifact made by the hands of one of them still brings joy to many in my home. Companions can be found everywhere; it is people who are the companions of people. I have seen civilized and uncivilized people of many races and classes in this world, but the honesty, simplicity, and loyalty I saw among the Gurkha people of that time, I have not seen elsewhere. When I went to live in that remote, lonely forest, I carried with me a great emptiness, but when I returned, not a trace of it remained—I came back with a life that was full. It was filled with affection, friendship, sweetness.

Those who do not shed a tear when their hands are cut without anesthesia, when we left, their eyes brimmed with tears, and that baptism of tears at the end of the best time of our lives filled it with glory.

In 1953, my daughter was married. Meanwhile, ten years earlier, in 1942, my mother’s household had completely fallen apart—the seeds of that destruction had been sown back in 1931, and over ten years, little by little, it had come to pass. Now, a family was utterly destroyed. My mother, with four children, had reached a state of utter destitution—there was only one house left. When even that was about to be lost, I took on the form of a lioness to protect it. Again, waves of criticism rose, this time in a different way—those who had heated up our household with their criticism of my father now began to say, in another tone, that the wives and daughters of such a learned man understood nothing but money, so of course he needed a suitable companion. That innocent children were deprived of their father’s property, that not even arrangements were made for their survival—none of this concerned the critics.

--- Page 110 ---
No goal. Only those who have faced various trials in life truly know that sometimes, what an event appears to be on the surface is not its true form. The same love that shows a person visions of paradise, that fills the air around them with fragrance, also has another aspect—one that can shatter a person, utterly destroy them. Outwardly, both faces seem the same, like twin brothers; but one gives life, the other takes it away.

My devoted mother is struggling to keep afloat with her young children—there is, of course, mental anguish, but the financial hardship is no less. Other than a house, we have nothing; and you cannot eat bricks from a house. The renowned lawyer of Calcutta, our dear friend Mr. Gupta, summoned me, “Your mother was the assignee of the insurance—unless she wrote it herself, no one could have reclaimed it. Why did she give it up?” I felt restless with anger toward my mother.

“That’s just how Ma is. I didn’t know—otherwise, I would never have let it happen.”

“Listen, Amrita, make sure your mother doesn’t sign any papers regarding the house, not even a blank sheet—actually, she shouldn’t sign anything at all.”

“I will follow your advice to the letter.”

“Then let me tell you something else: don’t let go of a single penny of your property—hold on to whatever you can, but let people go.”

“Let people go, let people go”—the words kept growing in my mind, as if a dark, dense cloud that had covered the sky suddenly burst open, breaking into small, white tufts like cotton, through which the blue appeared. It was as if I had been trapped in a closed room, and now I breathed in the open air. Of course—who can hold on to people? ‘Who can hold on to life?’ All this time, the council of relatives advising us had driven us nearly mad. The little boat of our broken family kept getting battered, springing leaks at every rock, following first one person’s advice, then another’s. In such disaster, can just anyone show the way? I listened to the words of a wise man.

I came to Ma and said, “Ma, at last I’ve found the way.” Ma was astonished—her eyes fixed after hearing Mr. Gupta’s words—“This is the advice? And you’ve come to repeat it a hundred times? Shame, shame, shame! I should let go of my husband and be content with his money? Are these people educated? My husband would leap into fire, and I should hold him back, or tend to the safe!” Ma was crying, but I felt no pity—‘would leap into fire’—still future tense—“So what will you do, can you stop him?”

“Look, Ru, I know the weaknesses of my immensely gifted husband—this isn’t the first time, I’ve saved him many times before; otherwise, he could never have become so great. I’ll do it again. If you don’t like what I say, then leave me alone.”

The one who could have been the possessor of immense fame and honor, who instead brought shame and sorrow upon himself and his entire family, and after ten years, died completely friendless—at the end, I’ve heard, he was desperate to return to Ma, saying again and again, “I must go to Calcutta, I have to ask for forgiveness—” But the news reached Ma too late.

This is the ultimate tragedy in the lives of my endlessly virtuous mother and my extraordinarily powerful father.

Through all these events, my small life has sometimes drifted with the current, sometimes been dashed against the rocks. I am no longer the little girl I once was—I have come to know the realities of family life well. My heart has grown calloused. The delicate skin of subtle feeling has been rubbed rough. I am a practical person now. Utterly domestic. Of course, it’s true—my energy for household work never seems to run out. Even after returning to Calcutta, I feel a sense of melancholy. Old, unfulfilled desires return to me—so does the world of literature...

--- Page 111 ---
I have gone far away from there; now it is so crowded here, so much jostling. I hardly get a chance to write.  
My life as a writer has lost its way, so I pick up whatever work comes before me. Just as I have had to endure criticism and insult, I have also received much honor and respect. Altogether, my life is not playing some discordant, jarring tune anymore.

In 1953, I left my children in Kolkata and went traveling in Europe with my husband. In all those fifteen long years before, Mircea had not once crossed my mind. Or rather, not in a way that was worth remembering. Perhaps sometimes I thought, if such an unpleasant incident had not happened, my life would have remained pure. I consoled myself—could the guilt of such a trivial childhood event still touch me today?

In Europe, I met Hiranyan. He was my father's student, now living abroad after marrying there. I saw him at a gathering and at first did not recognize him at all—he had become a complete sahib. Suddenly, he asked me, "Do you remember your Euclid?" I wondered what could be the meaning of such a strange question. Seeing me silent, he said himself, "He has dedicated a book to you."  
"I’m glad to hear that. How many children do you have?"  
With that, the conversation ended. It left not the slightest mark on my mind. We were whirling through Europe like a spinning top. In Paris, we met a professor and his wife—his name was Nicolai Stanenku. Where or how we had met them, I can no longer recall—I only see a large drawing room, sparsely furnished. The professor and his wife had invited us for tea; they were exiles from their homeland. They were from Mircea’s country.
