Paris was filled with refugees from their land—they spoke of their humiliation. The sorrows of exile are many. I listened to tales of human suffering in war-crazed Europe.  
As I listened, I wondered what had become of Mircea. Should I ask? Then again, I thought, what does it matter to me, whether he lives or dies. Yet, I do not wish for his death; somewhere in my heart, I still hope that in this vast world, someday, somewhere, I will meet him again—and on that day, I will be able to ask him why he deceived me so.

Part of me still doubts this whole matter, and asks: was it really deception? What could he have done, when Father drove him away? Could that tormented face as he left have been a lie? But from the other, darker side of my mind, where distrust has taken root, the answer comes: these Europeans are cunning hunters, it is their way. Could not a letter have reached me somehow? If it had, I would have done whatever was necessary.  
Then another answer comes: if it was all a deception, why did he wander through mountains and forests, why endure such hardship? My mind has been divided like this all my life, constantly questioning and answering itself. To trust brings peace; to distrust, pain. At this moment, I do not wish to fall into that pain again.  
Stuffing a sandwich into my mouth, I asked Madame Stanenku quite casually, "Do you know a writer named Euclid?"  
"Of course I do. He has written many books on Indian philosophy. He is an expert, a specialist on Indian civilization."  
"Is that so? Do you have any of his books?"  
"Certainly," she said, and brought me a large volume on yoga. I took the book in my hands—opened it—and was stunned. The book was dedicated to my father. It read: "To my most revered Guru."  
So? After all that had happened, did he still have a connection with Father? Now I see it clearly, he was a thorough careerist. Now I understood why he never wrote to me; writing that book was more important, it was necessary to become an expert on India. Fame, reputation, the pursuit of knowledge! I despised that scholarship, that hunger for glory, for renown. Inside, I...

--- Page 112 ---
After so many years, it has begun to burn again. My lips are trembling. I am biting down so hard that blood is drawn—  
my mouth has become a symbol of desire. Suddenly, I notice Mrs. Stanescu staring at me intently—her eyes full of curiosity. She asks, “Is your name Amrita?”  
“Yes! Why?”  
“Oh, I know you: aren’t you Euclid’s first frame?”  
The lady is smiling. I am burning with anger. Inwardly, I mutter, ‘Frame or ash, what does it matter?’  
Out of the corner of my eye, I see my husband listening to the professor’s stories. Who knows if he can hear what’s being said on this side! I did not ask any more questions about him. Enough—there is no need for any more news about that man, not even to recall his name. After twenty years, I learned of him again, and that very news crushed my heart.

We were traveling in Italy—Rome, Florence, Venice—and from there, we came to the south of France, to the Riviera. An exquisite land, my husband and I were happily exploring the country. No worries of household affairs, no anxieties of the workplace—only, from time to time, my heart ached for our little son. Otherwise, such a carefree, comfortable journey had been rare in my life.

Returning to Paris, we checked into a hotel near the Seine. After two days, when it was time to pay the bill, we were stunned! We could not afford such expenses. Hearing of our predicament, Satyenbabu directed us to a hotel in the Latin Quarter—where professors and students stayed, and the costs were much lower.

We stayed in this hotel for ten days. In the room next to ours lived a man, who would nod in greeting as we passed on the stairs, speaking a little English. I assumed he was French. In those ten days, apart from exchanging pleasantries, we had no other conversation. Had we spoken, who knows, perhaps this story would have been written differently.

On the day we were to leave Paris for England, I was sitting under the stairs in front of the reception—the area with tables and couches arranged for those waiting to depart. Our luggage was piled together. My husband was carrying them down one by one and stood at the clerk’s desk to settle the bill. At that moment, the man from next door came down, wandered about, and stopped by our suitcases—my name was written on mine—I saw him read the name and look at me. Hesitating a little, he approached me and said, “My name is Ion Popescu. Is your name Amrita?” I was startled, my heart pounding inside my chest—“Why do you ask?”

“I pay you my homage,” he said in his crooked accent, and kissed the tips of my fingers. Now there was no doubt about which country he was from.

“Oh, I see”—in an instant, everything became muddled—“Well, can you tell me where that writer lives?”

“Right here in Paris, just two blocks from here.”

“Only two blocks away, that way?”

“Yes,” he replied, suppressing a smile. Oh God. I could go with him right now and confront him. I could say: Deceiver, you have bought knowledge at the price of falsehood; you may become a scholar, but you will never be wise. Darvi suparsan ib—as a wooden spoon, even when dipped in the best soup, never tastes it, so too, one who has no truth in their life may know much, but will never be wise. Mircea, there is no truth in your life—you survived, you did not have to suffer. But why does my truth refuse to die?

The man is looking at me—“Will you come with me?”

“No. If I write a letter, will you deliver it?”

“Of course, of course,” he said, and ran to fetch paper and an envelope.

112

--- Page 113 ---
What I wrote, in brief, was more or less this—“Mircea, after so many days I have heard news of you. How are you? Are you married? I have come to Europe with my husband for a visit. I have a daughter and a son. I would be very happy if I could see you just once. We are leaving soon, will be crossing the Dover Channel shortly. I am giving you my address in London. If you receive this letter and can come even once, I would be so glad. Will you come?”

My husband came forward to ask me something, so I handed him the letter. “You remember, I told you about that foreign boy—that gentleman is here—I have invited him to London.” He glanced over the letter and handed it back to me.

“It’s all right, isn’t it?”

“Of course. It would be very nice if he comes.”

He went away again. That man was standing behind me. With gentle affection, he placed his hand on my shoulder. “Write a good letter, madam, you are trembling all over.”

“I am not trembling, I am shivering. It is a cold day today…” As I was putting the letter into the envelope, he said again, “Why don’t you write a proper letter? I don’t understand; does your letter need to be censored?”

“No, no, not for that reason—I let my husband see the English. My appropriate prepositions are never quite right.”

“Oh, is that so?” He winked, “Don’t do anything that isn’t appropriate.”

We went to London. He never replied to the letter. I waited for seven or eight days. For seven or eight days, tears fell from my eyes. My husband thought I was missing our son. Then I forgot about it.

In March of 1955, we returned to Calcutta. When I had left Calcutta twenty or twenty-two years before, the intellectual society here had a different character. There weren’t so many crowds. Talented people had the opportunity to display their gifts; writers didn’t have to take jobs in magazine offices just to get their work published. Women writers didn’t have to run to magazine offices to chat with editors. In art, literature, music—everywhere there were fewer crowds, so the path was straightforward. In these twenty years, everything has changed. Now, under the onslaught of egalitarianism, everything is valued the same, like puffed rice and sugar candy. Like floodwaters, people have poured into the fields of education, literature, politics—people who no longer respect learning for its own sake, or art for art’s sake, who have no inner resonance for poetry, yet still write poems. In this world, I feel isolated, because I am a stranger. Yet people know me, recognize me, I am busy with one thing or another, my working life is expanding despite obstacles.

In 1956, I went to Europe again—this time, for the last time, I heard news of him in Europe. In a large auditorium, as I was stepping down from the rostrum, a young girl with a beautiful face framed by curly hair came up to me, “Are you Amrita?”

“You pronounced my name quite well.”

“Yes, I know you.”

“Oh, I see. Do you know any writer named Mircea Euclid? Can you tell me where he is?”

“No, I don’t know anyone by that name.” Then, after a pause, she said, “Oh yes, he’s dead.”

Sometimes the dead give more comfort than the living, but my heart was pounding inside my chest. Dead! Truly gone? After death, nothing remains, and I never even got to ask, why did you betray me? I had always thought that one day, somehow, I would find him.

Whenever I think of him, my worldly mind—the mind that reasons, that deals with the problems of life, that alert, self-interested mind—says, it was not betrayal…

--- Page 114 ---
So what? If a dog tucks its tail between its legs and slinks away when hit by a stone, does that mean it has lost its humanity just because it fled after a single scolding from Father? I have no reverence for his scholarship, for his books—man is greater than any book. Then, a little deeper within me, another voice says, “Why are you angry? Perhaps he wrote to you and you never received it, or maybe your letter never reached his hands.” And even deeper, in the innermost cave of my heart, there is another self that logic cannot touch. It says, “Can everything be understood through events alone? Can truth be found through argument? Listen, without judgment, to the echoes of truth resounding in this kingdom of the heart—it has never deceived you.” Vera is looking at me—

I asked, “How did he die? He wasn’t old enough to die.”

Vera replied, “No, not exactly dead—he’s dead to us. He’s become a fascist.”

He’s become a fascist! I feel like laughing. I can almost see him now, sporting a Hitler-style mustache, standing at the door of a glass chamber. It suits him well. By not replying to my letters, he’s sent me there again and again. Fascist, indeed.

I haven’t heard his name in many years. In 1958, I was working at a university library outside of Calcutta. I was studying for a book I was writing. I had found good work and was content. Whenever I am idle, my mind breaks down—a vast, sky-wide wail seems to descend upon me. So I throw myself into whatever work I can find. Writing is the noblest of all. My mother and brother lived two miles away; I was staying there for a few days.

One day, as I was writing intently, Satyen Babu entered and leaned back against a cushion. We spoke about having met in Paris, and then suddenly he said, “I’ve read the book written by Euclid.” I remained silent. Those who have seen a grasshopper will understand what happens to me when I hear his name. The little insect hops around, but if it’s touched even slightly, or if the leaf it’s on trembles, it immediately stiffens and falls like a dry twig. That’s what happens to me. At the mere mention of him, my flesh-and-blood body suddenly turns into just such a wooden puppet. Satyen Babu continued, “After reading the book, I understood—you loved Rabindranath Tagore.” I listened in silence; that was just a comment. Then he asked, “Then why were you angry with your father?” I closed my book and stood up. I had always respected this man deeply, but now I was astonished—

“Look, Satyen Babu, you are a very learned man. Perhaps that’s why you enjoy catching people off guard.”

Without staying there a moment longer, I returned home. Riding the cycle rickshaw for two miles, dusk was falling, the pale darkness slowly deepening. I tried to think of the past, but nothing came to mind. It felt as if evening had descended over my entire past life. I thought, I have reached the end of this road—why look back? For this world, for my siblings, I have done all I could, to the best of my ability and wisdom. Now let people say what they will—may the road ahead be wide for me. I will do much work, I will fight against injustice and wrongs—but where is the opportunity? Everywhere there are obstacles. There is so much work to do, but the field of action is full of thorns. Still, may the incompleteness of the past not wound me.

When I returned home, I saw my mother standing by the door, waiting for me. At the sight of her, anger flared up in my head—this woman is the cause of all my suffering. She ruined my life, because ‘she’ cannot bear pain. Whatever ‘she’ wants must be done, the child must be given sweets. Other people’s joys and sorrows mean nothing. And yet, even then, she could not manage it. For ten years, little by little—

--- Page 115 ---
She broke up the household, and here she is, thinking that by covering the fire with her sari’s end, everything will be all right. She ruined her own life, my father’s, everyone’s—out of sheer willfulness. Those who cannot act wisely in the world, who are not practical—their goodness is not worthy of respect.

Now again, she boasts that she arranged a good marriage for me. What a good marriage it was. If there is any credit for the outcome of this marriage, whoever else may claim it, Ma certainly cannot. Yet Ma keeps insisting. Knowing everything, she sent me into exile, marrying me off at a young age to a man fourteen years my senior, a silent stranger, just to please her husband, and crushed my entire being under a heavy stone. And yet she says, “My son-in-law is a great yogi, Mahadev, how many girls are as fortunate as you to have such a husband—and then she goes on and on.” Perhaps he is a yogi. Why perhaps—certainly, the qualities others must strive to attain came to him effortlessly. But I never asked for a devotee of the Gita; I wanted a flesh-and-blood human being, a man.

All these thoughts are boiling inside me. Ma says, “Why are you so late?” I flare up. Poor thing, on the verge of tears, she says, “Why do you get so angry over words? All your anger is always directed at me.”

At night, we are lying together in one room, wintertime, wrapped in blankets inside the mosquito net. It’s late, but both of us are awake. After my scolding, Ma’s heart must be heavy. Suddenly Ma says, “Ruu, are you crying?” I remain silent. Ma lifts the net and comes out: “Ruu, what’s wrong, dear, why are you crying? Did I say something to make you cry?”

“Satyen Babu insulted me.”

“Oh dear, what’s this, is it senility, what did that old man do to insult you?”

“No, no, not like that. He said that by reading Euclid’s book, he realized I loved Rabindranath Tagore.”

“Listen to that! The more they study, the more foolish they become! Did he need to read Euclid to find that out?”

Ma comforted me, stroking my back. She added, “Then why were you angry with your father?” Ma was startled. “What are you saying? Can it really be that the more one studies, the more foolish one becomes! Your grandmother used to call your father ‘the bookish goat’—I see it’s true. Are these two things the same? Is all love the same? Did you ruin anyone’s home, seize anyone’s property, deprive a family, bring shame upon anyone in society? The love that is not of this world, it only uplifts and purifies a person! If it weren’t so, could you have remained a queen in your own home? Oh God, do these people have no sense?”

Ma stroked my back, “Come now, why cry over such nonsense? You know yourself what’s what!”

“Ma, I’m not crying for that—no matter what anyone says about the poet, I feel no shame at all. Can anyone insult me by speaking of him? He is beyond slander and shame. I only told you.”

“Then why are you crying?”

And then, for the first time in twenty-eight long years, I uttered his name in front of Ma. Lifting my face from the pillow, looking upward, I cried out—‘Mircea, Mircea, Mircea’—and then I just kept on crying. Ma was stunned, “What did you say, what did you say?” Perhaps it took her a moment to recall his nickname. In this house, everyone referred to him as Euclid. Ma placed her hand on my back, “Ruu, did you meet him? You’ve been abroad so many times, surely you must have met?”

--- Page 116 ---
“No, Ma, no. That day when he left, you made me stand on the veranda, and after that, I never saw him again!”

“Then you must have written letters, kept in touch—tell me the truth?”

“No, no, no. I wrote a letter, but he never replied.”

Mother sighed deeply. “It was all because of your father. A Frenchman, bringing him into the house, and then again—”

I feel like laughing—Mother simply cannot let go of the idea that he was a Frenchman!

“When did you write the letter, Ru?”

“In 1953, from Paris.”

“Then why didn’t he reply?”

“Would it have been better if he had replied? Would you have minded, Ma? Wouldn’t that have been wrong?”

Mother sighed again—“Now, nothing is wrong anymore, Ru—but perhaps it was for the best that you didn’t write. Even though he was French, he was a very sensible boy. Maybe he didn’t write back for fear it would harm your family. Ah, all this pain you suffered was only because of your father. And—who knows how fate works, we drove that boy away and from the very next year my own family began to fall apart!”

Seven years after this, that is, in 1965, worn down by illness and broken in spirit, Mother died, her being utterly shattered.

## Na Hanyate 11 Third Part

Another eleven years have passed. I have traveled to Europe twice, to various countries. Never again did I hear his name. I didn’t even remember him anymore. These eleven years, I took on certain responsibilities. My time is no longer my own, my age has reached the threshold of old age, my body too is growing frail. With my children and grandchildren, I have a happy family, but besides this, I have built another large family—of friends and colleagues. Among all of them, I have managed to establish a certain image of myself. As far as I believe, it is something like this—I am a very strict person, especially regarding unsanctioned love affairs, I am known to be merciless. So, if any such incident happens among my friends, it is carefully hidden from me. My views on right and wrong are inflexible, I speak harshly if there is even a slight deviation from propriety. And I am excessively practical. I have surely created an image of myself even in my own mind—that is, I do not compromise with wrongdoing. Any kind of excess, from drinking to anything else, is poison to my eyes. Every person has a responsibility to society; one cannot neglect that responsibility for personal indulgence or for the sake of literature. If anyone does, I criticize them. In short, all the virtues and vices of a minor leader are visible in me. Some may dismiss my altruistic zeal as idle busybodying, but I have received ample praise and help as well. On the whole, the circles of both my small and large families are complete. I feel no lack, no incompleteness.

At such a time, in 1971, I heard his name again. In a drawing room, in the midst of light conversation among colleagues and friends, Parbati suddenly said, “In such-and-such city, Amrita-di has an ‘Armayara’.”

Everyone was excited, “Really? Is that so?” I was not surprised. I have received enough of both criticism and praise over the years. So, it is not impossible that someone should have an ‘Armayara’.

“What is his name?”

“Mircea Euclid—”

--- Page 117 ---
Inside, I was startled. I kept thinking to myself—this girl loves me, I know she respects me. And yet, how she is humiliating me now! I must remain as composed as possible. Mrs. Parvati continued, saying that she had met 'J' about two years ago. That day, she was tired, even unwell. Upon seeing a Bengali, she began to talk about Calcutta—nowadays everyone criticizes Calcutta, but she had never seen a city to compare with the one she had known. Especially the women there, whose beauty is unparalleled, yet who know how to preserve their dignity, whose lips are always reciting Rabindranath’s poetry, and so on. Listening to all this, the clever 'J' felt that this was not praise for a place, but for a person. So he asked, “Sir, when you speak of Calcutta, are you talking about a place, or about a person?”

The professor said, “Come, let’s sit over there for a while.” Sitting in a secluded corner, he asked 'J', “Do you know Amrita? What is she like?” Then he gave him a book, on which was written in Bengali, “Do you remember, Amrita? If you do, can you forgive?”

But 'J' doesn’t know their language, how could he read the book? So he didn’t bring it. Someone remarked that he could have brought it, we could have had a look. The room was full of people, everyone was laughing heartily! Oh God, people seem to delight in tormenting others—friend or foe, it makes no difference! I am astonished at the cruelty of those I once considered friends. My head is spinning—after forty long years, for the first time I have heard of someone who has seen him, with whom he spoke about me. I hear someone in the room saying, “Amritadi, why don’t you write him a letter? We’ll get the address from 'J'. Write, write—it will be such fun.”

It feels as if my sari has caught fire, as if the flames will soon engulf my whole body. I must escape from here.

But the thought kept circling in my mind—now I have a proper address, why shouldn’t I write a letter? So he hasn’t forgotten me after all. What harm is there in a little communication? We are both old now. If I were to ask, “How are you?” what harm could it do?

These days, we have so much work. War is raging in Bangladesh—all of West Bengal is in turmoil, everyone is busy, so are we. Amidst all this, who knows where such lighthearted talk drifts away: in the face of human suffering and misery, these refined joys and sorrows seem so meaningless! Thousands upon thousands, hundreds of thousands of homeless, destitute, unclothed people are fleeing in search of shelter, their agony burns against our skin. We go to the border every day, cholera has broken out there. Around the refugee camps, dead bodies lie every day. One day, in front of a camp, I saw a dying woman about my own age lying by the roadside. She lay beneath a tree, on a torn quilt—her body almost motionless. Occasionally, convulsions would seize her. The workers were giving her saline right there on the road—soon, it would all be over. Evening was falling—the western sky streaked with the colors of blood—in the midst of this indifferent beauty of the world, a human life was ending, the lamp was about to go out. Tomorrow, at this time, there would be no trace left, that she had once come to this earth, that she had belonged to someone, that she had loved, had been loved—does any of it have any meaning then? Standing beside that dying woman, it was as if I saw my own deathbed. As far as I can imagine, I will not lie in the street; I will wait for death surrounded by relatives, on a bed. But after that? That body and mine are the same, no trace will remain. The detachment of the cremation ground has overwhelmed my mind. My eyes burn with tears—everyone thinks I am overcome with pity. Rashid said, “Take auntie away from here—you should go to Bongaon.”

But I am weeping for my own death, for this too will soon be over. What I have gained, what I have not—everything will become one—gold and dust, the same. For these few days on earth, man—

--- Page 118 ---
How much suffering people endure, and how little happiness they receive—pain is boundless. Suddenly, I seemed to see him beside my deathbed. As I was returning by car, his thoughts seized me in that strange atmosphere. Now that I have his address, why not write a letter? So many years have passed—now, what else is there? Can’t I write a letter to an old friend? But how will I ask Parvati for the address? What will she think? Let her think whatever she likes—what does it matter? Standing face to face with death, all the shame and hesitation I have clung to for so long seem unreal. Even if someone criticizes or thinks ill of me, I will not be hurt. 

The next day, I wrote a letter: “Dear Parvati, if you have Professor Euclid’s address, please give it to me.” The address came, along with a letter from ‘J’. She wrote, “He is a very good man, and his life is rooted in India. Calcutta is his dream, and his fame as a scholar is world-renowned.” Hearing of his fame as a scholar, my heart grew weary. I have no reverence for mere erudition—was it not for such temptations that…? Let that be, what does it matter what happened later? I know him—why shouldn’t I write to him? Let me write a letter and see if he replies. I wrote:

Mircea Euclid, I heard from ‘J’ that you inquired about me—I want to know if you are the same person I knew forty years ago—if so, please reply to this letter. Yours,
Amrita

No reply came. I waited, wondering if the letter had been posted properly. Meanwhile, replies came from abroad to other letters, but not to this one. Let it be. There is much work! Many thoughts! So many problems that occupy my mind—there’s no use brooding over this trivial matter.

In this very year, 1971, I heard again about his book. At that time, many people were coming from Europe to witness the Bangladesh war, to express sympathy, to offer help, to satisfy their curiosity. Many were also visiting our organization’s work. A Bengali couple was touring with us; they lived in Europe. I did not know them well—the girl’s name was Rina, a young woman who knew many languages, including theirs. We were sitting in a field, our companions scattered here and there—suddenly Rina said to me, “I read a book that mentions your name—is that really your name?” I quickly glanced around—luckily, no one was nearby—but I felt no need to answer Rina’s question, and she did not pursue it further.

Our stream of work had become tumultuous. We were faced with a multitude of diverse problems. Every day, we met new people. The boundary between home and outside had vanished; even at home, hundreds of people came and went each day. “When work is immense, it roars and covers all around”—we could hear the roar of that work. Never before had life felt so alive. In this indolent, work-averse country, many of us had found such meaningful work that even after exhausting ourselves completely, our hopes remained unfulfilled. How long could trivial personal matters linger in my mind now?

At this time—that is, from the end of 1971—an inexplicable longing kept returning to my mind: I thought of going somewhere far away. I told so many people that I would go abroad. By virtue of my work, I often met foreigners, and I would tell them, “I’ll visit your country soon.” I myself was surprised—why this longing, what was its cause, and where did it gain such strength? My state was like that: “I have no wings, I remain in this place, yet whoever passes by, I tell them so.”

In August 1972, Sergei Sebastian came to Calcutta—the one I mentioned at the beginning of this story. On the morning of September 1st, as I was going to meet him, I felt only a mild curiosity—let’s see what he says, that’s all. But after talking to him, I was astonished. I had many doubts about Mircea—but that he was such a terrible liar, that I did not know.

--- Page 119 ---
I did not understand before.

“What a terrible lie—shame, shame, your friend is so low, and yet you praise him?”

“I am his disciple, he is my guru, I am much younger than he is.”

“Some guru you have! He commits a crime and runs away, then takes revenge on me, disgracing me.”

Sergei says, “You don’t know what pain he has suffered. If you read his writing, you would understand his torment. In his imagination, he sought release from that suffering. If you touch the book, it bleeds.”

“No explanation can forgive his misdeeds. He gave me nothing in life, only shame. Sergei, does anyone write using real names? That would be libel.”

“But it’s a story—one has to invent a little for stories. Of course, it’s true that India is still the same about these matters.”

“Why did he use my real name? To insult me?”

Sergei is saddened—“You misunderstand him—have you forgotten his love? He still loves you; in almost all his writings, somewhere or other, there is your touch. Did he not become Indian for you? Can he ever break the bond of your name?”

“If he loved me so much, why doesn’t he reply to my letters?”

Sergei is startled—“He doesn’t? You wrote to him and didn’t get a reply? How many did you write?”

“I wrote three letters. Once every twenty years, I reminded him of my existence. I never received a reply.”

Sergei lowers his head in thought—“He tortures himself, he works against his heart—that is his nature, to do the opposite of what he desires. Surely he wanted to write, and that is why he did not.”

I remain silent. I remember this aspect of his character—who knows what is in his mind, such a mysterious man. The one who introduced me to every person in his country, why did he give me this false identity? On one hand, I feel unbearable anger; on the other, I remember his presence, his memory. I ask, “Sergei, do you ever see him anymore?”

“Why wouldn’t I? Both of you are alive in this world—someday, somehow, you will meet again.”

I think, Oh God, how I long to see him again.

“Tell me, Sergei, if I go to see him, will he meet me?”

“Of course, he will meet you. His heart is still here, in this country.”

As I speak with Sergei, I realize now that my subtle body has left my physical self—I am no longer here—that is, I can clearly see myself as two separate beings.

I returned home and somehow passed that festival day, as I have already mentioned, but again and again I slip from the present into the past. Each day passes once more, each event seems to happen again. What I have just written, I have felt directly. This did not happen easily. I do not have the words to express in sentences the mental agony of living in two states at once. On one side, my present life stands with its full demands—on the other, another existence of mine seems to stand behind me like a shadow, touching every moment of mine. My mind has become like a stringed instrument—at the slightest touch, it vibrates and rings out. Gradually, the past is coming to the fore—I do not know what to do with myself.

Some days, I become so absent-minded that I am unprepared before everyone. My son comes and asks me something, and I do not even hear. One day, I was going to a workplace with my husband, twenty...

--- Page 120 ---
We had traveled miles by car—usually I would speak and he would reply, but that day, perhaps he tried to say a few words, and getting no response from me, fell silent! When we reached our destination, I realized I hadn’t said a single word—I told my husband, “All these days you kept silent, now it’s my turn to be quiet.”

He said, “So I see, you’ve beaten me in this competition.”

Each day begins, and then suddenly it’s night again—I can’t even tell when the day slips into darkness. The night is terrifying, utterly terrifying—a dreadful burning—flames raging, flames of anger, I must do something to remedy this. Now that I know, I will not silently endure this injustice. I wrote to a friend of mine who lives in his country—the letter went like this—

“Sumita, surely you’ve read Euclid’s writings, yet you never told me—heard everything from Sergei instead. That man was my father’s student, lived in our house, benefited from us, and this is how he repays us—you must write a protest. It is your duty, too, to ensure the dignity of Indian women is not tarnished.”

I wrote this letter, but I know the accusation is only on the surface. The change happening deep within my mind has a different form, a different nature.

Sumita wrote back… “I repeatedly requested Sergei not to mention the name of the book to you. He is a man with a very fixed idea, I could not make him understand, not even after meeting you, that this discussion would not please you. In your political and social work you have seen all kinds of characters in the world—by your own virtue you will forgive. I can see you wrote the letter in a very agitated state—understandable. … Whatever Sergei says, I have discussed this with a couple of literary friends here, and they agree with me that disclosing your identity was inappropriate. Forty years have passed, no one remembers that book anymore. There is no possibility that anyone in your family knows about the contents of that book.”

Holding Sumita’s letter in my hand, I think—this girl has tried to comfort me—because this book is alive—even though it was written forty years ago, it is still alive. Sumita also wrote, if anything new is written about this now, it will only stir up mud. But where is the mud in all this? There was no mud in that pure young heart, no mud at all—the mud is what that man created in his imagination.

Night after night, day after day, I keep thinking how I can free my name from the stain of this falsehood. I am prepared to bear the burden of truth, but why should I bear the burden of a lie? I am consulting my friends—I have no choice but to tell them. They are astonished; this event in my life is unexpected. I tell them, look at the irony of fate—this household I have built over so many years, with my husband, children, relatives, those who have become one with my life—where will they all go? My real life will fade away like a shadow. All social bonds, even bonds of blood, end with death, dissolve into nothingness, but the bond he created is indestructible. What will happen, Parvati, that the one who is nothing to me, no one at all, whom I met for just a brief moment on the long journey of life—a stranger—his identity will remain the truest of all. The one who is nowhere in my life, in death I will remain bound to him—“Bound to another man, my name will remain joined in the union of death.”

Talking to my younger friends, I realized how much this world has changed. They have learned to see the truth. I had thought that, because of my unsocial and untimely emotional turmoil, they would reject me like something impure—but that did not happen. They accepted me, and in the light of the truth of my life, I became dearest to them. In our time, it was not like this. Whether today’s changes are good or bad, whether society was purer before, women more chaste, love more sacred—there is debate about this—I too have my doubts, sometimes I feel it is natural…

--- Page 121 ---
The regulation of instincts through strict self-restraint is essential for the development of humanity; otherwise, one cannot transcend the assaults of animality. Perhaps this is not entirely untrue, yet in our society, there is no end to examples where even tradition, reform, and rigid rules have led to the distortion of humanity—this is a land where women had to prove their chastity by self-immolation.

The greatest aspect of a human being is their truth-seeking mind. Many of our instincts are shared with animals, but only humans search for truth. The pursuit of truth in science has, for two hundred years, led us down diverse paths, its light marking a way through the forest of superstition and blind faith. Now, in a moment of profound realization, I see an astonishing change within society itself; people are trying to see the truth, clearing away the debris of scriptural injunctions, the dictates of gurus, and ancient beliefs and ideas. For this, I offer my reverence to this era. In a sense, this is the age of truth.

Day after day passes; I do not know to whom I should turn for help in this crisis—and that inscrutable person sits silently, withdrawn, as always, in the wheel of fate. So be it. But how is it that, exactly at this time—forty years after his passing—so many connections are being made, who can say? A close relative of mine went to their country. I was afraid as soon as I heard the news—my fear was not baseless. Upon arriving there, she heard, and told me with much concern, “No gentleman would do such a thing, Didi. Just because of some incident in a past life, to write a book like this!”

I heard this and realized that this book is not dead; it has lived for forty years—this long-lived work will only gain more strength as the world grows smaller. Alas, for so many days I have heard of it, yet why did I never once try to find out what was in it? I am always alert to my own fame and infamy; if anyone tells me that some news about me or my family has appeared in a certain paper, or that they saw my photograph in a report, I immediately have that paper brought to me. I cannot rest until I see it. And if I find something unpleasant, I become agitated with anger, call people to write a protest. Yet I have heard of this book again and again, and never once felt the urge to know what it contained? If my father had been alive, I would have taken steps. Father knew, but did nothing. That was his way—“He’s written a book, well and good.” After so many years, I love my father as I did in 1919, 1920, 1930. I call out to him, “Look, Father, look at your disciple’s deed—the one who, like Ekalavya, worshipped you from afar as his guru, see what he has done to me—then you wanted to save me from his hands, could you?”

Sergei said, “Because he could give you nothing in life, he wanted to immortalize his undying love in literature.” Oh God, can a lie ever be immortal? Can immortality be created from falsehood? I think of that person, whose memory I had guarded in the depths of my heart like a secret treasure, whose name no one ever heard me utter, and he has stripped me bare before the eyes of the world—and though he does not have the courage to reply to my letters, for forty years he has made money selling my flesh! This is the West!

But how can I explain to you that this fear, this thought, belongs to only one layer of my mind, it is my conscious mind that is agitated—while in the deepest cave of my being, there is another thought, entirely different.

Day after day passes, night after night I am sleepless. All my long-held values and sense of honor seem to be burning away. A flame of fear rises from within, scorching everything around, growing brighter. Bit by bit, I am melting, like a candle, my body spreading light as it melts, drop by drop, burning away, destroying my ego, my pride, my vanity of virtue, my longing for respect—all that I once thought precious is burning in the face of that flame. This ineffable state can only be described in song—“All my pride, O Lord, drown it in tears.” I realize that this ego, built up over ages of tradition—

--- Page 122 ---
It was truly like a candle—strong, upright, unyielding. But now, that sharp flame of fear has slowly begun to melt it away—yet is it really the flame of fear? Is it the fear that I might be disgraced? Am I so anxious about public censure? In the deep of night, I stand gazing up at the sky—I realize, no, it is not that. Even the fear that stood motionless behind the fear is being melted away. It is love—immortal love, and its radiant flame, that is melting and destroying even my fear, leaving only its luminous glow. Month after month, I have burned; everything within me is being consumed, even my age. It is as if I have returned entirely to the year 1930. My feelings have become just as vivid, just as real. The light of love is entering the deepest recesses of my heart, illuminating every corner, every dark alley, and the prideful habits of fear are fading away. All the pretenses I have made, all my efforts to make myself seem greater, all that I have been proud of—everything is dissolving, eroding, and I am seeing the full form of truth. My life is becoming meaningful in a new sense. I remember him—his half-forgotten face, his words, his many incomprehensible actions, his anger, his jealousy, and above all, his love. Gradually, I seem to have moved to another plane, another existence, from where the good and bad, the true and false, the imagined and the real, all of this outer world merges into one. My mind says, what does any of this matter—praise and blame are all the same; there is something truer than all of it. 

I wonder, why did he let this deathless love fail in such a way? So he had to leave—so what? Is physical closeness the only way to possess someone? If, over ten years, we could have exchanged even a single letter—then? That would have been enough, enough. With that one letter, we could have crossed the continents that lay between us, the vast ocean of separation. With that one letter, we would have become Ardhanarishvara, our dual selves finding completion in a single, united circle. But do they understand any of this? No, they do not, they do not. In their country, to fulfill love, one must share a bed! Oh God!

But he knew, he knew for certain. I see myself like a picture in the middle of a doorway, pressed close to his chest, and he says, “Not your body, Amrita, it is your soul within your body that I have sought to hold—”

This is true, true—there is no permanence in the body, the soul is immortal—‘Na hanyate hanyamane sharire.’ Where is that body of mine now? That garden of youth has withered away—snow has fallen on this aged, worn head, lines have creased my face—yet I see the ultimate feeling of this life just as undying. No one has been able to destroy it—not my father, not myself, not time, not my pride, not the rich experiences of my life. I feel immortality—

ya enam vetti hantāraṃ yaś cainaṃ manyate hatam
ubhau tau na vijānīto nāyaṃ hanti na hanyate.

Words I could never understand from any scripture, I now see so clearly—does not die, does not die, love does not die. At the doorway of the house in Bhabanipur, my soul, held in his two hands, still remains, surging within these infinite boundaries—here and yet not here, far and yet near.

I am a skeptic, I am an atheist. I have no faith in superstition—but what is happening in my body and mind is shaking the very foundation of my disbelief—someone tell me, what am I? Where has that sixteen-year-old, incorporeal existence of mine been living all this time?

I wrote to Sumita, you need not write any more protests! To me, now, all these small truths and falsehoods have merged into one. I want nothing more, nothing at all, only to see him once. I had held back for so long, but now I declare: this love is the ultimate truth, beside it my present life has become a mere shadow—let him write whatever he wishes, whether it brings honor or dishonor, this alone is the recognition life has given me—I accept even his accusations, with bowed head.

122

--- Page 123 ---
The trivial fear has left me, but I am suffering from exhaustion. My whole life seems to have slipped from my grasp—I sit here with empty hands. I wrote to Sergei—‘I feel as though I am sitting on the shore of the sea of time, holding an embarkation card, but the ship never comes. How long must I wait, brother? I cannot die without seeing him.’

I received a letter from Sumita. She writes—

‘For several days after receiving your letter, I was so overwhelmed that I could not think of what to write. I spoke with Sergei; that day I said, truth is stranger than fiction! When the author wrote that novel, did he know that the truth of the future would surpass even the imagination of the present? I withdraw all the objections I had against that book. To tell the truth, the ending of the novel is tragic, but today’s tragedy is even greater. The hand of life’s artist is far more skillful than ours…’

Sumita wrote further, ‘If you will forgive my presumption, I would like to say one thing—you wrote that this feeling is so overwhelming that even your Rabindranath Tagore is swept away, but is it not Rabindranath Tagore himself, seated within your heart, who is urging you to acknowledge the truth?’

On sleepless nights, I speak with Sergei—Sergei, truly, from where did you rise up from the depths and turn my life upside down? The wound that had been covered for forty years, while I lived in peace and happiness, now its lid has been removed. Now the bleeding will not stop, my bed and pillow are soaked in blood, the sky of my sleepless nights has turned blood red—

I look and see my husband sitting on the bed, gazing at me—there are tears in his eyes—“Will you not tell me what has happened to you?”

I must tell him. For a long time, my friends have been urging me, tell him. All my friends respect and love my husband. They have always said he will try, with great sympathy, to ease your sorrow. But I hesitate. I think, will it not hurt him? What right do I have to cause him pain? Can it be anything but pain? In our thirty-eight years of married life, the existence of this person has never touched him, not even for a moment, he does not even know his name—will this unseen, unknown person suddenly emerge from some cave, like a mass of coiled smoke becoming a giant the moment the bottle’s mouth is opened? He has no connection to this household, no one around me has ever seen a trace of him, for forty-three years I have not seen him, and yet, can that childhood memory become so real today as to shake the foundation of our long-standing home? Can he bear this? Thinking all this, I have not spoken. Not to hide anything: my mind is in no state to conceal. The real world has become like a mirage to me.

“I want to tell you, but you will be hurt.”

“Seeing you in pain hurts me too.”

Then I told my husband everything—. He was stunned, “We have been together for thirty-eight years and I never knew? Why didn’t you tell me, why didn’t you? There was no need for you to suffer so much.”

My heart is breaking. Has anyone ever faced such a trial? He has done so much for me, loves me so dearly, my happiness is his happiness, and now, in this old age, am I about to bring the sky crashing down on his head? The one who has given everything, must he see that he has received nothing? No, never, I am weeping, “Believe me, I have never deceived you.”

“That question does not arise, do you need to tell me that? I have never felt deprived, not for a moment, never thought there could be anything more than this.”

123

--- Page 124 ---
He is speaking a lot now, “What I have received is incomparable, I have no resentment, only sorrow that you had to suffer so much. And that gentleman—my heart aches more for him.”

“Forget about him, a deceiver, he got what he deserved.”

“Shame on you, will you find peace thinking like that? After all, you were with your parents—but that foreign boy, at such a young age, was driven out within a few hours, left to wander in the wilderness, what suffering! Your father truly cannot be forgiven.”

He is stroking my head, and I am astonished to see that he feels no sense of loss. Has he suffered any loss?

“Believe me, I haven’t felt pain all these years—if I had, wouldn’t you have noticed? Now you have.”

“That’s true, you haven’t suffered, I’ve never seen you in pain.”

“If I had been in pain, could you have been happy?”

“That’s true—but now, after half a century, you are suffering for that? What a strange thing to say!”

“Yes, that’s right, I too am quite surprised—sometimes I wonder if I have lost my mind, does this ever happen? I feel I am doing something terribly wrong, isn’t that so? Tell me.” I am crying, crying—after so many days, being able to speak openly with my husband feels like stepping into the open air—my tears are a release.

“What can I tell you about right or wrong? Have I ever seen, heard, or known of such an event in my life? Who am I to judge this? My only sorrow is having to witness your suffering. But you know, I feel you will be all right. I have always seen that there is a purpose working within your life.”

Having spoken to my husband, I feel at peace. For who is a greater friend to me than he? Where else would I find forgiveness for all my faults, consolation for all my sorrows, if not with him?

Writing about this time is very difficult, because there is no event to narrate—such longing can only be expressed in poetry or song, in no other way. After many days, I am writing poetry again; when I cannot sleep late at night, I sit in the next room and write poems. I have never written anything about him. The first things I wrote, I tore up. Now I will write, I will acknowledge, and it is because I have not acknowledged that I am suffering so—nothing else. Everyone must settle their dues.

I received a letter from Sergei—I wait for their letters as if I am receiving news of him.

Dear Amrita Devi,

I am so overwhelmed by the trust you have placed in me that I cannot express it. I only wonder whether I am worthy of this trust. I am witnessing the play of fate. I am astonished, thinking of what has happened and what happened after our meeting. My greatest experience in India was meeting you. When I was just a boy, bound by the threads of fate, that time is connected to our meeting. At that time, I read two books—‘Amrita’ and ‘Sadhana.’ The deep influence of these two books shaped me—and the Indian world enchanted me for life—I became acquainted with your culture—began to study it, and whatever my life has become, for good or ill, it is because of this. Finally, I was drawn to your country—and there I had the good fortune to meet you. Seeing your profound sorrow, I understood—what are we, what is the soul? Now I admit, though your letter has deeply unsettled me, even then, the moment I saw you, I understood the state of your inner being!

--- Page 125 ---
I had understood—and I had told you as well—that time and forgetfulness only touch the outer covering of our consciousness, but our true memory is an absolute entity. Therefore, in a certain sense, we are indeed immortal. I have always held a firm belief in this, yet as human beings, we require direct proof of every knowable thing. You have given me overwhelming and undeniable proof of immortality. I have realized that our consciousness is affected by time only superficially; if we so wish, we can overcome the influence of time and become timeless. Forgetfulness is death—if we can set aside this forgetfulness, we may attain immortality, we may become ‘amrita’—the undying.

No matter how intense your suffering, you must understand that, having fulfilled all your duties in this world, you now dwell in the grace of God—a state beyond sorrow and happiness. Only love can conquer time and forgetfulness. A person is destroyed, a person is defeated by time, when their power of love, the timeless force of love, is lost. Nothing gives me more joy than receiving your letters... I will always remain your faithful friend.

Sergei’s letter makes me ponder: what is truth, what is immortality? I am tasting the flavor of immortality, for I can touch with my mind the revived form of what had once died. Is that Amal, entering the room with a sheaf of papers, more real than the memory of us leaning over the catalog books in the library, making our own catalog? Is the past less true than the present? Has it gone somewhere, or is it standing still right here: the lid of my mind has been lifted—one by one, shadowy figures rise and make their existence real. Sometimes I am afraid that this emotion of mine, like a warm, bubbling spring rising from the depths of the earth and spreading over the surrounding soil, is just so—this searing love, suddenly rising from some unknown depth, is spreading over the carefully tended garden of my life. I am anxious, O God, will its flowers and leaves wither away?

Sometimes, the year 1930 and the year 1972 merge so completely that I am thrown into confusion—I simply cannot give language to the immediacy of this feeling. Perhaps language itself has not yet been created for such experiences. This does not usually happen; even a year ago, had someone told me this, I would not have believed them. Still, I am trying to express it—if, someday, this memoir-novel is published, perhaps psychologists will be able to determine its cause and origin. The night has grown deep; I heard the clock strike twice with a resonant dong. I see Shanti lying beside me—the piano downstairs is playing on and on. I am tossing and turning in bed—“Sister Shanti, I can’t sleep!”

“Me neither. Euclid-da just keeps playing the piano.”

“Isn’t it unfair?” I sit up in bed—

“I’ll go and ask him to stop—”

Shanti says, “No, no, not now, do it tomorrow.”

“I’ve gotten out of bed, an unspoken pull is drawing me toward him—the music is like a spell, my being is enchanted. I must go to him now, I must, I can’t help it, he’s calling, calling me, I’m moving toward the door. Shanti gets up too, ‘What are you doing, Ru?’”

“I’ll just go and ask him to stop playing.”

“Are you crazy? Look at the clock, it’s two o’clock—who goes at such an hour?”

“If one can go at eight in the evening, why not at two?”

“That’s not done. One never goes to a man’s room at night.”

“But you do, don’t you?”

125

--- Page 126 ---
"Where am I to go again, tell me, am I a good girl?”
“Go to any room you like, to uncle’s room, to father’s room—”
“At two in the night? Never, besides, we are relatives—.” I am getting angry, truly angry. I push her hand away and move towards the door—I will go, I must. I’ll just go and stand outside the door for a bit and tell him, don’t play now. What harm could there be in that? Shanti’s authority has grown. She’s twirling her stick at me. Shanti stands with her back against the door, refusing to let me pass.

“You’ve grown bold, haven’t you? Who do you think you are?” I try to push her aside. Shanti is angry too, she says, “Who do you think you are? You’ll see who you are, shall I call Ma?”

The spell has been cast on the snake’s head, I bury my face in the pillow and cry.

“Sister Shanti, you know what’s happened between us—aren’t we sinning?”

Shanti rests her cheek on her hand, thinking, “Of course we are.”

“Who is sinning? He or I?”

Shanti ponders, “I think both of you are.”

“Never, not at all. Why should I be at fault, when I always try to stop him?”

“Then don’t go to his room anymore.”

Seeing my face, Shanti softens—“Why are you worrying about sin, sister? Do as you wish, go in the morning, I’ll never tell anyone.”

But this is January of 1973, the clock strikes two—dong, dong. I sit up in bed, feeling I must hurry downstairs, or Shanti will stop me. I am about to get down from the bed—someone asks, “Where are you going?”

“Downstairs—” “Where downstairs, it’s two in the night—” “What’s the difference between two and eight?” Shanti grabs me from behind—I try to free my hand, and at that moment I realize this is the year seventy-two, and it is my husband holding me; not Shanti. I am trembling, trembling in fear, what disaster has befallen me—I have lost all sense of time—I cling to my husband’s neck and begin to cry, “Save me, save me—who else will save me but you, I am committing a great sin—I am causing you pain.” He strokes my head, soothing me to sleep, “I have no pain. Only one sorrow—that I have no way left to punish you now. But whatever little I can do, I will. Of course you will go, you must go and see him once.”

“What if everyone finds out?”

“How would they know, why would they?”

“Perhaps I’ll blurt it out myself, I can’t remember anything, nothing at all, tell me, who is calling me, who? Could it be a stranger? Someone I haven’t seen in forty-two years is surely a ‘Stranger’. Mircea is only the pretext, someone else is transforming my entire being, just as fire turns water into steam, I feel utterly changed. He is telling me, see the truth, see the truth—I know this feeling won’t last long. I will return to my household, I must, but will you believe, at this very moment, how much I have changed? I have even forgiven Roma, I bear no anger towards her. I remember her as a young girl, she was so sweet then, it’s true she took everything from my mother, but she too suffered. Just because she got the outward things, she has been obsessively, irrationally inserting her name into father’s books like a madwoman—think, for a man with six children like us before his eyes, whose devoted wife is still alive, how much did poor Roma really get? When I remember childhood, I think of all this, you know, perhaps now I could even love Roma, believe me, I do love her. Once I told Roma, can anyone ever condemn or humiliate someone they love?

--- Page 127 ---
Can it be? Yet today it seems to me that Rama is merely an instrument—whose game are we playing? What appears as loss from the outside may, from within, be gain. That which seems only futile, transient, and restless here—does it not, in some secret, wondrous new form, become fulfillment elsewhere? Bijoykrishna Goswami once told Father, “You will fall.” Father carried the seed of his own fall within himself—his sky-touching ego. We tried in every way to protect that ego. How many times did we wonder why Father was not made President of this country? Was he any less capable? For the sake of that very ego, he lost everything, and in its shelter, he died nameless and without fame. But today I wonder—would becoming President have been the ultimate gain? Perhaps something greater was achieved—perhaps, before departing, he bowed his head in utter egolessness, having lost everything outwardly but grown inwardly, his being reaching fullness. Perhaps the flaw in his vast genius was washed away by tears, and he became ennobled. Rama was the instrument for this, not us. Today I understand that this world is not as we see it—I begin to grasp the meaning of ‘chakna khela,’ or ‘apavṛṇu’—who is it that shows me the face of truth? It cannot be just some half-known, half-revealed person from my past.

I see him, I speak to him, I quarrel with him—the reason for my quarrel is that Rina is reading me the book. The book is full of lies. He never understood our world. I tell him, “Don’t think, Mircea, that I am reading your book because your memory has been awakened in me—far from it. In truth, it is because memory has come alive that I am reading your writing. Otherwise, I had heard of it so many times before, but never felt the urge to read it.” This book cannot awaken memory; it will only confuse it, for the book is a distortion of imagination wearing the mask of truth. He assumed from the start that my parents had set a trap to catch a suitor like him. I too was a conspirator in that trap—he took this for granted, and so he never truly understood me. Even if he did, he could not comprehend my mind. I never told him what was in my heart. There was never the chance, never the time, so he has put words in my mouth that I could never say, that are not even my language. What his unsatisfied heart did not receive, he has conjured in the book. There is a name for this in psychology, though I do not know it; I am not a devotee of that discipline—it is merely the audacity to wish to become a navigator of the boundless, mysterious human mind. Yet I can understand his mind—it is the opposite of mine. Through the door of falsehood, one can never reach the presence of truth. The face of truth is veiled by the debris of lies, and it is that veil which must be lifted. That is why I have taken up this pen.

I read the book and criticize him. I know he has just cause to be angry with Father, but how is it that, in this book where he so harshly criticizes him—criticisms that are not entirely untrue—he still dedicates the book to him as his guru, takes pride in being his disciple? I do not know such a mind—this ‘utilitarian’ mind—or perhaps it is something else, I do not know. All these arguments I hurl like little poisonous arrows—strangely, they dissolve into the air, never reaching their mark; my anger toward him is like small pebbles that cannot rest on the waves of my heart, but slip away, roll off, and sink in an instant.

All these years, I knew nothing of him, but now, as if by some conspiracy, I keep hearing news of him from all sides. Some connection has formed, so that at this time, fate will make me hear of him, learn of him. In “Smaraṇe,” there is a poem addressed to his deceased wife, where Rabindranath writes: “In this world, one day, dressed as a new bride, you came and stood by my side, placed your trembling hand in mine—was it the play of fate, was it an accident? No, this event is not of a single moment, it is the counsel of eternity.”

Do we do anything ourselves? Or are we merely puppets bound by strings? One day, when there was not even a trace of him, how did he suddenly become so significant, and why is it that now, of all times, I keep hearing news of him?

127

--- Page 128 ---
My friend’s husband met him two years ago, and I am only hearing about it now.  
He said that Professor Euclid is a wonderful man and becomes animated when speaking of Calcutta.  
Many streets in Calcutta are familiar to him; he was asking me if I had seen ‘Bakulbagan Road’.  
He lived in Calcutta forty years ago, but he remembers everything—Calcutta is the land of his dreams.

I have heard more about him; he lives in a world of imagination. India is the object of his meditation.  
Even in his writings, the place of fantasy and the supernatural is greater than that of the real world. He calls himself Indian.

The more I hear about him, the more restless I become. I have understood well that unless I see him once, my torment will not subside.  
Yet I also wonder, whom will I see? Where is that twenty-three-year-old boy! And whom will he see? Where is that sixteen-year-old girl!  
He does not want to see me, so I do not think about whom he will see, but the one I want to see—where will I find him?  
Do I want to see a twenty-three-year-old youth, a boy? Or what? To me now, he is a boy, could be my grandson.  
If today I were to see Mircea at twenty-three, surely there could be no camaraderie between us.  
On the other hand, the Mircea I can see today is an old man, whom I do not know at all—he is a stranger, an unknown visitor.  
How could I find peace seeing him?  
I cannot at all understand this restlessness of my sleepless nights, which constantly wants to set out on some aimless, unnameable quest in some inexplicable pain—could it be for some unknown person?  
Or is it that from some other realm, some other force is calling me, toward an unknown future?  
Could there be someone who is the cause of all knowledge, all love? Is the message coming from there?  
My ever-doubting mind does not want to accept such things, yet it does not let go of suspicion either.

At this twilight of my life, the light of dawn has fallen. Morning and evening have become one, time seems eternal and unmoving.

Sometimes I think of Sergei—is this the taste of immortality?  
That childhood poem of mine, I am only now beginning to understand—why I wrote it then, I do not know—  
“When moments and instants are lost in time—”  
In those embraces of a time without recognition, untouched by memory, I am bound; my past and present have become one—this is immortality.  
If that is so, then why am I in such pain? Why am I lost in tears?  
I cannot tell if this is truly pain or the taste of nectar.  
If it is pain, then surely I would want release—do I want release?  
Can I even imagine that these few months of experience should vanish into nothingness, that I should again become busy with my household, my property?  
People of my age all do just that.  
They cannot even imagine that, surrounded by grandchildren, sitting in the midst of real life, someone could slip away into such a world of dreams—  
Bodiless, untouchable, just the message of an existence could lift away the walls around her.  
The friction of worldly life makes the mind calloused—  
The material mind then calculates, so did mine—  
All these years I built houses, kept accounts of every penny, and now?  
If at this very moment someone told me, “Leave everything behind, come empty-handed, you will see him,” would I not go?  
If someone asked, “Do you want a four-story house on Theatre Road, or do you want to see him once?”  
Is there any doubt what I would say?  
This strange transformation that has come over me does not make me unhappy;  
I never want it to be destroyed.  
That such intensity of feeling has returned to the heart of someone my age, I consider it a blessing.

Whose, whose mercy I do not know  
In whose hand is the inexhaustible store  
Upon the mud flats left by the ebbing tide
