Who awakens the song in the rippling sound of water?
What fortune, what fortune, alas!
Who sends a call from the unknown estuary—
Looking toward this unknown estuary, I find not anyone else, but myself—
As Yajnavalkya said, a person cannot give anything to another, one gives only to oneself—“Atmanantu kamaya
putrah priyah bhavati”—love for the husband, love for the son, all are for our own sake, fulfilling only my own self. Remembering him, I wonder what I gave to whom—yet it is I who have gained a third eye to see this world.

Some of my relatives think that the memory I had so easily forgotten for forty years, living my domestic life, why has it suddenly awakened at this age—and why am I not forcing myself to forget it? I do not know the answer. I only feel within that this bodiless love is playing such a wondrous music in my body and mind that I never knew before, a music not found by chance on the roadside. “Is Krishna’s love a mere coin, to be picked up by whoever finds it?”

Seren’s words are true, within this there is a message of immortality, in the melody a hint of tears, in love an eternal dissatisfaction that speaks of the infinite—I am receiving just that. I do not want to forget, not at all.

Besides, whatever others may say, my husband does not object. We talk, and the other day he told me, “The same event does not occur twice in a household, each matter must be judged separately—It is true that I am not connected to every part of your life, I do not know what you think when you write poetry, your literary world is unknown to me, nor do I know all of your professional world, so this too is a part of you; how does it harm me?” I am astonished that he feels no loss or injury. If he did, it would be very difficult for me. I could not even think of going to see him, for I could never gain anything precious by causing my husband pain—there is no greater sin than to wound love, can I embark on a pilgrimage with such a burden of sin on my head?

Gradually, it is becoming so difficult that I can hardly do my work. How am I to converse with people on various matters? I am going to Delhi for work, but if I sit in the Deputy Secretary’s office and tears come to my eyes, what will I do? I am trying with all my might to regain my composure. I sit back in my chair, tears streaming down my face, and the servants enter the room, astonished—for at my age, the only reason for tears could be a quarrel with my daughter-in-law! For all Bengali women of my age, that is a duty, but the servants see no such thing. Besides, if something sorrowful had happened, it would be discussed, everyone in the house would be affected, but this is not the case. Everyone is human, they too notice my strange state and cannot understand it—I am embarrassed too, but my mind is not under my control, not at all. If I see a car passing by, I feel like getting down—perhaps he is inside. Whenever I hear someone is going abroad, my heart aches. I fear that my aged body will not be able to bear this unbearable weight of separation—I do not wish to fall ill, for then I would not be able to meet him. But strangely, my health has improved—these past few months, I have had no heart trouble, no blood pressure.

Those who tell me that with a little effort I could free myself from the grip of these memories, I argue with them in my mind, question and answer. For this is my own question too. They say, the more you dwell on it, the more it will possess you, the more you will suffer. Like the wisdom of the ignorant, this saying—

--- Page 130 ---
Although it may be generally true, what is truth in this case? For forty years it did not trouble me, when I was young, when perhaps there was still some possibility, its memory drifted over my mind like a shadow but did not grip me in its iron fist. For example, when I heard in Paris that he lived just two blocks away, I could have gone to see him with Popescu—and then? Who knows what might have happened then. How old was I at that time? Though I thought, having just married off my daughter, that I was already an old woman—we Bengali women are used to thinking that way—but in truth, I was only forty. Now, standing at the threshold of sixty, I realize that age was not much at all.

Yet, at that time, my heart was not churned as it is now. Why is it happening now, standing at the banks of this Vaitarani? The banks of Vaitarani? I say this knowingly, it is just a figure of speech, for I do not feel the weight of age, the moss of old age has fallen away from me. Can anyone do this by will? Or can one stop it by will? The day Mircea Euclid sat before my father’s desk and my father said, “This is my student, he will stay here”—did he orchestrate that? And on the 1st of September, when Sergei Sebastian arrived, did I summon him?—“This was ordained from time immemorial”—when the world was still a nebula, this event was already linked in the chain of cause and effect! This is what is said in the poem De Profundis—“Where all that was to be in all that was.”

Some say, “If you reminisce a little, what harm is there?” But where will you go? Whom will you see? Others say, “It will be good for you to see him, your heart will be freed from its enchantment, the infatuation for that young man will fade.” My worldly mind, my surface mind, says the same—I imagine I will see an unfamiliar old man, and at once I will feel embarrassed, thinking, “Who have I come to see?” But my deeper mind says, “This is not such a thing—the one you wish to see is not to be seen with the eyes. He does not age. Just as you have not.” Age is but a covering, it can be cast off—some do so through spiritual practice, some are granted the grace to find their true self—whose grace? I do not know. But I am beginning to understand the nature of the soul. I have no doubts left. One who does not know cannot be made to understand this feeling. Just as light cannot be explained to the blind. This realization is beyond argument, it cannot be grasped by intellect, nor learned from scripture. “Na medhaya bahudha na shrutena”—not by intelligence, nor by much hearing. Today I understand that this love, which transcends time, can never be conquered by worldly life; it will only grow stronger, carrying me far from the whirlpool of the world—just as the many streams of a river, despite crossing the world’s varied attractions, flow only towards the sea, not away from it.

I must go to see him once, but going is not so easy. Many arrangements are needed—besides, I do not want to inform him beforehand, for then he would surely leave the country. I have understood that he does not wish, or is unable, to meet me. Why he cannot, I do not fully understand. After writing such a poem about me, could he feel guilty? No, that is not it—he did not even realize that it was wrong, just as Sergei did not. In their country, such things do not matter. Now, it does not matter to me either; outward associations are like ripples of wind on water. I am not so foolish as to be agitated for that reason alone. My resentment is because he has mixed falsehood with truth.

Then why did he avoid me all his life? I called ‘J’ and asked, “Can you tell me, if I go, will he meet me?”

“Of course he will. The person who spoke of you to me with such feeling—why do you think he would not meet you?”

“He does not reply to my letters!”

“He does not reply? Why is that?”

“Well, ‘J’, could it be that he promised my father he would not keep any contact with me, and that is why he does not reply to my letters?”

--- Page 131 ---
“That’s quite possible.”
“How strange! A promise worth keeping?”
“Why not? Can’t someone be truthful?”
I think about leaving. During the day, I make various efforts—it’s not easy these days to go abroad. But at night, I am afraid. Where will I go alone? What if I faint in the middle of the street, what if I have a stroke? Parvati’s husband said the same thing, “From what I’ve heard about that gentleman, who knows how he will behave? You’re letting Amrita go alone—what if she falls ill?” Everyone is worried, and so am I. Only my husband reassures me, “Nothing will happen, there’s nothing to fear, no one will harm you, you won’t fall ill. Everything will be arranged, you’ll return safe and sound.” My husband is always concerned about my health, but now he seems unworried. I am astonished—if I take ten steps back, he takes twenty steps forward. And he does it so effortlessly that we are all amazed. “Go, you must go. Go there and bring your fairy tale to its end.”

That day, I was sitting in the Air India office. The man helping us was named Purohit. Sitting in the Air India office, I was afraid. How would I go to such an unknown country? Every time I had gone abroad before, it was either with a group or as an invited guest. They took care of everything for me; otherwise, how could I go alone? I was waiting for an invitation—if it came, then I would go; otherwise, it was impossible to travel alone just to see him. I said, “Let’s leave the ticket for today.”

My husband said, “Why? Let the ticket be booked—the invitation will come, you’ll see, it will surely come.”
“No, no, not today. I’m afraid.”
“Why are you afraid? I should be the one to be afraid!”
“Why would you be afraid?”
“Shouldn’t I be?” He laughed heartily, “You’re taking Purohit’s help.”

After so long, I found myself laughing too. My heart felt light, clear, generous—this selfless man could inspire such pure laughter.

I was still uncertain whether the invitation would come or not. The people I am associated with in various capacities here are the ones who would invite me. But only if there’s a conference—otherwise, they’ll take me next year for sure. They don’t know what urgency drives me. Who knows when the conference will be? It’s been over six months since Sergei came—not a short time. In these six months, I haven’t slept more than an hour a night, yet my health hasn’t suffered, nor do I feel sleepy during the day.

The other day, a professor came to see me. He had taken on a major responsibility. In the midst of our conversation, he suddenly said, “I have an astrologer—on his advice, I took up this work.”

I was surprised. I’ve seen many astrologers, but I’ve never heard any of them say anything accurate—they all shoot arrows in the dark. And then, a ritual—offering two flowers here as if to change the immutable laws of the universe.

I said, “Do you believe in such trickery?”
“There are many tricksters, but not all.”
“Will you take me to him sometime?” I couldn’t help but smile. When people become weak, even their backbone bends. That’s what has happened to me. Now I have to rely on the supernatural! I thought, let’s see—what harm can it do? So I went to the astrologer’s house with the professor. He dropped me off and left. The astrologer was intently studying my horoscope. I...

--- Page 132 ---
I wonder, who has the power to say what is happening to me? My age itself is my religion.

The astrologer asked, “Do you wish to know?”

“Tell me much about my future—do not speak of the past—though, of course, there is not much life left, so there is hardly any future either.”

“That is not so, you will live many more years. And very soon, I will give you the date, I am writing it down: by the 11th of April, you will cross the seas—you will go abroad.”

“How will I go? I have no money.”

I was stunned. I had not even introduced myself, and yet he said so much—how could he?

“And then? What will I gain by going abroad?”

“There, you will meet someone, someone you have wished to meet your whole life.”

I tried my best to remain impassive—I said gravely, “What kind of person is he?”

“A foreigner.” I was startled, and spoke cautiously.

This time, the gentleman used an English word: “Romantic.”

“Ha ha ha—” I laughed—“Astrologer sir, to whom are you saying such things? Look at my hair, snow has fallen there.”

My laughter irritated him. “I do not look at anyone’s hair, teeth, or nails; I look at their planets and stars.”

Now there was no doubt—‘this ancient counsel of time’—forty-three years later, Mircea and Amrita would meet. Do I, insignificant as I am, have the capacity to understand the meaning of this deep mystery?

“Sir, you are saying something dreadful,” I said lightly, “If such a thing happens, will I not be disgraced? Will I not be slandered?”

“No, no, why would there be slander?”

“Why not? Why not?” The gentleman was annoyed. “Why would you be disgraced? There is no question of dishonor.”

The invitation arrived, and so did the money. One by one, all the tangled knots were loosening, the path to departure was becoming easier. How strange—who is taking me by the hand and leading me toward this unknown future? Can life change so much while sitting in an ordinary middle-class Bengali household? My friends are urging me to write this novel of my life. Since Mircea has written, it is only right to complete that unfinished story. When I am about to bring to a close that beginning of 1930, why should it not become literature? Here it comes, that ‘feast of life for which the first was made—’

Whenever I think of writing a memoir, I think of Rabindranath.

Old age brings childhood memories vividly to mind. I used to see him always telling stories of his youth. That is how the idea for ‘Chhelebela’ was born. I often told him then, “Write your life story—if not now, it can be published later. Again and again, I asked him to write about his inner life.”

He would say, “It is the inner life I am writing—what more do you want to hear, about my romantic life?” “Not just I, many would want to hear. In your long life, so many people have loved you—why wouldn’t they? You are worthy of love. When you are present, it is impossible to love the Panchanan of the house next door. But the way you honor that love, giving it its rightful place, letting its sweetness unfold, taking from it what is worthy, and making it meaningful—that too is a poem, and it is no less beautiful than your compositions. No less wondrous. If you do not write this—it will be lost.” I would tell him further—“Later, many significant events of your life will be understood by ordinary people according to their own minds—vulgar people will write about them in vulgar ways, in the name of research—those who know nothing of who you truly are, they will write your life—”

--- Page 133 ---
They will write and write, with their worn-out pens, casting small shadows from their small minds. Your greatest poem will be lost.

He used to say, “What is meant to go will go, how much of me can you really keep? Will you be able to keep this body? Will you be able to preserve this very hand of mine? What is meant to go will leave, and what is meant to stay will remain in my songs and poetry.”

He would also say, “The path I have walked can never be retraced; that is a great sorrow.” Now I understand the meaning of those words, as I look back and feel lost under the weight of my present pain.

But it is also true that I did not summon the past—it broke through the door and merged into my present. For forty years, I heard nothing of him, and now, for the past few months, I hear of him constantly. I have heard that he has a wife, but no children. The two of them live together in one house; he spends day and night buried in his library, his face always in books. There is nothing in his life except reading and writing. This frightens me—then the man I am about to see is not a whole person at all. How can someone who has never felt the anklets on his chest, the kohl in his eyes, the playful dance of Bal Gopal in his hands, ever be complete? I hear his wife is very watchful; some say she never let him receive any of my letters. But I cannot believe that. There was nothing in my letters that would warrant withholding them. Why would she be jealous of me? I am nothing but a dream from the past.

That afternoon of September 18th, 1930, when Mircea left our house, I never received a single letter or any sign of his existence. That very sorrow burned a part of my heart, smoldering on—my love suffocated in the poisonous smoke of disbelief. And now, after so many years, I hear that he told someone, “I sent her a photograph from the gates of heaven. I could not write to her, so I sent it through Khoka. Seeing my bearded face in the photo, she told Khoka, ‘Trim the beard from the sides.’”

I wonder, is that true? Did I ever see such a photograph? When did I see it, where was I standing? These days, the events of 1930 float before my eyes like pictures. I can see where Father was standing when he said, “He’s kept a full beard,” and where I was. But when Khoka showed me that photograph, where was I, and where was he? I cannot recall this scene at all. Besides, if I had seen such a photo, would I have only said, “Trim the beard from the sides, please,” and nothing more? Is that possible? No, no, surely I would have wept and pleaded, “Oh Khoka, please let me meet him just once.” None of this comes back to me. I keep thinking and thinking, and as I think, it seems perhaps he only sent the photograph—then did he send a letter too? Surely he wrote a letter, for he wrote to have the photograph shown to me—then that letter I never saw.

Now I know that Khoka used to ask him for money from time to time, and to extract that money, he would tell him strange stories of my supposed suffering, distressing him and taking money from him. I decided I would go to Khoka. What shame do I have now, after so many ages?

One evening, I went to Khoka’s house. His misfortune has never ended, nor will it ever, with his twelve children! They are all grown now, yet the unending poverty of three generations persists. As I reached the front of the house, I saw it was dark. They live in a distinguished neighborhood of Kolkata, have been there for many years, the surroundings are bright with lights, but only this house is shrouded in darkness. After calling out for a while, Khoka came out. I saw him after so many years. He had grown completely old, his ugly face even uglier. The huge, flattened nose and the loose skin hanging beside his mouth—

--- Page 134 ---
He said, “Who? Who is it?”
“It’s me, don’t you recognize me?”
He peered into the darkness for a moment and said, “Oh, Ru. I haven’t seen you in so long, what’s the matter?”
“Why is there no light?”
“That’s not hard to guess—they cut it off because I couldn’t pay the bill.” Then, “Will you come inside? Let me bring a candle—”
Bringing the candle, Khoka looked at me, “You’re still very beautiful, Ru. How many women your age can say the same? The way girls are these days—”
“Is that so? I’m glad to hear it. Khoka, give me those letters from Mircea that you had, will you?”
“Mircea’s letters! After all these years, you remember him?”
“I’m going to write my memoirs, and I’ll have to write about him a little, won’t I?”
“If you have the courage, you certainly will. But if you’re writing your memoirs just to boast about your virtue, then don’t bother.”
“I’ve decided to write about Mircea very honestly.”
“I’m glad to hear it, truly happy. Everyone says you’re very brave. Our childhood was so beautiful, Ru, those days were so sweet. And what a wonderful boy Mircea was,” Khoka reminisced, “To throw him out like that in just an hour—uncle had no sense of justice, to act like that just because of the words of a silly little girl…” He kept grumbling.
“Khoka, give me the letters. I can’t remember anything. Maybe if I read them, many things will come back to me. How many letters were there, do you remember?” I tried to estimate in my mind—how many could he have written in ten months, maybe two a month.
“There were quite a few, but I tore them all up!”
“Why did you tear them up?”
“To be careful—who knows who might have seen them.”
“Khoka, what about his bearded photograph? There was no need to tear that up.”
“I gave that to you.”
“Never. I never saw that photo. I wouldn’t have forgotten if I had.”
“Oh, really! You completely forgot a person and went on living your domestic life, and now you say you wouldn’t have forgotten.”
“Khoka, give it to me, give me the photo, there must be some letters left, give them to me.”
“Come on, do you think I’ve been carrying around letters from forty years ago in my pocket? I’ll look for them, come back in a week.”
A week later I went again, my heart burning with anger—he wrote so many letters and I knew nothing—those letters were surely written to me, why would he write so many to this foolish man!
After making me wait several days, he finally sent word that he had found the letters. In the evening I went again—Khoka came down from upstairs, his hairy body bare, wearing only a loose lungi. The letters were tucked into the side of his lungi. Seeing those letters pressed against his sweaty, half-naked body made my skin crawl.
I reached out my hand—“Give them to me!”
“After much searching, I found these three letters. I’ll look for more!”
Taking the letters, I began to read them by candlelight. Khoka said, “Read them at home, Ru—let’s talk a bit, let’s talk about childhood; you know, I used to see the shadows of you two on the glass doors…”

--- Page 135 ---
I saw it. Then I said to Mircea, “Come, sit on this chair,” and then I went and stood there.

“Did you see? I saw your shadows there.” He was startled. Khoka kept on talking, while I began to read the letter—an exquisite letter—a European mind blossoming under the magic wand of ancient India, burning in the fire of love, and I could see the portrait of that beautiful mind. The letters had grown worn with age. It was clear he had written even before this letter.

Swargashram, Rishikesh  
1st November, 1930

Dear Khoka,  
After spending many days in solitude in the forests of Brahmapuri, I returned to the ashram last night and received your letter. There can never be any misunderstanding between you and me. There were some things you did not know, so I wrote openly. Let people call me Sri Chaitanya if they wish, such cheap jests do not touch me. I am happy that my two friends do not believe so. When you think of my renunciation, do not think like Europeans. Be Indian. Why should you understand better than I why Jesus had to spend twenty years in the desert in penance before proclaiming his message to the world? Think about it—Jesus preached for only eighteen months, and he changed the world. And I know of many intelligent minds of our time who, even after a lifetime of preaching, cannot change even their own minds. I do not wish to return to worldly life in haste. When Ramakrishna attained realization, the whole world came to his door. And Swami Vivekananda traveled the world, yet could not initiate anyone. There is no point in endless arguments—‘sadhana’ does not mean traveling the world giving lectures and writing books. Nor is it about whispering mantras into the ears of countless donkeys of this age. At least for me, sadhana means something else, something much more.

My father, mother, and sister are hoping for my return home, so I have not received any letters from them for a long time. Because my short visit home had already been arranged. My book, “Je Alo Nibe Geche,” reached my friend on the 18th of September—strange, isn’t it? Show this letter to Amrita—Yours, Mircea.

As I read the letter, I could feel his heart burning in anger toward my father. He does not like his lectures abroad, his pursuit of knowledge or teaching, but because he had thought of him as a great man, he compares him to those great souls. My father’s misfortune was that he wanted to be a god to his loved ones—had he been an ordinary man with faults and virtues, neither he nor we would have suffered so.

Swargashram  
25th November, 1930

Dear Khoka,  
I was waiting for your letter and read it quickly. Why am I not writing about that matter to my family? I have written a little to my sister. None of them know enough about me, about my thoughts, to fully understand my letter. They know only that I have decided to marry a Hindu girl and stay in India for another five years. They will blame me because that is all they know. But I never tell anyone the feelings of my heart… never—anyway, I will tell them, but not yet. I am waiting for dawn; do you think the morning will be long in coming? Do you see now what use my literary fame has been? That is why I no longer care for literature or fame. I read the Upanishads day and night, and parts of the Vedas. I went to the Gurukul—the college run by the Arya Samaj is famous—I was invited to give a lecture there on comparative religion, and I agreed because I would have the chance to speak Sanskrit at the Gurukul. Here, the students are inspired by the great ideals of ancient India—

--- Page 136 ---
Beneath the trees, in the open air, they live in a sacred way. Now, this is the only life I can endure. You asked me why I am not returning home, even for a short while. Because if I do, they will never let me come back! They will look into my eyes and understand what India has taught me. They will be afraid and will imprison me in Europe. Europe is now the continent of my dreams—there, I was free, I was young, and while living there, I was indifferent to life and at peace, in happiness. Now, all of this feels like a dream to me. The shores of Sicily and the ruins of Rome, the deep blue sky of Florence and the solitude of Switzerland—perhaps I will never see these again. I must stay here and fight. I will stay in India and fight with India itself. India has bound me, and India alone will show me the path to liberation. I am deceiving no one—not myself, not my country—so what is the use of returning home and speaking nonsense about my spirituality or boasting about my knowledge of yoga, when the truth is pain, nothing but pain? I wish you would try to understand with a little more sympathy. You still do not know what a terrible disaster has befallen me, how deep it runs. I will stay here. I am reading Sanskrit and Indian philosophy like a madman. I have found a lamp, and I read late into the night. It is very cold, very lonely, and there are a few scholars here. Please, show Amrita that photograph of mine—she knows how much it pains me that I do not even have the right to send her a picture of myself.

With love—Yours, Mircea

Swargashram  
5th December, 1930

Dear Khoka,

I have read your letter. There is no question of forgiving you. You have done the right thing, writing to your friend about your troubles; it is my own failing that I cannot help you... I am working like a madman. I am finishing my essay on the philosophy of the Upanishads. All I can say about the Upanishads is that this book is the consolation of my life, and will be my refuge in death as well. Whenever I grow weary, I write literature—not for leisure or for fame, but out of duty to my editor. Writing is my evening rest—that is why I am doing so much work... Now I must ask you something: have you shown Amrita my photograph? What is she doing now? In every letter you write that her crisis has not passed here. But that does not say anything, it is just a phrase. I hope you understand how anxious I am for news of her. I know, I am certain, that she is doing exactly what she should, and will continue to do so in the future, but I want to know more about her. Why are you not showing her my letters? When she is alone, show her my letters, and let me know her thoughts by any means. I want to know the true state of her mind. There is no use in writing that she will not forget the past—because 'the past' is just a word. Do you understand what I mean?

The mad experience I have had (you may call it love), which for the past three months has caused me infinite suffering—now it has begun to show its bright side. That girl has changed my life, changed it for the better a thousandfold. This awakening of mine is as glorious as the sunrise. I have seen the truth, and I have seen the filth of the social world and the world of knowledge as well. Amidst all the cowardice and filth around me, I feel pure. The more I see the commerce of lies, the more peace I find in my suffering—I do not wish to enjoy this life, nor will I; I do not want happiness, I do not want it—Yours, your friend Mircea

... I am reading the letters and thinking, Mircea has scattered pearls in the forest of Benares. He had promised my father not to write to me, so he wrote to Khoka—he was certain Khoka would give me the letters.

--- Page 137 ---
He believed in me, and I would do what was necessary. He trusted that I would do the right thing at the right time. He had so much faith in me—and what did I do? All my life, I thought that he had deceived me. But wasn’t it I who could have acted? This was my country, I had so many friends here, all of them exceptional men. Was there truly no one to help me? Then why did I do nothing? Because I believed that in such matters, it was the man who must take action—why should a woman step forward, why should she be the one to pursue? That, too, was a superstition, a prejudice. I was a foolish, inept girl. Of course, I did not know that my father had sent the police and warned that if he tried to write to me, he would be expelled from the country. Father had written to him, “You have defiled my home—you were a snake hidden in the grass, and when a snake raises its head, it must be struck. That is what I have done...” 

So he had no way out, and yet, all my life, I doubted him and suffered. And meanwhile, Khoka lied to him—at least, when he spoke of showing him the photograph, he must have mentioned the letters as well, so is it impossible that he, too, thought I deliberately did nothing, that I, too, was not true... These letters were within two miles of my house, and I went to them forty-three years later. This is karma, fate. Khoka is going on in his grand way, “Let it burn later. Now, let’s have a little chat—”

I said, “Owl,” trembling with anger. He was startled. He could never have imagined such a word would come from the mouth of a refined woman like me.

“What are you saying, Ru, what are you saying?”

“Nothing, just the name of an animal.”

“To whom are you saying it?”

“To that animal.” 

“Khoka, why didn’t you show me these letters?”

“But I did show you.”

“You showed me? Liar! Tell me, why didn’t you show me?”

“If I had, what would you have done? What was there for you to do?”

I looked at that man—his face glistening with sweat—in the candlelight, he seemed like some primitive beast. Why did this man do such a thing, act with such enmity? There are no answers to these questions, and even if there were, what would it matter?

“I’m leaving, Khoka. Thank you for keeping these three letters all this time.” In my mind, I knew my anger at him was false; could he even understand what these letters meant, perhaps he hadn’t even read them properly. I opened my bag and put the letters away. Khoka said, “You know what times we’re living in, how long it’s been since I’ve earned anything—if you could give me fifty rupees, I could collect my rations tomorrow.” I threw some money down and left—my skin crawling with disgust. Khoka called after me, “Do you know where he is?”

“Of course—”

“Tell me, where?”

“Exactly where he always was.”

Khoka was astonished, “Is that an address?”

“That’s the only address I know, brother.”

I was amazed, thinking how the year 1930 seemed to conspire to keep us apart, and now, in 1973, the fates had conspired for us to meet. My confidence was growing; perhaps I would be able to reach him. I was astonished that someone who wrote such letters in 1930, so true and steady—how could he write such a book in 1933? I was afraid that the gentle, tender heart revealed in every line of these letters—was that heart still alive, or had it withered under the weight of scholarship? I had heard of his scholarly fame, and I was afraid. I know that the burden of knowledge can crush a person. My father used to say, “Knowledge is a load on many of our backs, like a beast of burden—

--- Page 138 ---
As we move forward, I have seen only in Rabindranath that knowledge runs in his blood and has sprouted wings on both sides, making him light and letting him soar in the sky.

As the time of departure draws nearer—I cannot understand from where this whirlpool has suddenly come and turned the course of this river of life, what its purpose is, what its outcome will be!

My husband reassures me, “You can be certain that something good will come of this in life. There is a purpose to it, and both of us will benefit from it.”

“What benefit will you get from it?”

“Won’t I? I already am. Not just me, all of us are coming to know him better, we are revering him. He too is coming to know himself. He is being tested—how much love, how much patience, how much freedom from resentment he can possess—and he is passing these tests effortlessly. Sometimes I wonder, sitting in the midst of our ordinary household, what my husband has become—could that silent ascetic judge sitting in a cave in Rishikesh have become the same?”

I go about my tasks as if in a dream! I am writing replies to all the invitations I have received, taking care of all the worldly affairs, but my mind is detached from all this, absorbed in thought. I have picked up my pen only to write about this feeling, not to write about what happened in my childhood, because a childhood love and its sudden, untimely end is not a special event—such things have always happened, are happening, and will happen. Many have written about it, with much stronger pens. I am writing about what happened forty-two years later, about that particular event, because it is a wondrous event, perhaps even unprecedented—I do not know the language to express this feeling—the words I wish to say are not being said. The sentences seem lifeless, mere shadows—incapable of containing my emotion.

I have already mentioned that nearly a year before Mircea came to mind again—that is, before Sergei’s arrival—I had begun to feel a strange restlessness within myself—I cannot express the intensity of that restlessness. The tune of that song would float through my mind—“I am restless, I am thirsty for the distant”—this feeling of mine could only be expressed in melody. My restlessness had grown so much that I would say to this person or that, “I want to go abroad, somewhere, can’t something be arranged?” We have a colleague who is a priest, he knows many people, I would pester him again and again—“Please arrange for me to go somewhere abroad.”

“Where do you want to go?”

“Anywhere, outside the country—”

“Why, what has this country done wrong?”

I would sulk. This person won’t do it for me.

“All right, all right, it can be arranged, but you must tell me where you want to go?”

“To Japan, America, England—anywhere.”

The gentleman would look at me, “You are an amazing woman.”

This restlessness was like the agitated sea at moonrise, as if some unknown force from far away was calling me. It was untying my bonds—

“From the horizon I hear your call, through the earth a sprout rises.
The prison trembles.”

My prison would break, Mircea is only the instrument for that. What prison? The prison of believing this world, as known by the five senses, to be the only reality, and holding that knowledge as absolute truth—that prison of delusion. I know this feeling of mine will last only a few days, it cannot last forever, but even for a short while, that prison broke for me, and I glimpsed a wondrous form of eternity. I was elevated to another state and saw that what we call the past is, in truth, not the past at all, it has not gone anywhere. It has no beginning, no middle, no end.

--- Page 139 ---
There is none. ‘Nayantam na madhyam na punastavadim, pashyami Vishweshwara Vishwarupam’—what was the use of seeing this? I ask in anguish, if there is a God, then tell me, what did I gain from it? But I already know the answer—there is no accounting for profit and loss here. So many events occur in life that have no practical purpose, yet it is with these unreal, seemingly meaningless events that the world of humans is built—not that of beasts. At least in these past eight months, I have come to understand this much—I am not a beast, I am human, and I have the right to behold the cosmic form.

Mahakala cradles me in his arms and dances, his loosened matted hair enveloping my eyes, my face, my whole body. What has become of my past and future, east and west, far and near—I do not know. My prison has shattered, shame, fear, the bonds of family and kin have all fallen away. Only love—timeless love—like the steadfast pole star in the radiant blue sky, is guiding my way. It will carry me across the vast ocean.

Upon reaching the airport, I felt the urge to bow to my husband. The gentle breeze of his love surrounds me, soothing my body and soul. But I cannot bow here—he would be embarrassed. So I said softly, “All my life, you have given me so much freedom.” He smiled a little, “Does your freedom reside in my pocket, that I can take it out and hand it to you from time to time? Your freedom is your own.”

## Na Hanyate: Part Four

On the grand city’s avenue, I said to my newly acquainted companion, John, “When we reach Woodland Road, let me know.” I had obtained his address with much difficulty—back in my own country, I had collected his address and telephone number. It was hardly necessary to go through so much trouble, but back home, the act of obtaining it had become a ritual. I want the correct address—how long have I wandered with only vague directions?

The car moved on—new country, new city, unfamiliar faces passing by, yet I was headed toward a goal that was deeply familiar. My mind was dazed. I felt as though I were in a dream. This body of mine, this life I have lived for so long, and the ‘me’ who now sits here with the address in hand—there is a great distance between them. It is difficult to unite these two. It is hard to remember—who am I?

John said, “We are entering Woodland now.”

On either side—are there rows of sprawling forests, or city houses? I do not know. I see large houses with little gardens, and then again, I see shaded roads covered by tall trees—Woodland. Which is real? Who knows? One scene inside, another outside! The inner one is more real, for it is the mind that sees, the mind that is the true spectator. The world I now inhabit is a world of the mind. How many sleepless nights have I thought of this road, imagining a winding path shaded by trees—even if there are no trees, where will that vision go? I sit with my eyes half-closed—Woodland seems to drift toward me from some vast void—fragmented roads, full of shadows.

The car stopped. Molly said, “Amrita, this girl will take you wherever you want to go.”
I asked, “Did you call the Professor? Do you know when he will be at home?”

“We did. But his secretary said his schedule is uncertain.”
Shirley, a young girl—a student at this university. We sat down to eat at a cafeteria. Shirley set the food on the table, but I could not recognize the dishes, and the fork kept slipping from my hand. Shirley looked a little surprised. As we stepped outside, I asked, “Do you know where the Theological College is?”

139

--- Page 140 ---
He said, “Isn’t it just a block away from here?”
“Will I be able to walk there?”
“Of course, it’s just a block that way, why wouldn’t you?”
We move forward. I try to make conversation, striving to sound perfectly calm and natural.
—“You’re going to meet someone at that college.”
“Yes, I have a friend there.”
“Does he know you’re coming?”
“No—”
“How long has it been since you last met?”
“Only forty-two years.”
“Forty-two years you haven’t seen each other? Then you won’t even recognize one another.”
“I don’t think so. Is forty-two years really such a long time? How old is this earth, who knows the date of the sun’s birth?” Suddenly it occurs to me that perhaps I shouldn’t have spoken these thoughts aloud. What must she be thinking? But Sharly isn’t surprised, her astonishment is for another reason; she says, “I can’t even imagine what it would be like to meet a friend after forty-two years.”
“You will, when the time comes!”
“Dramatic.”
“Everyone says life itself is a drama, let’s see what kind of drama this turns out to be.”
Sharly said, “Here we are.” Suddenly, as if from the void, a signboard appeared above the garden. Just a signboard with an address—can it really carry so much meaning? Have I seen Holy Grail?
I float on air up to the large front door of the house—Sharly opens the door, beside it a huge board lists many names. She asks, “Who do you want to meet?”
My breath is catching, it’s impossible to speak, so I point with my finger.
“Oh, him—he’s a great man, a famous person...”
I move forward, Sharly slips her hand into mine and leads me on—why, who knows? Am I some frail old woman? I ask, “What is he famous for?”
“He’s a great scholar.”
Scholar! My heart sinks again. Who have I come to see! I ask again, “Sharly, is he a scholar or a wise man?”
Sharly is flustered. She says, “I don’t know, I’m just a simple student, I don’t know him.”
We’ve reached the elevator. Someone standing there says, “This is a private lift—visitors have to take the stairs, it’s only three floors.”
Sharly says firmly, “Open the lift door, she crossed thousands of miles last night—she won’t be able to climb even a single step.”
I wonder why she says this—am I trembling? Sharly is holding me inside the lift, and who knows from where, in this faraway foreign land, a little mother has appeared for me—she’s carrying me as if in her arms!
The lift stops on the third floor. We arrive at a library. It’s as if I open my eyes from sleep—oh, a library, Shubharanj, I pass through the library into a corridor—rooms on both sides—I pass one room, and Sharly pauses and says, “There, the professor is inside.”
I enter the room.
The very moment I step in, the elderly man utters a sound, “Oh,” then stands up,

--- Page 141 ---
He sat down again, then stood up once more, then turned his back to me. What is this behavior! Has he recognized me? How could he? He hasn’t even looked at me—after all these years, could he recognize me by the sound of my footsteps? Impossible. In any case, this little drama shouldn’t unfold in front of Sharly. When I returned to the door, I saw Sharly standing there as if petrified, her eyes wide with astonishment. The sole witness to this extraordinary meeting between two old women. I said, “Sharly, you go on ahead. I’ll come to you in a little while.”

My mind is calm now, steady, no excitement remains; whatever needed to be done, has been done.

“It’s your first day in this vast city—won’t you lose your way?”

“No, no, I won’t lose my way.” In my heart I thought, having come this far, having crossed the great ocean of time, I shall not lose my way even in this universe.

Turning away from the door, I find I cannot reach Mircea—books are scattered everywhere, mountains of books all around, piled up to the ceiling. A strange shiver runs through me, my hands and feet tremble—not from cold, but from fear! I have heard that delicate creatures, when crushed under stone, become fossils—has something like that happened to him? ... I see him—his head completely bald, only some grey hair at the nape—his frame as thin as ever—just as restless—one moment picking up papers from the table, the next moment putting them down again. His frail body seems to tremble in the air!

“Mircea, why are you standing with your face turned away?”

“I will not look at you. I am waiting for someone else.”

“For whom are you waiting, for whom?”

“For an income tax officer.”

“Income tax officer?”

“Yes, yes.”

“Don’t be foolish, Mircea. Do you know who I am?” Without waiting for his answer, I continued, “Have you recognized me? Do you know who I am, who I am?”

“Certainly, certainly,” he nods his head. It is the same Mircea, exactly the same young man of twenty-three; within his sixty-three years, that same boy sits. He used to tell me, “You use the word ‘terrible’ so much—everything is ‘terrible’ to you.” Others have told me the same. Perhaps I still speak that way. The way he says, “Certainly, certainly,” nodding his head—it’s just the same. What a profound mystery, what infinite wonder—with my whole being, I know it is him. It is he—he alone, no one else. And I? Who am I? I too am that same self, “The undying spirit of my seventeen-year-old self still seeks.” “Tell me, who am I, tell me, you—”

“You are Amrita. The moment you set foot on this land, I knew it....”

“How did you know?” Silence.

“Tell me, tell me, won’t you?”

“Hmm, Ed told me.”

“Ed didn’t even know when I was coming.”

“Well, I knew—I just knew, that’s all.”

“Turn around, turn around, dearest Mircea. I have come from so far just to see you—won’t you turn around, won’t you look at me?”

“Listen, Amrita,” he grips the bookshelf with his restless hand—as if he might fall without its support—“Listen, I see the whole matter differently. I’m not saying my way is right. Perhaps yours is right—no, not perhaps, certainly yours is right.”

“Why didn’t you answer my letter? Did you receive it?”

“Yes, a short one, just a few lines.”

--- Page 142 ---
"Even so, why didn’t you answer? There’s such a thing as courtesy, isn’t there?"

"Who was thinking of courtesy?" Then, after a brief pause, "I’m telling you, my experience was so incomparable, so beautiful, that I never thought it could be touched again—that’s why I have kept you beyond the bounds of time and place."

"The real truth is, you thought I had forgotten you in my new life."

"No, no, no, Amrita, not for a moment, not for a single instant did I think you could ever forget me." He pulled a book down from the shelf. "I just didn’t know that you wanted to see me—"

"Why?"

"We know there are so many beautiful things—the peaks of Sumeru, the snow-crowned Himalayas—can we go there? We know they are ours, yet we cannot reach them. Still, that does not mean they are lost to us; they remain the most beautiful dreams, hidden in the secret depths of my inner world."

"But look, I managed it, I came."

"You are Amrita, indestructible Amrita—you can do what I cannot. Your culture is thousands of years old, your history and mine are not the same—immortal India—"

"Is that so? But I heard you’re Indian too?"

"Oh yes, I tell everyone I’m Indian."

"I want to hear all this. Be Mircea again, let me see you today."

He is standing, but not still—there are at least three yards between us, I am speaking loudly—after all, we are old now, your hearing must be failing... He seems agitated, almost frantic.

"How can I see you? Did Dante ever think he would see his Beatrice again with mortal eyes?"

I am trembling too, his madness is making me angry. This man has truly created an unreal world and lives inside it. Where did he bring in Dante and Beatrice from—‘beyond time and space’ again? Have I become a ghost? In what otherworldly, misty heaven do you dwell, Mircea? I am the flesh-and-blood Amrita of this real world, standing in your study. This is the truth. You must accept this truth. Let go of your escapist tendencies."

"What am I to do, Amrita? You have a husband, I have a wife—what can I say now?"

I am astonished, dumbfounded. What is Mircea doing?

"—Mircea, you have read so much, yet gained no wisdom? Is love a thing, that you can take it from one and give it to another? Is it property? Your jewelry? Love is a light, Mircea, a light. Just as there is the light of intellect, the light of knowledge, so too is there the light of love. The light of intellect has its limits, it shines in one place only, but the light of love is the most radiant of all, it reveals the true form of everything—when this light shines, the whole universe is filled with love—the unlovable becomes beloved—believe me, Mircea, after remembering you, my husband has become even dearer to me, I have never loved him so much before. Will you believe that?"

He nods, "Of course, of course, it’s true, it’s true—"

"What is true?"

"What you say is the eternal truth, you always speak the infallible truth."

"Yes, after truth comes the mind, I have surrendered mine. That is why I have come to acknowledge the truth. It was not easy, Mircea, to descend from the pedestal of honor before family, society, relatives, sons and daughters. Some may think I’ve gone senile. When more people know, what will they think—don’t you know our country? They’ll throw dust at me. Coming to see you after forty-two years—is that an easy thing?"

--- Page 143 ---
"Not at all, I could never do it. So many times I had the chance to visit my dreamland, India, but I never went—how could I go there?"

"Because I am here?"

He shakes his head—"That's right..."

"And I came only because you are here. Tell me, where did I find such courage?"

"I am wondering that myself, where did you find it?"

"From Gandhiji. I thought, if he could do it, why couldn't I? And if I couldn't, then what use was it to give speeches on the day of his death! I do give a lot of speeches, you know."

"Did Gandhi become so great?"

"Yes, yes, by 1930, he had become much greater than you knew him to be. He had devoted himself to the service of people, he wasn't just reading scriptures anymore... That's why I'm asking you, what kind of character did you draw of me in your book? I couldn't find myself there."

"Fantasy, fantasy—I wanted to make you a mysterious goddess whose actions have no explanation. Like Kali, the mistress of miracles and the impossible."

"Don't you dare compare me to Kali... Always this with you... Am I really that dark?"

"Alright, alright, like Durga then, who can accomplish the impossible; inscrutable, a weapon in one hand, a blessing in the other—an enigma. The enigma that you were."

"I'm telling you, fantasy has its own beauty, but the beauty of truth is brighter, and half-truths are terrifying. That's why your book is a nightmare to me. Besides, I was a very simple, straightforward girl, Mircea, at most I sometimes pretended to be a philosopher, that's all. The mystery was your creation—you love mysteries. But this time, I have come to do the impossible—"

He has turned away, and I have reached out a hand toward him—my heart is steady, unwavering—this haze of mystery around him, I will dispel it—we will see each other on the real earth of this world.

"Beloved, awaken, awaken, awaken."

Mircea does not turn, his head is bowed—he refuses to turn.

"What do you want, Amrita?"

"Peace, I want peace from you."

"Ha ha ha," he laughs like a madman. "How can I give you peace when I have no peace myself? How can I give peace when I have no peace in me..."

What will happen, I am anxious with fear—has he lost his mind? Can he not touch something so beautiful, so deep, so supremely true? Suddenly I say—"Mircea, you used to play the piano so wonderfully, do you still play?"

"No, no, I gave that up long ago."

"Why?"

"What's the use, waste of time—"

I wonder, with what would he play music now, his fingers are as stiff as book covers, the veins and nerves have dried up—no, it will never be, that sweetness will never rain from the sky again.

"Mircea, here I am, standing in your room, flesh and blood, a real person, not a symbol, not a myth, but a human being full of happiness and sorrow, full of infinite possibilities. Beatrice went to heaven as a ghost and met Dante there, but I have come in this life—does that mean nothing?"

He turns around, a little out of breath—"Astonishing! What a supreme wonder, truly, that's what I tell the pessimists, who knows the infinite possibilities of life, who knows what might happen! One day—"

--- Page 144 ---
"Then return."
"So he returned. But he does not lift his face, he keeps his head bowed—he is not yet ready to look at me. I plead, 'Why won't you look at me? Didn't you write in your book that the day we meet, you would look into my eyes—have you forgotten?'

'That was so long ago. Forty years, ah, forty years.'

'You know, people ask me how long you stayed at our house—I can't remember—tell me, how long was it?'

'A thousand years—'

'Then? Then do you not know who you are, who we are? I have come to see that very you, whom weapon cannot pierce, fire cannot burn—shastra chhede na, agni dohe na jare—'

He said it in Sanskrit, 'Na hanyate hanyamane sharire—'

'Then? You are the one who has neither beginning nor end—na antam na madhyam na punastavadim—I want to see that very you—look at me once. Believe me, in a moment I will carry you across forty years—you will see, we are standing right there where we first met. If you look at me, you will become immortal, Mircea, you will become immortal.'

Mircea lifted his face... I saw his gaze was fixed. What a disaster! What I had feared has come to pass—his eyes have turned to stone. He will never be able to see me again. What will happen! What will happen! Oh God, what is the way! I can no longer light a lamp in those eyes. I have no lamp in my hand—walking so far, I do not know when the oil ran out and the wick burned to ash. Frightened, I am no longer Amrita—I have become a mortal being. I began to think like him—ah, forty years—how late it has become—a heart-breaking sorrow circles the room as a long sigh, and now I turned away. I must reach that door. I must turn the brass handle—then the door will open. I walk the same path, stepping over the stones of books, to go to Sharly. Suddenly, from behind, I heard Mircea’s voice—as if drifting from a great distance—

'Wait a moment, Amrita—why are you breaking down now when you were so brave for so many years? After showing such courage all this time, why do you falter now? I tell you, I will come to you, not here, but there on the banks of the Ganges, I will show you my real self, I will show you my real self on the shores of the Ganges....'

I am not a pessimist, so all this while, in my broken heart, a little bird of hope fluttered with broken wings. The moment I heard Mircea’s words, something happened. That tiny bird suddenly became a phoenix—have you ever seen a phoenix? It looks just like an albatross—that immense king of birds shook its vast white wings and soared upward with me, and then the ceiling of his study opened like Pandora’s box—the walls vanished, and the stones of books turned into waves of water, I heard the sound of rippling water.

Caught in the illusion of hope, that great-winged bird, crossing Lake Michigan, told me as it flew toward an unknown continent—'Do not fear, Amrita, you yourself will light the lamp in his eyes.'

I asked anxiously, 'When? When?'

It replied, 'The day you meet on the Milky Way, that day is not far away—'
